# Te Reo Māori Warm-Up — Full Lesson Plans

All curriculum content across Terms 1–4, Weeks 1–10. Each weekday (Monday–Thursday) follows an I Do / We Do / You Do structure, with a Friday quiz reviewing the week's content.

---

## Term 1

### Week 1

#### Monday: Ngā mihi me ngā kupu hou

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greet, farewell and acknowledge people · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings & farewell; AO 1.6 — Politeness conventions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Listen & discover**

Today we are going to learn our very first words in te reo Māori. I am going to say some words and phrases, and I want you to just listen and notice the sounds. There is nothing to write down — just watch and listen.

> **[Kia ora](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kia%20ora)** — Hello / Thank you
> **[Tēnā koe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%93n%C4%81%20koe)** — Hello (formal, to one person)
> **Kei te [pēhea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%93hea) koe?** — How are you?
> **Kei te [pai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pai) au** — I am good
> **E [noho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=noho) rā** — Goodbye (to someone staying)
> **Hei [konā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kon%C4%81) rā** — Goodbye (casual)

Notice the vowels. In te reo Māori, every vowel makes only ONE sound — always:

**A** = "ah"  ·  **E** = "eh"  ·  **I** = "ee"  ·  **O** = "oh"  ·  **U** = "oo"

Say "Kia ora" — KIA = "kee-ah", ORA = "oh-rah". Kia ora. Simple, consistent, beautiful.

*Tip: The vowel sounds never change. Once you know them, you can pronounce any Māori word. That's a superpower.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build together**

Let's practise these greetings together. I will say the Māori — you say it back. Then we will figure out when you would use each one.

- **Kia ora** — When would you say this? (casual hello or thank you)
- **Tēnā koe** — This is more formal. When might a student use it? (to a teacher, elder, or visitor)
- **Kei te pēhea koe?** — Break it apart: Kei te = right now, pēhea = how, koe = you. Put it together!
- **Kei te pai au** — kei te = right now, pai = good, au = I/me. Say it: "Right now good I."

_Discussion prompt: Which greeting would you use for your best friend? Which for the school principal? Why does te reo have different words for this?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your turn**

Practise saying these out loud — alone or with a partner. Then try the written challenges.

- Say **Kia ora** to three different people in the room right now.
- Your teacher walks in. Which greeting do you use? Say it aloud: **Tēnā koe**.
- Someone asks **Kei te pēhea koe?** — how do you reply? Write and say your answer.
- ✦ Bonus: Teach the five vowel sounds to a partner. Can they say **Tēnā koe** perfectly?

#### Tuesday: Ko wai koe? Ko wai au.

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.2: Introduce self and others · AO 1.4: Personal information (name)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.4 — Personal information

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Watch & discover**

Yesterday we learned how to greet someone. Today we learn how to tell people who we are. In te reo Māori, there is a special word we use when we identify or name something. Watch how it works.

> **Ko** Aroha **tōku [ingoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ingoa)**.
> = My name is Aroha.
> 
> **Ko** [wai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wai) **tōu ingoa?**
> = What is your name?
> 
> **Ko** Wiremu **tōna ingoa**.
> = His/her name is Wiremu.

The word **Ko** is used whenever we are saying what something **IS** — its identity or name.

The pattern is simple:
**Ko** + [name] + [whose] + **ingoa**

"Ingoa" means "name". "Tōku" = my. "Tōu" = your. "Tōna" = his or her.

Notice: te reo Māori does NOT have separate words for "he" and "she". Tōna covers both.

*Tip: Ko is like a pointer — it points at what something IS. Ko = "it is" or "my name is."*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build together**

Let's build Ko sentences together. I will give you the pieces — you put them in the right order.

- How would you say "My name is [your name]"? What are the pieces? Ko + [your name] + tōku + ingoa
- How would you ask someone their name? Remember: "wai" means "who/what". Ko wai tōu ingoa?
- Your friend's name is Hana. How would you tell someone her name? Ko Hana tōna ingoa.
- Now put it all together — greet someone AND introduce yourself: **Kia ora. Ko [name] tōku ingoa.**

_Discussion prompt: Why do you think te reo Māori uses Ko rather than just saying "my name is"? What does it feel like to say your name this way?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your turn**

Use the Ko structure to introduce yourself — written or spoken.

- Write: **Ko [your name] tōku ingoa.** Say it aloud.
- Ask the person next to you their name using **Ko wai tōu ingoa?** Then introduce them to someone else.
- Write three Ko sentences: your name, a friend's name, your teacher's name.
- ✦ Bonus: Add a greeting. Write a two-sentence introduction: hello + your name. Practise saying it smoothly.

#### Wednesday: Kei te haere au — I am going

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Communicate about location · AO 2.5: Physical characteristics and feelings*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Location & movement; AO 2.5 — Feelings & states

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Watch & discover**

So far we have learned how to greet people and say our names. Now we are going to learn how to talk about what is happening RIGHT NOW. Watch how te reo Māori builds a present-tense sentence.

> **Kei te** [haere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=haere) **au**.
> = I am going.
> 
> **Kei te** [kai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kai) **koe**.
> = You are eating.
> 
> **Kei te** [moe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=moe) **ia**.
> = He/she is sleeping.

The pattern for right-now actions is always:

**Kei te** + [action word] + [person]

"Kei te" is like "-ing" in English — it shows the action is happening NOW.

Action words: **haere** = go/going · **kai** = eat/eating · **moe** = sleep/sleeping

People: **au** = I/me · **koe** = you · **ia** = he/she

*Tip: In te reo Māori, the action comes FIRST, then the person. "Is-going I" not "I am going." The structure is always Time/Action → Action word → Person.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build together**

Use the Kei te structure to build these sentences together. I will give you the pieces — you put them in order.

- How do you say "I am eating"? Pieces: Kei te · kai · au → **Kei te kai au.**
- How do you say "She is sleeping"? Pieces: Kei te · moe · ia → **Kei te moe ia.**
- How do you say "You are going"? Pieces: Kei te · haere · koe → **Kei te haere koe.**
- Now say all three out loud one after another — notice how the structure never changes, only the action word and person swap.

_Discussion prompt: The structure Kei te + action + person never changes. How is that different from English, where we change "I go / she goes"? Which feels easier?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your turn**

Build your own Kei te sentences using the words you have learned.

- Write and say: **Kei te kai au.** Now swap to "you" — Kei te kai koe.
- Use **haere** (going) in three sentences — one for au, one for koe, one for ia.
- Look around the room. What are people doing? Write one Kei te sentence about what a classmate is doing.
- ✦ Bonus: Can you invent a sentence using a new action? Try **kōrero** (speaking) or **noho** (sitting).

#### Thursday: He aha tēnā? Kōrero!

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.2: Introductions · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.6 — Politeness

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Watch & discover**

Today we put everything together into a real conversation. Watch me roleplay a full exchange — from greeting to introducing to asking what someone is doing.

> **A:** Kia ora! Ko wai tōu ingoa?
> **B:** Ko Mere tōku ingoa. Ko wai tōu?
> **A:** Ko Tama tōku ingoa. Kei te pēhea koe?
> **B:** Kei te pai au, kia ora. Kei te [aha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aha) koe?
> **A:** Kei te haere au ki te [kura](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kura).
> **B:** [Āe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%80e)! Hei konā rā.
> **A:** Hei konā rā!

New phrase: **Kei te aha koe?** — What are you doing?
**Aha** = what. So "Kei te aha koe?" literally means "Right now what you?" → "What are you doing?"

New phrase: **ki te kura** — to school (**ki** = to, **kura** = school)

New word: **Āe** — Yes!

You now have everything you need for a genuine short conversation in te reo Māori.

*Tip: A real conversation has a rhythm: greet → introduce → ask → answer → farewell. Once you know the pattern, you just swap in the pieces.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Practise together**

Let's build the conversation step by step as a class. I will be Person A — you are Person B. We go slowly and swap roles.

- I say: **Kia ora!** — You reply with a greeting and ask my name.
- I tell you my name is Rangi. You introduce yourself. Then ask: **Kei te pēhea koe?**
- I say: **Kei te pai au.** Now ask me what I am doing: **Kei te aha koe?**
- I say I am going to school. How do you say goodbye? Use **Hei konā rā** or **E noho rā**.

_Discussion prompt: Which part of the conversation felt most natural? Which part do you want more practice with? What other things might you want to say that we haven't learned yet?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your turn**

With a partner, have a full conversation using only te reo Māori. Use every structure from this week.

- With a partner: greet each other using **Kia ora** or **Tēnā koe**.
- Introduce yourselves using **Ko [name] tōku ingoa**. Ask each other's names.
- Ask **Kei te pēhea koe?** and answer **Kei te pai au**.
- ✦ Bonus: Add **Kei te aha koe?** and answer with a Kei te sentence. Try to have a 6-line conversation entirely in te reo!

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Kei te pēhea koe? — What does this mean?
  - Where are you going? 
  - How are you? ✅
  - What is your name? 
  - Are you good? 
- Which vowel rule is correct for te reo Māori?
  - Vowels change depending on surrounding letters 
  - Each vowel has only one fixed sound, always ✅
  - Vowels are usually silent at the end of a word 
  - Only "a" and "e" have consistent sounds 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko Hana tōna ingoa." What does this mean?
  - Ko is her greeting word 
  - Her name is Hana ✅
  - Hana's name is different 
  - Hana says hello 
- How do you say "What is your name?" in te reo Māori?
  - Ko wai tōku ingoa? 
  - Ko wai tōna ingoa? 
  - Ko wai tōu ingoa? ✅
  - Ko tōu wai ingoa? 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Kei te moe ia." The word "ia" means:
  - I / me 
  - you 
  - he or she ✅
  - going 
- What is the correct order for a present-tense sentence in te reo Māori?
  - Person → Kei te → action 
  - Action → Kei te → person 
  - Kei te → person → action 
  - Kei te → action → person ✅

**Thursday questions**

- "Kei te aha koe?" — What does this question ask?
  - How are you? 
  - Where are you going? 
  - What are you doing? ✅
  - Who are you? 
- Which phrase means "Goodbye" to someone who is leaving?
  - Kia ora 
  - E noho rā 
  - Hei konā rā ✅
  - Tēnā koe 

---

### Week 2

#### Monday: Ngā kupu hou — Kīnaki me ngā kai

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Communicate about number · AO 2.5: Physical characteristics and feelings (food/taste)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Number & quantity; AO 2.5 — Feelings & taste

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Commas in lists and new food vocabulary**

This week we are building our vocabulary for food and drink — kupu pai mō te kai me te inu. We are also going to learn how to say lists of things in te reo Māori. Listen carefully to how we join words together.

> **He [āporo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81poro)** — an apple
> **He [panana](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=panana)** — a banana
> **He [tōmate](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%8Dmate)** — a tomato
> **He [parāoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=par%C4%81oa)** — bread
> **He [miraka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=miraka)** — milk
> **He [wai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wai)** — water
> **He [tī](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%AB)** — tea
> **He inu [kōhua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Dhua)** — hot drink / coffee

In te reo Māori, when we list things, we simply say them one after another — no word for "and" needed in a basic list:

**He āporo, he panana, he tōmate.**
An apple, a banana, a tomato.

But when we want to say "and also" to add something extra, we use **me**:

**He āporo me he panana.**
An apple and a banana.

And to say "I want" something, we combine what you already know:
**Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i te āporo.**
I want to eat the apple.

*Tip: Me = and (joining two things). In a list of three or more, you can just list them — no joining word needed until the last item.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build food lists together**

Let's practise the new food words and build some lists. I will show you the items — you say them in te reo.

- Look at these three things: an apple, bread, water. How do you say them as a list? **He āporo, he parāoa, he wai.**
- Now say: "I want to drink the water." Use Kei te hiahia + ki te inu + i te wai → **Kei te hiahia au ki te inu i te wai.**
- Add me to join two things: "milk and tea" → **He miraka me he tī.**
- Now put it together: "I want bread and milk." → **Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i te parāoa me te miraka.**

_Discussion prompt: Which of these food words sound similar to the English word? Why do you think that is? What does this tell us about how languages borrow from each other?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your turn with food words**

Use the new vocabulary to build your own sentences about food and drink.

- Write a list of three foods you like using **He… me he… me he…**
- Say what you want to eat right now: **Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i te…**
- Ask a partner what they want to drink: **Kei te hiahia koe ki te inu i te aha?**
- ✦ Bonus: Can you say five food words from memory without looking? Write them with their English meanings.

#### Tuesday: He aha tēnei? He aha tērā? — This and that

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.2: Introduce self and others · AO 2.5: Physical descriptions — He structure with demonstratives*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.2 — Introductions & identity; AO 2.5 — Descriptions — He structure

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Demonstratives: tēnei, tēnā, tērā**

You already know He — "it is a…". Today we add pointer words so we can say THIS thing or THAT thing. Watch how they work.

> **[tēnei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%93nei)** — this (near me)
> **[tēnā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%93n%C4%81)** — that (near you)
> **[tērā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%93r%C4%81)** — that (over there, away from both of us)
> 
> **He aha tēnei?** — What is this?
> **He āporo tēnei.** — This is an apple.
> **He aha tēnā?** — What is that (near you)?
> **He parāoa tēnā.** — That is bread.
> **He aha tērā?** — What is that over there?
> **He kura tērā.** — That is a school.

The three pointer words work just like "this" and "that" in English — they tell us HOW FAR AWAY the thing is.

**tēnei** — near ME (the speaker)
**tēnā** — near YOU (the listener)
**tērā** — away from BOTH of us

They slot into your He sentence at the end, after the noun:
**He [noun] tēnei/tēnā/tērā.**

And the question is always: **He aha** + the pointer word.

*Tip: Think of tēnei/tēnā/tērā as three distances: HERE · THERE · WAY OVER THERE. They work the same way every time.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Point and describe together**

I will point at things in the room. You tell me what they are in te reo using tēnei, tēnā, or tērā.

- I hold up a pencil near me. You ask: **He aha tēnā?** I answer: **He pene tēnā.** (pene = pencil)
- Something is on the far wall. How do you ask? → **He aha tērā?** How might you answer if it is a window (matapihi)? → **He matapihi tērā.**
- You hold something. I ask **He aha tēnā?** You answer with a food word from Monday. Try it!
- Now add an adjective: "This is a big apple." → **He āporo nui tēnei.** Remember — adjective comes AFTER the noun.

_Discussion prompt: What is the difference between tēnā and tērā? If you had to explain it to a younger student, how would you describe it?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Point and say**

Walk around the room (or look around) and describe what you see using tēnei, tēnā, tērā.

- Write three sentences about things near you using **He [noun] tēnei.**
- Write two sentences about things near your partner using **He [noun] tēnā.**
- Ask a partner **He aha tēnā?** about something they are holding. They answer in te reo.
- ✦ Bonus: Add an adjective to each of your sentences. E.g. **He pene hou tēnei.** (This is a new pencil.)

#### Wednesday: Expanding with adjectives — He [noun] [adjective] tēnei

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Communicate about physical characteristics · Grammar Level 1–2: He + noun + adjective*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Physical descriptions; AO 1.6 — Politeness & classroom language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Building richer descriptions**

You now have He, demonstratives, and adjectives. Today we combine them all into full, rich descriptions. Watch how a simple sentence grows.

> **He āporo.**
> An apple.
> 
> **He āporo tēnei.**
> This is an apple.
> 
> **He āporo [whero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whero) tēnei.**
> This is a red apple.
> 
> **He āporo whero [nui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=nui) tēnei.**
> This is a big red apple.
> 
> **Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i tēnei āporo whero nui.**
> I want to eat this big red apple.

Notice how we can stack adjectives after the noun:
**He āporo [whero] [nui] tēnei.**

Colour words you now know:
**whero** = red · **kōwhai** = yellow · **kākāriki** = green
**kikorangi** = blue · **mā** = white · **mangu** = black

And that final sentence shows something powerful — you can plug your whole He description into a Kei te hiahia sentence as the object. The building blocks connect.

*Tip: Noun first, then adjectives — always. You can have two or three adjectives, just keep stacking them after the noun.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Grow the sentence together**

Start with a bare noun and keep adding — I will prompt you for each piece.

- Start: **He whare.** Add "big": **He whare nui.** Add "that over there": **He whare nui tērā.**
- Start: **He panana.** Add "yellow": **He panana kōwhai.** Add "this": **He panana kōwhai tēnei.**
- Now use Kei te with your full description: "I am eating this yellow banana." → **Kei te kai au i tēnei panana kōwhai.**
- Try a new combo: He + [noun from Monday] + [colour] + [size] + tēnā. Build it piece by piece.

_Discussion prompt: You now have three layers: He + noun + adjectives + demonstrative. How is building a sentence like stacking building blocks? What stays fixed and what changes?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Stack the building blocks**

Build the most complete description sentences you can using everything you know.

- Write a two-layer sentence: **He [noun] [adjective] tēnei.**
- Write a three-layer sentence: **He [noun] [colour] [size] tēnā.**
- Write a full Kei te sentence using your description as the object: **Kei te kai/inu au i tēnei…**
- ✦ Bonus: Describe a classmate's belongings in three sentences using tēnā (things near them). Read them aloud.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He aha ōu hiahia? What do you want?

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions · AO 2.3: Communicate about likes and dislikes*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Likes, dislikes & wants; AO 1.6 — Politeness conventions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — A café conversation**

Today we put all of this week's learning into a real conversation. Imagine you are in a café in Tāmaki Makaurau. Listen to how the conversation builds using everything from Monday to Wednesday.

> **Kaimahi:** Tēnā koe! He aha ōu [hiahia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hiahia)?
> **Manuhiri:** Tēnā koe. Kei te hiahia au ki te inu i tētahi tī [māhana](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81hana).
> **Kaimahi:** He aha tēnā? He tī māmā, he tī [kaha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaha) [rānei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81nei)?
> **Manuhiri:** He tī māmā, tēnā koe.
> **Kaimahi:** He āporo, he panana rānei hei kai māu?
> **Manuhiri:** He āporo tēnā? Āe, tēnā koa.
> **Kaimahi:** Ka pai! E hia te [utu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=utu)? E rima [tāra](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81ra).
> **Manuhiri:** Tēnā koe. Hei konā rā!

New words from this conversation:
**Kaimahi** = worker / staff
**Manuhiri** = visitor / customer
**ōu hiahia** = your wants / what you would like
**tētahi** = a (certain) one
**māhana** = warm
**māmā** = light / mild
**kaha** = strong
**rānei** = or
**hei kai māu** = for you to eat
**tēnā koa** = yes please (polite)

Notice: **rānei** is used for "or" in questions — just like FANBOYS in English but for choices.

*Tip: tēnā koa = yes please. This is the polite, warm way to accept something offered to you. Use it generously!*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build the café conversation**

Let's build the conversation together, one line at a time. I will be the kaimahi — you are the manuhiri.

- I greet you: Tēnā koe! You greet me back. Then I ask: **He aha ōu hiahia?** What do you say you want to drink?
- I offer you a choice: **He miraka, he wai rānei?** — milk or water? You answer using **He… tēnā koa.**
- I ask: **He aha ōu hiahia hei kai?** — What would you like to eat? You tell me using **Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i…**
- I tell you the price: **E whā tāra.** You say thank you and goodbye. Which goodbye word do YOU use — and which do I use? Why?

_Discussion prompt: Tēnā koa vs Āe — when might you use each one? What does the "koa" add? Think about how politeness works differently in different situations._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your café roleplay**

With a partner, perform a full café conversation using this week's vocabulary and structures. Swap roles.

- Partner A is the kaimahi — greet, ask what they want, offer a choice using rānei.
- Partner B is the manuhiri — respond politely, say what you want, use tēnā koa.
- Include at least one He description with an adjective: **He [food] [colour/size].**
- ✦ Bonus: Extend the conversation to 10 lines. The kaimahi can describe the food: **He āporo whero nui tēnei — he pai!**

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "He āporo me he panana." What does "me" mean here?
  - but 
  - or 
  - and ✅
  - with 
- How do you say "I want to drink the water" in te reo Māori?
  - Kei te kai au i te wai 
  - Kei te hiahia au ki te inu i te wai ✅
  - Kei te inu au ki te wai 
  - He wai hiahia au 

**Tuesday questions**

- "He aha tērā?" — Which object is being asked about?
  - Something next to the speaker 
  - Something next to the listener 
  - Something far from both speaker and listener ✅
  - Something the speaker is holding 
- How do you say "This is a big house" in te reo Māori?
  - He nui whare tēnei 
  - He whare tēnei nui 
  - He whare nui tēnei ✅
  - Tēnei he whare nui 

**Wednesday questions**

- "He āporo whero nui tēnei." What is the correct order here?
  - Demonstrative → adjectives → noun 
  - Noun → demonstrative → adjectives 
  - Noun → adjectives → demonstrative ✅
  - Adjectives → noun → demonstrative 
- What is the te reo Māori word for "yellow"?
  - whero 
  - kikorangi 
  - kōwhai ✅
  - kākāriki 

**Thursday questions**

- A customer wants to say "yes please" politely when offered something. What do they say?
  - Āe 
  - Tēnā koa ✅
  - Kia ora 
  - Kāo 
- "He miraka, he wai rānei?" — What does rānei mean in this sentence?
  - and 
  - but 
  - or ✅
  - also 

---

### Week 3

#### Monday: Ngā kupumahi hou — More action words

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Communicate about location and movement · AO 2.5: Physical characteristics and feelings — expanding verb vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Location & movement; AO 2.5 — Actions & feelings

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Expanding your verb toolkit**

You already know haere (go), kai (eat), inu (drink), moe (sleep), kōrero (speak), and ako (learn). Today we are going to add a whole new set of action words — kupumahi — so you can describe much more of what happens around you. Listen carefully and notice the sounds.

> **haere** — go / travel
> **[hoki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoki)** — return / come back
> **[tū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%AB)** — stand
> **noho** — sit / stay / live
> **[tākaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81karo)** — play
> **[mahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mahi)** — work / do
> **[pānui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%81nui)** — read
> **[tuhituhi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuhituhi)** — write
> **[whakarongo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakarongo)** — listen
> **[titiro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=titiro)** — look / watch
> **[omaoma](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=omaoma)** — run
> **[kaukau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaukau)** — swim

You now have enough verbs to describe almost everything that happens in a school day.

All of these slot straight into your **Kei te** structure:
**Kei te** + [verb] + [person]

**Kei te tākaro ngā tamariki.**
The children are playing.

**Kei te pānui ia.**
She is reading.

**Kei te mahi au.**
I am working.

New word: **ngā tamariki** = the children (ngā = the, plural)

*Tip: Every new verb you learn multiplies your sentences. One new verb = dozens of new sentences using structures you already know.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build action sentences**

I will call out a verb and a person — you build the Kei te sentence. Fast and fluent.

- "She is swimming" → **Kei te kaukau ia.** Now swap to "I am swimming" → **Kei te kaukau au.**
- "The children are playing" — new word: ngā tamariki = the children → **Kei te tākaro ngā tamariki.**
- "I am writing" → **Kei te tuhituhi au.** "You are listening" → **Kei te whakarongo koe.**
- Now try two in a row: "She is reading and I am writing." Say both sentences back to back — notice the structure never changes.

_Discussion prompt: You have now learned 12 action words. Which three do you think you will use most often in te reo? Why? What actions are missing that you would want to be able to say?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Action sentences**

Use the new verbs to describe what is happening around you right now.

- Write three Kei te sentences about what three different people in the room are doing right now.
- Write a sentence using **ngā tamariki** (the children) as the person doing the action.
- Say five new verbs from memory — can you do it without looking at the list?
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-sentence sequence describing what YOU do each morning from waking up to arriving at school. Use Kei te for each.

#### Tuesday: Ināianei — Kei te haere ngā tamariki ki hea?

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Communicate about location · AO 1.3: Number and reference · Grammar Level 1–2: ki hea (where to), i hea (where from)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Location — ki hea & i hea; AO 1.3 — Reference & direction

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Asking and answering where**

You know ki already — it means "to". Today we build on that to ask and answer "where?" questions. Watch how two small words unlock a whole new layer of conversation.

> **Ki hea?** — Where (to)?
> **I hea?** — Where (from)? / Where (was it)?
> **Kei hea?** — Where (is it right now)?
> 
> **Kei te haere koe ki hea?**
> Where are you going?
> 
> **Kei te haere au ki te kura.**
> I am going to school.
> 
> **Kei hea a Hana?**
> Where is Hana?
> 
> **Kei te kura ia.**
> She is at school.
> 
> **I hea koe inanahi?**
> Where were you yesterday?

Three location question words, three different times:

**Kei hea?** — WHERE IS IT right now? (present location)
**Ki hea?** — WHERE TO? (direction of movement)
**I hea?** — WHERE WAS IT? (past location)

Place words to use in answers:
**te kura** = school · **te [kāinga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%81inga)** = home
**te [toa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=toa)** = shop · **te [māra](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ra)** = garden
**te [awa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=awa)** = river · **te [roto](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=roto)** = lake
**te [hōpua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%8Dpua)** = swimming pool

New word: **[inanahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=inanahi)** = yesterday

*Tip: Kei hea = where IS it (now). Ki hea = where is it GOING. I hea = where WAS it. The first word tells you the time.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Where questions and answers**

I ask the question — you build the answer. Then we swap and you ask me.

- I ask: **Kei te haere koe ki hea?** You answer that you are going home → **Kei te haere au ki te kāinga.**
- I ask: **Kei hea a Tama?** (Where is Tama?) You say he is at the shop → **Kei te toa ia.**
- Now you ask ME where I am going. Use **Kei te haere koe ki hea?** I answer with a place — you decide if it makes sense.
- Past tense: **I hea koe inanahi?** — Where were you yesterday? Try to answer using a place word: **I te kura au.**

_Discussion prompt: What is the difference between Kei hea and Ki hea? Can you make up a rule that helps you remember which one to use and when?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Location challenge**

Use ki hea, kei hea, and i hea in your own sentences and questions.

- Write and ask a partner: **Kei te haere koe ki hea?** They answer with a real or imagined destination.
- Write two sentences about where two different people are right now: **Kei [place] ia.**
- Write one past-tense location sentence: **I [place] au inanahi.**
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-line dialogue — one person asks where the other is going, why, and when they will come back (hoki mai). Use **Āpōpō** (tomorrow) in the answer.

#### Wednesday: Compound sentences — joining ideas with ā, engari, nō reira

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Communicate about likes, dislikes and reasons · Grammar Level 1–2: Conjunctions and compound sentences*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Reasons & conjunctions; AO 1.5 — Extended communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Joining sentences together**

So far every sentence has stood alone. Today we learn how to join two ideas together — making compound sentences just like you do in English with "and", "but", and "so". Watch how these three words work in te reo Māori.

> **ā** — and then / and (joining actions in sequence)
> **engari** — but / however
> **nō reira** — so / therefore
> 
> **Kei te haere au ki te toa ā Kei te hoko au i te āporo.**
> I am going to the shop and (then) I am buying an apple.
> 
> **Kei te hiahia au ki te tākaro engari kei te mahi au.**
> I want to play but I am working.
> 
> **Kei te hiakai au nō reira kei te haere au ki te toa.**
> I am hungry so I am going to the shop.

In English you might say "and", "but", "so". In te reo:

**ā** — use this to chain two actions that happen in sequence or together
**engari** — use this for contrast — something unexpected follows
**nō reira** — use this for result or reason — "therefore / so"

New verb: **[hoko](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoko)** = buy

The trick: each joining word tells you the RELATIONSHIP between the two ideas.
Adding? → ā
Contrasting? → engari
Showing result? → nō reira

*Tip: Before you choose the joining word, ask yourself: am I ADDING, CONTRASTING, or showing a RESULT? That decides which word fits.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Join the sentences**

I give you two separate sentences — you decide which joining word fits and combine them.

- "I am hungry." + "I am going to the shop." — What is the relationship? → **Kei te hiakai au nō reira kei te haere au ki te toa.**
- "She wants to swim." + "She is working." — Contrast! → **Kei te hiahia ia ki te kaukau engari kei te mahi ia.**
- "I am going to school." + "I am learning te reo." — Sequence → **Kei te haere au ki te kura ā kei te ako au i te reo Māori.**
- Now make your own: give me a sentence, I will give you a second idea, you join them with the right word.

_Discussion prompt: Which of the three joining words — ā, engari, nō reira — is most like the English word "but"? Which is most like "therefore"? Can you think of a sentence where you could use any of the three but the meaning would change?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Join your ideas**

Write compound sentences in te reo using ā, engari, and nō reira.

- Write a sentence using **ā** — two actions in sequence.
- Write a sentence using **engari** — something unexpected.
- Write a sentence using **nō reira** — a cause and result.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-sentence paragraph about your morning — use at least two joining words. Read it aloud to a partner.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō te kura

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.7: Simple classroom language · AO 2.3: Likes and dislikes with reasons · AO 1.5: Location*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.7 — Classroom language; AO 2.3 — Likes, dislikes & reasons

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about school**

Today we bring together all three days of this week — new verbs, location words, and joining sentences — in a conversation about school life. This is a conversation two students might have on the way to class.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! Kei te haere koe ki hea?
> **B:** Kei te haere au ki te kura. Kei te ako au i te pāngarau ināianei.
> **A:** He pai ki a koe te pāngarau?
> **B:** Āe, he pai ki a au te pāngarau engari he uaua.
> **A:** He aha tōu akomanga?
> **B:** Ko te akomanga toru tōku. Kei hea tōu akomanga?
> **A:** Ko te akomanga whā tōku. Kei te haere tāua ki reira ā ka tīmata te akoranga!
> **B:** Āe! Kia tere!

New words from this conversation:
**[pāngarau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%81ngarau)** = maths
**[ināianei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=i%CC%84n%C4%81ianei)** = right now / at this moment
**He pai ki a koe?** = Do you like it? (literally: Is it good to you?)
**he [uaua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uaua)** = it is difficult
**tōu [akomanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=akomanga)** = your classroom
**akomanga toru/whā** = classroom 3/4 (number after noun)
**tāua** = we two (including you) — from Week 2!
**ki reira** = there / to there
**ka [tīmata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABmata)** = it begins / is starting / is starting
**Kia [tere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tere)!** = Hurry up! / Be quick! / Be quick!

*Tip: He pai ki a koe? = Do you like it? He pai ki a au = I like it. This is one of the most useful patterns in te reo — learn it cold.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — School conversation**

Build the school conversation step by step. I will start each exchange — you complete it.

- I ask: **Kei te haere koe ki hea?** You say you are going to your classroom (use your actual room number if you know it in Māori).
- I ask: **He pai ki a koe te [subject]?** Choose a school subject. You answer with **Āe/Kāo, he pai/uaua ki a au.**
- Add a joining word: "I like reading but it is difficult." → **He pai ki a au te pānui engari he uaua.**
- Use tāua: "Let's go to class together." → **Kei te haere tāua ki te akomanga.**

_Discussion prompt: He pai ki a au = I like it. How does this differ from how you say "I like it" in English? What does "ki a au" literally mean — and what does that tell you about how te reo Māori thinks about liking things?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your school kōrero**

With a partner, have a full conversation about your school day using everything from this week.

- Ask and answer: **Kei te haere koe ki hea?** Use a real location from your school.
- Ask and answer: **He pai ki a koe te [subject]?** Give a reason using **nō reira** or **engari**.
- Use **tāua** to suggest doing something together — go to class, eat lunch, play outside.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 6-line dialogue between two students that uses: a location question, a likes/dislikes exchange with a reason, and at least one joining word.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What is the te reo Māori word for "write"?
  - pānui 
  - tuhituhi ✅
  - whakarongo 
  - titiro 
- "Kei te tākaro ngā tamariki." What does "ngā tamariki" mean?
  - the teacher 
  - the children ✅
  - the school 
  - the classroom 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Kei te haere koe ki hea?" — What is this question asking?
  - Where are you right now? 
  - Where were you yesterday? 
  - Where are you going? ✅
  - Where is she? 
- Which question word asks about where something IS right now (not where it is going)?
  - Ki hea 
  - I hea 
  - Kei hea ✅
  - Nō hea 

**Wednesday questions**

- You want to say "I am hungry SO I am going to the shop." Which joining word do you use?
  - ā 
  - engari 
  - nō reira ✅
  - me 
- "Kei te hiahia au ki te kaukau engari kei te mahi au." What does engari signal here?
  - A result — swimming caused the work 
  - A sequence — swimming then working 
  - A contrast — wanting to swim but working instead ✅
  - An addition — swimming and working 

**Thursday questions**

- "He pai ki a koe te pāngarau?" — What is pāngarau?
  - reading 
  - writing 
  - maths ✅
  - science 
- How do you say "Let's go to class" using the inclusive we-two pronoun?
  - Kei te haere māua ki te akomanga 
  - Kei te haere tāua ki te akomanga ✅
  - Kei te haere mātou ki te akomanga 
  - Kei te haere kōrua ki te akomanga

### Week 4

#### Monday: Nōku, nāku — mine! Possessive pronouns

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.2: Communicate about possessions · Grammar Level 1–2: Possessive pronouns, ō/ā categories introduced*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.2 — Possessions; AO 1.4 — Personal information

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Whose is it? Nō wai? Nā wai?**

Today we learn how to say who things belong to. In te reo Māori there are two ways to say "mine" or "yours" — and which one you use depends on what KIND of thing you are talking about. Listen carefully and you will start to feel the difference.

> **Nō wai tēnā?** — Whose is that? (things you don't control)
> **Nōku tēnā.** — That is mine.
> **Nōu tēnā.** — That is yours.
> **Nōna tēnā.** — That is his/hers.
> 
> **Nā wai tēnā?** — Whose is that? (things you do control)
> **Nāku tēnā.** — That is mine.
> **Nāu tēnā.** — That is yours.
> **Nāna tēnā.** — That is his/hers.

This is one of the most interesting things about te reo Māori — things you POSSESS are divided into two groups:

**Ō-category (nō-)** — things you do NOT control or things that are senior to you:
your name, your parents, your feelings, your land, your canoe, your school

**Ā-category (nā-)** — things you DO control or things junior/subordinate to you:
your food, your tools, your book, your pet, your children (in a nurturing sense)

A quick feel for it:
**Nōku tōku ingoa** — My name belongs to me (ō — you didn't choose it)
**Nāku tāku pukapuka** — My book is mine (ā — you picked it up)

Don't stress about getting it perfect yet — just start to notice the pattern.

*Tip: Ō = things that own YOU or came to you. Ā = things YOU own or control. Your name, land, canoe → ō. Your food, book, pen → ā.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Sort and say**

I will name an item. You decide: ō-category or ā-category? Then say who it belongs to.

- A pencil (pene) — do you control it? Yes → ā-category. "It is mine" → **Nāku tēnā pene.**
- Your school (kura) — is it senior to you? Yes → ō-category. "This school is mine/ours" → **Nōku tēnei kura.**
- Your lunch (tina) — do you control it? Yes → ā. Someone asks Nā wai tēnā? You answer: **Nāku tēnā.**
- Your feelings (kei te pai au) — can you control your feelings? Mostly not → ō. "My feeling is good" — we use tōku: **He pai tōku ngākau.**

_Discussion prompt: Why do you think te reo Māori makes this distinction? What does it tell us about how Māori culture thinks about ownership and relationship? Can you think of something that might be tricky to categorise?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Mine, yours, his/hers**

Practise using nōku/nāku and nōu/nāu in real situations.

- Point to three things on your desk. For each one, say whether it is ō or ā category and say **Nāku tēnei** or **Nōku tēnei.**
- Someone picks up your book. What do you say? Write and say it: **Nāku tēnā!**
- Ask a partner whose pencil case is: **Nā wai tēnā kete pene?** They answer using **Nāku/Nāu/Nāna.**
- ✦ Bonus: Write four sentences — two using nō- words and two using nā- words. Make sure the category fits each item.

#### Tuesday: Tōku, tāku — my (as part of a sentence)

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.2: Communicate about possessions · AO 1.4: Personal information · Grammar Level 1–2: tōku/tāku, tōu/tāu, tōna/tāna*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.2 — Possessives in sentences; AO 1.4 — Personal information

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Tōku vs tāku inside a sentence**

Yesterday we used nōku and nāku to say "it is mine." Today we learn how to say "my [thing]" INSIDE a sentence — like "my book" or "my name". These are the words tōku and tāku. Watch how they work.

> **tōku** — my (ō-category)
> **tāku** — my (ā-category)
> **tōu** — your (ō-category)
> **tāu** — your (ā-category)
> **tōna** — his/her (ō-category)
> **tāna** — his/her (ā-category)
> 
> **Ko [name] tōku ingoa.**
> My name is [name]. (ingoa = ō — you didn't choose your name)
> 
> **Kei te pānui au i tāku [pukapuka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pukapuka).**
> I am reading my book. (pukapuka = ā — you control it)
> 
> **He pai tōna [ngākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C4%81kau).**
> His/her heart/feeling is good.

The pattern is the same as nō/nā — just a different form used INSIDE a sentence:

**tō-** for ō-category things: tōku (my), tōu (your), tōna (his/her)
**tā-** for ā-category things: tāku (my), tāu (your), tāna (his/her)

You already used tōku in Week 1 without knowing it:
**Ko [name] tōku ingoa.**

Now you know WHY it is tōku — ingoa (name) is ō-category.

Useful pairs to learn cold:
**tōku [ingoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ingoa)** — my name · **tāku [pukapuka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pukapuka)** — my food · **tāku kai** — my food
**tōku kura** — my school · **tāku [pene](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pene)** — my pen

*Tip: Tōku before ō-things (name, feelings, land). Tāku before ā-things (food, book, pen). If you already know nō/nā, just swap the n for t.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — My this, your that**

Build sentences using tōku, tāku, tōu, tāu. I give you the noun — you choose the right possessive and build the sentence.

- "My name is…" — ingoa is ō → **Ko [name] tōku ingoa.** Everyone say their own name using this.
- "I am reading my book" — pukapuka is ā → **Kei te pānui au i tāku pukapuka.**
- "Your school is big" — kura is ō → **He nui tōu kura.**
- "She is eating her food" — kai is ā → **Kei te kai ia i tāna kai.**

_Discussion prompt: You now know tōku, tāku, tōu, tāu, tōna, tāna — six possessive words. How do they compare to English? In English we only have "my", "your", "his/her" — three words. Why does te reo Māori need six? What extra information do they carry?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Possessives in action**

Use tōku, tāku, tōu, tāu, tōna, tāna in full sentences.

- Introduce yourself fully: Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Nō [place] au. Then add one more sentence using tāku.
- Write two sentences about a classmate using tōna/tāna — one ō and one ā item.
- Complete these: **Kei te kai ia i ___ kai.** / **Ko ___ tōna ingoa.**
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-sentence paragraph introducing yourself AND a friend. Use tōku, tāku, tōna, and tāna at least once each.

#### Wednesday: Topic sentences — He kōrero matua

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Communicate about likes and dislikes · AO 1.7: Classroom language · Grammar Level 1–2: He and Ko for topic sentences*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Expressing opinions & topics; AO 1.7 — Structured classroom language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Starting a paragraph in te reo**

In English, every paragraph starts with a topic sentence that tells the reader what the paragraph is about. Te reo Māori writing does the same thing. Today we learn how to write a strong opening sentence — a kōrero matua — using the structures you already know.

> A strong topic sentence in te reo Māori often uses **He** or **Ko**:
> 
> **He nui ngā āhuatanga pai o te kura.**
> There are many good things about school.
> 
> **Ko te kura tōku wāhi pai rawa atu.**
> School is my absolute favourite place.
> 
> **He tino pai tāku kura nō te mea he nui ngā hoa ōku.**
> My school is very good because I have many friends.
> 
> **Ko te pānui tāku mahi pai rawa atu.**
> Reading is my absolute favourite activity.

A topic sentence in te reo Māori does three things:
1. States the TOPIC clearly (what the paragraph is about)
2. Makes a CLAIM about it (a point of view, not just a fact)
3. Is strong enough to stand alone

New words:
**ngā [āhuatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81huatanga)** = the things / aspects / aspects
**[wāhi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=w%C4%81hi)** = place
**pai rawa atu** = the very best / absolute favourite / absolute favourite / absolute favourite
**ngā [hoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoa)** = friends
**[nō te mea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=n%C5%8D+te+mea)** = because (more formal than nā te mea) (more formal than nā te mea)

Notice: **nō te mea** and **nā te mea** both mean "because" — nō te mea is slightly more formal and used in writing.

*Tip: A great topic sentence = TOPIC + CLAIM + (optional reason). He or Ko at the start signals "here is what this is about."*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build topic sentences together**

I give you a topic — you build a topic sentence using He or Ko. Then we improve it together.

- Topic: your favourite food. Start with He: **He [food] tāku kai pai rawa atu.** Add a reason with nō te mea.
- Topic: your school. Start with Ko: **Ko tōku kura…** What claim can you make? He nui? He pai? He tino ātaahua?
- Topic: playing. Start with He: **He pai ki a au te tākaro nō te mea…** You finish it with your own reason.
- Now make it stronger — take your first attempt and add either an adjective, a reason, or a demonstrative to make it more specific.

_Discussion prompt: What makes a topic sentence strong? Compare these two: "Ko tōku kura he nui" vs "Ko tōku kura tōku wāhi pai rawa atu nō te mea he nui ōku hoa." Which is better and why?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Write your own topic sentences**

Write topic sentences in te reo for each of these topics.

- Write a He topic sentence about your favourite sport or activity.
- Write a Ko topic sentence about your school.
- Write a topic sentence with a reason using **nō te mea**.
- ✦ Bonus: Choose one of your topic sentences and write two more sentences to follow it — make a mini paragraph in te reo Māori.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — Whakaatu i a koe anō — Introducing yourself fully

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.2: Introduce self and others · AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 2.2: Possessions · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.4 — Personal information; AO 2.2 — Possessions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — A full self-introduction**

We have now learned Ko, nō, possessives, descriptions, and location. Today we bring them all together into a complete mihi — a self-introduction. This is one of the most important things you can do in te reo Māori. Listen to this full mihi and notice how each piece connects.

> **Tēnā koutou katoa.**
> Greetings to you all.
> 
> **Ko [mountain] tōku maunga.**
> … is my mountain.
> 
> **Ko [river] tōku awa.**
> … is my river.
> 
> **Ko [iwi] tōku iwi.**
> … is my tribe.
> 
> **Ko [school] tōku kura.**
> … is my school.
> 
> **Ko [name] tōku ingoa.**
> My name is …
> 
> **Nō [place] ahau.**
> I am from …
> 
> **Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.**
> Greetings, greetings, greetings to you all.

This is the structure of a traditional pepeha — a way of introducing yourself by connecting to the land and people you belong to.

Key new words:
**[maunga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=maunga)** = mountain
**[awa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=awa)** = river
**[iwi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=iwi)** = tribe / people
**[katoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=katoa)** = all / everyone
**Tēnā koutou katoa** = greetings to you all (koutou = you all, 3+)
**[ahau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ahau)** = I / me (more formal form of au) (more formal form of au)

Every Ko sentence here uses tōku — maunga, awa, iwi, kura, ingoa are all ō-category. They are senior to us. They connect us to something bigger than ourselves.

You don't need to know your iwi or maunga perfectly yet — you can use your town or a nearby landmark for now.

*Tip: Tēnā koutou katoa — said three times at the end of a mihi. Three times is a tradition of completeness and respect in te ao Māori.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build your mihi together**

We will build a mihi line by line as a class. I will prompt each piece — you supply the content.

- Start with the greeting — which one do we use for the whole class? **Tēnā koutou katoa.** Everyone say it together.
- Now add your school: **Ko ___ tōku kura.** Why is kura ō-category? (It is senior to you — it was here before you.)
- Add your name: **Ko ___ tōku ingoa.** Add where you are from: **Nō ___ ahau.**
- Close with the three-part farewell: **Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.** Say it all the way through.

_Discussion prompt: Why do you think Māori introductions start with the land — mountains and rivers — before the person's name? What does this tell us about the relationship between people and place in te ao Māori?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your own mihi**

Write and practise your own mihi using the full structure.

- Write your mihi in full — all six Ko/Nō lines plus the opening and closing greetings.
- Practise saying it aloud until it feels smooth. Aim to say it without looking at the page.
- Say your mihi to a partner. They listen and then say theirs back to you.
- ✦ Bonus: Add two extra lines to your mihi — one about your favourite place (wāhi pai) and one about what you love doing (mahi pai). Use tōku/tāku correctly.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Nāku tēnā pene." What does this sentence tell us about the pencil?
  - It belongs to someone else 
  - It is yours (ā-category — you control it) ✅
  - It is yours (ō-category — it is senior to you) 
  - It is lost 
- Your school (kura) is ō-category. How do you say "That school is mine"?
  - Nāku tērā kura 
  - Nōku tērā kura ✅
  - Tāku tērā kura 
  - Tōku tērā kura 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko [name] tōku ingoa." Why is tōku used here instead of tāku?
  - Because ingoa is an ā-category word — you control your name 
  - Because ingoa is an ō-category word — your name is senior to you and you did not choose it ✅
  - Because tōku is always used with Ko sentences 
  - Because tāku is only used with food 
- How do you say "She is eating her food"?
  - Kei te kai ia i tōna kai 
  - Kei te kai ia i tāna kai ✅
  - Kei te kai ia i nāna kai 
  - Kei te kai tāna ia 

**Wednesday questions**

- What does "pai rawa atu" mean?
  - quite good 
  - not good 
  - the very best / absolute favourite ✅
  - good enough 
- Which sentence is the strongest topic sentence?
  - He kura tōku kura 
  - Ko tōku kura he pai 
  - Ko tōku kura tōku wāhi pai rawa atu nō te mea he nui ōku hoa ✅
  - He nui tōku kura 

**Thursday questions**

- In a pepeha, why are maunga and awa mentioned before the person's name?
  - Because mountains and rivers are more important than people 
  - Because Māori introductions follow alphabetical order 
  - Because connecting to land shows who you are and where you belong — land is senior to people ✅
  - Because it is easier to say 
- How do you say "Greetings to you all" to a group of more than two people?
  - Tēnā kōrua 
  - Tēnā koe 
  - Tēnā koutou katoa ✅
  - Tēnā tātou

### Week 5

#### Monday: Ngā pātai katoa — The full set of question words

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number · AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 1.5: Location · Grammar Level 1–2: He aha, Ko wai, Kei hea, E hia, Pēhea*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Location questions; AO 1.3 — Number & quantity questions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Every question word you need**

You have been picking up question words gradually — Ko wai, He aha, Ki hea. Today we gather every key question word into one place and make sure you can use them all fluently. This is a big unlock — once you know all the question words, you can ask about anything.

> **Ko wai?** — Who?
> **He aha?** — What?
> **Kei hea?** — Where (is it now)?
> **Ki hea?** — Where (is it going)?
> **Nō hea?** — Where (is it from)?
> **[Āhea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%80hea)** — When (future)?
> **[Nōnāhea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=N%C5%8Dn%C4%81hea)** — When (past)?
> **E hia?** — How many (things)?
> **[Tokohia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Tokohia)** — How many (people)?
> **Pēhea?** — How? / What is it like?
> **He aha te take?** — Why? (What is the reason?)

Each question word slips into a familiar sentence frame:

**Ko wai tōu ingoa?** — Who are you? (What is your name?)
**He aha tēnei?** — What is this?
**Kei hea tō kura?** — Where is your school?
**Āhea tāua haere ai?** — When are we going?
**E hia ngā ākonga?** — How many students are there?
**Tokohia ngā kaiako?** — How many teachers?
**Kei te pēhea koe?** — How are you?
**He aha te take i haere ai koe?** — Why did you go?

Key distinction: **E hia** = how many THINGS. **Tokohia** = how many PEOPLE. Always.

*Tip: E hia for things, Tokohia for people — never mix them. And Pēhea always asks about quality or condition: "How is it? What is it like?"*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Question word rapid fire**

I give you an answer — you give me the right question. Then we flip it.

- Answer: "Ko Mere tōku ingoa." → What was the question? → **Ko wai tōu ingoa?**
- Answer: "E rua ngā pukapuka." (There are two books.) → Question about how many things → **E hia ngā pukapuka?**
- Answer: "Tokorima ngā ākonga." (There are five students.) → **Tokohia ngā ākonga?** — Why tokohia not e hia?
- Answer: "Kei te pai au." → What question prompted this? → **Kei te pēhea koe?**

_Discussion prompt: E hia vs Tokohia — this distinction treats people differently from things. What does this tell you about how te reo Māori values people? Can you think of other languages that make a similar distinction?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Questions only**

Your challenge today: only write questions. No statements allowed.

- Write one question using each of: **Ko wai, He aha, Kei hea, E hia, Tokohia.**
- Write a question using **Pēhea** that you would genuinely want to ask someone.
- Write a question using **Āhea** about something happening this week.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a 6-question interview in te reo Māori that you could use to find out about a new student. Cover name, origin, likes, and plans.

#### Tuesday: Ngā āhua — Degrees of adjectives: nui, nui ake, nui rawa atu

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical characteristics · Grammar Level 1–2: Comparative and superlative adjectives — ake, rawa atu, tino*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Descriptions — degree adjectives; AO 2.3 — Preferences & comparisons

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Good, better, best in te reo**

You know adjectives like nui, pai, iti, roa. Today we learn how to compare things — to say something is BIGGER, or the BIGGEST, or SO big. Te reo Māori has a clean, consistent system for this. Watch how three words do all the work.

> **nui** — big
> **nui ake** — bigger (comparative — more than one other)
> **nui rawa atu** — biggest / too big (superlative — most of all)
> **tino nui** — very big (intensifier)
> 
> **He nui tēnei [whare](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whare).** — This house is big.
> **He nui ake tērā whare.** — That house is bigger.
> **He nui rawa atu tērā whare.** — That house is the biggest / too big.
> **He [tino](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tino) nui tērā whare.** — That house is very big.

The system works the same way for every adjective:

[adjective] = base
[adjective] + **ake** = more / -er (comparative)
[adjective] + **rawa atu** = most / -est / too (superlative)
**tino** + [adjective] = very (intensifier)

Let's try with pai (good):
**He pai** — good
**He pai ake** — better
**He pai rawa atu** — best / the very best
**He tino pai** — very good

And with iti (small):
**He iti ake** — smaller
**He iti rawa atu** — smallest
**He tino iti** — very small

*Tip: Ake = more. Rawa atu = most/too much. Tino = very. They work with ANY adjective — learn the system, not each word separately.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Compare and contrast**

I will give you two things to compare. You build the comparative sentence.

- This apple is big. That apple is bigger. → **He nui tēnei āporo. He nui ake tērā āporo.**
- Te reo Māori is good. Maths is better (for me). → **He pai te reo Māori. He pai ake te pāngarau ki a au.**
- My bag is small. Your bag is very small. → **He iti tāku kete. He tino iti tāu kete.**
- Now use rawa atu: "This is the best food I have ever eaten." → **He kai pai rawa atu tēnei.**

_Discussion prompt: In English we say "good / better / best" — three different words. Te reo Māori uses "pai / pai ake / pai rawa atu" — the same root every time. Which system do you think is more logical? Which is easier to learn?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Bigger, better, best**

Use the comparison system with adjectives you already know.

- Write three sentences comparing two objects in the room using **[adjective] / [adjective] ake.**
- Write a superlative sentence about something you love: **He [adjective] rawa atu tāku…**
- Use tino with three different adjectives: **He tino [adj]…**
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short "top three" list in te reo — e.g. your three favourite foods ranked from good to best, using pai, pai ake, pai rawa atu.

#### Wednesday: Sentence variety — poto me te roa

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.5: Descriptive language · Grammar Level 1–2: Varying sentence length for effect*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Extended communication; AO 2.5 — Descriptive variety

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Short sentences punch. Long sentences build a picture.**

Good writers — in any language — vary the length of their sentences. A short sentence lands hard. A long sentence with detail and joining words draws the reader in. Today we practise doing both deliberately in te reo Māori.

> SHORT — punchy, clear, immediate:
> **Kei te ua.** It is raining.
> **Kei te hiakai au.** I am hungry.
> **Ka tū ia.** She stood.
> 
> LONG — descriptive, connected, flowing:
> **Kei te ua tino nui ā kei te [makariri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=makariri) hoki au nō reira kei te hoki au ki tōku kāinga.**
> It is raining very hard and I am cold too so I am going home.
> 
> MIXED — the most effective:
> **Kei te ua. He [makariri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=makariri), he [kino](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kino) hoki. Nō reira kei te hoki au ki tōku kāinga ā ka kai au i tāku tina.**
> It is raining. It is cold and unpleasant. So I am going home and I will eat my lunch.

Short sentences work best for:
— Drama and surprise
— Starting a new idea
— Describing sudden action

Long sentences work best for:
— Building a scene or explaining a reason
— Connecting several related ideas
— Showing cause and effect

You already have all the tools:
Short: **Kei te** + verb + person
Long: add **ā / engari / nō reira** to join
Longer still: add **tino / ake / rawa atu** for degree
Longest: combine everything with a description and a location

New word: **[hoki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoki)** = also / too (placed after the word it modifies)

*Tip: Read your te reo aloud. If every sentence sounds the same length, mix it up — drop in a short one for impact, or join two ideas for flow.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Mix the lengths**

I give you a long sentence — you shorten it. I give you a short one — you extend it.

- Shorten this: "Kei te haere au ki te toa ā kei te hoko au i tāku kai." → **Kei te haere au ki te toa.** When would each version be better?
- Extend this: "Kei te ua." Add a feeling, a reason, and a result. → **Kei te ua tino nui. Kei te makariri au nō reira kei te noho au i roto.**
- Write one short dramatic sentence followed by one long explanatory sentence about something happening right now in the classroom.
- Read your two sentences aloud. Does the contrast work? Does the short one feel punchy and the long one feel full?

_Discussion prompt: Listen to these two versions read aloud: "Kei te ua. Kei te makariri." vs "Kei te ua tino nui ā kei te makariri hoki au." Which version would you use in a story? Which in a quick text message? Why?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Control your sentence length**

Write deliberately — choose your lengths with purpose.

- Write three short sentences (under 5 words each) about what is happening right now.
- Now write one long sentence that combines all three ideas using ā and nō reira.
- Write a "mixed" paragraph: short sentence, long sentence, short sentence. Read it aloud — notice the rhythm.
- ✦ Bonus: Describe your morning using exactly five sentences — make the first and last short and punchy, and the middle three progressively longer.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — Whakaaro ōrite, whakaaro rerekē — Agree and disagree

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Likes and dislikes with reasons · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions · AO 2.5: Expressing opinions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Opinions & reasons; AO 1.6 — Polite disagreement

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Agreeing and disagreeing politely**

You can now describe, compare, question, and explain. Today we learn one of the most important conversation skills — how to agree, disagree, and give your opinion politely. Listen to how this conversation flows between two students debating which subject is better.

> **A:** He pai ake te pāngarau i te tuhituhi ki a au.
> **B:** Āe, he tika tāu — he pai te pāngarau engari he pai ake ki a au te tuhituhi.
> **A:** Nā te aha?
> **B:** Nō te mea ka taea e au te whakaaro nui ake i te wā e tuhituhi ana au.
> **A:** Ka pai tāu whakaaro. Engari ki a au, he uaua rawa atu te tuhituhi.
> **B:** Āe pea. He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro!
> **A:** He rerekē — ā he pai tērā!

New phrases for agreeing and disagreeing:

**He [tika](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tika) tāu.** — You are right. / That is correct.
**Āe, engari…** — Yes, but…
**Kāo, ki a au…** — No, in my opinion…
**Ki a au nei…** — In my opinion… / I think…
**Ka pai tāu [whakaaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakaaro).** — That is a good point.
**Nā te aha?** — Why? / What for? (asking for a reason)
**Ka taea e au te…** — I am able to… / I can…
**Āe [pea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pea).** — Maybe. / Perhaps.
**He [rerekē](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rerek%C4%93) ā tātou [whakaaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakaaro).** — Our opinions are different.

**i te wā e… ana** = when I am [doing something] — a new time clause

*Tip: He tika tāu = "you are right" — a gracious way to acknowledge someone before offering your own view. Use it even when you are about to disagree.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Agree, disagree, explain**

I will make a claim. You agree or disagree, then give a reason. We keep the conversation going for at least four exchanges.

- I say: **He pai ake te tākaro i te ako.** (Playing is better than learning.) Do you agree? Respond with Āe/Kāo + a reason.
- You make a claim using **He [adjective] ake te X i te Y ki a au.** I respond with **Ka pai tāu whakaaro, engari…**
- Practice: **Nā te aha?** — ask me why I said what I said. I must give a reason using **nō te mea.**
- Close the exchange: one person says **He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro** — the other responds with **Āe — ā he pai tērā!**

_Discussion prompt: Why is "He tika tāu, engari…" a more powerful response than just "Kāo"? What does acknowledging the other person's point do for the conversation? Can you think of a time when this approach would be really useful?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Te taupatupatu — The debate**

With a partner, hold a mini debate in te reo Māori. Use all the new phrases.

- Choose a topic to debate — which is better: te kura (school) or te kāinga (home)? Sport or reading? Summer or winter?
- Each person makes their opening claim using **He pai ake te X i te Y ki a au nō te mea…**
- Respond to your partner using **He tika tāu, engari…** or **Āe pea, engari ki a au…**
- ✦ Bonus: Write up your debate as a short dialogue — at least 6 lines, using he tika tāu, nā te aha, nō te mea, and he rerekē ā tātou whakaaro.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Tokohia ngā kaiako?" vs "E hia ngā pukapuka?" — Why are different question words used?
  - Tokohia is formal, e hia is casual 
  - Tokohia asks about people, e hia asks about things — te reo Māori distinguishes between them ✅
  - They mean the same thing and can be swapped freely 
  - E hia is only used for numbers over ten 
- How do you ask "When are we going?" (future) in te reo Māori?
  - Nōnāhea tāua haere ai? 
  - Āhea tāua haere ai? ✅
  - He aha te wā? 
  - Ki hea tāua haere ai? 

**Tuesday questions**

- "He pai rawa atu tēnei kai." What does rawa atu tell us?
  - The food is quite good 
  - The food is better than other food 
  - The food is the best / extremely good ✅
  - The food is too good to eat 
- How do you say "That house is bigger" using the comparative form?
  - He nui tino tērā whare 
  - He nui rawa atu tērā whare 
  - He nui ake tērā whare ✅
  - He tino nui ake tērā whare 

**Wednesday questions**

- The word "hoki" in "Kei te makariri hoki au" means:
  - but 
  - because 
  - also / too ✅
  - however 
- A writer uses one short sentence followed by a long one. What is the main effect?
  - It fills more space on the page 
  - The short sentence loses impact next to the long one 
  - The short sentence creates impact and the long one adds explanation and flow ✅
  - Long sentences should always come first 

**Thursday questions**

- "He tika tāu, engari…" — What is the speaker doing?
  - Completely agreeing with the other person 
  - Changing the subject 
  - Acknowledging the other point before offering a different view ✅
  - Asking a question 
- How do you say "In my opinion, school is better than home" in te reo Māori?
  - Ki a au nei, he pai ake te kura i te kāinga ✅
  - He pai ake te kura i te kāinga ki a koe 
  - Nō te mea he pai te kura 
  - He tika tāu he pai te kura
