### Week 6

#### Monday: Ngā tūāhua — Adverbs: how, when, where

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Location · AO 2.4: Time and weather · Grammar Level 1–2: Adverbs of manner, time, and place*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Time expressions; AO 1.5 — Location & manner

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Adding when, where, and how to your sentences**

You can already build solid Kei te sentences. Today we learn how to add extra information — when something happens, where it happens, and how it happens. These are adverbs — and in te reo Māori they are beautifully flexible. Watch where they sit in a sentence.

> **HOW (manner):**
> **[āta](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81ta)** — slowly / carefully / gently / gently
> **tere** — quickly / fast
> **kaha** — strongly / hard
> **[mārie](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81rie)** — calmly / peacefully / peacefully
> 
> **WHEN (time):**
> **ināianei** — right now
> **[āpōpō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81p%C5%8Dp%C5%8D)** — tomorrow
> **inanahi** — yesterday
> **ā tōnā [wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=w%C4%81)** — soon / in due course / in due course
> **i [muri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=muri) mai** — afterwards / later / later
> 
> **WHERE (place):**
> **i konei** — here
> **i reira** — there (at that place)
> **i [runga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=runga)** — above / on top / on top
> **i [raro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=raro)** — below / underneath / underneath
> **i [waho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waho)** — outside
> **i [roto](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=roto)** — inside

In te reo Māori, adverbs usually come RIGHT AFTER the verb they describe:

**Kei te haere tere ia.**
She is going quickly.

**Kei te kōrero āta ia.**
He is speaking carefully/slowly.

**Kei te mahi kaha ngā ākonga.**
The students are working hard.

Time words often come at the START of a sentence (like in English):

**Āpōpō ka haere au ki te toa.**
Tomorrow I will go to the shop.

**Inanahi i haere ia ki te kura.**
Yesterday she went to school.

Place words usually come AFTER the verb phrase:
**Kei te noho ia i konei.**
She is sitting here.

*Tip: Manner adverbs (how) go right after the verb. Time words can go at the start. Place words go at the end. When in doubt — say it aloud and feel what sounds right.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Add the adverb**

I give you a bare sentence — you add the adverb I specify. Then we check where it landed.

- Bare: "Kei te haere ia." Add "quickly" → **Kei te haere tere ia.** Now add "tomorrow" at the start → **Āpōpō ka haere tere ia.**
- Bare: "Kei te kōrero ngā tamariki." Add "loudly/hard" → **Kei te kōrero kaha ngā tamariki.**
- Bare: "Kei te noho au." Add "here" → **Kei te noho au i konei.** Now add "calmly" → **Kei te noho mārie au i konei.**
- Build a full sentence with ALL THREE — time + verb + manner + place: "Yesterday she worked hard outside." → **Inanahi i mahi kaha ia i waho.**

_Discussion prompt: In English, adverbs can move around a lot — "She quickly ran" or "She ran quickly" or "Quickly, she ran." Does te reo Māori feel more fixed or more flexible? Try moving the adverb in one of your sentences and see if it still works._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Adverb challenge**

Add at least one adverb to every sentence you write today.

- Write three sentences — one with a manner adverb, one with a time adverb, one with a place adverb.
- Write one sentence using ALL THREE types: time + manner + place.
- Take this bare sentence and add two adverbs: **Kei te ako au.** (I am learning.)
- ✦ Bonus: Write five sentences describing your ideal day — every sentence must include at least one adverb. Read them aloud and listen to how much richer they sound than bare sentences.

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupu pakiaka — Word families and word building

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.7: Classroom language · AO 2.5: Vocabulary development · Grammar Level 1–2: kai-, whaka-, -nga, tāngata compound words*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.7 — Vocabulary & word building; AO 2.5 — Extended vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — How te reo Māori builds new words**

One of the most powerful things about te reo Māori is that it builds new words from existing ones using prefixes and suffixes. Once you know a handful of roots, you can decode and create dozens of new words. Watch how this works.

> **kai-** prefix = person who does something (agent noun):
> **[kaimahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaimahi)** — worker (mahi = work) (mahi = work)
> **[kaiako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaiako)** — teacher (ako = learn/teach) (ako = learn/teach)
> **[kaipānui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaip%C4%81nui)** — reader (pānui = read) (pānui = read)
> **[kaituhituhi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaituhituhi)** — writer (tuhituhi = write) (tuhituhi = write)
> **[kaikōrero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaik%C5%8Drero)** — speaker (kōrero = speak) (kōrero = speak)
> **[kaitākaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kait%C4%81karo)** — player (tākaro = play) (tākaro = play)
> 
> **whaka-** prefix = to cause or make something happen:
> **whakarongo** — to listen (cause yourself to hear)
> **[whakamāori](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakam%C4%81ori)** — to translate into Māori into Māori
> **[whakaaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakaaro)** — thought / to think
> **[whakapai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakapai)** — to improve / make better / make better
> 
> **-nga** suffix = the act of / the result of:
> **kōrero** → **kōrerorero**/**kōrerotanga** — conversation
> **ako** → **akoranga** — lesson / learning
> **mahi** → **mahinga** — the work / the doing

This is called word building — pakiaka (root) + prefix/suffix = new word.

You can BUILD words you have never seen before:
**kai-** + **omaoma** (run) = **kaiomaoma** — a runner
**kai-** + **kaukau** (swim) = **kaikaukau** — a swimmer
**whaka-** + **nui** (big) = **whakanui** — to celebrate / make bigger
**whaka-** + **iti** (small) = **whakaiti** — to be humble / make smaller

And you can DECODE words you have never learned:
If you see **kaitiaki** — you know kai- means "person who does" and tiaki means "to guard/protect" → kaitiaki = guardian/protector

You have been a kaitiaki of your own learning all term.

*Tip: See kai- at the start of a word → it is a person who does that action. See whaka- → something is being caused or made to happen. These two prefixes unlock hundreds of words.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build and decode**

I give you a root word — you build a new word using kai- or whaka-. Then I give you an unknown word — you decode it.

- Root: **haere** (go). Build: kai- + haere = ? **Kaihaere** could mean "traveller / one who goes." Does that feel right?
- Root: **pai** (good). Build: whaka- + pai = **whakapai** — to make better / improve. Use it: **Kei te whakapai ia i tāna mahi.**
- Decode: **kaitiaki** — kai- = person who does + tiaki = guard/protect. What does kaitiaki mean? Why is this an important word in Aotearoa?
- Decode: **whakaaro** — whaka- = cause/make + aro = face toward / attend to. So whakaaro = thought (turning your mind toward something). Use it: **He pai tāu whakaaro.**

_Discussion prompt: English builds words too — "teacher" comes from "teach", "runner" from "run". But te reo Māori is much more systematic — kai- ALWAYS means person who does. Why might a systematic system be easier to learn than the English system of -er, -or, -ist, -ian?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Word builder**

Practise building and using new words from roots you already know.

- Build three new kai- words from verbs you know. Write each with its meaning.
- Build two whaka- words. Use each in a sentence.
- Decode this word you have never seen: **kaiwhakaako**. What does it mean? (whaka- + ako = to cause learning, kai- = person who does it)
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph about your class using at least four kai- words and one whaka- word. E.g. "In our class there is a kaiako, many kaipānui, and some kaituhituhi..."

#### Wednesday: Expanding sentences — who, where, when phrases

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Location · AO 2.4: Time · Grammar Level 1–2: Expanding noun phrases and adding circumstantial information*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Location & circumstance; AO 2.4 — Time phrases in sentences

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Packing more into every sentence**

This week you learned adverbs (how/when/where) and word building. Today we combine them — learning how to pack time, place, and participant information into a single rich sentence. Think of it like adding layers to a painting.

> Start bare:
> **Kei te ako ngā tamariki.**
> The children are learning.
> 
> Add WHERE:
> **Kei te ako ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
> The children are learning in the classroom.
> 
> Add WHEN:
> **Ināianei kei te ako ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
> Right now the children are learning in the classroom.
> 
> Add HOW:
> **Ināianei kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
> Right now the children are learning hard in the classroom.
> 
> Add WHO WITH (using me — with):
> **Ināianei kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki me tō rātou kaiako i te akomanga.**
> Right now the children are learning hard with their teacher in the classroom.

The layering order in a te reo Māori sentence:

[TIME] + Kei te + [verb] + [manner] + [person/subject] + [with whom: me] + [where: i/ki]

New word: **me** (when meaning "with" — different from "me" meaning "and"!) 
→ **Kei te haere au me ōku hoa.** I am going with my friends.

New possessive: **tō rātou** = their (ō-category, three or more)
**tā rātou** = their (ā-category, three or more)

You can always strip back to the bare sentence — the extra layers are optional. But adding even one layer makes your language significantly richer.

*Tip: Build sentences in layers — start bare, then add one piece at a time. You can always stop when it feels complete. More layers = more precise, but even two layers is a great sentence.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Layer by layer**

We will build one sentence together, adding a new layer with each step. Everyone contributes one layer.

- Layer 1 — bare: **Kei te tākaro ngā tamariki.** The children are playing.
- Layer 2 — add where: **Kei te tākaro ngā tamariki i waho.** ...outside.
- Layer 3 — add how: **Kei te tākaro kaha ngā tamariki i waho.** ...hard outside.
- Layer 4 — add time at the start: **Ināianei kei te tākaro kaha ngā tamariki i waho.** Right now...

_Discussion prompt: At which layer did the sentence start to feel really alive? Was there a point where adding more felt like too much? What is the "just right" amount of information for a te reo sentence?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The layering challenge**

Take a bare sentence and build it up — then strip it back to find the best version.

- Start with **Kei te kai ia.** Add three layers one at a time — write all four versions.
- Start with **Kei te kōrero ngā ākonga.** Add time, manner, and place.
- Now take your fullest sentence and remove layers until you have the most PRECISE version — not longest, best.
- ✦ Bonus: Describe what is happening outside your window right now (real or imagined) in three sentences — bare, medium, and fully layered. Read all three aloud and decide which you prefer.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero hou mō tēnei wiki — This week in conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time and events · AO 2.3: Extended communication with reasons · AO 1.5: Location*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Talking about events & time; AO 2.3 — Extended reasons & opinions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about your week**

Today we use everything from this week — adverbs, word-building words, layered sentences — in a real conversation about what has happened and what is coming up. This is the kind of conversation you might have with a friend on a Friday afternoon.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! He pēhea tōu wiki?
> **B:** He tino pai! I mahi kaha au i tēnei wiki. I ako au i ngā kupu hou e tino pai ana ki a au.
> **A:** He aha ngā mea hou i ako ai koe?
> **B:** I ako au i ngā tūāhua — pēnei i te "tere" me te "āta". Ka taea e au te kōrero ināianei me ērā kupu.
> **A:** Ka rawe! Āpōpō he aha āu mahi?
> **B:** Āpōpō ka haere tere au ki te hākinakina me ōku hoa. Ka tākaro kaha mātou i waho.
> **A:** He pai ake te hākinakina i te noho ki te kāinga ki a au!
> **B:** Āe! He tika tāu — engari he pai hoki te okioki!

New words and phrases from this conversation:

**He pēhea tōu wiki?** — How was your week?
**e tino pai ana ki a au** — that I really liked / that are very good for me
**ngā mea hou** — the new things
**i ako ai koe** — that you learned (relative clause — more on this in Term 2!)
**pēnei i te** — like / such as
**Ka taea e au te kōrero** — I am able to speak
**[hākinakina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C4%81kinakina)** — sport / physical activity / physical activity
**[okioki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=okioki)** — rest / to rest / to rest
**ērā kupu** — those words (ērā = those, plural of tērā)

Notice how the conversation uses adverbs naturally — tere, kaha, tino pai, ināianei, āpōpō, i waho. This is everything from this week embedded in real speech.

*Tip: He pēhea tōu wiki? — How was your week? Learn this cold. It is a conversation starter you will use every single Friday for the rest of the year.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Friday kōrero**

Build the "how was your week" conversation step by step. I start — you respond, then lead the next exchange.

- I ask: **He pēhea tōu wiki?** You answer with at least two sentences — what you did and how (use an adverb).
- I ask: **He aha ngā mea hou i ako ai koe?** — What new things did you learn? Name something from this week using a kai- word if you can.
- I say something I liked: **I pai ki a au te ako i ngā tūāhua.** You agree or add your own view using **He tika tāu / Āe, engari…**
- Close: I ask **Āpōpō he aha āu mahi?** You tell me your plans — use āpōpō, a verb, and a place.

_Discussion prompt: He pēhea tōu wiki? is a question you could ask and answer every Friday in te reo. If you did that for the whole year, what do you think would happen to your fluency? Why does regular, short conversation practice matter more than occasional long study sessions?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your own Friday kōrero**

With a partner, have a full "end of week" conversation using everything from Weeks 1–6.

- Ask and answer: **He pēhea tōu wiki?** Both partners answer in at least three sentences.
- Each person shares one new thing they learned using a kai- or whaka- word.
- Make a plan for tomorrow using **Āpōpō ka…** — include a place and a manner adverb.
- ✦ Bonus: Write up your full conversation as a dialogue — at least 8 lines. Use adverbs, word-building words, layered sentences, and the agree/disagree phrases from Week 5.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Kei te haere tere ia." Where does the adverb "tere" sit in this sentence?
  - Before the verb 
  - After the verb and before the subject ✅
  - At the start of the sentence 
  - After the subject 
- "Inanahi i haere ia ki te kura." What type of adverb is "inanahi"?
  - Manner — how she went 
  - Place — where she went 
  - Time — when she went ✅
  - Degree — how much she went 

**Tuesday questions**

- The prefix "kai-" in te reo Māori makes a word that means:
  - to cause something to happen 
  - a place where something happens 
  - a person who does that action ✅
  - the result of an action 
- What does "whakapai" most likely mean?
  - a person who is good 
  - to make something better / improve ✅
  - the goodness of something 
  - a very good thing 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Kei te haere au me ōku hoa." What does "me" mean in this sentence?
  - and (joining two sentences) 
  - but 
  - with ✅
  - also 
- In a layered te reo sentence, where does a TIME word usually go?
  - After the verb 
  - After the subject 
  - At the start of the sentence ✅
  - At the very end 

**Thursday questions**

- How do you ask "How was your week?" in te reo Māori?
  - He aha tōu wiki? 
  - Kei te pēhea tōu wiki? 
  - He pēhea tōu wiki? ✅
  - Ko wai tōu wiki? 
- "Ka taea e au te kōrero." What does this mean?
  - I want to speak 
  - I used to be able to speak 
  - I am able to speak ✅
  - I am speaking quickly 

---

### Week 7

#### Monday: Ngā rārangi — Lists, colons, and ko ēnei/ko ērā

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number and reference · AO 2.5: Vocabulary and description · Grammar Level 1–2: Ko ēnei/ko ērā, listing structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Reference & listing; AO 2.5 — Descriptive vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Making lists and introducing them**

You have been building individual sentences well. Today we learn how to introduce a list of items — giving your listener a heads-up that several things are coming. In English you use a colon for this. Te reo Māori has its own elegant way of doing the same thing.

> **Ko ēnei** — these are (introducing a list of things near you)
> **Ko ērā** — those are (introducing a list of things away from you)
> **Ko ēnei ōku mea:** — These are my things:
> 
> **Ko ēnei ōku mea: he pene, he pukapuka, he kete.**
> These are my things: a pen, a book, a bag.
> 
> **Ko ērā ngā āhuatanga pai o tōku kura:**
> Those are the good things about my school:
> **he nui ngā hoa, he pai ngā kaiako, he ātaahua ngā papa tākaro.**
> there are many friends, the teachers are good, the playgrounds are beautiful.

You already know tēnei/tēnā/tērā (this/that — singular).
Now meet their PLURAL cousins:

**[ēnei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%93nei)** — these (plural of tēnei, near me) (plural of tēnei, near me)
**[ēnā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%93n%C4%81)** — those (plural of tēnā, near you) (plural of tēnā, near you)
**[ērā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%93r%C4%81)** — those (plural of tērā, away from both) (plural of tērā, away from both)

When used with Ko to introduce a list:
**Ko ēnei…** = These are… (here is the list)

New descriptive words for the list:
**[ātaahua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81taahua)** — beautiful / wonderful / wonderful
**papa tākaro** — playground
**ngā kaiako** — the teachers (plural — you built kaiako from kai- in Week 6!)

The list items after the colon each start with **he** — just like individual He sentences, strung together with commas.

*Tip: Ko ēnei/ko ērā = "these are / those are" — use them whenever you want to introduce a list. Think of them as te reo's colon.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build lists together**

I give you a topic — you introduce it with Ko ēnei or Ko ērā and then list three items.

- Topic: things on your desk. → **Ko ēnei ōku mea i runga i tōku tēpu: he pene, he pukapuka, he aho.** (aho = ruler)
- Topic: the good things about te reo Māori. → **Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai o te reo Māori: he ātaahua, he ngāwari, he tino pai hoki.**
- Now try Ko ērā — things on the far side of the room: **Ko ērā ngā mea i reira: he…, he…, he…**
- Challenge: use Ko ēnei to introduce a list of three things you did yesterday — use the past tense I for each item in the list.

_Discussion prompt: Ko ēnei works like a colon in English writing — it signals "here comes the list". Can you think of other situations in daily life where introducing a list first would be useful? When would Ko ērā be better than Ko ēnei?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — List maker**

Use Ko ēnei or Ko ērā to introduce lists in te reo.

- Write a Ko ēnei sentence introducing three things you like: **Ko ēnei ōku mea pai: he…, he…, he…**
- Write a Ko ērā sentence about three things you can see across the room.
- Write a list about your school using ātaahua, pai, and nui as describing words for each item.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai o…" paragraph about your town, your class, or your family. At least five items in your list.

#### Tuesday: Ngā wā — Mixing past and present tense fluently

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: I (past) and Kei te (present) used in the same passage*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Tense & time; AO 2.3 — Extended communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Switching between past and present**

You know Kei te for now and I for the past. Real language constantly moves between tenses — you say what happened, then describe what is happening now, then say what you thought about it. Today we practise switching fluently without losing track of when things are happening.

> Watch how tenses shift in this short account:
> 
> **Inanahi i haere au ki te hākinakina me ōku hoa.**
> Yesterday I went to sport with my friends.
> 
> **I tākaro kaha mātou — he tino pai!**
> We played hard — it was very good!
> 
> **Ināianei kei te ngenge au engari kei te hari hoki au.**
> Right now I am tired but I am also happy.
> 
> **He pai ake te hākinakina i te noho ki te kāinga ki a au.**
> Sport is better than staying home in my opinion.

The time word at the START of a sentence tells your listener which tense is coming:

**Inanahi** (yesterday) → use **I**
**Ināianei** (right now) → use **Kei te**
**Āpōpō** (tomorrow) → use **Ka**

New word: **[ngenge](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ngenge)** — tired
New word: **[hari](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hari)** — happy / joyful / joyful

Think of time words as SIGNALS — they prime your listener for the tense before the verb even arrives. This is why putting time words at the START is so powerful in te reo Māori.

When there is NO time word, the tense marker (Kei te / I / Ka) does all the work on its own.

*Tip: Time word at the start = instant tense signal. Inanahi → I. Ināianei → Kei te. Āpōpō → Ka. Your listener knows what is coming before the verb.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Tense switching**

I will give you a sequence of events across two time periods. You build each sentence with the right tense marker.

- Yesterday: you went to the shop. Now: you are eating what you bought. → **Inanahi i haere au ki te toa. Ināianei kei te kai au i tāku kai hou.**
- Yesterday: she was tired. Now: she is happy. → **Inanahi i ngenge ia. Ināianei kei te hari ia.**
- Yesterday: the children played outside. Now: they are sitting inside. → **Inanahi i tākaro ngā tamariki i waho. Ināianei kei te noho rātou i roto.**
- Now try WITHOUT time words — just the tense markers. Tell me two things that happened yesterday and one thing happening right now.

_Discussion prompt: In English we rely on verb endings to show tense — "played" vs "play". In te reo Māori the verb never changes — only the tense marker changes. Which system do you think is easier for a learner? Why?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Past and present**

Write a short account that moves between past and present — at least four sentences.

- Write two past-tense sentences about something you did inanahi (yesterday).
- Write two present-tense sentences about what you are doing ināianei (right now).
- Combine them into a flowing four-sentence account — use time words to signal the switches.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a six-sentence account of your day so far — start in the past (this morning), arrive in the present (right now), and end with a Ka sentence about what you will do next.

#### Wednesday: Complex sentences — i te wā, ahakoa, mehemea

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication with reasons · Grammar Level 1–2: Subordinate clauses — when, although, if*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Complex reasoning; AO 2.4 — Time clauses

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — When, although, if — subordinate clauses**

Last week you learned to join sentences with ā, engari, and nō reira. Today we go one step further — subordinate clauses. These are mini sentences that add a condition, a time, or a concession to your main sentence. Three small words unlock a huge range of complex ideas.

> **[i te wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=i+te+w%C4%81)** — when (referring to a time in the past or ongoing) (referring to a time in the past or ongoing)
> **[ahakoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ahakoa)** — although / even though / even though
> **[mehemea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mehemea)** — if / supposing that / supposing that
> 
> **I te wā i haere au ki te kura, i kite au i tōku hoa.**
> When I went to school, I saw my friend.
> 
> **Ahakoa kei te ua, kei te tākaro ngā tamariki i waho.**
> Although it is raining, the children are playing outside.
> 
> **Mehemea ka haere koe ki te toa, hoko mai he miraka māku.**
> If you go to the shop, buy some milk for me.

The pattern for each:

**i te wā** + [past clause] + , + [main clause]
→ When [this happened], [that happened].

**ahakoa** + [clause] + , + [main clause]
→ Although [this is true], [this still happens].

**mehemea** + [Ka clause] + , + [instruction or result]
→ If [this happens], [then do/say this].

New words:
**i [kite](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kite)** — saw / noticed (past of kite = see/find) / noticed (past of kite = see/find)
**[hoko](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoko) mai** — buy and bring back (hoko = buy, mai = towards speaker) (hoko = buy, mai = towards speaker)
**[māku](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ku)** — for me (mā = for + ku = me) (mā = for + ku = me)

These three words turn simple sentences into sophisticated ones — and they all work the same way: subordinate clause first, comma, main clause.

*Tip: Subordinate clause FIRST, then comma, then main clause. The smaller idea sets up the bigger one. Same rule for i te wā, ahakoa, and mehemea.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build complex sentences**

I give you two simple sentences. You join them with i te wā, ahakoa, or mehemea — whichever fits.

- "She was tired." + "She finished her work." — when? → **I te wā i ngenge ia, i mutu tonu tāna mahi.**
- "It is cold." + "We are swimming." — although? → **Ahakoa he makariri, kei te kaukau mātou.**
- "You go to the shop." + "Please buy bread." — if? → **Mehemea ka haere koe ki te toa, hoko mai he parāoa māku.**
- Now write your own: use ahakoa to say something surprising — something that happens DESPITE an obstacle.

_Discussion prompt: Ahakoa is particularly powerful — it signals that something unexpected is happening despite an obstacle. Think of a situation in your life right now where ahakoa would be the perfect word. Can you say it in te reo?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Subordinate clause challenge**

Write three complex sentences — one with each new subordinating word.

- Write a sentence using **i te wā** about something that happened this week.
- Write a sentence using **ahakoa** about something surprising or unexpected.
- Write a sentence using **mehemea** — give someone a condition and an instruction.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-sentence paragraph that uses all three: i te wā, ahakoa, and mehemea. Tell a mini-story that has a condition, a time clause, and a surprising twist.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō tērā rā — Telling a story about the past

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Time and sequencing · AO 1.5: Location*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Storytelling & sequencing; AO 2.4 — Past events & time

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Telling what happened**

Today we put this whole week together — lists, tense switching, and complex clauses — into a story about something that happened. Storytelling is one of the most important things you can do in any language. Listen to how these sentences flow.

> **Inanahi he rā tino pai.**
> Yesterday was a very good day.
> 
> **I tīmata i te ata — i omaoma au i waho me ōku hoa.**
> It started in the morning — I ran outside with my friends.
> 
> **I te wā i ngenge mātou, i hoki mātou ki roto ā i kai mātou.**
> When we were tired, we went back inside and ate.
> 
> **Ko ēnei ngā mea i kainga e mātou: he parāoa, he āporo, he miraka.**
> These are the things we ate: bread, an apple, milk.
> 
> **Ahakoa i makariri ā i ua hoki, he tino pai tō mātou rā.**
> Although it was cold and raining too, our day was very good.
> 
> **Mehemea ka pērā anō āpōpō, ka haere anō mātou!**
> If it is the same again tomorrow, we will go again!

Notice what this story does:
1. Opens with a scene-setting sentence (He rā tino pai)
2. Uses I te wā for the turning point
3. Uses Ko ēnei to introduce a food list
4. Uses ahakoa for the surprising twist
5. Closes with mehemea — looking forward

New words:
**i te ata** — in the morning
**i tīmata** — it started / began
**i kainga** — was eaten (passive — the eating happened TO the food)
**e mātou** — by us (the agent after a passive verb)
**[pērā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%93r%C4%81) anō** — the same again / like that again
**ka haere anō** — will go again

*Tip: Every good story has a beginning (scene-set), a middle (what happened, including a surprise or challenge), and an end (result or look forward). Use your subordinate clauses for the middle.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build the story together**

We will build a shared class story about an imaginary school trip. I provide the skeleton — you fill in the details.

- Open with a scene-setter: **Inanahi i haere mātou ki te…** (somewhere exciting — you choose the place).
- What happened first? Use I + verb + a time word: **I te ata, i… mātou.**
- Add a surprise using ahakoa: **Ahakoa…, i… tonu mātou.** Something went wrong but you carried on.
- Close with a Ko ēnei list of the best things about the trip, then a mehemea sentence about doing it again.

_Discussion prompt: Good storytellers control time — they slow down for the important parts and speed up through the routine ones. Which sentence in our story felt most important? How could you make it slower and more detailed using the tools you have?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tāu kōrero — Your story**

Tell a story about something that actually happened to you recently — entirely in te reo Māori.

- Write an opening scene-setter: one He or I sentence that sets the time and mood.
- Write the middle: at least two sentences using I, plus one i te wā or ahakoa sentence.
- Include a Ko ēnei or Ko ērā list somewhere in your story.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full story — at least six sentences. Use past tense throughout, include a complex clause, a list, and close with a mehemea or Ka sentence looking forward.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ko ēnei ōku mea: he pene, he pukapuka, he kete." What is the role of "Ko ēnei" here?
  - It means "these things are bad" 
  - It introduces a list — like a colon in English ✅
  - It is a question asking what these things are 
  - It means "I have a pen, book and bag" 
- What is the plural of tēnei?
  - tēnā 
  - tērā 
  - ēnei ✅
  - ēnā 

**Tuesday questions**

- Which tense marker do you use after the time word "Inanahi"?
  - Kei te 
  - Ka 
  - I ✅
  - E…ana 
- "Ināianei kei te hari ia." What does hari mean?
  - tired 
  - hungry 
  - happy / joyful ✅
  - cold 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ahakoa kei te ua, kei te tākaro ngā tamariki i waho." What does ahakoa signal?
  - A time — when the rain started 
  - A cause — the rain made them play 
  - A contrast — they play DESPITE the rain ✅
  - A condition — if it rains they will play 
- "Mehemea ka haere koe ki te toa, hoko mai he miraka māku." What does māku mean?
  - for you 
  - for them 
  - for me ✅
  - for us 

**Thursday questions**

- "I te wā i ngenge mātou, i hoki mātou ki roto." What is the function of "i te wā" here?
  - It means although we were tired 
  - It means if we are tired 
  - It sets a time — WHEN we were tired, something happened ✅
  - It means we were very tired 
- In a te reo story, which sentence type works best to open with a scene-setting mood?
  - A mehemea (if) sentence 
  - A He or I sentence that sets the time and feeling ✅
  - A Ko ēnei list 
  - A question using Ko wai

### Week 8

#### Monday: Ngā kupu ōrite — Words that sound alike

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.6: Politeness conventions · AO 2.5: Vocabulary development · Grammar Level 1–2: Distinguishing similar-sounding words, macrons*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Vocabulary precision; AO 1.6 — Clear communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Words that trip people up**

As your vocabulary grows, you will notice some words look or sound very similar but mean completely different things. Today we look at the most common confusable pairs — and the key things that make them different. Pay close attention to the macrons (the long vowel marks) — they change everything.

> **[wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=w%C4%81)** (long ā) — time / period / space / period / space
> **wa** (short a) — not a standard word on its own — macron matters!
> 
> **[kēmu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%93mu)** — game
> **kemu** — would not mean game without the macron
> 
> **[mō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C5%8D)** — for / about / concerning / concerning
> **mo** — a type of lizard (unlikely but real!)
> 
> **nō** — belonging to (ō-category possession)
> **nō** vs **nā** — ō vs ā category — these two trip everyone up
> 
> **ō** — of (ō-category connector)
> **ā** — of (ā-category connector)
> 
> **tōku** — my (ō-category)
> **tāku** — my (ā-category)

The most important thing about macrons:

A macron (the line over a vowel: ā ē ī ō ū) makes that vowel LONG — held twice as long as a short vowel. It is not just a spelling rule — it changes the MEANING of the word completely.

Most confusable pairs in te reo come from:
1. **Macron vs no macron**: [keke](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=keke) (cake) vs [kēkē](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%93k%C4%93) (armpit!)
2. **Ō vs ā category**: tōku vs tāku, nō vs nā
3. **Similar-looking prepositions**: ki (to), i (at/past), o (of-ō), a (of-ā)

A useful pair to know:
**He aha tō ingoa?** — What is your name? (tō = your, short form of tōu)
**He aha tā mahi?** — What is the work/task? (tā = the, ā-category)

New word: **[keke](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=keke)** — cake · **[kēkē](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%93k%C4%93)** — armpit (classic example!)

*Tip: When in doubt about ō or ā — ask yourself: is this thing senior to me and connected to identity (ō), or is it something I control and do things with (ā)? That feeling will guide you right more often than not.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Spot the difference**

I will give you two similar-looking words or phrases. You tell me the difference and use each correctly in a sentence.

- **tōku** vs **tāku** — which one for "my book"? Which for "my name"? → tāku pukapuka · tōku ingoa
- **nō** vs **nā** — "Whose is this house?" (house = senior, ō) → **Nō wai tēnei whare?** "Whose is this food?" (food = ā) → **Nā wai tēnei kai?**
- **ki** vs **i** — "I am going TO school" vs "I went FROM/AT school" → **Kei te haere au ki te kura** · **I te kura au inanahi**
- **keke** vs **kēkē** — "I want to eat the cake" → **Kei te hiahia au ki te kai i te keke.** (NOT kēkē — very different!)

_Discussion prompt: Why do you think macrons matter so much in te reo Māori? If you left the macron off "nō", what confusion might happen? Can you think of English words where a single letter changes the meaning completely — similar to how macrons work?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Precision practice**

Write sentences that deliberately use confusable words correctly.

- Write two sentences — one using **tōku** correctly and one using **tāku** correctly.
- Write a sentence using **mō** (for/about): **He kōrero mō te kura tēnei.** (This is a talk about school.)
- Write a sentence using **ki** (to) and one using **i** (at/past location) — make them clearly different.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph (4 sentences) that includes tōku, tāku, nō, and mō — all used correctly. Underline each one.

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupu tūāhua — Prepositions: i, ki, o, a, nō, mō, hei

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Location · AO 2.2: Possessions · Grammar Level 1–2: Prepositions of location, direction, possession, purpose*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.5 — Prepositions of location & direction; AO 2.2 — Prepositions of possession & purpose

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The small words that hold everything together**

Prepositions are the small words that show relationships between things — where something is, where it is going, who it belongs to, what it is for. In te reo Māori, a small set of prepositions does enormous work. Today we look at them all together so you can see the full picture.

> **i** — at (location in past) / from / by
> **ki** — to / towards / into
> **kei** — at (location right now)
> **o / ō** — of (ō-category possession/connection)
> **a / ā** — of (ā-category possession/connection)
> **nō** — belonging to (ō-category) / from (origin)
> **nā** — belonging to (ā-category) / by/through (agent)
> **[mō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C5%8D)** — for / about / concerning
> **[mā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81)** — for (ā-category recipient)
> **[hei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hei)** — at (future location) / as / for (purpose) / as / for (purpose)
> **ko** — identity marker (not a preposition but works like one)

See them in action:

**Kei te noho ia i Ōtautahi.** — She lives in Christchurch. (i = at, general location)
**Kei te haere au ki Ōtautahi.** — I am going to Christchurch. (ki = to)
**Kei Ōtautahi ia.** — She is in Christchurch right now. (kei = at, present)
**He kōrero mō Aotearoa tēnei.** — This is a talk about New Zealand. (mō = about)
**Hoko mai he miraka māku.** — Buy some milk for me. (mā = for, ā-recipient)
**He taonga hei homai māu.** — A gift to give to you. (hei = for the purpose of)

New place name: **[Ōtautahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C5%8Ctautahi)** = Christchurch

*Tip: Ki = to (movement toward). Kei = at (present location, no movement). I = at (past) or from. Mō = about/for (ō things). Mā = for (ā recipient). These five do 90% of the work.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Fill the gap**

I give you a sentence with a gap — you choose the right preposition.

- "I am going ___ school." Moving toward → **ki** → **Kei te haere au ki te kura.**
- "She is ___ the shop right now." Present location → **kei** → **Kei te toa ia.**
- "This book is ___ you." For a recipient (ā) → **māu** → **He pukapuka māu tēnei.**
- "He is speaking ___ te reo Māori." About/concerning → **mō** → **Kei te kōrero ia mō te reo Māori.**

_Discussion prompt: English has dozens of prepositions — at, in, on, to, for, about, by, with, from, through… Te reo Māori does the same work with about seven. How does a smaller set of prepositions make a language easier or harder to learn?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Preposition challenge**

Use six different prepositions from today in six different sentences.

- Write sentences using **ki**, **kei**, and **i** — make the difference between them clear.
- Write a sentence using **mō** about something you care about.
- Write a sentence using **māku** or **māu** — something for you or me.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short paragraph (5 sentences) about your day that uses at least five different prepositions. Underline each preposition and name it.

#### Wednesday: Ngā kupu matua i mua — Fronted time and place phrases

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time · AO 1.5: Location · Grammar Level 1–2: Fronted adverbials — time and place phrases at the start of sentences*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Fronted time phrases; AO 1.5 — Fronted place phrases

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Put time and place first for impact**

In Week 6 you learned that time words can go at the start of a sentence. Today we go deeper — putting whole TIME PHRASES and PLACE PHRASES at the front, not just single words. This is called fronting, and it is one of the most powerful tools for creating rhythm and emphasis in your te reo.

> Single time word at front (Week 6):
> **Āpōpō ka haere au ki te toa.**
> Tomorrow I will go to the shop.
> 
> Full time PHRASE at front:
> **I te ahiahi o tērā rā, i haere au ki te toa.**
> In the afternoon of that day, I went to the shop.
> 
> Place PHRASE at front:
> **I Ōtautahi, i noho mātou i tētahi whare nui.**
> In Christchurch, we stayed in a big house.
> 
> Both together:
> **I te ata o te Mane, i roto i te kura, i ako ngā tamariki i te reo Māori.**
> On Monday morning, inside the school, the children learned te reo Māori.

The pattern for a fronted time phrase:
**I te [time word/period] o [day/event],** + [main sentence]

Useful time phrase builders:
**i te ata** — in the morning
**i te [ahiahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ahiahi)** — in the afternoon/evening/evening
**i te pō** — at night
**i te [tīmatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABmatanga)** — at the beginning
**i te [mutunga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mutunga)** — at the end
**i tērā wiki** — last week
**i tēnei rā** — today / on this day

The pattern for a fronted place phrase:
**I [place],** + [main sentence]

The comma after the fronted phrase signals to the listener: "scene set — now here comes the main action."

*Tip: Front-loading gives your listener the CONTEXT before the action. It is like setting the stage before the actors enter. Always add a comma after a fronted phrase.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Front it!**

I give you a plain sentence. You move the time or place phrase to the front and add a comma.

- Plain: "I learned te reo in the morning." → Front the time: **I te ata, i ako au i te reo Māori.**
- Plain: "The children played outside yesterday." → Front the time: **Inanahi, i tākaro ngā tamariki i waho.**
- Plain: "We ate in the classroom at lunchtime." → Front the place: **I roto i te akomanga, i kai mātou i te tina.**
- Now try both — front the time AND place: "At the end of school, in the hall, we sang a waiata." → **I te mutunga o te kura, i roto i te whare hui, i waiata mātou.**

_Discussion prompt: Read these two versions aloud: "I went to school in the morning yesterday" vs "Inanahi, i te ata, i haere au ki te kura." Which feels more dramatic? Why does putting time and place first change the feel of a sentence?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Front-loaded sentences**

Write sentences that open with a time or place phrase.

- Write two sentences with fronted TIME phrases — use i te ata and i te ahiahi.
- Write two sentences with fronted PLACE phrases — use i roto i te kura and i waho.
- Write one sentence with BOTH a fronted time phrase AND a fronted place phrase.
- ✦ Bonus: Rewrite this bare sentence with as much fronted detail as you can: "We played." Aim for a sentence that paints a complete picture of when, where, and how — all before the main verb.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō tōku kāinga — Talking about home

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 2.2: Possessions · AO 2.5: Descriptions · AO 1.5: Location*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Personal information & home; AO 2.5 — Describing your home

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Describing where you live**

This week you learned confusable words, prepositions, and fronted phrases. Today we use all three in a conversation about home — where you live, what it is like, and what happens there. This is deeply personal language — the kind that helps you really connect with someone.

> **A:** Nō hea koe?
> **B:** Nō Ōtautahi ahau. He tāone nui, he ātaahua hoki.
> **A:** Kei hea tō kāinga i ināianei?
> **B:** Kei te taha o te awa tōku kāinga. He whare hou, he whare māmā hoki.
> **A:** He aha ngā mea pai o tō wāhi noho?
> **B:** Ko ēnei ngā mea pai: he nui ngā rākau, he mārie te noho, ā he tata ki te kura hoki.
> **A:** Ka pai! I te ahiahi, he aha āu mahi i tō kāinga?
> **B:** I te ahiahi, i te māra, ka mahi au. Ka tiaki hoki au i ngā kararehe o mātou.

New words from this conversation:
**[tāone](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81one)** — town / city
**te taha o** — the side of / beside
**awa** — river
**māmā** — light / easy (also: mild earlier) — context tells you which
**wāhi noho** — place of living / neighbourhood
**[rākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81kau)** — tree / trees
**[tata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tata)** — close / nearby
**māra** — garden
**[tiaki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tiaki)** — to look after / guard / guard
**[kararehe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kararehe)** — animal / pet

Notice the fronted phrase at the end: **I te ahiahi** — in the afternoon. It sets the time before the action. And **ko ēnei ngā mea pai** from Week 7 — introducing a list of good things.

*Tip: Nō hea koe? = Where are you from? Kei hea tō kāinga? = Where is your home right now? Two different questions — know which one you are answering.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Describe your home**

Build the home conversation piece by piece. I ask — you answer using this week's structures.

- I ask: **Nō hea koe?** You answer with your town/city and one He description of it.
- I ask: **Kei hea tō kāinga?** Use a preposition and a location: **Kei te taha o te…** or **Kei roto i te…**
- I ask: **He aha ngā mea pai o tō wāhi noho?** Use Ko ēnei to introduce a list of three good things.
- I ask: **I te ahiahi, he aha āu mahi?** Answer with a fronted time phrase and two activities.

_Discussion prompt: Your home and where you are from is deeply connected to your identity in te ao Māori. The pepeha from Week 4 connected you to mountains, rivers, and iwi. How does today's conversation connect to that same idea — that place and identity are inseparable?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He kōrero mō tōku kāinga**

Write a full description of your home and neighbourhood in te reo Māori.

- Write where you are from and one thing that makes your town special: **Nō… ahau. He…**
- Write where your home is using a preposition: **Kei te taha o / Kei roto i / Kei tata ki…**
- Use Ko ēnei to list three good things about your neighbourhood.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a six-sentence paragraph about your home using: a fronted time phrase, a Ko ēnei list, at least three prepositions, and one confusable word pair used correctly (tōku/tāku or nō/nā).

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What is the difference between "keke" and "kēkē" in te reo Māori?
  - They are spelling variations of the same word 
  - keke = cake, kēkē = armpit — the macron completely changes the meaning ✅
  - keke is formal, kēkē is informal 
  - kēkē is the plural of keke 
- Which possessive would you use for "my school"? (kura is ō-category)
  - tāku kura 
  - nāku kura 
  - tōku kura ✅
  - māku kura 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Kei te haere au ki te kura." Which preposition shows movement TOWARD a place?
  - kei 
  - i 
  - ki ✅
  - o 
- "He pukapuka māu tēnei." What does māu mean?
  - for me 
  - for him/her 
  - for you ✅
  - for us 

**Wednesday questions**

- "I te ata, i ako au i te reo Māori." What is the purpose of putting "i te ata" at the front?
  - It makes the sentence shorter 
  - It sets the time context before the main action — fronting for impact ✅
  - It is a grammar rule that verbs must come last 
  - It replaces the need for a tense marker 
- What punctuation always follows a fronted time or place phrase in te reo Māori?
  - A full stop 
  - A semicolon 
  - A comma ✅
  - A colon 

**Thursday questions**

- "Nō hea koe?" vs "Kei hea tō kāinga?" — what is the difference?
  - They mean exactly the same thing 
  - Nō hea asks where you are from (origin), Kei hea asks where your home is right now (location) ✅
  - Nō hea is formal, Kei hea is casual 
  - Nō hea asks about the future, Kei hea asks about the past 
- What does "kararehe" mean?
  - garden 
  - tree 
  - animal / pet ✅
  - neighbour

### Week 9

#### Monday: Ngā kupu oro — Sound words and vivid language

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Descriptive and figurative language · AO 2.3: Expressive communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Onomatopoeia, sound imagery, expressive vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Vivid & expressive vocabulary; AO 2.3 — Expressive communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Words that sound like what they mean**

Some of the most powerful words in any language are ones that imitate sounds or carry a strong physical feeling. Te reo Māori is rich with these — words that bring a scene alive the moment you hear them. Today we collect the best ones and learn to use them deliberately.

> **[ngunguru](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ngunguru)** — to growl / rumble (deep sound)
> **[hūhū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%ABh%C5%AB)** — to hum / buzz (insect or engine)
> **[pakū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pak%C5%AB)** — to bang / explode / crack
> **[ngāoro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C4%81oro)** — to roar / rushing sound of water or wind
> **[tangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tangi)** — to cry / sound of weeping (also: funeral / to ring)
> **[kō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8D)** — the sound of a bird call
> **[oro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=oro)** — sound / noise in general
> **[papā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pap%C4%81)** — to slap / crack loudly
> **[hūpē](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%ABp%C4%93)** — runny, dripping (wet sound)
> **[hau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hau)** — wind / breath / spirit

These words do double work — they describe AND create the sound at the same time.

In a sentence:
**I ngunguru te whatitoka i te hau kino.**
The door growled/rumbled in the fierce wind.

**Ka pakū te whatitoka i tōna tūkitanga.**
The door cracked/banged at its closing.

**I tangi ngā tamariki ā te pō.**
The children cried through the night.

New words:
**whatitoka** — door
**hau kino** — fierce wind / bad weather
**tūkitanga** — a striking / closing / impact

You can also pair these sound words with tino for intensity:
**He tino ngāoro te awa i te ua nui.**
The river roared greatly in the heavy rain.

*Tip: Sound words wake a listener up. Drop one into a sentence and the whole thing comes alive. Use them sparingly — one per paragraph is enough.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Bring the scene alive**

I describe a scene — you choose a sound word that fits and build a sentence around it.

- Scene: a storm outside at night. Which sound word fits? → ngāoro, hau, pakū → **I ngāoro te ua i te pō — he pakū hoki te uira.**
- Scene: a dog in the yard early morning. → **I ngunguru te kurī i te ata.** (kurī = dog)
- Scene: a child who has hurt themselves. → **I tangi kaha te tamaiti — i rongo ia i te mamae.** (rongo = feel, mamae = pain)
- Now you make one: pick a sound word and describe something you heard today using it in a Kei te or I sentence.

_Discussion prompt: English has onomatopoeia like "buzz", "crash", "splash". Which of today's te reo sound words do you think sounds most like what it means? Why do you think languages around the world independently develop words that imitate sounds?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Sound sentences**

Use sound words deliberately to make your writing vivid.

- Write three sentences — each one using a different sound word from today.
- Take this bare sentence and add a sound word: "The wind came." → Make it vivid.
- Describe your school at lunchtime using at least two sound words.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a six-sentence scene — morning at your home, or a storm, or a sports match — using at least three sound words. Read it aloud. Does it sound alive?

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupu hono katoa — All conjunctions: ā, engari, nō reira, ahakoa, mehemea, i te wā

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Full range of coordinating and subordinating conjunctions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Conjunctions & complex sentences; AO 2.5 — Connecting ideas

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Your full conjunction toolkit**

Across this term you have learned conjunctions one by one. Today we gather them all in one place, compare them side by side, and practise choosing the right one deliberately. By the end of this lesson you should be able to join any two ideas in te reo Māori.

> **Coordinating (joining equal ideas):**
> **ā** — and then / and (sequence or addition)
> **engari** — but (contrast)
> **nō reira** — so / therefore (result)
> **me** — and / with (linking nouns or people)
> 
> **Subordinating (one idea depends on the other):**
> **nō te mea / nā te mea** — because (reason)
> **[ahakoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ahakoa)** — although / even though / even though (concession)
> **[mehemea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mehemea)** — if / supposing that / supposing (condition)
> **[i te wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=i+te+w%C4%81)** — when (referring to a time in the past or ongoing) (time clause, past)
> **ā tōnā wā** — when (future — when the time comes)

See them all in action in one connected passage:

**I haere au ki te kura i te ata, ā i ako mātou i te reo Māori.**
I went to school in the morning, and then we learned te reo Māori.

**I te wā i mutu te akoranga, i tākaro mātou i waho.**
When the lesson finished, we played outside.

**Ahakoa i ua, i tākaro tonu mātou.**
Although it rained, we kept playing.

**I ngenge au nō reira i hoki au ki te kāinga.**
I was tired so I went home.

**Mehemea ka pērā anō āpōpō, ka haere anō au.**
If it is the same again tomorrow, I will go again.

Five conjunctions, one story. Every sentence connected.

*Tip: Ask yourself before you write: am I ADDING (ā/me), CONTRASTING (engari/ahakoa), SHOWING A RESULT (nō reira), giving a REASON (nō te mea), a CONDITION (mehemea), or a TIME (i te wā)? The answer tells you which conjunction to reach for.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Choose the right conjunction**

I give you two ideas. You choose the conjunction that shows the right relationship and join them.

- "She was tired." + "She finished the work." Time relationship → **i te wā** → **I te wā i ngenge ia, i mutu tonu tāna mahi.**
- "I want to swim." + "It is cold." Contrast → **ahakoa** → **Ahakoa he makariri, kei te hiahia au ki te kaukau.**
- "He worked hard." + "He won." Result → **nō reira** → **I mahi kaha ia nō reira i toa ia.** (toa = win)
- Now your turn: give me two ideas. The class decides which conjunction fits — and WHY.

_Discussion prompt: Can you use the wrong conjunction and still be understood? Yes — but it changes the meaning. "I mahi kaha ia ā i toa ia" (he worked hard AND won) vs "i toa ia nō reira i mahi kaha ia" (he won, therefore he worked hard) — very different stories. Which conjunction you choose SHAPES the meaning._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Conjunction challenge**

Write one sentence using each conjunction. Make each one clearly different.

- Write sentences using **ā**, **engari**, and **nō reira** — one each.
- Write sentences using **ahakoa**, **mehemea**, and **i te wā** — one each.
- Write a four-sentence mini-story that uses at least three different conjunctions naturally.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full paragraph (6–8 sentences) about something that happened this term. Use every conjunction from today at least once. Underline each one and label which type it is.

#### Wednesday: He kōrero tūhono — Paragraph cohesion in te reo

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended and structured communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Paragraph structure, topic sentences, pronoun links, connective flow*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Cohesion & paragraph structure; AO 1.7 — Structured written language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Making paragraphs flow**

You can write great individual sentences. Today we work on how sentences CONNECT — how a paragraph flows so that each sentence leads naturally into the next. In te reo Māori, there are three main tools for cohesion: pronoun links, connective words, and sentence echoing.

> WEAK — no flow:
> **He pai tōku kura. He nui ngā ākonga. He kaiako pai tōku kaiako. I ako ngā ākonga i te reo Māori.**
> 
> STRONG — connected:
> **He pai tōku kura nō te mea he nui ngā ākonga. Ko rātou ngā tamariki māhoe o tō mātou hapori. I ako rātou i te reo Māori i tēnei wiki, ā i tino pai tā rātou mahi. Nō reira, he kura tino motuhake tōku kura.**

Three tools for paragraph cohesion:

**1. Pronoun links** — replace a noun with a pronoun so you don't repeat the noun:
"ngā ākonga… Ko rātou… I ako rātou…"
(the students… They are… They learned…)

**2. Connective words** — signal the relationship between sentences:
nō reira (therefore), ā (and then), engari (but), nō te mea (because)

**3. Sentence echoing** — repeat a key word or idea to tie sentences together:
"He pai tōku kura… he kura tino motuhake tōku kura."
(My school is good… my school is truly special.)

New words:
**[māhoe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81hoe)** — talented / bright
**[hapori](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hapori)** — community
**[motuhake](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=motuhake)** — special / unique / independent / independent

*Tip: After writing each sentence, ask: does this connect to what came before? If you had to jump to a new idea, use a connective. If you mentioned a noun, replace it with a pronoun next time. If you want to land a final point, echo your opening.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Fix the paragraph**

I give you a weak, disconnected paragraph. We improve it together — one sentence at a time.

- Sentence 1 is fine: **He pai tōku kura.** Sentence 2 repeats the noun: "He nui ngā ākonga o tōku kura." → Replace with a pronoun: **He nui ō rātou kaha.** (There is much ability among them.)
- Sentence 3 has no connective: "I ako ngā ākonga." → Add one: **I tēnei wiki, i ako rātou i ngā kupu hou.**
- Sentence 4 needs to land with an echo: "He kura pai." → Echo the opening: **Nō reira, he kura tino motuhake tōku kura.**
- Now read the full improved paragraph aloud together. Can you feel the difference in how it flows?

_Discussion prompt: A paragraph should feel like a conversation where each sentence responds to the last. Which of the three cohesion tools — pronoun links, connectives, or echoing — do you think is the most powerful? Why?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Write a cohesive paragraph**

Write a full paragraph in te reo Māori that flows from beginning to end.

- Write a topic sentence using **He** or **Ko** about your class, school, or something you care about.
- Write two or three supporting sentences — use pronoun links to avoid repeating nouns.
- Add a connective (nō reira / ā / engari) before one of your sentences.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full five-sentence paragraph and deliberately use all three cohesion tools — pronoun link, connective, and sentence echo. Highlight each tool in a different colour.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He arotake — Looking back, moving forward

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 2.5: Expressive vocabulary · AO 1.4: Personal information*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Reflection & extended kōrero; AO 2.5 — Expressive vocabulary review

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Reflecting on what you have learned**

This is the second-to-last week of Term 1. Today we use everything you have built — vivid words, conjunctions, cohesion — in a reflective conversation about your learning journey this term. This kind of reflective language is important in te ao Māori and in life.

> **A:** He pēhea tōu whakaaro mō tēnei wiki?
> **B:** He tino pai ki a au tēnei wiki. I ako au i ngā kupu oro — pēnei i te "ngunguru" me te "pakū". Ka taea e au te whakamahi i ērā kupu ināianei.
> **A:** He aha te mea i uaua ki a koe?
> **B:** I uaua ki a au ngā kupu tūhono — engari ināianei, ka mārama ana au ki a rātou.
> **A:** He aha tāu whainga mō te kāhui o tērā wiki?
> **B:** Ko tāku whainga: ka ako au i ngā kupu arataki katoa o Wīki 10 — ā ka kōrero māmā au i te reo Māori!
> **A:** Ka pai tāu whakaaro! He ātaahua tō ara ako.

New words and phrases for reflection:

**whakaaro** — thought / opinion / idea
**He aha tāu whakaaro mō…?** — What do you think about…?
**Ka taea e au te…** — I am able to… / I can now…
**Ka mārama ana au** — I understand / I am understanding
**i uaua ki a au** — it was difficult for me
**[whainga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whainga)** — goal / aim / target / target
**[arataki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=arataki)** — to guide / lead
**[ara ako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ara+ako)** — learning pathway / journey / journey
**He ātaahua tō ara ako.** — Your learning journey is beautiful.

Notice: **ka… ana** is a continuous present form — "I am (currently) understanding." It implies the understanding is ongoing and growing.

*Tip: Ka taea e au te kōrero i te reo Māori — I am able to speak te reo Māori. Learn this sentence cold. Say it at the start of every te reo session for the rest of the year.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Reflect together**

We have a structured reflection conversation. I ask — you answer honestly, then ask me back.

- I ask: **He aha te mea i pai rawa atu ki a koe i tēnei wāhanga?** (What did you like most this term?) Answer with at least two sentences.
- I ask: **He aha te mea i uaua ki a koe?** (What was difficult for you?) Use **i uaua ki a au… engari…** to show you overcame it.
- I ask: **Ka taea e koe te kōrero i te reo Māori ināianei?** (Can you speak te reo Māori now?) Answer using **Ka taea e au te…** + three things you can do.
- Close: state your goal for Week 10 — **Ko tāku whainga mō tērā wiki:** + your aim.

_Discussion prompt: He ātaahua tō ara ako. What does it mean to say your learning journey is beautiful? In te ao Māori, learning is not just about knowledge — it connects you to people, to land, and to identity. How has learning te reo Māori this term connected you to something bigger than just words?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu ara ako — Your learning journey**

Write a reflective piece about your learning this term — entirely in te reo Māori.

- Write two sentences about what you can do now that you couldn't do in Week 1. Use **Ka taea e au te…**
- Write one sentence about something that was difficult using **i uaua ki a au… engari…**
- Write your goal for Week 10 using **Ko tāku whainga:**
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full reflective paragraph (6 sentences) about your Term 1 journey. Use a topic sentence, pronoun links, a conjunction, and close with an echo of your opening. Share it with a partner.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Which te reo Māori word best describes a deep rumbling or growling sound?
  - tangi 
  - pakū 
  - ngunguru ✅
  - hūhū 
- "I tangi ngā tamariki ā te pō." What does tangi mean in this context?
  - the children laughed through the night 
  - the children cried through the night ✅
  - the children played through the night 
  - the children slept through the night 

**Tuesday questions**

- Which conjunction would you use to show a RESULT or CONCLUSION?
  - ā 
  - ahakoa 
  - nō reira ✅
  - i te wā 
- "Ahakoa i ua, i tākaro tonu mātou." What relationship does ahakoa show?
  - a time — when it rained we played 
  - a result — because it rained we played 
  - a concession — DESPITE the rain, we still played ✅
  - a condition — if it rains we will play 

**Wednesday questions**

- What is "sentence echoing" in a paragraph?
  - Saying the same sentence twice in a row 
  - Repeating a key word or idea at the end to connect back to the opening ✅
  - Using the same verb in every sentence 
  - Writing sentences that are all the same length 
- What does "motuhake" mean?
  - difficult 
  - tired 
  - special / unique / independent ✅
  - beautiful 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ka taea e au te kōrero i te reo Māori." What does this sentence mean?
  - I want to speak te reo Māori 
  - I used to be able to speak te reo Māori 
  - I am able to speak te reo Māori ✅
  - I am learning to speak te reo Māori 
- What does "whainga" mean?
  - thought / opinion 
  - learning journey 
  - goal / aim / target ✅
  - community 

---

### Week 10

#### Monday: Arotake — Term 1 vocabulary and pronunciation review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.3: Numbers · AO 2.5: Full vocabulary review · All Level 1–2 AOs consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings review; AO 2.5 — Vocabulary consolidation

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Everything we know: vocabulary celebration**

This is the final week of Term 1. Today we celebrate how much vocabulary you have built — from simple greetings in Week 1 to vivid sound words and reflective language in Week 9. We are going to do a full vocabulary review, organised by theme, and find the gaps worth filling before the end of term.

> **GREETINGS & FAREWELL** (Week 1)
> Kia ora · Tēnā koe · Tēnā koutou katoa · E noho rā · Hei konā rā
> 
> **PEOPLE & IDENTITY** (Weeks 1–2, 4)
> au · koe · ia · tāua · mātou · tātou · Ko [name] tōku ingoa · Nō [place] ahau
> 
> **FOOD & DRINK** (Week 2)
> āporo · panana · parāoa · miraka · wai · tī · keke
> 
> **ACTIONS** (Weeks 1, 3, 6)
> haere · kai · inu · ako · kōrero · tākaro · mahi · pānui · tuhituhi · omaoma · kaukau
> 
> **PLACES** (Weeks 3–4, 8)
> kura · kāinga · toa · awa · roto · māra · akomanga
> 
> **DESCRIPTIONS** (Weeks 2, 5, 7)
> nui · iti · roa · poto · pai · kino · ātaahua · tino · ake · rawa atu
> 
> **TIME** (Weeks 6–8)
> ināianei · āpōpō · inanahi · i te ata · i te ahiahi · i te pō

Let's count what you know:

✓ Greetings: 5+ phrases
✓ Personal pronouns: 8 (au, koe, ia, māua, tāua, mātou, tātou, koutou…)
✓ Verbs/actions: 12+
✓ Nouns (places, food, objects): 20+
✓ Adjectives: 10+
✓ Numbers: 1–100
✓ Question words: 11
✓ Conjunctions: 7
✓ Prepositions: 8
✓ Time expressions: 8+
✓ Possessives: 6 (tōku, tāku, tōu, tāu, tōna, tāna)

That is well over 100 pieces of te reo Māori in 9 weeks.
**Ka rawe!**

*Tip: The fastest way to review vocabulary is to use it — not read it. Close the list and try to say everything from memory. What comes easily? What do you need to look at again? That gap is your target.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Vocabulary blitz**

Speed round — I call a category, you give me as many words as you can. Class builds the list together on the board.

- ACTIONS: How many verbs can we list in 60 seconds? Aim for 10+. Teacher writes each one on the board.
- DESCRIPTIONS: Call out every adjective you know — including the comparison forms (ake, rawa atu, tino).
- QUESTION WORDS: Can someone recite all 11 question words from memory? Ko wai, He aha, Kei hea…
- WILD CARD: Say a sentence using a word from EACH category — greetings, action, description, time, place — all in one sentence.

_Discussion prompt: Look at everything on the board. Which category surprised you — you know more than you thought? Which category has the most gaps? Set yourself a personal target: I will learn ___ more words from ___ category before the end of this week._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Personal vocabulary audit**

Do a personal review of your own vocabulary knowledge.

- Without looking at the list: write down every te reo Māori word you can remember in 3 minutes. Count them.
- Go back to Week 1 in your mind — what are the five most important things you learned that week? Write them.
- Write the one word or phrase from this term that you find most useful. Write three sentences using it.
- ✦ Bonus: Create your own personal "Term 1 kupu list" — organised by category, from memory. Then check it against the lesson summaries. What did you miss? What did you remember perfectly?

#### Tuesday: Arotake — Term 1 grammar review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All Grammar Level 1–2 structures consolidated: Kei te, I, Ka, Ko, He, Kāore, possessives, prepositions, conjunctions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Grammar consolidation; AO 2.5 — Structure review

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Every structure you know**

Today we put every grammar structure from this term on the table. You have learned more than you realise. Watch how all the pieces fit together — and how one structure leads naturally into the next.

> **IDENTITY: Ko**
> Ko Aroha tōku ingoa. Ko tōku kura tōku wāhi pai.
> 
> **DESCRIPTION: He**
> He ātaahua tōku kura. He nui ake tēnei whare i tērā.
> 
> **PRESENT: Kei te**
> Kei te ako au i te reo Māori. Kei te pēhea koe?
> 
> **PAST: I**
> I haere au ki te kura inanahi. I ngenge ia nō reira i hoki ia ki te kāinga.
> 
> **NARRATIVE: Ka**
> Ka haere ia ki te toa. Ka hoko ia i te āporo.
> 
> **NEGATIVE: [Kāore](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%81ore)**
> Kāore au e kai ana. Kāore au i haere.
> 
> **POSSESSION: tōku/tāku**
> Ko Mere tōku ingoa. Kei te pānui au i tāku pukapuka.
> 
> **COMPLEX: ahakoa / mehemea / i te wā**
> Ahakoa i ua, i haere tonu mātou. Mehemea ka haere koe, kōrero mai.

The full picture of what you can do:

→ Identify things and people (Ko)
→ Describe things (He + adjective)
→ Talk about now (Kei te)
→ Talk about the past (I)
→ Tell a story (Ka)
→ Say something is NOT happening (Kāore)
→ Show who things belong to (tōku/tāku and friends)
→ Ask any question (Ko wai / He aha / Kei hea / E hia / Tokohia / Pēhea…)
→ Join ideas (ā / engari / nō reira / ahakoa / mehemea / i te wā)
→ Describe in detail (adjectives, adverbs, fronted phrases)

That is a complete communication toolkit. You can express almost any idea with these structures.

*Tip: You do not need to remember the grammar names — you need to feel when to use each structure. The best way to do that is to speak and write as much as possible. The names help you think about it; the practice makes it automatic.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Structure challenge**

I give you a concept to express — you choose the right structure and build the sentence.

- Express who you are: → Ko + name/identity. Express what something is like: → He + description.
- Express something happening right now AND something that happened yesterday: → Kei te… / I…
- Say something is NOT happening: → Kāore au e… ana. Say it did NOT happen: → Kāore au i…
- Join these with the right conjunction: "I was tired" + "I kept working" → **Ahakoa i ngenge au, i mahi tonu au.**

_Discussion prompt: Which structure do you feel most confident with? Which one still feels uncertain? Be honest — the gaps you name now are the ones you can fix in Week 10 and Term 2._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Grammar self-assessment**

Test yourself on every structure from this term.

- Write one sentence using each of: Ko, He, Kei te, I, Ka, Kāore. (Six sentences.)
- Write one sentence using tōku and one using tāku — make sure the category is right.
- Write a complex sentence using one of: ahakoa, mehemea, i te wā.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a ten-sentence paragraph that uses EVERY structure from this term at least once. Tell a story about a day in your life — past, present, description, and all.

#### Wednesday: Arotake — Term 1 sentence types review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.5: Descriptive language · All sentence types from Term 1 consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Sentence variety review; AO 2.5 — Rich description review

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From bare to brilliant: the full sentence toolkit**

Today we review every sentence type you have built this term — simple, compound, complex, descriptive, fronted, layered — and practise moving between them fluently. A skilled speaker can shift from a short punchy sentence to a long flowing one in a heartbeat. Let's do that.

> **SIMPLE — bare and clear:**
> Kei te ua. Ka tū ia. He pai tēnei.
> 
> **COMPOUND — joined:**
> I haere au ki te toa ā i hoko au i te āporo.
> 
> **COMPLEX — subordinate clause:**
> Ahakoa i ua, i haere tonu mātou ki te kura.
> 
> **DESCRIPTIVE — layered:**
> He āporo whero nui tēnei — he tino pai hoki!
> 
> **FRONTED — time or place first:**
> I te ata o Mane, i roto i te akomanga, i ako ngā tamariki.
> 
> **LISTED — Ko ēnei:**
> Ko ēnei ngā mea i ako ai mātou: he kupu hou, he hanganga hou, he kōrero hou.
> 
> **COMPARATIVE:**
> He pai ake te reo Māori i te pāngarau ki a au — he pai rawa atu!

The progression from bare to brilliant:

BARE: **Kei te ako ngā tamariki.**
+WHERE: **Kei te ako ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
+HOW: **Kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
+TIME: **Ināianei, kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki i te akomanga.**
+WITH WHOM: **Ināianei, kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki me tō rātou kaiako i te akomanga.**
+JOINED: **Ināianei, kei te ako kaha ngā tamariki me tō rātou kaiako i te akomanga — ā he tino pai tā rātou mahi!**

Six layers. One idea. Completely alive.

*Tip: You do not need to use every layer every time. The skill is knowing WHICH layers to add and WHEN. Short when you want impact. Long when you want richness. You now have full control.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build up and strip back**

We take one bare sentence and build it to maximum richness — then strip back to find the "just right" version.

- Start: **I tākaro ia.** Add where, how, time, with whom — one layer at a time. Stop when it feels complete.
- Start: **He pai tōku kura.** Add a reason, a comparison, and a Ko ēnei list. How long can it get?
- Now strip back your longest sentence to its most precise version — not shortest, most precise.
- Read both versions aloud — the fully layered one and the stripped-back one. Which works better? When would you use each?

_Discussion prompt: You have been building sentences for ten weeks. Look back at a sentence you wrote in Week 1. How would you write it differently now? What does that difference tell you about how much you have grown?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Showcase sentences**

Write the best sentences you have ever written in te reo Māori.

- Write your best SIMPLE sentence — punchy and precise.
- Write your best COMPLEX sentence — with a subordinate clause.
- Write your best LAYERED sentence — time, manner, place, with whom.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "best of Term 1" paragraph — eight sentences that show off everything you have learned. Use every sentence type at least once. This is your showcase. Make it sing.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He whakamātautau — End-of-term spoken assessment

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–1.7 and 2.1–2.5 assessed · All six language modes: Whakarongo, Kōrero, Pānui, Tuhituhi, Mātakitaki, Whakaatu*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.4 — Personal information; AO 2.3 — Extended communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What a Term 1 conversation looks like**

Today is your end-of-term spoken assessment. Before we begin, let's look at what a full Term 1 conversation sounds like when everything comes together. This is the standard — and you are closer to it than you think.

> **A:** Tēnā koe. Ko wai tōu ingoa?
> **B:** Tēnā koe. Ko Hēmi tōku ingoa. Nō Ōtautahi ahau. Ko wai koe?
> **A:** Ko Aroha tōku ingoa. Nō [Tāmaki Makaurau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=T%C4%81maki+Makaurau) ahau. Kei te pēhea koe i tēnei rā?
> **B:** Kei te pai au, kia ora. Ahakoa he makariri, kei te hari au — he wiki pai tēnei.
> **A:** He aha ngā mea hou i ako ai koe i tēnei wāhanga?
> **B:** I ako au i ngā hanganga katoa — Kei te, I, Ka, Ko, He, me ngā kupu hono. Ka taea e au te kōrero pai ake ināianei.
> **A:** Ka pai! He aha tāu whainga mō tērā wāhanga?
> **B:** Ko tāku whainga: ka ako au i ngā tūāhua o Wāhanga Rua — ā ka kōrero māmā ake au.
> **A:** Ka rawe! Tēnā koe, tēnā koe, tēnā koe.
> **B:** Tēnā koe, tēnā koe, tēnā koe.

What this conversation demonstrates:
✓ AO 1.1: Tēnā koe · greeting and farewell
✓ AO 1.2: Ko [name] tōku ingoa · introduction
✓ AO 1.4: Nō [place] ahau · personal information
✓ AO 1.6: Tēnā koe (×3) · politeness convention
✓ AO 2.3: Extended kōrero with reasons and reflection
✓ AO 2.5: Descriptive and expressive vocabulary
✓ Grammar: Kei te, I, Ka, Ko, He, ahakoa, Ka taea e au te

You have everything you need. Now go show it.

*Tip: If you forget a word mid-conversation, do not stop — keep going. Say what you CAN say. A real conversation in te reo Māori, even with gaps, is far more impressive than silence.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final practice round**

One full practice conversation with a partner before the assessment. Use the full structure from the model above.

- Greet and introduce: Tēnā koe · Ko [name] tōku ingoa · Nō [place] ahau.
- Ask and answer: Kei te pēhea koe? Answer with a feeling AND a reason using ahakoa or nō te mea.
- Ask and answer: He aha ngā mea hou i ako ai koe? Name three things using Ka taea e au te…
- State your Term 2 goal and close with the three-part farewell: Tēnā koe, tēnā koe, tēnā koe.

_Discussion prompt: You started this term not knowing a single word of te reo Māori. You are finishing it able to have a real conversation. What does that feel like? What would you tell your Week 1 self about what is coming?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whakamātautau — Your assessment**

Perform your end-of-term conversation assessment with a partner.

- **Task 1:** Have a full conversation — at least 8 lines each. Cover greeting, introduction, how you are feeling, what you learned, and a goal.
- **Task 2:** Self-assess against the checklist below. Be honest.
- **Task 3:** Write three sentences in te reo about how you feel about your learning this term.
- ✦ Checklist: ☐ Greeted my partner correctly · ☐ Introduced myself with Ko and Nō · ☐ Used at least one tense (Kei te / I / Ka) · ☐ Used at least one conjunction · ☐ Said something descriptive · ☐ Closed with Tēnā koe (×3)

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Which of these is the correct plural demonstrative for "those things over there" (plural of tērā)?
  - ēnei 
  - ēnā 
  - ērā ✅
  - tērā 
- "Ka taea e au te kōrero i te reo Māori" contains which grammar structure?
  - Kāore negation 
  - Ka taea e… te — I am able to… ✅
  - Kei te present tense 
  - Ko identity marker 

**Tuesday questions**

- Which sentence correctly uses Kāore in the PAST tense negative?
  - Kāore au e haere ana 
  - Kāore au i haere ✅
  - Kāore haere au 
  - Kāore au ki haere 
- You want to say "Although it was cold, we kept working." Which conjunction do you use?
  - nō reira 
  - ā 
  - ahakoa ✅
  - i te wā 

**Wednesday questions**

- What is the main purpose of "sentence echoing" in a paragraph?
  - To fill space when you run out of ideas 
  - To confuse the reader with repetition 
  - To tie the paragraph together by returning to a key word or idea from the opening ✅
  - To make every sentence the same length 
- "I te ata o Māne, i roto i te akomanga, i ako ngā tamariki." What type of sentence is this?
  - A simple sentence 
  - A negative sentence 
  - A fronted sentence with time and place phrases before the main clause ✅
  - A comparative sentence 

**Thursday questions**

- In the end-of-term conversation, which greeting is used when addressing MORE than two people formally?
  - Tēnā koe 
  - Tēnā kōrua 
  - Tēnā koutou katoa ✅
  - Kia ora 
- He wāhanga tino pai tēnei. What does "wāhanga" mean?
  - sentence 
  - question 
  - term / section / part ✅
