## Term 2

### Week 1

#### Monday: I haere au — The past tense unlocked

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: I (past tense marker), action verbs in past*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Past tense & time; AO 2.3 — Retelling events

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Welcome back: from now to then**

Nau mai, hoki mai — welcome back. Last term you mastered Kei te for right now. This term we begin by going back in time. You already met the past tense briefly in Term 1 — today we make it solid. Watch how simple the switch is.

> **Kei te** haere au. — I am going. (RIGHT NOW)
> **I** haere au. — I went. (PAST)
> 
> **Kei te** kai ia. — She is eating.
> **I** kai ia. — She ate.
> 
> **Kei te** ako ngā tamariki. — The children are learning.
> **I** ako ngā tamariki. — The children learned.
> 
> **Kei te** tākaro rātou i waho. — They are playing outside.
> **I** tākaro rātou i waho inanahi. — They played outside yesterday.

The switch from present to past in te reo Māori is the simplest thing you will ever do:

Replace **Kei te** with **I**.

That is it. The verb does not change. The person does not change. The word order does not change. Only the tense marker changes.

**Kei te** → RIGHT NOW
**I** → PAST (it happened, it is done)

In English, you must change the verb: "go → went", "eat → ate", "run → ran" — and you must memorise dozens of irregular forms. Te reo Māori never does this. The verb is ALWAYS the same. Only the marker changes.

Time words that go with I:
**inanahi** — yesterday
**i tērā wiki** — last week
**i tērā tau** — last year
**i mua** — before / previously
**i tēnei ata** — this morning

*Tip: Kei te = now. I = then. Swap the marker, keep everything else exactly the same. That is the whole rule.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Switch the tense**

I give you a present tense sentence — you switch it to past. Then we add a time word.

- Present: **Kei te haere au ki te kura.** → Past: **I haere au ki te kura inanahi.**
- Present: **Kei te kai rātou i te tina.** → Past: **I kai rātou i te tina i tērā wiki.**
- Present: **Kei te mahi kaha ngā ākonga.** → Past: **I mahi kaha ngā ākonga i tēnei ata.**
- Now you give ME a present tense sentence — I will switch it to past. Then swap roles.

_Discussion prompt: In English, switching to past tense means changing the verb — "play → played", "go → went", "eat → ate". Te reo Māori never changes the verb. Only the marker changes. Which system feels more logical to you? Which is easier to learn?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tell me what happened**

Write past tense sentences about real things that happened.

- Write three past tense sentences about what you did inanahi (yesterday).
- Write two past tense sentences about what happened i tērā wiki (last week).
- Take this sentence and make it past: **Kei te omaoma kaha ia i waho.**
- ✦ Bonus: Write a six-sentence recount of something that happened in the holidays. Use I for every sentence, and include at least two time words.

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupumahi o mua — More past tense action verbs

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.5: Location · AO 2.3: Retelling · Grammar Level 1–2: Past tense with action verbs, object marker i*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Retelling past events; AO 1.5 — Past location

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Actions in the past with objects**

Yesterday we switched tense markers. Today we add objects — the THINGS that actions happen to. In te reo Māori, when a verb has a direct object, you mark it with the small word i (different from the past tense I — notice the capital). Watch how they work together.

> **I hoko au** i te āporo.
> I bought an apple. (hoko = buy, i te āporo = the apple)
> 
> **I pānui ia** i tāna pukapuka.
> She read her book.
> 
> **I tuhituhi ngā tamariki** i ā rātou reta.
> The children wrote their letters. ([reta](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=reta) = letter)
> 
> **I kite au** i tōku hoa.
> I saw my friend. (kite = see/find)
> 
> **I [rongo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rongo) ia** i te tangi o te manu.
> She heard the cry of the bird. ([rongo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rongo) = hear/feel, [tangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tangi) = cry/sound, [manu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=manu) = bird)

The structure with an object:

**I** + [verb] + [person] + **i** + [object]

The small **i** before the object is the OBJECT MARKER — it signals "and here is the thing the action happened to."

Do not confuse:
→ **I** (capital, start of sentence) = PAST tense marker
→ **i** (lowercase, before object) = OBJECT marker

Both are in this sentence:
**I** hoko au **i** te āporo.
PAST + buy + I + OBJECT + the apple.

New verbs:
**kite** — see / find / discover
**rongo** — hear / feel / sense
**hoatu** — give (away from speaker)
**homai** — give (toward speaker)
**tango** — take / pick up
**reta** — letter

*Tip: Capital I at the start = past tense. Lowercase i before an object = object marker. Same sound, completely different job. Context and position always make it clear.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Past tense with objects**

Build past tense sentences that include an object. I give you the verb and object — you build the sentence.

- Verb: kite (see), Object: tōku hoa (my friend) → **I kite au i tōku hoa.**
- Verb: hoko (buy), Object: he panana (a banana) → **I hoko ia i he panana.**
- Verb: homai (give to me), Object: tāna pukapuka (her book) → **I homai ia i tāna pukapuka ki a au.**
- Now make your own: something you saw, heard, or found yesterday. Use I + verb + au + i + object.

_Discussion prompt: The object marker i does the same job as "the" in "I bought THE apple" — it points at a specific thing. Why do you think te reo Māori needs a separate marker for this? What would happen to the sentence without it?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — What did you do?**

Write past tense sentences that include direct objects.

- Write three sentences using different verbs: kite, rongo, hoko. Include an object in each.
- Write a sentence using homai: something someone gave you.
- Write a sentence using tango: something you picked up or took.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (5 sentences) about a trip to a shop or market. Use I for every sentence. Include at least three different objects using the object marker i.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Building past tense stories

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Time · Grammar Level 1–2: Sequencing past events with time words and conjunctions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Story sequencing; AO 2.4 — Time & sequence

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Chaining past events into a story**

You can write individual past tense sentences. Today we chain them into a flowing story — connecting events in sequence using time words and conjunctions you already know. Watch how a story builds from simple parts.

> **I [ara](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ara) au i te ata.**
> I woke up in the morning.
> 
> **I kai au i tōku [parakuihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=parakuihi), ā i inu au i tōku tī.**
> I ate my breakfast and I drank my tea.
> 
> **I te wā i mutu tōku parakuihi, i [horoi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=horoi) au i ōku [niho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=niho).**
> When my breakfast was finished, I brushed my teeth.
> 
> **I haere au ki te kura — i tere haere au nō te mea i tata ahau ki te [tūreiti](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%ABreiti)!**
> I went to school — I went quickly because I was nearly late!
> 
> **Ahakoa i [oma](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=oma) au, i [tae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tae) tonu ahau i muri i te [pirangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pirangi).**
> Although I ran, I still arrived after the bell.

New words in this story:
**ara** — wake up / rise
**parakuihi** — breakfast
**horoi niho** — brush teeth (horoi = wash/clean, niho = teeth)
**tūreiti** — late
**tae** — arrive / reach
**pirangi** — bell
**i muri i** — after
**tata** — nearly / close to

Notice the conjunctions doing their jobs:
→ **ā** joining two actions in sequence
→ **i te wā** setting a time clause
→ **nō te mea** giving a reason
→ **ahakoa** showing a surprise

All from Term 1 — now used in a past tense story.

*Tip: A good past tense story has a beginning (scene-set), a middle (sequence of events), and an end (result or reflection). Use your conjunctions to connect the parts — they are the glue.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build the morning story**

We build a class morning story together. I give the first sentence — you add the next one, and we keep going.

- Opening: **I ara au i te ata tino mutunga.** (I woke up very late.) What happened next? Add a sentence with ā.
- Add a when-clause: **I te wā i…, i…** — something that happened when something else did.
- Add a reason using nō te mea — why did something happen the way it did?
- Close with ahakoa — something surprising happened despite the situation.

_Discussion prompt: Notice how the conjunctions create tension and surprise in the story. "Ahakoa i oma au, i tae tonu ahau i muri i te pirangi" — the ahakoa sets up an expectation, then the main clause breaks it. How does that feel different from just saying "I ran. I was late."?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tell your morning story**

Write a past tense story about your morning today — from waking up to arriving at school.

- Write four I sentences about the sequence of what you did this morning.
- Add at least one i te wā clause and one nō te mea reason.
- Add one ahakoa sentence — something surprising or unexpected.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the full story in eight sentences. Include: a fronted time phrase, an object marker, a conjunction, and one vivid sound word from Term 1 Week 9.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō ngā hararei — Talking about the holidays

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.5: Expressive vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Holiday recount; AO 2.4 — Past time expressions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What did you do in the holidays?**

The most natural first conversation after a holiday is "what did you do?" Today we have that conversation entirely in te reo Māori. Listen to how the past tense flows naturally in real back-and-forth dialogue.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! He pēhea ōu hararei?
> **B:** He tino pai! I haere au ki Ōtautahi me tōku whānau.
> **A:** Ka pai! He aha ngā mea i mahia e koutou?
> **B:** I haere mātou ki te [taiao](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=taiao), ā i kite mātou i ngā [manu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=manu) ātaahua o reira.
> **A:** I ngenge koutou?
> **B:** Āe, i ngenge mātou — engari i hari hoki mātou! Ko tērā tōku hararei pai rawa atu.
> **A:** He pēhea te āhua o te [rangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rangi)?
> **B:** I [makariri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=makariri) engari i ātaahua hoki. I ua i tētahi rā — ahakoa i tākaro tonu mātou i waho!

New words for holiday talk:
**hararei** — holiday/holidays
**whānau** — family
**taiao** — nature / the natural world / outdoors
**manu** — bird
**reira** — there / that place (referring back to somewhere mentioned)
**He aha ngā mea i mahia e koutou?** — What things did you (all) do?
**i mahia** — was done (passive — the doing happened to the things)
**e koutou** — by you all (agent marker)
**He pēhea te āhua o te rangi?** — What was the weather like?
**āhua** — appearance / nature / what something is like
**Ko tērā tōku hararei pai rawa atu.** — That was my best holiday ever.

*Tip: He pēhea ōu hararei? — How were your holidays? Learn this cold. It is the first question of every new term for the rest of your school life.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Holiday conversation**

Build the holiday conversation together. I ask — you answer. Then swap.

- I ask: **He pēhea ōu [hararei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hararei)?** You answer with at least two sentences — where you went and one thing you did.
- I ask: **He aha ngā mea i mahia e koe?** List three things using Ko ēnei or just sequencing with ā.
- I ask: **He pēhea te āhua o te rangi?** Describe the weather using He + adjective.
- I ask: **Ko ēhea ngā mea pai rawa atu?** (What were the best things?) Use Ko tērā or He pai rawa atu…

_Discussion prompt: Ko tērā tōku hararei pai rawa atu. — That was my best holiday ever. What makes this sentence work so well as a closing line? How does it use Ko, possession, and a superlative all in one? Could you write an equally strong closing line about something else?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu kōrero hararei**

With a partner, have a full holiday conversation in te reo Māori.

- Ask and answer: **He pēhea ōu hararei?** Both partners respond in at least three sentences.
- Each person shares the best thing about their holidays: **Ko tērā tōku mea pai rawa atu…**
- Describe the weather during your holiday using past tense: **I… te rangi.**
- ✦ Bonus: Write up your holiday recount as a paragraph (7 sentences). Use: past tense throughout, at least two conjunctions, a Ko ēnei list, and one fronted time phrase.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review

  - Change "haere" to "haerea" 
  - Replace "Kei te" with "I" — I haere au ✅

  - Change "au" to "ahau"

  - i tērā wiki ✅
  - i tērā tau 
  - inanahi 
  - i tēnei ata 

**Tuesday questions**

- "I hoko au i te āporo." What is the role of the lowercase "i" before "te āporo"?
  - It is the past tense marker 
  - It marks the object — the thing the action happened to ✅
  - It means "at" 
  - It is part of the verb 
- What does "kite" mean?
  - hear / feel 
  - give (toward speaker) 
  - see / find / discover ✅
  - take / pick up 

**Wednesday questions**

- "I ara au i te ata." What does "ara" mean?
  - eat breakfast 
  - brush teeth 
  - wake up / rise ✅
  - arrive 
- You want to say "When breakfast was finished, I brushed my teeth." Which conjunction do you use?
  - ahakoa 
  - ā 
  - i te wā ✅
  - nō reira 

**Thursday questions**

- How do you ask "How were your holidays?" in te reo Māori?
  - He aha ōu hararei? 
  - He pēhea ōu hararei? ✅
  - Kei te pēhea ōu hararei? 
  - Ko wai ōu hararei? 
- What does "whānau" mean?
  - holiday 
  - nature / outdoors 
  - bird 
  - family ✅

### Week 2

#### Monday: Ka haere ia — The Ka narrative tense

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Time · Grammar Level 1–2: Ka (sequential/narrative tense marker)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Narrative & storytelling; AO 2.4 — Sequential time

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Ka: the storytelling tense**

You know Kei te (now) and I (past). Today we meet the third tense marker — Ka. Ka is the storyteller's tense. It moves a story forward, event by event, like a chain. Listen to how it feels different from I.

> Using **I** — reporting what happened:
> **I haere ia ki te toa. I hoko ia i te āporo. I hoki ia ki te kāinga.**
> She went to the shop. She bought an apple. She returned home.
> 
> Using **Ka** — telling it as a story:
> **Ka haere ia ki te toa. Ka hoko ia i te āporo. Ka hoki ia ki te kāinga.**
> She went to the shop. She bought an apple. She went back home.
> 
> Combined — I for context, Ka for story movement:
> **I te ahiahi o inanahi, ka haere ia ki te toa. Ka hoko ia i te āporo whero nui. Ka hoki ia ki te kāinga, ā ka kai ia i tāna āporo i te pō.**

When to use **Ka**:
→ When telling a story and moving it forward step by step
→ When describing what happened in sequence (like "and then… and then…")
→ When the action follows on naturally from what came before
→ In instructions: Ka tū koe, ka titiro, ka whakarongo (You stand, you look, you listen)

When to use **I**:
→ When reporting a single completed past event
→ When giving a specific past time reference (I haere au inanahi)
→ When negating: Kāore au i haere

The feel: **I** = "it happened." **Ka** = "and then this happened, and then this…"

New word: **i te pō** — at night / in the evening

*Tip: Ka is the "and then" of te reo Māori. Once a story is underway, Ka keeps it moving. Think of it as a chain: Ka… Ka… Ka… each link connected to the last.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Tell it as a story**

I give you a sequence of events — you tell them as a Ka story.

- Events: wake up → eat breakfast → go to school → learn te reo → come home. Tell it: **Ka ara ia. Ka kai ia i tōna parakuihi. Ka haere ia ki te kura…**
- Now combine I and Ka: set the scene with I (past context), then use Ka for the story: **I te Mane ka haere ia ki te toa. Ka hoko ia…**
- Try instructions with Ka: tell a partner how to make a cup of tea step by step. **Ka tango koe i te tī. Ka ringihia e koe te wai…** (ringihia = pour)
- Now swap I and Ka in one of your sentences — how does the meaning or feel change?

_Discussion prompt: Ka is used in traditional Māori storytelling — kōrero pūrākau — to move the narrative forward. Why do you think storytellers prefer Ka over I? What does Ka add to the feeling of a story that I does not?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tell a Ka story**

Use Ka to tell a story with at least five steps.

- Write a five-step Ka story about a character going on a journey. Each sentence starts with Ka.
- Combine I and Ka: open with I te [time], then continue with Ka sentences.
- Write a Ka instruction sequence: how to do something (make a snack, pack a bag, play a game).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full eight-sentence story using I for the opening scene-set and Ka for the body. Include an object marker, a conjunction, and a fronted phrase.

#### Tuesday: Kāore — Negation in present and past

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Kāore e…ana (present negative), Kāore i (past negative)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Negation; AO 2.4 — Negative tense forms

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Saying what is NOT happening**

You can say what IS happening. Now we learn how to say what is NOT. Negation in te reo Māori is completely regular — one word, Kāore, does all the work. Watch how the structure changes between present and past.

> **PRESENT NEGATIVE — Kāore… e… ana:**
> **Kāore** au **e** haere **ana**. — I am not going.
> **Kāore** ia **e** kai **ana**. — She is not eating.
> **Kāore** ngā tamariki **e** tākaro **ana**. — The children are not playing.
> 
> **PAST NEGATIVE — Kāore… i:**
> **Kāore** au **i** haere. — I did not go.
> **Kāore** ia **i** kai. — She did not eat.
> **Kāore** ngā tamariki **i** tākaro. — The children did not play.

The pattern:

PRESENT: **Kāore** + [person] + **e** + [verb] + **ana**
PAST: **Kāore** + [person] + **i** + [verb]

Key differences from the positive:
→ The person comes AFTER Kāore (not after the verb)
→ Present adds e…ana around the verb
→ Past uses i (same as positive past, but person comes first)

Compare:
Positive present: Kei te haere **au** → person at end
Negative present: Kāore **au** e haere ana → person right after Kāore

Positive past: I haere **au** → person at end
Negative past: Kāore **au** i haere → person right after Kāore

The person always moves to right after Kāore in negatives.

*Tip: Kāore pushes the person forward — right after itself. Present negative wraps the verb in e…ana. Past negative uses bare i + verb. Two patterns, one rule about person position.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Make it negative**

I give you a positive sentence — you make it negative. Both present and past.

- Positive present: **Kei te haere au ki te kura.** → Negative: **Kāore au e haere ana ki te kura.**
- Positive past: **I kai ia i te tina.** → Negative: **Kāore ia i kai i te tina.**
- Positive: **Kei te mōhio ngā tamariki.** (The children know.) → Negative: **Kāore ngā tamariki e mōhio ana.**
- Now make your own — say something true that you did NOT do yesterday using Kāore au i…

_Discussion prompt: Kāore is one word that handles all negation in te reo Māori. English uses "not", "didn't", "isn't", "wasn't", "haven't", "can't" and more. How does having one negation word make te reo Māori simpler? Can you think of any disadvantages?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The honest negative**

Practise both forms of negation — present and past.

- Write three present negative sentences using Kāore… e… ana.
- Write three past negative sentences using Kāore… i…
- Write a pair: one positive and one negative about the same event (e.g. I went / I did not go).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (5 sentences) where someone explains what they did NOT do and why. Use Kāore for at least three sentences, and include a reason using nō te mea.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — I, Ka, Kāore in one passage

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Fluent use of multiple tense markers in connected text*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Fluent extended writing; AO 2.4 — Tense control

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Three markers, one story**

You now have I, Ka, and Kāore. Today we use all three in a single connected passage — controlling tense and negation deliberately, the way a confident te reo speaker does. Watch how the three markers each do a different job.

> **I tēnei ata**, ka ara au i te tino ata. Ka [horoi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=horoi) au i ōku [kanohi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kanohi) ā ka kai au i tōku parakuihi.
> <i>This morning, I got up very early. I washed my face and I ate my breakfast.</i>
> 
> Ka haere au ki te kura — **kāore** au i tūreiti i tēnei rā!
> <i>I went to school — I was not late today!</i>
> 
> **I** kite au i tōku hoa Mere i te ara. **Kāore** ia e haere ana ki te kura i tēnei rā nō te mea **i** māuiui ia.
> <i>I saw my friend Mere on the path. She is not going to school today because she was sick.</i>
> 
> Ka [pōuri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C5%8Duri) au mōna — he hoa pai rawa atu ia.
> <i>I felt sad for her — she is the very best friend.</i>

See how the three markers divide the work:

**Ka** — tells the story, moves it forward step by step (ka ara, ka horoi, ka kai, ka haere, ka pōuri)

**I** — reports specific completed events (i kite au, i [māuiui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81uiui) ia)

**Kāore i** — says something did NOT happen in the past (kāore au i tūreiti)
**Kāore e…ana** — says something is NOT happening now (kāore ia e haere ana)

New words:
**kanohi** — face
**ara** — path / road (also: wake up — same word, different context)
**māuiui** — sick / unwell
**pōuri** — sad / dark
**mōna** — for her/him (mō = for/about, na = him/her)

*Tip: Let Ka carry the story forward. Use I to anchor specific facts. Use Kāore to correct, deny, or show what did NOT happen. These three together give you full control of any narrative.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Control the tense**

I give you a story prompt — we build it together, choosing the right marker for each sentence.

- Story: a student wakes up late, rushes to school, but still makes it on time. Which markers? Ka for the rush, Kāore i for "did not miss the bell".
- Story: you were going to visit a friend but they were sick. Use I for the plan, Kāore i for what didn't happen, Kāore e…ana for what is currently not happening.
- Add a pōuri (sadness) sentence using Ka: **Ka pōuri au nō te mea…**
- End the story with a positive Ka sentence — something that happened instead.

_Discussion prompt: Look at the model passage. Count how many times each marker appears: Ka, I, Kāore. Which one appears most? Why do you think Ka dominates in storytelling? What would the passage feel like if you replaced every Ka with I?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Three-marker story**

Write a passage that uses all three markers deliberately.

- Write a Ka chain of at least four events — a morning sequence.
- Add two I sentences — specific facts or things you saw/heard.
- Add one Kāore i sentence and one Kāore e…ana sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a complete eight-sentence story that uses Ka for movement, I for facts, and Kāore for at least two negatives. Tell the story of a day where something went differently than expected.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero kōrero — A flowing conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 2.4: Past and present*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Fluent conversation; AO 1.1 — Greetings & social language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — A real conversation using all three tenses**

Today we put Term 2 Week 2 together in conversation. A natural conversation moves between past and present — what happened, what is happening now, what you think. Watch how Ka, I, and Kāore all appear naturally.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! I haere koe ki hea i tērā rā?
> **B:** I haere au ki te hākinakina. Ka tākaro mātou ā te ahiahi katoa!
> **A:** Ka pai! I toa koutou?
> **B:** Kāore mātou i toa — i [toa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=toa) ērā atu. Engari i tino pai tā mātou tākaro.
> **A:** He pēhea koe ināianei?
> **B:** Kāore au e ngenge ana — kei te hari tonu au! Ka haere anō mātou ā tērā wiki.
> **A:** Mā wai koutou e [ārahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81rahi)?
> **B:** Mā tō mātou kaiako e [ārahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81rahi). He pai rawa atu ia!

New phrases from this conversation:
**I toa koutou?** — Did you (all) win?
**i toa** — won (toa = win/victorious)
**ērā atu** — those others / the others
**tā mātou tākaro** — our (exclusive) playing / our game
**Ka haere anō mātou** — We will go again
**Mā wai koutou e ārahi?** — Who will lead/coach you?
**Mā… e ārahi** — [Person] will lead (mā = by/through for future agent)
**ārahi** — to lead / guide / coach

*Tip: Mā wai e…? = Who will do [something]? Mā [person] e… = [Person] will do it. This is how you talk about who does future actions — very useful for classroom and sports contexts.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Sports or activity conversation**

Build a conversation about a sport or activity you enjoy. Use all three tense markers.

- Opening: greet and ask where they went: **I haere koe ki hea i tērā rā?** Answer with a past event.
- Ask about the result: **I toa koutou?** Answer honestly — win OR use Kāore mātou i toa.
- Ask about now: **He pēhea koe ināianei?** Answer using Kāore e…ana or Kei te.
- Ask who leads/coaches using Mā wai e ārahi? Answer with Mā [name/role] e ārahi.

_Discussion prompt: Kāore mātou i toa — we did not win. But then: i tino pai tā mātou tākaro — our game was very good. This is gracious losing in te reo Māori. How does te ao Māori think about winning and losing? What values does this kind of language reflect?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your activity conversation**

Have a full conversation about a sport or activity with a partner.

- Ask and answer about a past sports or activity event — use I and Ka.
- Ask about the result — use Kāore i toa or i toa.
- Ask how they feel now — use Kei te pēhea koe and Kāore e…ana or Kei te.
- ✦ Bonus: Write up the full conversation (8 lines minimum). Use every tense marker at least once. Include Mā wai e ārahi and end with a Ka haere anō sentence.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka haere ia ki te toa. Ka hoko ia i te āporo." What does Ka signal here?
  - These are future events that have not happened yet 
  - Ka marks the past tense like I does 
  - Ka moves the story forward — and then this happened, and then this ✅
  - Ka is used only for commands 
- When would you use I rather than Ka for a past event?
  - When the event is part of a flowing story 
  - When reporting a specific completed event or giving a time reference ✅
  - Ka and I mean exactly the same thing 
  - I is only used with time words like inanahi 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Kāore au e haere ana." What is the structure of this present negative?
  - Kāore + verb + person + ana 
  - Kāore + person + e + verb + ana ✅
  - Kāore + e + person + verb + ana 
  - Person + Kāore + verb + e + ana 
- "Kāore ia i kai i te tina." What does this sentence mean?
  - She is not eating lunch right now 
  - She did not eat lunch ✅
  - She will not eat lunch 
  - She never eats lunch 

**Wednesday questions**

- What does "māuiui" mean?
  - sad 
  - tired 
  - sick / unwell ✅
  - angry 
- "Ka pōuri au mōna." What does mōna mean?
  - for me 
  - for you 
  - for him/her ✅
  - for them 

**Thursday questions**

- "Mā wai koutou e ārahi?" — What is this question asking?
  - Where are you all going? 
  - Who will lead/coach you all? ✅
  - What will you all do? 
  - How will you all get there? 
- What does "toa" mean in the context of a game or competition?
  - team 
  - player 
  - win / victorious ✅
  - score 

---

### Week 3

#### Monday: Ngā tae — Colours and detailed description

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical characteristics · Grammar Level 1–2: Colour words, He structure with multiple adjectives*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Colours & descriptions; AO 1.6 — Descriptive communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Painting pictures with colour**

You already know a few colours. Today we learn the full colour palette in te reo Māori — and something fascinating about how Māori colour words often come from nature. Listen carefully to the connections.

> **whero** — red (from the redness of the tōtara tree heartwood)
> **[kākāriki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%81k%C4%81riki)** — green (from the kākāriki parrot — bright green bird) (from the kākāriki parrot — bright green bird)
> **[kōwhai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Dwhai)** — yellow (from the kōwhai flower) (from the kōwhai flower)
> **[kikorangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kikorangi)** — blue (kiko = flesh/colour + rangi = sky) (kiko = flesh/colour + rangi = sky)
> **[māwhero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81whero)** — pink (mā = white + whero = red) (mā = white + whero = red)
> **[karaka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=karaka)** — orange (from the karaka tree berry) (from the karaka tree berry)
> **[parauri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=parauri)** — brown (para = residue/sediment + uri = dark) (para = residue/sediment + uri = dark)
> **[mā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81)** — white
> **[mangu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mangu)** — black
> **[hina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hina)** — grey / silver
> **[kōura](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Dura)** — gold / golden (from the kōura/crayfish shell) (from the kōura/crayfish shell)

Notice the pattern — almost every colour word connects to something in the natural world:
→ kākāriki (green) = the parrot
→ kōwhai (yellow) = the flower
→ kikorangi (blue) = sky-flesh (sky colour)
→ karaka (orange) = the tree

This is te ao Māori — the Māori world view sees language growing from the land and its creatures.

In a sentence, colours follow the noun as always:
**He pōtae kākāriki tāku.** — I have a green hat. (pōtae = hat)
**He pukapuka kikorangi tēnā.** — That is a blue book.
**He kuri parauri tō rātou.** — They have a brown dog. (kuri = dog)

And you can stack them:
**He pōtae kākāriki māmā tēnei.** — This is a light green hat.

*Tip: Colour names in te reo come from nature — a parrot, a flower, the sky. Knowing the source helps you remember the colour. kākāriki = the bright green parrot = bright green.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Colour the room**

Look around the room. I point to objects — you describe them using He + noun + colour.

- Your pencil case: **He kete pene [tae] tāku.** What colour is it? Fill in the colour.
- The door: **He whatitoka [tae] tērā.** What colour? Add a size adjective too.
- Something outside: **He [noun] [tae] [tae2] tērā.** Can you stack two colour/description words?
- Ask a partner: **He aha te tae o tōu [object]?** (What colour is your [object]?) They answer with He…

_Discussion prompt: Almost all te reo Māori colour words come from plants, birds, or natural phenomena. English colour words come from many different places — "orange" from the fruit, "purple" from a sea snail dye, "blue" from Old French. What does it tell us about a culture when its colour words come from nature?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Colour descriptions**

Use colour words to describe things in detail.

- Write five He sentences describing objects around you — each with a different colour word.
- Write a description of your school bag using at least three descriptive words including a colour.
- Write a He sentence using māwhero (pink) and one using kōura (gold).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a description of your favourite place using colours from nature — trees, sky, water, birds. Use at least four colour words and make your description feel like a painting.

#### Tuesday: He pai ake, he pai rawa atu — Comparatives in new contexts

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Descriptions · AO 2.3: Opinions · Grammar Level 1–2: Comparative ake, superlative rawa atu in persuasion context*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Comparative descriptions; AO 2.3 — Opinions & argument

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Comparing things to argue a point**

Last term you learned ake (more) and rawa atu (most/best). Today we use those same tools in a new context — argument and persuasion. When you want to convince someone of something, you compare. Watch how comparison becomes opinion.

> **He pai ake te hākinakina i te karo i.**
> Sport is better than avoiding it.
> 
> **He ātaahua ake ngā manu o Aotearoa i ngā manu o ērā atu [whenua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whenua).**
> The birds of New Zealand are more beautiful than those of other countries.
> 
> **He nui ake ngā [hua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hua) o te ako i te reo Māori i ngā [uauatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uauatanga).**
> The benefits of learning te reo Māori are greater than the difficulties.
> 
> **Ko te reo Māori te reo tino ātaahua rawa atu o te ao.**
> Te reo Māori is the most beautiful language in the world.

The comparison structure:
**He [adjective] ake te X i te Y.**
[X] is more [adjective] than [Y].

The i before Y means "than" in this comparison context — a new use of i you haven't seen before!

New words:
**hākinakina** — sport / physical activity
**whenua** — land / country
**hua** — fruit / benefit / result
**uauatanga** — difficulty / the state of being hard
**te ao** — the world / the realm (literally: the daylight world)
**ērā atu whenua** — other countries (ērā atu = those others)

And for superlatives in comparison:
**Ko te X te [adjective] rawa atu o [group].**
X is the most [adjective] of [group].

*Tip: He [adj] ake te X i te Y = X is more [adj] than Y. The i here means "than" — a third job for little i (past tense, object marker, and now "than" in comparisons).*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Make your argument**

I give you two things to compare — you build a comparative sentence and defend your position.

- Compare: te reo Māori (te reo Māori) vs pāngarau (maths) for fun. → **He ngāwari ake te reo Māori i te pāngarau ki a au.**
- Compare: ināianei vs inanahi for weather. → Build a He ātaahua ake / He makariri ake sentence.
- Use a superlative: **Ko [something] te [adj] rawa atu o…** Make a bold claim about something you love.
- Now defend your superlative — can you add nō te mea + a reason to justify it?

_Discussion prompt: Ko te reo Māori te reo tino ātaahua rawa atu o te ao. Do you agree? How would you argue against this? In what ways is comparison a form of argument? Can comparison ever be unfair?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your comparisons**

Use ake and rawa atu to express and defend opinions.

- Write three He [adj] ake te X i te Y sentences — compare things you genuinely have opinions about.
- Write one Ko te X te [adj] rawa atu o… sentence — a bold superlative claim.
- Add a nō te mea reason to your superlative sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a five-sentence "argument" paragraph in te reo — make a claim, support it with two comparatives, add a superlative, and close with nō reira. Persuade your reader.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — SVA and tricky agreement

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Accurate communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Subject-verb-object alignment, plural markers ngā and rātou/mātou/tātou*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Grammatical accuracy; AO 2.5 — Plural agreement

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Making singular and plural agree**

In te reo Māori, making sure your nouns and pronouns agree is important for clarity. The key words are the articles — te (the, singular) and ngā (the, plural) — and the pronouns — ia (he/she, one person) vs rātou (they, three or more). Today we make sure these always line up.

> **SINGULAR — te and ia:**
> **Kei te haere te [tamaiti](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tamaiti).** — The child is going.
> **Kei te haere ia.** — He/she is going.
> 
> **PLURAL — ngā and rātou:**
> **Kei te haere ngā [tamariki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tamariki).** — The children are going.
> **Kei te haere rātou.** — They (3+) are going.
> 
> **WATCH:**
> **I kai te kaiako i tāna kai.** — The teacher ate his/her food.
> **I kai ngā kaiako i ā rātou kai.** — The teachers ate their food.
> 
> Notice: tāna (his/her, singular ā) → ā rātou (their, plural ā)

The agreement chain:

SINGULAR noun → te → ia → tāna/tōna
PLURAL noun → ngā → rātou/mātou/tātou → ā rātou/ō rātou

Possessives also go plural:
**tāna** (his/her, ā) → **ā rātou** (their, ā) — the ā stays, rātou replaces na
**tōna** (his/her, ō) → **ō rātou** (their, ō) — the ō stays, rātou replaces na

Common mistakes to avoid:
✗ Kei te haere **ngā tamariki**. Kei te noho **ia**. (mixing plural noun with singular pronoun)
✓ Kei te haere **ngā tamariki**. Kei te noho **rātou**.

New words:
**kaiako** — teacher (you built this with kai- in Term 1 Week 6!)
**ā rātou** — their (ā-category)
**ō rātou** — their (ō-category)

*Tip: Ngā needs rātou. Te needs ia. Never mix them. Check every pronoun — does it match the noun it refers back to? Singular or plural?*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Spot and fix**

I give you sentences — some correct, some with agreement errors. You find the error and fix it.

- "Kei te ako ngā ākonga. Kei te mōhio ia." — Error? → ia should be rātou: **Kei te mōhio rātou.**
- "I kai te kaiako i ō rātou kai." — Error? → One teacher (te) should have tōna: **I kai te kaiako i tōna kai.**
- "Kei te haere mātou. I hoko tāna kai." — Error? → mātou is plural, tāna is singular: **I hoko ā mātou kai.**
- Now write two correct pairs yourself — one singular, one plural — showing perfect agreement.

_Discussion prompt: Why does agreement matter? If you say "ngā tamariki… ia" (the children… he/she), your listener gets confused — who are we talking about now? Agreement is the glue that keeps a listener following the right thread through your sentences._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Agreement practice**

Write sentences that demonstrate correct singular and plural agreement.

- Write three pairs: one sentence with te/ia, one with ngā/rātou — same verb, matching articles and pronouns.
- Write a sentence about your teacher (singular) using tōna/tāna correctly.
- Write a sentence about your classmates (plural, 3+) using ō rātou or ā rātou correctly.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) about your class — mix singular and plural freely, but make sure every pronoun matches its noun. Check each sentence before moving to the next.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He taupatupatu — A class debate

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.5: Formal vocabulary · AO 1.6: Politeness in disagreement*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Debate & persuasion; AO 1.6 — Polite disagreement

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Debating in te reo Māori**

This week you have learned colours, comparatives, and agreement. Today we put them all to work in a debate. Using comparatives to argue, politeness phrases to disagree, and accurate grammar to be credible — this is real te reo in action.

> **[Kaiwhakahaere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Kaiwhakahaere):** Ko tā tātou [kaupeka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaupeka): He pai ake te hākinakina i te karo i.
> **Taha 1:** Āe, he tika tērā. He nui ake ngā hua o te hākinakina — he [hauora](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hauora), he kaha, he hoa hou hoki.
> **Taha 2:** Kāo, kāore au e whakaae ana. He pai ake te noho ki te kāinga me ōu hoa tata nō te mea he mārie ake tērā.
> **Taha 1:** Ka pai tāu whakaaro, engari ko ngā hua hauora o te hākinakina he nui ake i ngā hua o te noho.
> **Taha 2:** He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro — engari he pai tērā! Ko tēnei te akoranga o tēnei kōrero.

New debate vocabulary:
**Kaiwhakahaere** — chairperson / facilitator (whaka + haere = one who makes things go)
**kaupeka** — topic / branch / proposition
**Taha 1 / Taha 2** — Side 1 / Side 2 (taha = side)
**whakaae** — agree / consent
**Kāore au e whakaae ana.** — I do not agree.
**he hauora** — health / wellbeing
**he hoa hou** — new friends
**ko ngā hua hauora** — the health benefits
**Ko tēnei te akoranga o tēnei kōrero.** — This is the lesson of this discussion.

Debate structure in te reo:
1. State position (Ko tā tātou kaupeka…)
2. Support with comparatives (He nui ake ngā hua…)
3. Respond politely (Ka pai tāu whakaaro, engari…)
4. Close graciously (He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro — he pai tērā!)

*Tip: He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro — our opinions are different. This is the gracious way to end a debate in te ao Māori. Disagreement is acknowledged — but the relationship is preserved.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Mini debate**

Class splits into two sides. Teacher (kaiwhakahaere) introduces the topic. Each side argues in te reo.

- Kaupeka: **He pai ake te reo Māori i te pāngarau.** Side 1 agrees — argue why using He nui ake ngā hua…
- Side 2 disagrees: **Kāore mātou e whakaae ana. He pai ake te pāngarau nō te mea…**
- Side 1 responds: **Ka pai tā koutou whakaaro, engari…** Add a comparative to counter.
- Close: everyone together: **He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro — he pai tērā!**

_Discussion prompt: Ko tēnei te akoranga o tēnei kōrero — this is the lesson of this discussion. What IS the lesson? Not who won — but what did the discussion teach you? In te ao Māori, a debate is not about defeating the other side. What is it about?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your debate argument**

Write your side of a debate argument in te reo Māori.

- Choose a side on: "He pai ake te ako i te kāinga i te ako i te kura." Write your position statement.
- Write two supporting comparatives using He [adj] ake…
- Write a polite response to the other side: Ka pai tā koutou whakaaro, engari…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full six-sentence debate contribution — position, two supports, a response to opposition, a superlative claim, and a gracious close with He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Which te reo Māori colour word comes from a New Zealand bird?
  - kikorangi 
  - kōwhai 
  - kākāriki ✅
  - karaka 
- "He pōtae kikorangi tāku." What colour is the hat?
  - green 
  - yellow 
  - blue ✅
  - red 

**Tuesday questions**

- "He pai ake te reo Māori i te pāngarau." What does the "i" before "te pāngarau" mean here?
  - at 
  - than ✅
  - past tense marker 
  - object marker 
- How do you say "Te reo Māori is the most beautiful language in the world" using a superlative?
  - He ātaahua rawa atu te reo Māori i ērā atu reo 
  - Ko te reo Māori te reo tino ātaahua rawa atu o te ao ✅
  - Te reo Māori he ātaahua ake i ngā reo katoa 
  - He reo ātaahua rawa atu te reo Māori 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Kei te ako ngā ākonga. Kei te mōhio ia." What is wrong with this passage?
  - The verb is incorrect 
  - ia (singular he/she) does not match ngā ākonga (plural) — should be rātou ✅
  - The tense marker is wrong 
  - There is no object marker 
- What is the plural ā-category possessive for "their" (three or more people)?
  - tā rātou 
  - ā rātou ✅
  - ō rātou 
  - tō rātou 

**Thursday questions**

- What does "whakaae" mean?
  - disagree 
  - argue 
  - agree / consent ✅
  - debate 
- "He rerekē ā tātou whakaaro — he pai tērā!" How is this phrase used in a debate?
  - To declare a winner 
  - To insult the other side 
  - To graciously acknowledge different views and preserve the relationship ✅
  - To ask for more time

### Week 4

#### Monday: Māua, tāua — Dual pronouns: we two

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.1: Relationships between people · Grammar Level 1–2: Dual pronouns māua/tāua, kōrua, rāua*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.1 — Relationships — dual pronouns; AO 1.6 — Inclusive & exclusive we

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — We two (and why it matters which "we")**

English has one word for "we" — it covers two people, ten people, and everyone in between, and it doesn't tell you if the person you're talking to is included. Te reo Māori has a separate word for every combination. Today we learn the "we two" set — and discover something beautiful about how te reo Māori thinks about relationships.

> **[māua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ua)** — we two (me and someone else — NOT including you) — NOT including you)
> **[tāua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81ua)** — we two (me and you — INCLUDING you) — INCLUDING you)
> **[kōrua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Drua)** — you two
> **[rāua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81ua)** — they two / those two / those two
> 
> **Ka haere māua ki te toa.**
> We two (not you) are going to the shop.
> 
> **Ka haere tāua ki te toa.**
> We two (you and I) are going to the shop.
> 
> **Kei te kōrero rāua.**
> Those two are talking.
> 
> **He aha ā kōrua mahi?**
> What are you two doing?

The inclusive/exclusive distinction:

**tāua** = WE TWO INCLUDING YOU → "come with me"
**māua** = WE TWO EXCLUDING YOU → "just us, not you"

This distinction exists because in te ao Māori, being included or excluded from a group matters deeply — it affects relationships, responsibilities, and mana.

Think of it this way:
→ "Ka haere tāua" = an invitation (we're doing this TOGETHER)
→ "Ka haere māua" = an announcement (we're going — you're not part of this)

The dual pronouns at a glance:
**tāua** — we two (inclusive)
**māua** — we two (exclusive)
**kōrua** — you two
**rāua** — they two

*Tip: Tāua includes the person you're talking to — it's an invitation. Māua excludes them — it's just you and a third person. When in doubt: would you invite them along? Tāua. Would you not? Māua.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Choose the right we**

I describe a situation — you choose tāua or māua and explain why.

- You and your best friend are planning a trip. You're talking to your friend about it. → **tāua** — they ARE included: "Ka haere tāua ki te moana."
- You and your mum went to the shop. You're telling your friend about it now. → **māua** — friend was NOT there: "I haere māua ko tōku māmā ki te toa."
- You're looking at two students across the room: → **rāua**: "Kei te kōrero rāua."
- You're asking two students directly what they're doing: → **kōrua**: "He aha ā kōrua mahi?"

_Discussion prompt: English speakers often find the inclusive/exclusive distinction the hardest thing about te reo Māori pronouns — because English doesn't have it at all. But many languages do — Japanese, Fijian, and many others. What does having this distinction tell us about what te ao Māori values in human relationships?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — We two in action**

Use dual pronouns in real situations.

- Write two sentences using tāua — invite someone to do something with you.
- Write two sentences using māua — report something you and a third person did (excluding the listener).
- Write one sentence about two others using rāua, and one question to two people using kōrua.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short dialogue (6 lines) between two students planning something together. Use tāua at least three times. Make the "inclusive we" feel warm and inviting.

#### Tuesday: Mātou, tātou — Plural pronouns: three or more

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.1: Relationships · Grammar Level 1–2: Plural pronouns mātou/tātou, koutou, rātou*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.1 — Group relationships; AO 1.6 — Inclusive & exclusive plurality

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — We many (and the same choice)**

Yesterday you learned māua/tāua (we two). The same inclusive/exclusive choice applies when there are THREE OR MORE people. Meet the group pronouns.

> **mātou** — we (3+ people, NOT including you)
> **tātou** — we (3+ people, INCLUDING you)
> **koutou** — you all (3+ people)
> **rātou** — they (3+ people)
> 
> **Ka haere mātou ki te kura.**
> We (our group, not you) are going to school.
> 
> **Ka haere tātou ki te kura.**
> We all (including you) are going to school.
> 
> **Kei te ako koutou i te reo Māori.**
> You all are learning te reo Māori.
> 
> **I tākaro rātou i waho inanahi.**
> They (all those people) played outside yesterday.

The full pronoun set — now complete:

SINGULAR:
→ au/ahau (I) · koe (you) · ia (he/she)

DUAL:
→ tāua (we 2, incl.) · māua (we 2, excl.) · kōrua (you 2) · rāua (they 2)

PLURAL:
→ tātou (we 3+, incl.) · mātou (we 3+, excl.) · koutou (you 3+) · rātou (they 3+)

The pattern:
→ tā- = inclusive we
→ mā- = exclusive we
→ kou-/kō- = you (plural)
→ rā- = they/those

Every pronoun you will ever need — all sixteen.

*Tip: tā- always means "including you" (tāua, tātou). mā- always means "not including you" (māua, mātou). The prefix tells you the relationship.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — All the pronouns**

Rapid fire — I describe who is involved, you give the right pronoun.

- The whole class, including the teacher, and you are all in the discussion. → **tātou**
- Your team of five played a game. You're telling someone who wasn't there. → **mātou**
- You're talking to the whole class (everyone there, 3+). → **koutou**
- Three students across the room are working together. → **rātou**

_Discussion prompt: Tātou is one of the most beautiful words in te reo Māori — it means "all of us, including you." It is an act of inclusion. How might saying tātou change the feeling of a meeting, a classroom, or a family gathering? When a rangatira says "tātou katoa" (all of us together) — what is the effect?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — All sixteen pronouns**

Use the full range of pronouns in your writing today.

- Write a sentence using each of: mātou, tātou, koutou, rātou.
- Write a pair: one with mātou (excluding listener) and one with tātou (including listener) about the same event.
- Write a question to the whole class using koutou.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) about a class event or group activity. Use at least four different pronouns from the full set — singular, dual, and plural.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Pronouns in complex sentences

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Pronoun consistency and reference across sentences*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.1 — Pronoun reference; AO 2.3 — Cohesive pronoun use

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Keeping track of who is who**

Now that you have all sixteen pronouns, the challenge is keeping track of them in longer passages. When you shift from talking about rāua (two people) to rātou (a group), or from tāua (we two) to tātou (all of us), the reader needs to follow. Today we practise pronoun clarity.

> UNCLEAR — who is "rātou" here?
> **Ka haere māua ko Hana ki te kura. I kite māua i ō māua hoa. Ka tākaro rātou.**
> We two (Hana and I) went to school. We saw our friends. They played.
> (Who played? Just the friends? Or us too?)
> 
> CLEAR — pronoun signals the group precisely:
> **Ka haere māua ko Hana ki te kura. I kite māua i ō māua hoa tokowhitu. Ka tākaro rātou katoa — ko māua ko rātou.**
> Hana and I went to school. We saw our seven friends. They all played — us two and them.

Tools for pronoun clarity:

1. **State the group first**, then use the pronoun:
"I kite māua i ō māua hoa. Ka tākaro **rātou**." — ✗ unclear
"Ko māua ko rātou — ka tākaro **tātou** katoa." — ✓ clear (now = all of us)

2. **Use katoa (all) to be explicit:**
**Ka tākaro rātou katoa.** — They all played.
**Ka tākaro tātou katoa.** — We all played.

3. **Name the people when switching:**
"Ko māua ko rātou" = "the two of us and them"

New words:
**tokowhitu** — seven (people) (toko- + whitu = counting people)
**katoa** — all / everyone (you've seen this before in Tēnā koutou katoa)
**Ko māua ko rātou** — us two and them

*Tip: When you change who you're talking about, signal it clearly — name the group, use katoa, or restate with Ko [person] ko [person]. Never leave your listener guessing who "rātou" is.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Clarify the pronouns**

I give you an unclear passage — we improve the pronoun clarity together.

- Unclear: "Ka haere rātou. Ka noho ia." → Who is ia? → Make it clear: state the person or repeat the noun.
- Unclear: "I kite māua i rātou. Ka tākaro mātou." → Who is in mātou now? → Add "Ko māua ko rātou" to clarify.
- Practice: write two sentences where you clearly shift from tāua (we two) to tātou (all of us).
- Challenge: write a four-sentence story that uses four different pronouns — and make every one crystal clear.

_Discussion prompt: Pronoun clarity is an act of respect for your listener. When you are vague about who you mean, your listener has to guess — and guessing takes energy and creates confusion. How does clear pronoun use connect to the value of manaaki (caring for others)?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Pronoun precision**

Write passages with deliberate, clear pronoun use.

- Write a four-sentence passage about a group event — use three different pronouns clearly.
- Write a pair of sentences that shift from māua to tātou — make the shift clear.
- Write a sentence using Ko māua ko rātou to clarify a group.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) about your class doing an activity together. Use singular, dual, and plural pronouns — make sure every pronoun is crystal clear. Read it aloud and ask a partner if they always knew who you meant.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He hui ā-kāinga — A family gathering

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 2.3: Extended communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.1 — Family & relationships; AO 1.4 — Personal & family information

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about family**

This week's pronouns are really about relationships — who is in your group and whether you are included. Today we use them to talk about the most important group of all — whānau. Listen to how the pronouns naturally reflect the warmth and structure of a family gathering.

> **A:** He pēhea tō [whānau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wh%C4%81nau)?
> **B:** He pai rātou katoa — he nui hoki ō mātou whānau!
> **A:** Tokohia koutou i roto i tō whānau?
> **B:** Tokowhitu mātou — ko ōku [mātua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81tua), ko ōku [tuākana](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tu%C4%81kana) tokorua, ko ōku [tēina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%93ina) tokotoru, ko au.
> **A:** Ka haere koutou ki hea i ngā hararei?
> **B:** Ka haere tātou katoa ki tō mātou [marae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=marae) i ngā hararei. He tino pai — ka [hui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hui) mātou, ka kai tātou, ka [waiata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waiata) tātou!
> **A:** He ātaahua tērā! Ko wai te [kaiārahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kai%C4%81rahi) o tō koutou whānau?
> **B:** Ko tōku kuia — ko ia te [pou](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pou) o tō mātou whānau.

New family and relationship vocabulary:
**mātua** — parents (plural of mātua/matua)
**tuākana** — older sibling of the same gender
**tēina** — younger sibling of the same gender
**tokorua / tokotoru / tokowha…** — two / three / four (people) (toko- prefix for counting people)
**marae** — marae (the communal gathering place)
**hui** — gather / meeting
**waiata** — sing / song
**kaiārahi** — leader / guide (kai- + ārahi = person who leads)
**kuia** — elder woman / grandmother
**pou** — pillar / post / foundation
**Ko ia te pou o tō mātou whānau.** — She is the pillar of our family.

*Tip: Toko- is the prefix for counting PEOPLE: tokorua (2), tokotoru (3), tokowha (4), torima (5), tokoono (6), tokowhitu (7), tokowaru (8), tokoriwa (9), tekau (10). Use it whenever you count people specifically.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Describe your whānau**

Build a whānau conversation using this week's pronouns and new family vocabulary.

- I ask: **Tokohia koutou i roto i tō whānau?** You answer using tokorua/tokotoru etc. + list who is in your family.
- I ask: **Ka haere koutou ki hea i ngā hararei?** Answer using Ka haere tātou/mātou and a place.
- I ask: **Ko wai te pou o tō koutou whānau?** Answer: Ko [name/role] — ia te pou o tō mātou whānau.
- Ask ME a question about my family using Tokohia or He pēhea.

_Discussion prompt: Ko ia te pou o tō mātou whānau — she is the pillar of our family. The word pou (pillar/post) comes from the central pillar of a wharenui (meeting house). What does calling someone the pou of the family say about their role? What makes someone a pou in your whānau?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu whānau**

Describe your family using this week's pronouns and vocabulary.

- Write who is in your family using Tokohia and Ko [name] tōku [relationship].
- Write what your family does together: Ka [verb] tātou/mātou…
- Write who the pou of your family is: Ko [name] te pou o tō mātou whānau nō te mea…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a six-sentence paragraph about your whānau. Use: tokohia, tātou/mātou (and explain the choice), a Ka sequence of what you do together, and close with a Ko sentence about who your family's pou is.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka haere tāua ki te moana." Who is going to the beach?
  - The speaker and a third person (not the listener) 
  - The speaker and the listener — both of them together ✅
  - The speaker alone 
  - Three or more people 
- You went to town with your mum. You're telling your friend (who wasn't there). Which pronoun?
  - tāua 
  - tātou 
  - māua ✅
  - mātou 

**Tuesday questions**

- What does "tātou" mean?
  - we (3+, not including you) 
  - you all (3+) 
  - we (3+, including you) ✅
  - they (3+) 
- Which prefix is always associated with an inclusive "we"?
  - mā- 
  - kou- 
  - rā- 
  - tā- ✅

**Wednesday questions**

- "I kite māua i ō māua hoa. Ka tākaro rātou." Why is this unclear?
  - The tense is wrong 
  - Rātou is unclear — it is not certain whether māua is included in rātou or not ✅
  - Māua is the wrong pronoun 
  - There is no object marker 
- What does "katoa" mean?
  - some 
  - none 
  - all / everyone ✅
  - a few 

**Thursday questions**

- What is the toko- prefix used for?
  - Counting things 
  - Counting places 
  - Counting people ✅
  - Counting time 
- "Ko ia te pou o tō mātou whānau." What does pou mean here?
  - the youngest member 
  - the leader who travels most 
  - the pillar / foundation of the family ✅
  - the cook

### Week 5

#### Monday: Tekau mā tahi — Numbers 11–20

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number · Grammar Level 1–2: Numbers 11–20, tekau mā pattern*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Numbers 11–20

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Ten and one more: the tekau mā pattern**

In Term 1 you learned tahi through tekau (1–10). Today we discover how te reo Māori builds numbers above ten — and it is beautifully logical. Once you know the pattern, you never need to memorise another number combination. Watch.

> **tekau mā tahi** — 11 (ten and one)
> **tekau mā rua** — 12 (ten and two)
> **tekau mā toru** — 13
> **tekau mā whā** — 14
> **tekau mā rima** — 15
> **tekau mā ono** — 16
> **tekau mā whitu** — 17
> **tekau mā waru** — 18
> **tekau mā iwa** — 19
> **rua tekau** — 20 (two tens)

The pattern:
**tekau mā** + [ones digit] = 11 through 19

**tekau** = 10
**mā** = and (joining tens and ones)
**tahi** = 1 → tekau mā tahi = 11
**rua** = 2 → tekau mā rua = 12
…and so on up to 19.

For twenty: **rua tekau** (two tens) — the tens digit comes FIRST.

Used in real contexts:
**E tekau mā rima ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga.**
There are 15 students in this classroom.

**Tokorua tekau mā tahi ō mātou whānau.**
There are 21 people in our family.

**Kei te hoko mātou i te miraka — e tekau mā rua tāra te utu.**
We are buying milk — it costs $12.

*Tip: Tekau mā = "ten and". Just add the ones digit after it. For twenty onward: [tens digit] + tekau. Rua tekau = 20. Toru tekau = 30. It could not be more logical.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Count together**

Call and response — I say a number in English, you say it in te reo. Then we use numbers in sentences.

- Numbers 11–19: rapid fire. Teacher says "thirteen" — students say **tekau mā toru.** Keep going to 19.
- In a sentence: "There are 14 students going." → **Tokorua tekau mā whā ngā ākonga e haere ana.** Wait — how many? Count who is actually going.
- Price: "The book costs $16." → **E tekau mā ono tāra te pukapuka.**
- Age: "I am 11 years old." → **Tekau mā tahi ōku tau.** (tau = years/age)

_Discussion prompt: The te reo Māori number system is completely regular — no "eleven" or "twelve" (irregular English words from old Germanic). Every number above ten follows the same pattern. How does a regular system help language learners? Are there any disadvantages to regular systems?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Numbers in context**

Use numbers 11–20 in real sentences.

- Write your age in te reo using tekau mā [number] ōku tau.
- Write how many students are in your class: E [number] ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga.
- Write three prices using E [number] tāra te [item].
- ✦ Bonus: Write five sentences using numbers 11–20 in different contexts — age, price, quantity, distance, time. Use E hia? to ask how many, and answer each one.

#### Tuesday: Rua tekau, toru tekau — Numbers 20–100

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number · Grammar Level 1–2: Tens and compound numbers to 100*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Numbers 20–100

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — All the way to one hundred**

Yesterday you learned 11–20. The same logic takes you all the way to 100. Te reo Māori numbers are completely regular — once you know 1–10 and the pattern, you know every number.

> **rua tekau** — 20
> **rua tekau mā tahi** — 21
> **rua tekau mā rima** — 25
> **toru tekau** — 30
> **toru tekau mā whā** — 34
> **whā tekau** — 40
> **rima tekau** — 50
> **ono tekau** — 60
> **whitu tekau** — 70
> **waru tekau** — 80
> **iwa tekau** — 90
> **[kotahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kotahi) [rau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rau)** — 100 (one hundred) (one hundred)

The full pattern:
[tens digit] + tekau = the tens number
[tens digit] + tekau + mā + [ones digit] = the in-between number

Examples:
**whā tekau** = 40
**whā tekau mā rua** = 42
**iwa tekau mā iwa** = 99
**kotahi rau** = 100

Used in context:
**E iwa tekau mā rua ngā tau o tōku tīpuna.**
My ancestor is 92 years old. (tīpuna = ancestor/grandparent)

**E rima tekau tāra te utu o tērā kākahu.**
That piece of clothing costs $50. (kākahu = clothing)

**E toru tekau mā whā ngā ākonga i tō mātou kura.**
There are 34 students in our school.

*Tip: The pattern never breaks: [tens] tekau (mā [ones]). Memorise the tens (rua tekau, toru tekau… iwa tekau) and you can say any number to 99. Add kotahi rau for 100.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Numbers in real life**

Use numbers 20–100 in sentences about real things.

- How many days in a year? → **E toru rau ono tekau mā rima ngā rā i tētahi tau.** Wait — that's 365. Break it into hundreds: toru rau + ono tekau mā rima.
- Your school: how many students? → **E [number] ngā ākonga i tō mātou kura.**
- A price: something that costs between $20 and $99. → **E [number] tāra te [item].**
- An age: how old is someone in your whānau? → **E [number] ōna tau.**

_Discussion prompt: Three hundred and sixty-five in te reo: toru rau ono tekau mā rima. Every single digit stated in order — no shortcuts. Compare to English: "three hundred and sixty-five" — same structure! What does this tell you about how number systems often work across languages?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Number sentences**

Use numbers 20–100 across a range of real contexts.

- Write how old three people in your whānau are using E [number] ōna/ōna tau.
- Write three prices for things you know using E [number] tāra te [item].
- Write how many students are in your year group and school.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "number story" — seven sentences, each using a different number between 20 and 100 in a different context (age, price, distance, quantity, score, year, temperature).

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Numbers in extended sentences

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Numbers in complex sentence contexts*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Numbers in context; AO 2.3 — Extended number sentences

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Using numbers to tell stories**

Numbers become powerful when they appear inside rich sentences. Today we practise embedding numbers into the complex sentence structures you have built this term and last — past tense, comparatives, fronted phrases, and lists.

> With past tense:
> **I haere mātou tokowhitu ki te hākinakina inanahi.**
> Seven of us went to sport yesterday.
> 
> With comparison:
> **He nui ake ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga i tērā — e toru tekau mā rua ki e rua tekau mā whā.**
> There are more students in this class than that one — 32 versus 24.
> 
> With Ko ēnei:
> **Ko ēnei ngā utu: e whā tekau tāra mō te [pōtae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C5%8Dtae), e rua tekau mā rima tāra mō ngā [tū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%AB).**
> These are the prices: $40 for the hat, $25 for the shoes. (tū = shoes/footwear)
> 
> With a fronted phrase:
> **I te Hātarei o tērā wiki, e toru tekau mā rua ngā [tāngata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81ngata) i haere mai ki tō mātou hui.**
> Last Saturday, 32 people came to our meeting.

Numbers in different positions:

As SUBJECT count: **E [number] ngā [noun]** — there are [number] [things]
As PEOPLE count: **Toko[number] mātou** — there are [number] of us
In COMPARISON: **e [number] ki e [number]** — [number] versus [number]
In a LIST: **e [number] tāra mō te [item]** — [number] dollars for the [item]

New words:
**Hātarei** — Saturday
**hui** — meeting / gathering
**tū** — shoes / footwear
**haere mai** — come (toward the speaker)

*Tip: Put the number as close to the noun it counts as possible. E rua tekau ngā ākonga — 20 students. The number and the noun stay together like a pair.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Numbers in context**

Build sentences that embed numbers naturally in complex structures.

- Past + people count: "14 of us played sport yesterday." → **I tākaro mātou tokorua tekau mā whā i te hākinakina inanahi.**
- Comparison with numbers: "This class has more students than that one — 28 vs 21." → **He nui ake ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga — e rua tekau mā waru ki e rua tekau mā tahi.**
- Fronted phrase + number: "Last Friday, 45 people came to the school concert." → Build it step by step.
- Ko ēnei list with prices: List three items and their prices using Ko ēnei ngā utu…

_Discussion prompt: Numbers make claims precise. "Many people came" is vague. "E whā tekau mā rima ngā tāngata i haere mai" is exact. When does precision matter? Can being too precise ever be inappropriate in te ao Māori contexts?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Rich number sentences**

Write sentences that use numbers inside complex structures.

- Write a past tense sentence with a people count using toko[number].
- Write a comparison sentence using two numbers and "ki" (versus).
- Write a Ko ēnei list with prices for three items.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) about an event — a sports day, a school fair, or a family gathering. Include at least four numbers in different positions: as a count, as a price, in a comparison, and in a fronted phrase.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He toa hākinakina — Sports day

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number · AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.1: Relationships*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Numbers in sports context; AO 2.3 — Sports event conversation

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about sports day**

Today we use numbers, pronouns, tenses, and comparatives in a sports day conversation. Sports is one of the richest contexts for te reo — scores, people, comparisons, and feelings all come together.

> **A:** He pēhea tā koutou toa hākinakina inanahi?
> **B:** He tino pai! I toa mātou — e whā tekau mā rima ki e rua tekau mā toru ngā ira!
> **A:** Tokohia koutou i tākaro?
> **B:** Tokorua tekau mā rua mātou. I mahi kaha tātou katoa!
> **A:** Ko wai te kaitākaro pai rawa atu?
> **B:** Ko tōku hoa ko Rangi — i eke ia i ngā [ira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ira) e tekau mā whā!
> **A:** Ka rawe! He pai ake tēnei hua i tērā tau?
> **B:** Āe — he nui ake ngā ira i tēnei tau! He tino [harikoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=harikoa) mātou katoa.

New sports and achievement vocabulary:
**toa hākinakina** — sports competition / tournament (toa = victorious/champion)
**ira** — points / score
**eke** — to score / reach / mount
**kaitākaro** — player (kai- + tākaro)
**harikoa** — joyful / elated / very happy
**i [eke](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=eke) ia i ngā ira e tekau mā whā** — she scored 14 points (eke = scored/reached)
**hua** — result / outcome (also: benefit, from Term 2 Week 3)
**i tērā tau** — last year

Notice the score format:
**e [score1] ki e [score2] ngā ira** — [score1] to [score2] points

*Tip: E [number] ki e [number] ngā ira = [number] to [number] the score. This is how you report any score in te reo Māori. Learn it cold — you will use it constantly in sports contexts.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Sports day conversation**

Build a sports day conversation using numbers, pronouns, and tenses.

- I ask: **He pēhea tā koutou toa hākinakina?** Answer with a result — did you win? Use I toa or Kāore mātou i toa.
- I ask: **Tokohia koutou i tākaro?** Answer using toko[number].
- I ask: **He aha te ira mutunga?** (What was the final score?) Answer: E [number] ki e [number] ngā ira.
- I ask: **Ko wai te kaitākaro pai rawa atu?** Name someone and say what they did: i eke ia i ngā ira e [number].

_Discussion prompt: I eke ia i ngā ira e tekau mā whā — she scored 14 points. The word "eke" also means "to mount" or "to ride" — as in riding a wave or climbing a hill. Why might te reo Māori use the same word for scoring points as for riding a wave? What does that connection suggest about achievement?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your sports day report**

Write a sports day report in te reo Māori.

- Write the result: I toa/Kāore i toa + E [score] ki e [score] ngā ira.
- Write how many players: Toko[number] mātou i tākaro.
- Write who the best player was: Ko [name] te kaitākaro pai rawa atu — i eke ia i ngā ira e [number].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full sports report (7 sentences) — result, score, player count, best player, atmosphere (harikoa/pōuri), and a comparison with last year. Use at least five different numbers.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- How do you say "17" in te reo Māori?
  - tekau mā ono 
  - tekau mā whitu ✅
  - tekau whitu 
  - whitu tekau 
- "Tekau mā rima ōku tau." What does this sentence tell us?
  - There are 15 things 
  - I am 15 years old ✅
  - The price is $15 
  - There are 15 people 

**Tuesday questions**

- How do you say "43" in te reo Māori?
  - toru tekau mā whā 
  - whā tekau mā toru ✅
  - toru whā tekau 
  - whā tekau toru 
- What is "kotahi rau" in English?
  - ten 
  - fifty 
  - one hundred ✅
  - one thousand 

**Wednesday questions**

- "E rua tekau mā waru ki e rua tekau mā tahi ngā ira." What does this describe?
  - A comparison of prices 
  - A score of 28 to 21 ✅
  - The number of students in two classes 
  - Ages of two people 
- "Tokorua tekau mā rua mātou i tākaro." What does toko- before a number indicate?
  - The number refers to things 
  - The number refers to people ✅
  - The number is approximate 
  - The number is a price 

**Thursday questions**

- "I eke ia i ngā ira e tekau mā whā." What happened?
  - She missed 14 points 
  - She scored 14 points ✅
  - She played for 14 minutes 
  - She won by 14 points 
- What does "harikoa" mean?
  - tired after sport 
  - sad about losing 
  - joyful / elated / very happy ✅
  - competitive
