### Week 6

#### Monday: Wā, rā, marama, tau — Days, months, and years

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Days, months, dates · AO 2.4: Time · Grammar Level 1–2: Days of the week, months of the year, date expressions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Days, months & dates; AO 2.4 — Time expressions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — When? The days, months, and years**

AO 1.3 asks us to communicate about number, days, months, and dates. Today we complete that picture — you already have numbers to 100. Now we add the days of the week, months of the year, and how to say a date.

> **DAYS OF THE WEEK:**
> **[Rāhina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81hina)** — Monday
> **[Rātū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81t%C5%AB)** — Tuesday
> **[Rāapa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81apa)** — Wednesday
> **[Rāpare](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81pare)** — Thursday
> **[Rāmere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81mere)** — Friday
> **[Rāhoroi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81horoi)** — Saturday
> **[Rātapu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=R%C4%81tapu)** — Sunday
> 
> **MONTHS:**
> **[Kohitātea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Kohit%C4%81tea)** — January · **[Huitanguru](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Huitanguru)** — February · **[Poutūterangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Pout%C5%ABterangi)** — March
> **[Paengawhāwhā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Paengawh%C4%81wh%C4%81)** — April · **[Haratua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Haratua)** — May · **[Pipiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Pipiri)** — June
> **[Hōngongoi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=H%C5%8Dngongoi)** — July · **[Hereturikōkā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Hereturik%C5%8Dk%C4%81)** — August · **[Mahuru](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Mahuru)** — September
> **[Whiringa-ā-nuku](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Whiringa-%C4%81-nuku)** — October · **[Whiringa-ā-rangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Whiringa-%C4%81-rangi)** — November · **[Hakihea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Hakihea)** — December

How to say a date:
**I te [number] o [month], [year]**

E.g. "On the 14th of March, 2024":
**I te tekau mā whā o Poutūterangi, rua mano rua tekau mā whā.**

Simpler date questions:
**Ko tēhea rā tēnei?** — What day is today?
**Ko Rāhina tēnei.** — Today is Monday.
**Ko tēhea marama tēnei?** — What month is this?
**Ko Pipiri tēnei.** — This is June.
**Ko tēhea tau tēnei?** — What year is this?

Days of the week come from:
Rā- (sun/day) + the number of the day (Rāhina = Moon-day, Rātū = Tuesday…)

*Tip: Ko tēhea rā/marama/tau tēnei? = What day/month/year is this? Ko [answer] tēnei. = This is [answer]. These two sentences handle almost every date question you will ever need.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Days, months, dates**

Answer date questions and use them in sentences.

- I ask: **Ko tēhea rā tēnei?** Answer with today's day.
- I ask: **Ko tēhea marama tēnei?** Answer with this month.
- Use a day in a sentence: "Last Monday I went to…" → **I te Rāhina o tērā wiki, i haere au ki…**
- Say today's full date: **Ko te [number] o [month] tēnei rā.**

_Discussion prompt: The Māori month names are not translations of English months — they come from the traditional Māori lunar calendar and describe natural events: Pipiri (June) = the fetal position of animals huddling for warmth; Mahuru (September) = the earth becomes warm. What does this tell you about how Māori understood time and nature?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Dates in context**

Use days, months, and dates in real sentences.

- Write today's full date: Ko te [number] o [month], [year] tēnei rā.
- Write what day your birthday falls on this year.
- Write three sentences using different days of the week in past tense: I te Rāhina, i te Rātū…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a weekly diary — one sentence for each day of the school week (Rāhina to Rāmere) about what happened or what will happen. Use past tense for days gone, Ka for future days.

#### Tuesday: He aha te wā? — Telling the time

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Time · AO 2.4: Time expressions · Grammar Level 1–2: Clock time, He aha te wā?*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Clock time; AO 2.4 — Daily time expressions

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What time is it?**

You know how to say dates. Now let's tell the time. Te reo Māori time-telling uses a simple structure that relies on your numbers — another reason why knowing your numbers to 60 is so useful.

> **He aha te [wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=w%C4%81)?** — What time is it?
> 
> **Ko te [hour] [karaka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=karaka).** — It is [hour] o'clock.
> **Ko te toru karaka.** — It is 3 o'clock.
> 
> **Ko te whā karaka mā rua tekau.** — It is 4:20.
> (hour + karaka + mā + minutes)
> 
> **Ko te iwa karaka māwhā tekau mā rima.** — It is 9:45.
> 
> **Ā te [hour] karaka.** — At [hour] o'clock. (for scheduling)
> **Ā te rima karaka ka tīmata te hākinakina.**
> At 5 o'clock sport starts.

The structure:
**Ko te [hour] karaka** = It is [hour] o'clock
**Ko te [hour] karaka mā [minutes]** = It is [hour]:[minutes]

**karaka** = clock (from English "clock" → karaka)
**mā** = and (joining hours and minutes — same mā as in tekau mā tahi)

Time of day expressions:
**i te ata** — in the morning / AM
**i te ahiahi** — in the afternoon / evening
**i te pō** — at night

Full time with AM/PM:
**Ko te tekau karaka i te ata.** — It is 10 AM.
**Ko te rua karaka i te ahiahi.** — It is 2 PM.

*Tip: Ko te [hour] karaka mā [minutes]. Just put the hour, say karaka (clock), add mā (and), then the minutes. Exactly like tekau mā tahi — but for time.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — What time is it?**

I show you a time — you say it in te reo. Then use it in a sentence.

- 3:00 PM → **Ko te toru karaka i te ahiahi.**
- 8:30 AM → **Ko te waru karaka mā toru tekau i te ata.**
- Scheduling: "School starts at 9 AM." → **Ā te iwa karaka i te ata ka tīmata te kura.**
- Ask and answer: **He aha te wā?** → **Ko te [current time] karaka.** What time is it right now?

_Discussion prompt: Te reo Māori uses "karaka" (from English "clock") for time-telling. This is a loanword — a word borrowed from another language. Why do languages borrow words? Can you think of other loanwords in te reo Māori? In English?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your daily timetable**

Write your school day timetable in te reo Māori.

- Write what time school starts and finishes: Ā te [time] karaka ka tīmata/mutu te kura.
- Write three things you do at specific times today using Ā te [time] karaka ka…
- Ask a partner: He aha te wā i tīmata ai tō kura? (What time did your school start?) They answer.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full school day as a timetable — every lesson with its start time, using Ā te [time] karaka ka [subject/activity]. At least six time entries.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Dates, times, and events in full sentences

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Number/date/time · AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.3: Extended communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Dates & times in context; AO 2.3 — Scheduling & events

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Invitations, events, and schedules**

Dates and times become truly useful when you can use them to make plans, describe events, and write schedules. Today we combine everything from this week — days, months, times, numbers — into the kinds of sentences you would use for a real school event.

> **He hui tēnei ā te Rāpare, ko te tekau mā rima o Hōngongoi, ā te whitu karaka i te ahiahi.**
> There is a meeting this Thursday, the 15th of July, at 7 PM.
> 
> **Ka tīmata te hākinakina ā te Rāhoroi i te iwa karaka i te ata.**
> Sport starts on Saturday at 9 AM.
> 
> **I haere mātou ki te hui ā te Rāhina. Tokowhitu mātou. Ka tīmata ā te rua tekau mā rua karaka, ā ka mutu ā te toru karaka i te ahiahi.**
> We went to the meeting on Monday. There were seven of us. It started at 12:22 and finished at 3 PM.

Building event sentences:

[WHAT] + [WHEN: day] + [WHEN: time] + [WHEN: date]

Key connectors for scheduling:
**ā te [day]** — on [day] (upcoming)
**i te [day]** — on [day] (past)
**ā te [time] karaka** — at [time] o'clock
**ka tīmata** — starts / will start
**ka mutu** — finishes / will end
**mō te [duration]** — for [duration]

New words:
**hui** — meeting / gathering (you've seen this)
**duration: mō ngā haora e [number]** — for [number] hours (haora = hour, from English)

*Tip: Build event sentences in layers: what → what day → what time → how many people → what happened. Each layer adds precision. The listener always knows exactly what, when, and who.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Plan an event**

Together we plan and describe a school event in te reo Māori.

- Name the event and day: **He [event] ā te [day].** What is happening this week at school?
- Add the date and time: **Ko te [number] o [month]. Ka tīmata ā te [time] karaka.**
- Add how many people: **Toko[number] mātou/tātou.**
- Describe what will happen: Use Ka sentences to sequence the events of the evening.

_Discussion prompt: I te iwa karaka i te ata ka tīmata te kura. Does your school really start at 9? What time does it start in te reo? The act of putting your real life into te reo Māori — your actual school time, your actual class size — makes the language genuinely yours. Why does that matter?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Design your event**

Plan and write about a real or imagined school event.

- Write what the event is and what day it is: He [event] ā te [day], ko te [date] o [month].
- Write when it starts and finishes: Ka tīmata ā te [time] karaka, ka mutu ā te [time] karaka.
- Write how many people will be there and what will happen: Ka [verb] tātou…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full event invitation (7 sentences) for a real school event — include the event name, day, full date, start and end time, how many people, what will happen, and who will lead (Mā wai e ārahi).

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He pānui — Reading and giving announcements

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.3: Date/time · AO 1.7: Classroom language · AO 2.3: Extended communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.3 — Announcements with dates/times; AO 1.7 — Classroom & school language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Making and understanding announcements**

Today we use dates, times, and events in the most practical way possible — making real school announcements. This is AO 1.7 (classroom language) meeting AO 1.3 (dates and numbers) in real action.

> **Tēnā koutou katoa.**
> Greetings to you all.
> 
> **He [pānui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%81nui) āku mōu katoa.**
> I have an announcement for you all. (pānui = announcement/notice/reading)
> 
> **Ā te Rāpare, ko te rua tekau o Pipiri, ka tū he [hui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hui) mō ngā [mātua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81tua) i te kura.**
> On Thursday the 20th of June, there will be a parent meeting at school.
> 
> **Ka tīmata ā te whitu karaka i te ahiahi, ka mutu ā te iwa karaka.**
> It starts at 7 PM and finishes at 9 PM.
> 
> **E tū ana i te [whare](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whare) hui. Haere mai ngā mātua katoa!**
> It is being held in the meeting room. All parents come!
> 
> **Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.**

Announcement vocabulary:
**pānui** — announcement / notice (also: to read — same word!)
**He pānui āku mōu.** — I have an announcement for you.
**ka tū** — will be held / will stand (tū = stand)
**E tū ana** — is being held (ongoing location)
**whare hui** — meeting room / hall
**Haere mai!** — Come! (invitation toward the speaker)

The announcement structure:
1. Greet: Tēnā koutou katoa
2. Signal: He pānui āku
3. Event + day + date
4. Time (start and end)
5. Location
6. Call to action: Haere mai!
7. Close: Tēnā koutou (×3)

*Tip: He pānui āku mōu katoa — I have an announcement for you all. This is your attention-getting phrase. Say it with confidence and the class will listen.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Make an announcement**

Together we write and perform a class announcement for a real upcoming school event.

- Opening: Everyone together — **Tēnā koutou katoa.** Signal: **He pānui āku.**
- Name the event and when: **Ā te [day], ko te [date] o [month], ka tū he [event].**
- Time: **Ka tīmata ā te [time] karaka, ka mutu ā te [time] karaka.**
- Location + call: **E tū ana i te [place]. Haere mai [who]!** Then close with Tēnā koutou ×3.

_Discussion prompt: Making an announcement in te reo Māori to your class — even a simple one — is a real act of language use. How does it feel different from just writing sentences in an exercise? What does it add to do something with language that has a real purpose and audience?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your announcement**

Write and perform your own school announcement in te reo Māori.

- Choose a real upcoming school event (or invent one).
- Write the full announcement following the seven-step structure.
- Practise delivering it — aim to say it without looking at the page.
- ✦ Bonus: Deliver your announcement to the class. The class listens and, without looking at a text, writes down the key details: event, day, date, time, location. Check for accuracy.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What is Wednesday in te reo Māori?
  - Rāhina 
  - Rāapa ✅
  - Rātū 
  - Rāmere 
- Which month is "Pipiri"?
  - March 
  - April 
  - June ✅
  - July 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko te whā karaka mā rua tekau." What time is this?
  - 4:02 
  - 4:12 
  - 4:20 ✅
  - 24:00 
- "Ā te rima karaka ka tīmata te hākinakina." What does this sentence tell us?
  - Sport finished at 5 o'clock 
  - Sport starts at 5 o'clock ✅
  - There are 5 players 
  - The game lasted 5 hours 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ka tīmata ā te rua tekau mā rua karaka, ka mutu ā te toru karaka." When does the event end?
  - At 2:22 
  - At 3:00 ✅
  - At 22:03 
  - At 12:22 
- What is "haora" a loanword for?
  - minute 
  - second 
  - hour ✅
  - day 

**Thursday questions**

- "He pānui āku mōu katoa." What is the speaker doing?
  - Asking a question 
  - Saying goodbye 
  - Giving an announcement ✅
  - Introducing themselves 
- What does "Haere mai!" mean?
  - Go away! 
  - Come here! (invitation toward speaker) ✅
  - Be quiet! 
  - Sit down!

### Week 7

#### Monday: He pēhea te āhua o te rangi? — Weather and seasons

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time, weather and seasons · Grammar Level 1–2: Weather expressions, Ka for weather narrative*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Weather & seasons; AO 1.5 — Environmental description

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about the weather**

Weather is one of the most universal conversation topics. In te reo Māori, weather expressions are beautifully descriptive and often poetic — reflecting the deep connection between Māori culture and the natural environment. Today we build a full weather vocabulary.

> **WEATHER CONDITIONS:**
> **He [ua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ua).** — It is raining. (ua = rain)
> **He hau.** — It is windy. (hau = wind)
> **He rā pai.** — It is a fine day.
> **He [kapua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kapua).** — It is cloudy. (kapua = cloud)
> **He [hukarere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hukarere).** — It is snowing. (hukarere = snow)
> **He [āniwaniwa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81niwaniwa).** — There is a rainbow. (āniwaniwa = rainbow)
> **He [uira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uira).** — There is lightning.
> **He [whatitiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whatitiri).** — There is thunder.
> **He [kohu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kohu).** — There is fog/mist.
> 
> **TEMPERATURE:**
> **He makariri.** — It is cold.
> **He [mahana](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mahana).** — It is warm.
> **He [wera](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wera).** — It is hot.

Asking about weather:
**He pēhea te āhua o te rangi?** — What is the weather like?
**He pēhea te rangi?** — How is the weather? (shorter form)

Weather in the past:
**I pēhea te āhua o te rangi inanahi?**
What was the weather like yesterday?

Weather with Ka (forecasting or narrative):
**Ka ua āpōpō.** — It will rain tomorrow.
**Ka mahana te rangi ā tērā wiki.** — The weather will be warm next week.

Seasons:
**kōanga** — spring
**raumati** — summer
**hōtoke** — winter
**kārearea** — NOT a season — it is a falcon! The season is...
**kōanga, raumati, ngahuru, hōtoke** — spring, summer, autumn, winter

*Tip: He [weather word] for right now. I [weather word] te rangi for past. Ka [weather word] te rangi for future. Same pattern as any other tense — just weather vocabulary.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Weather report**

Together we create a week-long weather report for an imaginary or real location.

- Today's weather: **He pēhea te āhua o te rangi ināianei?** → **He [weather] te rangi.**
- Yesterday's weather: **I pēhea te āhua o te rangi inanahi?** → **I [weather] te rangi.**
- Tomorrow's forecast: **Ka pēhea te āhua o te rangi āpōpō?** → **Ka [weather] te rangi āpōpō.**
- Compare two days: **He [adj] ake te rangi o tēnei rā i tērā rā.**

_Discussion prompt: The word āniwaniwa (rainbow) contains the word rangi (sky). Hukarere (snow) contains huka (foam/froth) + rere (to flow/fall). Many Māori weather words describe what is happening visually and physically. How does this way of naming things differ from English weather words like "fog" or "snow"?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Weather sentences**

Use weather vocabulary across past, present, and future.

- Write today's weather report: He pēhea te āhua o te rangi ināianei? Answer with three details.
- Write yesterday's weather using past tense: I pēhea…? Use at least two weather words.
- Write a three-day forecast using Ka: Ka [weather] te rangi ā te [day].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a weather comparison paragraph — compare this week's weather to last week's using He [adj] ake and fronted time phrases. Include at least five different weather words.

#### Tuesday: Ngā āhuatanga o te taiao — Seasons and the natural world

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Seasons · AO 2.5: Natural world vocabulary · AO 1.4: Connection to land*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Seasons & nature; AO 2.5 — Natural world vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The seasons and what they bring**

In te ao Māori, the seasons are not just weather patterns — they mark the rhythms of planting, harvest, fishing, and ceremony. Today we learn how to describe each season and what happens in it.

> **Ko [kōanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Danga) te wā o te tipu.**
> Spring is the time of growth. ([tipu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tipu) = grow/growth)
> 
> **Ko [raumati](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=raumati) te wā o te wera me te tākaro.**
> Summer is the time of heat and play.
> 
> **Ko [ngahuru](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ngahuru) te wā o te hauhake.**
> Autumn is the time of harvest. ([hauhake](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hauhake) = harvest)
> 
> **Ko [hōtoke](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%8Dtoke) te wā o te makariri me te okioki.**
> Winter is the time of cold and rest.

Season vocabulary:
**kōanga** — spring
**raumati** — summer
**ngahuru** — autumn
**hōtoke** — winter

**Ko [season] te wā o te [noun/activity].**
[Season] is the time of [noun/activity].

Nature vocabulary:
**tipu** — grow / plant / growth
**hauhake** — harvest
**okioki** — rest
**hua rākau** — fruit (hua = fruit/benefit, rākau = tree)
**puāwai** — blossom / flower / bloom
**rau** — leaf / leaves
**repo** — mud / swamp
**huka** — frost / foam
**ngahere** — forest / bush
**moana** — sea / ocean / large lake

*Tip: Ko [season] te wā o te [activity]. This sentence works for ANY season and ANY activity — learn the frame, swap in the vocabulary.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Seasons and activities**

We build descriptions of each season and what people do in them.

- Describe kōanga: **Ko kōanga te wā o te tipu. Ka puāwai ngā gete, ka hāpai te ngahere i ōna rau.**
- Describe raumati: **Ko raumati te wā o te wera. Ka kaukau ngā tamariki i te moana.**
- Describe hōtoke: **Ko hōtoke te wā o te makariri. Ka huka te whenua i te ata.**
- Which season is your favourite? **Ko tōku wā pai rawa atu ko [season] nō te mea…**

_Discussion prompt: Ko hōtoke te wā o te okioki — winter is the time of rest. In te ao Māori, rest and conservation are values, not just necessities. How does framing winter as "the time of rest" change how you think about that season? What value does it give to rest?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Season descriptions**

Write about the seasons using Ko [season] te wā o te… and nature vocabulary.

- Write a Ko [season] te wā o te… sentence for all four seasons.
- Write what you do in each season: Ko raumati tōku wā pai rawa atu. Ka [activity] au.
- Write a sentence about nature in two seasons using rau (leaves), puāwai (blossom), huka (frost).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a poem or descriptive paragraph about your favourite season. Use at least six nature words, Ko [season] te wā o te… as your opening line, and at least one comparative (He [adj] ake te [season] i…).

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Weather in stories and recounts

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Weather/seasons · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Weather as narrative context-setter*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Weather as scene-setting; AO 2.3 — Weather in recounts

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Weather as the opening of a story**

Great writers use weather to set the scene — the opening line of a story often describes the weather because it tells us the mood before anything happens. Today we learn how to use weather as a powerful scene-setter in te reo Māori.

> WEATHER OPENING — sets the mood immediately:
> **He pō makariri, he pō [kōpeka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Dpeka).**
> It was a cold night, a dark night.
> 
> **I te ata o Rāhina, ka ua kaha — ka tangi hoki te hau i ngā [rākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81kau).**
> On Monday morning, it rained hard — and the wind cried through the trees.
> 
> **He rā pai, he rā ātaahua — ko te raumati tonu.**
> It was a fine day, a beautiful day — it was truly summer.
> 
> **I [ngaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ngaro) te [rā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81) i roto i ngā kapua makariri.**
> The sun disappeared into the cold clouds.

Weather as scene-setting techniques:

1. **Double description** (two weather words for emphasis):
"He pō makariri, he pō kōpeka." (cold night, dark night)

2. **Weather + effect on nature**:
"Ka ua kaha — ka tangi hoki te hau i ngā rākau."
(It rained hard — and the wind cried through the trees)

3. **Weather + contrast**:
"Ahakoa he ua, he ātaahua tonu te rangi."
(Although it rained, the sky was still beautiful)

New words:
**kōpeka** — dark / gloomy
**i ngaro** — disappeared / was lost
**ka tangi te hau** — the wind cried/wailed (personification!)
**ngahere** — forest / bush

*Tip: Open your story with weather to set the mood before a single character appears. Warm weather = possibility. Cold dark weather = difficulty ahead. Your reader will feel it before they know it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Weather openings**

I give you a story mood — you write a weather opening that creates that mood.

- Mood: excitement and adventure → **He rā pai, he rā ātaahua — ko te raumati tonu. Ka whiti te rā i ngā maunga.**
- Mood: sadness or difficulty → **He pō makariri, he pō kōpeka. I ngaro te marama i roto i ngā kapua.**
- Mood: warmth and safety → Use mahana, ātaahua, and whiti (shine) to create the feeling.
- Now write your own weather opening for a story about something that happened to you.

_Discussion prompt: Ka tangi te hau i ngā rākau — the wind cried in the trees. This is personification — giving the wind a human emotion. Te reo Māori naturally personifies nature. How does this connect to the Māori understanding of the natural world as a living, feeling entity rather than a backdrop?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Story openings**

Write three different story openings — each one starting with weather.

- Write a weather opening for a story about a storm using pakū, hau, ua, and kapua.
- Write a weather opening for a summer adventure using mahana, whiti, ātaahua, and moana.
- Write a weather opening for a sad or difficult story using makariri, kōpeka, and i ngaro.
- ✦ Bonus: Take your best weather opening and write the next two sentences — what happens after you set the scene? Include a character, a Ka action sentence, and at least one conjunction.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō te taiao — Nature conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Weather/seasons · AO 1.4: Connection to land · AO 2.5: Natural world vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Nature & seasons; AO 1.4 — Connection to land & environment

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about the natural world**

This week has been about weather, seasons, and nature. Today we bring it all together in a conversation about the natural world around us — connecting to the land in the way te ao Māori teaches us to.

> **A:** He pēhea te āhua o te rangi i tēnei wiki?
> **B:** He makariri, he ua hoki — he hōtoke tonu tēnei!
> **A:** He aha āu i pai ai mō tēnei wā o te tau?
> **B:** He pai ki a au te hōtoke nō te mea ka okioki au, ka noho au i roto me tōku whānau.
> **A:** Kei hea koe i noho ai i ngā hararei?
> **B:** I noho mātou i te taha o te [moana](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=moana) — i ātaahua te rangi, ahakoa i makariri.
> **A:** He ātaahua! He pēhea te āhua o te moana i ērā rā?
> **B:** I nui, i parauri, i [ngāoro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C4%81oro) hoki — ka pōuri au i te hoki ki te kāinga!

New conversational phrases about nature:
**He aha āu i pai ai mō tēnei wā o te tau?**
What do you like about this time of year?
**i pai ai** — that you like (pai + ai = relative clause "that is good/liked")
**wā o te tau** — time of year
**te taha o te moana** — the side of the ocean / by the sea
**i ērā rā** — in those days / during that time
**parauri** — brown (you learned this in Week 3!) — used for ocean colour
**i ngāoro** — roared (sound word from Term 1 Week 9!)
**Ka pōuri au i te hoki ki te kāinga.**
I felt sad about going home.

*Tip: He aha āu i pai ai mō tēnei wā o te tau? — What do you like about this time of year? This question is a beautiful invitation to talk about nature and seasons. Learn it and ask it often.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Nature conversation**

Build a conversation about the natural world and seasons.

- I ask: **He pēhea te āhua o te rangi i tēnei wiki?** Answer with this week's actual weather.
- I ask: **He aha āu i pai ai mō tēnei wā o te tau?** Answer with your favourite season and why.
- I ask: **He aha te wāhi āhua rawa atu i Aotearoa ki a koe?** (What is the most beautiful place in NZ for you?) Answer using Ko and description.
- Close: **He taonga te taiao o Aotearoa.** (The natural environment of NZ is a treasure.) Do you agree? Why?

_Discussion prompt: He taonga te taiao o Aotearoa — the natural environment of New Zealand is a treasure. What responsibilities come with living in a taonga? In te ao Māori, kaitiakitanga (guardianship) means caring for the natural world as a duty. What does kaitiaki mean for you personally?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu wāhi āhua**

Write about your favourite place in nature.

- Name your favourite natural place and say why: Ko tōku wāhi āhua rawa atu ko [place] nō te mea…
- Describe the weather there using at least three weather words.
- Write what you do there using Ka sentences.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full description of your favourite natural place (7 sentences) — name it, describe the weather and seasons there, what you do there, why you love it. Include a Ko ēnei list of its best features and close with He taonga tēnei nō te mea…

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "He pēhea te āhua o te rangi?" — What is this question asking?
  - What time is it? 
  - What is the weather like? ✅
  - What season is it? 
  - How cold is it? 
- What does "hukarere" mean?
  - fog / mist 
  - lightning 
  - snow ✅
  - cloud 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko ngahuru te wā o te hauhake." What does hauhake mean?
  - growth 
  - rest 
  - harvest ✅
  - rain 
- What are the four seasons in te reo Māori in order from spring?
  - hōtoke, kōanga, raumati, ngahuru 
  - kōanga, raumati, ngahuru, hōtoke ✅
  - raumati, ngahuru, hōtoke, kōanga 
  - ngahuru, hōtoke, kōanga, raumati 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ka tangi te hau i ngā rākau." What literary device is being used?
  - simile — comparing wind to something 
  - metaphor — replacing one word with another 
  - personification — giving the wind a human emotion ✅
  - alliteration — repeated sounds 
- What does "kōpeka" mean?
  - cloudy 
  - windy 
  - rainy 
  - dark / gloomy ✅

**Thursday questions**

- "He taonga te taiao o Aotearoa." What does taonga mean in this context?
  - problem 
  - weather 
  - treasure / something precious ✅
  - danger 
- "Ka pōuri au i te hoki ki te kāinga." What does pōuri mean here?
  - happy 
  - excited 
  - tired 
  - sad / heavy-hearted ✅

---

### Week 8

#### Monday: Ngā tūāhua o te hinengaro — Feelings and emotions

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical characteristics, personality and feelings · Grammar Level 1–2: Kei te/He for emotions, feeling vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Feelings & emotions; AO 2.3 — Expressing inner states

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Saying how you feel inside**

You already know a few feeling words — pai, ngenge, hari, pōuri. Today we build a full emotional vocabulary. Being able to express how you feel in te reo Māori is one of the most intimate and important things you can do in a language.

> **Kei te hari au.** — I am happy / joyful.
> **Kei te [pōuri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C5%8Duri) au.** — I am sad.
> **Kei te [mataku](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mataku) au.** — I am scared / frightened.
> **Kei te [māia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ia) au.** — I am brave / bold.
> **Kei te [whakamā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakam%C4%81) au.** — I am embarrassed / shy.
> **Kei te [hīhī](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C4%ABh%C4%AB) au.** — I am excited / eager.
> **Kei te [riri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=riri) au.** — I am angry.
> **Kei te [māharahara](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81harahara) au.** — I am worried / anxious.
> **Kei te [ohorere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ohorere) au.** — I am surprised / startled.
> **Kei te [hōhā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%8Dh%C4%81) au.** — I am bored / fed up.
> **Kei te [aroha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aroha) au.** — I am feeling love / compassion.

Emotion structure:
**Kei te [emotion] au.** — I am feeling [emotion].

With a reason:
**Kei te pōuri au nō te mea…** — I am sad because…
**Kei te hari au nā te mea…** — I am happy because…

Asking about feelings:
**Kei te pēhea tōu ngākau?** — How is your heart/feeling?
**He aha tōu rongo?** — What do you feel? (rongo = feeling/sense)

New concept: **ngākau** = heart (as the seat of emotion, not the physical heart which is manawa)

Deep emotion phrase:
**Ka aroha au ki a koe.** — I feel love/compassion toward you.
**Aroha** — love, compassion, empathy (one of the most important words in te reo Māori)

*Tip: Kei te [emotion] au = I am feeling [emotion]. Add nō te mea + reason to explain why. The emotion word goes right after Kei te — just like any other verb.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Express the feeling**

I describe a situation — you say how you would feel using Kei te [emotion] au.

- You just found out you won a prize. → **Kei te hari au! Kei te ohorere hoki au!**
- You have a big test tomorrow. → **Kei te māharahara au mō āpōpō.**
- Someone said something unkind to you. → **Kei te pōuri au. Kei te riri hoki au.**
- You are speaking te reo Māori in front of the class for the first time. → How do you feel? Be honest — use two emotion words.

_Discussion prompt: Kei te aroha au ki a koe — I feel love/compassion toward you. The word aroha appears everywhere in te ao Māori — in greetings, farewells, and daily life. How is aroha different from just "love" in English? What does it include that "love" sometimes doesn't?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Feelings**

Express real emotions in te reo Māori.

- Write how you feel right now using Kei te [emotion] au and give a reason.
- Write how you felt inanahi — use past tense: I [emotion] au nō te mea…
- Write three different emotions you have felt this week using three different emotion words.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "feelings journal entry" for today (6 sentences). Describe two emotions you have felt, why, how they changed during the day, and how you feel now. Use Kei te, I, and nō te mea.

#### Tuesday: Ahua tangata — Personality and character

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Personality · Grammar Level 1–2: He for character description, ko ia te tangata…*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Character & personality; AO 2.1 — Describing relationships

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Describing who someone is**

Last term you learned He + noun + adjective for describing things. Today we use that same structure to describe people's character — who they are as people, not just what they look like.

> **He tangata āpōpō ia.** — She is a generous person. (āpōpō here = generous? No...)
> 
> Let's build the vocabulary first:
> **He tangata [atawhai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=atawhai) ia.** — He is a kind/caring person./caring person. (atawhai = kind)
> **He tangata [māia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ia) ia.** — She is a brave person.
> **He tangata [mōhio](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C5%8Dhio) ia.** — He is a knowledgeable/clever person.
> **He tangata [hūmārie](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%ABm%C4%81rie) ia.** — She is a gentle/humble person.
> **He tangata [māngere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ngere) ia.** — He is a lazy person.
> **He tangata urupā ia.** — She is a hardworking person. (urupā? No — that's a cemetery!)
> 
> Let me give you the right words:
> **[urupā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=urup%C4%81)** = cemetery · **[ngākaunui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C4%81kaunui)** = enthusiastic/passionate · **[pukumahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pukumahi)** = hardworking

Character vocabulary:
**atawhai** — kind / caring / generous
**māia** — brave / bold
**mōhio** — knowledgeable / clever / aware
**hūmārie** — gentle / humble / peaceful
**māngere** — lazy
**pukumahi** — hardworking
**ngākaunui** — enthusiastic / passionate (ngākau = heart, nui = big)
**aroha nui** — loving / full of aroha
**māia** — brave
**tūmataiti** — shy / reserved

Used in a sentence:
**He tangata pukumahi, he tangata atawhai hoki ia.**
She is a hardworking and kind person.

**Ko ia te tangata ngākaunui rawa atu o tō mātou akomanga.**
She is the most enthusiastic person in our classroom.

*Tip: He tangata [character word] ia — He/She is a [character] person. Stack two character words with "he tangata X, he tangata Y hoki ia" — or use the superlative: Ko ia te tangata [X] rawa atu o…*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Describe someone's character**

Think of someone you admire. We will describe their character together using this week's vocabulary.

- Who is your person? State: **Ko [name] tōku [relationship].** Then: **He tangata [character] ia.**
- Add a second character trait: **He tangata [X] hoki ia.** Stack two traits.
- Give evidence using nō te mea: **He tangata atawhai ia nō te mea ka [action] ia i ngā wā katoa.**
- Use a superlative: **Ko ia te tangata [character] rawa atu o tōku ao.** (ao = world/life)

_Discussion prompt: Ko ia te tangata ngākaunui rawa atu o tōku ao — she is the most enthusiastic person in my world. What makes this sentence beautiful? What does "tōku ao" (my world) add that "tōku akomanga" (my classroom) doesn't? When would you use each?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Character descriptions**

Write character descriptions of real people in your life.

- Write a He tangata [character] ia sentence about someone you admire.
- Write two or three stacked character traits for that person.
- Write a Ko ia te tangata [X] rawa atu o… superlative about them.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "tribute paragraph" (6 sentences) about someone important to you — Ko [name] tōku [relationship]. Describe their character (3 traits), give evidence for one trait, and close with a superlative.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Feelings and character in stories

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Feelings/personality · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Emotion and character in narrative context*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Emotion in narrative; AO 2.3 — Character in stories

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Show, don't tell — emotions in stories**

There is a saying in writing: "show, don't tell." Instead of writing "she was happy", you show the happiness — what she did, how she moved, what she said. Today we practise this in te reo Māori — describing feelings and character through action rather than just naming them.

> TELLING (weak):
> **Kei te hari ia.** She is happy.
> 
> SHOWING (strong):
> **Ka omaoma ia ki te kāinga. Ka karanga ia i tōna māmā. Ka oma hoki tōna māmā ki a ia, ka [awhi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=awhi) rāua.**
> She ran home. She called out to her mum. Her mum ran to her too, and they hugged.
> 
> TELLING (weak):
> **He tangata atawhai ia.** He is a kind person.
> 
> SHOWING (strong):
> **I kite ia i te kurī i [mauiui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mauiui) ana. Ka [tango](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tango) ia i tōna [koti](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=koti) ā ka [hipoki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hipoki) ia i te kurī. Ka noho ia ki reira ā te [taenga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=taenga) mai o tōna [rangatira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rangatira).**
> He saw the sick dog. He took off his coat and covered the dog. He stayed there until its owner arrived.

Show-don't-tell techniques:

1. **Replace the emotion word with an action**:
Telling: Kei te hari au.
Showing: Ka oma au, ka waiata au, ka kata au! (run, sing, laugh)

2. **Replace the character word with evidence**:
Telling: He tangata atawhai ia.
Showing: Ka tūāhu ia ki te tangata māuiui. Ka homai ia i āna kai.

New words:
**karanga** — to call out / summon
**awhi** — to embrace / hug
**hipoki** — to cover
**taenga mai** — the arrival (tae = arrive, -nga = nominaliser)
**rangatira** — chief / leader / owner (of an animal in this context)
**kata** — to laugh
**tūāhu** — to tend to / look after (person in need)

*Tip: To show an emotion — ask yourself: what does someone DO when they feel this? Then write the action, not the feeling word. Ka [kata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kata), ka waiata, ka omaoma — these show happiness better than "Kei te hari".*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Show, don't tell**

I give you a telling sentence — you turn it into a showing sentence using actions.

- Telling: **I pōuri ia.** → Showing: What did she DO when she was sad? **Ka noho ia i tōna rūma. Kāore ia i kōrero. Ka tangi ia i te pō.**
- Telling: **He tangata māia ia.** → Showing: What did he DO to show bravery? **Ka tū ia i mua i te whakamā. Ka kōrero ia ahakoa i ohorere ia.**
- Telling: **I harikoa ngā tamariki.** → Showing: Three actions that show their joy.
- Now you write one: tell me an emotion — then show it through three actions in te reo.

_Discussion prompt: Ka kata, ka waiata, ka omaoma — a laughing, singing, running person. You have not said "hari" (happy) once. But the reader FEELS it. Why is showing more powerful than telling? When might telling be the right choice?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Show it**

Rewrite telling sentences as showing sentences in te reo Māori.

- Telling: "Kei te mataku ia." — Show the fear through three actions.
- Telling: "He tangata pukumahi ia." — Show the hard work through evidence.
- Telling: "I hari mātou katoa." — Show the group's joy through action.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a scene (8 sentences) where a character experiences a strong emotion — but NEVER NAME the emotion. Only use actions, dialogue, and description. Read it to a partner — can they identify the emotion?

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō ngā tāngata — Talking about people

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Character/feelings · AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 1.2: Introducing others*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Character & feelings; AO 1.2 — Introducing others

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Introducing and describing people**

This week you have learned feelings and character. Today we use them to introduce and describe real people — friends, whānau, teachers — in a natural, warm conversation.

> **A:** Ko wai tōu hoa pai rawa atu?
> **B:** Ko Hēni tōku hoa pai rawa atu. He tangata atawhai, he tangata ngākaunui hoki ia.
> **A:** He pēhea tōna āhua?
> **B:** He tangata [māhorahora](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81horahora) ia — ka kata ia i ngā wā katoa. Ka ora tōku ngākau i tōna āhua!
> **A:** Ka aroha! He aha āu e pai ai mō tōna āhua?
> **B:** Ka pai ki a au tōna māia — ka tū tonu ia ahakoa he uaua te āhua o ngā mea. He [tūāhuatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%AB%C4%81huatanga) tino nui tērā.
> **A:** E ai ki tōu kōrero, ko ia te hoa tino pai rawa atu o tō tātou ao!
> **B:** Āe! Ko ia te pou o tōku ao.

New conversational phrases about people:
**He pēhea tōna āhua?** — What is he/she like? (personality/appearance)
**He tangata māhorahora ia.** — He/she is a joyful/light-hearted person.
**Ka ora tōku ngākau i tōna āhua.** — My heart comes alive at her presence.
**He aha āu e pai ai mō tōna āhua?** — What do you like about their personality?
**Ka tū tonu ia ahakoa…** — He/she stands firm even though…
**tūāhuatanga** — quality / characteristic (tūāhua + tanga)
**E ai ki tōu kōrero** — According to what you say
**Ko ia te pou o tōku ao.** — She is the pillar of my world.

*Tip: He pēhea tōna āhua? = What is their personality/character like? This is different from He pēhea ia? (How is he/she?). Āhua focuses on their nature — who they are as a person.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Introduce your favourite person**

Build a conversation introducing someone you admire.

- I ask: **Ko wai tōu hoa pai rawa atu?** Introduce them: Ko [name] tōku [relationship].
- I ask: **He pēhea tōna āhua?** Give two character traits using He tangata [X] ia.
- I ask: **He aha āu e pai ai mō tōna āhua?** Show one quality through action (show don't tell).
- Close: **Ko ia te pou o tōku ao.** Why is she/he your pou? Add nō te mea.

_Discussion prompt: Ko ia te pou o tōku ao — she is the pillar of my world. Last week you heard "Ko ia te pou o tō mātou whānau." Now it's "tōku ao" (my world) rather than "tō mātou whānau" (our family). What is the difference in feeling between these two? When would you use each?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He mihi ki tōu hoa**

Write a tribute to someone important in your life.

- Introduce them: Ko [name] tōku [relationship]. He tangata [character] ia.
- Show their best quality through an action (show don't tell — no emotion words).
- Write what they mean to you: Ka ora tōku ngākau i tōna āhua nō te mea…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full tribute (8 sentences) about your most important person. Introduce them, describe two character traits, show one quality through evidence, say what they mean to you, and close with Ko ia te pou o tōku ao.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Kei te māharahara au." How is this person feeling?
  - excited and eager 
  - angry 
  - worried / anxious ✅
  - surprised 
- "Kei te pēhea tōu ngākau?" What does ngākau mean in this context?
  - your body 
  - your stomach 
  - your heart (as the seat of emotions) ✅
  - your head 

**Tuesday questions**

- What does "pukumahi" mean?
  - lazy 
  - shy 
  - passionate 
  - hardworking ✅
- "Ko ia te tangata ngākaunui rawa atu o tō mātou akomanga." What does ngākaunui mean?
  - the most intelligent 
  - the most hard-working 
  - the most enthusiastic / passionate (big-hearted) ✅
  - the most kind 

**Wednesday questions**

- Which of these is an example of "showing" rather than "telling" an emotion?
  - Kei te hari ia. 
  - I pōuri ia nō te mea he uaua tāna mahi. 
  - Ka omaoma ia, ka kata ia, ka waiata ia! ✅
  - He tangata māia ia. 
- What does "awhi" mean?
  - to call out 
  - to cover 
  - to embrace / hug ✅
  - to laugh 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ka ora tōku ngākau i tōna āhua." What does this sentence express?
  - Her face is beautiful 
  - My heart comes alive at her presence / personality ✅
  - She is the most important person 
  - I feel sad when I see her 
- "He pēhea tōna āhua?" — What is this question asking about?
  - Their physical height 
  - Their age 
  - Their health today 
  - Their character / personality / nature ✅

### Week 9

#### Monday: He kōrero mō te aho — Linking ideas: cohesion review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Connective vocabulary, paragraph cohesion across all text types*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Cohesion & linking; AO 2.5 — Connective vocabulary review

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — All the linking tools, together**

This term you have learned many ways to link ideas — conjunctions, pronouns, connective words. Today we gather all the linking tools in one place and practise choosing between them deliberately. A well-connected passage is what separates good te reo from great te reo.

> **COORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS (equal ideas):**
> ā · engari · nō reira · me
> 
> **SUBORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS (one idea depends on the other):**
> nō te mea · nā te mea · ahakoa · mehemea · i te wā
> 
> **CONNECTIVE ADVERBS (linking sentences):**
> [heoi anō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=heoi+an%C5%8D) — however / but anyway
> [ā muri ake nei](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81+muri+ake+nei) — furthermore / going forward
> hei tā tēnei — according to this / as a result
> nō reira — therefore (can work as both conjunction and connective)
> [waihoki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waihoki) — moreover / and also
> engari rā — however (more formal than engari alone)
> 
> **TIME CONNECTIVES (for sequence):**
> [i mua ake](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=i+mua+ake) — beforehand / previously
> [ā muri iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81+muri+iho) — afterwards
> i te wā hōhonu — in the long run

Using connective adverbs (sentence starters):

**I mahi kaha ngā ākonga. Heoi anō, i ngenge rātou ā te mutunga o te rā.**
The students worked hard. However, they were tired by the end of the day.

**Ko te reo Māori he reo rangatira. Waihoki, he reo o te ngahere, o te moana, o ngā maunga.**
Te reo Māori is a chiefly language. Moreover, it is a language of the forest, the sea, and the mountains.

**I mahi kaha mātou. Ā muri iho, ka ora ai tā mātou mahi.**
We worked hard. Afterwards, our work flourished.

New words:
**rangatira** — chiefly / of high status
**ora** — alive / well / flourishing
**heoi anō** — however / but anyway
**waihoki** — moreover / and also

*Tip: Connective adverbs (heoi anō, waihoki, ā muri iho) go at the START of a sentence and signal the relationship to what came before. They are stronger than conjunctions because they create a full pause — a new sentence — before the connection.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Connect the ideas**

I give you two sentences — you connect them using the most appropriate linking word from today's list.

- "The team won." "They were still humble." → The contrast needs: **heoi anō** → **I toa rātou. Heoi anō, i hūmārie tonu rātou.**
- "She studied hard." "She passed the test." → Result: **nō reira / ā muri iho** → **I ako kaha ia. Nō reira, i paahi ia i te whakamātautau.**
- "Te reo Māori is beautiful." "It connects us to our ancestors." → Adding more: **waihoki**.
- Write your own: two sentences about your school, connected with heoi anō OR waihoki.

_Discussion prompt: Heoi anō and engari both mean "however/but". Heoi anō starts a NEW sentence. Engari stays INSIDE a sentence. When would you choose heoi anō over engari? What does starting a new sentence add to the contrast?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Connected writing**

Write a passage where every sentence connects to the next.

- Write four sentences — connect each to the next with a different linking word.
- Use heoi anō at the start of one sentence to create a contrast.
- Use waihoki to add more information to a point.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a persuasive paragraph (7 sentences) about something you care about — your favourite sport, your school, te reo Māori. Use at least four different linking words from today's list. Underline each one.

#### Tuesday: Arotake — Term 2 vocabulary and structures

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · Full Term 2 review · Grammar Levels 1–2 all structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Term 2 review; AO 2.5 — Vocabulary consolidation

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Everything Term 2 has added**

Term 1 gave you the foundations. Term 2 has been building on them. Today we celebrate everything new that you have added — and see how much bigger your te reo Māori world has become.

> **TENSES you now control:**
> Kei te (now) · I (past event) · Ka (story/sequence) · Kāore e…ana (not now) · Kāore i (didn't happen)
> 
> **PRONOUNS you now have:**
> All 16: au, koe, ia / tāua, māua, kōrua, rāua / tātou, mātou, koutou, rātou
> 
> **NUMBERS you now know:**
> 1–100, dates, times, people counts (toko-)
> 
> **NEW VOCABULARY DOMAINS:**
> Colours (12+) · Feelings (12+) · Character (8+) · Weather (10+) · Seasons (4) · Days/months (19) · Family vocabulary · Sports · Announcements

The growth from Term 1 to Term 2:

Term 1: **Kei te haere au ki te kura.**
Term 2: **Ā te Rāhina, i te iwa karaka i te ata, ka haere mātou tokowhitu ki te kura — ahakoa he ua tino nui, i tino pai tā mātou rā.**

Same basic idea — going to school. But now:
→ day of week (Rāhina)
→ time (iwa karaka i te ata)
→ Ka narrative
→ people count (tokowhitu)
→ ahakoa (despite)
→ past tense (i tino pai)
→ possessive (tā mātou rā)

That is what a term of daily practice gives you.

*Tip: Read that Term 2 sentence aloud. You could not have written it in Week 1 of this term. That is your progress. Say it again and own it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Term 2 blitz**

Speed review — category by category. Teacher calls a category, students generate examples.

- FEELINGS: How many emotion words can we list? Go — hari, pōuri, mataku… aim for 10+.
- PRONOUNS: Say all 16 — starting with singular, then dual, then plural. Together.
- NUMBERS: Count from 11 to 20 together. Then say these: 34, 57, 82, 99.
- WEATHER: Give a full three-day forecast in te reo — today, tomorrow, next week.

_Discussion prompt: Look back at Week 1 of this term — the holiday conversation. Could you have had that conversation at the start of Term 1? No. Could you have it now? Easily. What does it feel like to look back and see how far you have come?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Term 2 self-audit**

Test your own Term 2 knowledge.

- Write all 16 pronouns from memory — organised by singular, dual, plural.
- Write one sentence using Ka, one using I, one using Kāore i, one using Kāore e…ana.
- Write today's date and time fully in te reo Māori.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "Term 2 showcase sentence" — one sentence that uses as many Term 2 structures as possible: past tense, a time/date, a number, a colour, a feeling, and a conjunction. Make it a real sentence, not just a list.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Term 2 sentence type showcase

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · All sentence types from Terms 1 and 2*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Sentence type review; AO 2.5 — Rich vocabulary in sentences

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The full sentence toolkit, Terms 1 and 2**

Across two terms you have built an enormous range of sentence types. Today we display them all — see the full range you now control, and choose which ones to use deliberately.

> **IDENTITY:** Ko Tāmara tōku ingoa. Nō Tāmaki Makaurau ahau.
> **DESCRIPTION:** He tangata pukumahi, he tangata atawhai hoki ia.
> **PRESENT:** Kei te ako kaha rātou i te reo Māori i tēnei wā.
> **PAST:** I te Rāhina o tērā wiki, i haere mātou tokowhitu ki te hākinakina.
> **NARRATIVE:** Ka ara ia i te ata, ka kai ia i tōna parakuihi, ka haere ia ki te kura.
> **NEGATIVE:** Kāore ia i tae i tēnei rā nō te mea i māuiui ia.
> **COMPLEX:** Ahakoa i ngenge mātou, i mahi tonu mātou nō te mea he mahi nui tō mātou.
> **COMPARATIVE:** He nui ake ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga i tērā — e rua tekau mā waru ki e rua tekau mā tahi.
> **FRONTED:** I te ahiahi o Rāpare, i roto i te whare hui, ka kōrero tō mātou kaiako mō ngā whainga o tērā tau.
> **LISTED:** Ko ēnei ngā mea hou i ako ai mātou i tēnei wāhanga: ngā tae, ngā tau, ngā tūāhua, me ngā kupu hono.

Ten sentence types. Two terms. You can write all of them.

The key to fluency is not knowing all the types — it is choosing the RIGHT type for what you want to say:

→ Want to identify? Ko
→ Want to describe? He
→ Want to say now? Kei te
→ Want to tell a story? Ka
→ Want to report a fact? I
→ Want to deny? Kāore
→ Want to connect? Conjunction
→ Want to compare? He [adj] ake
→ Want to set the scene? Fronted phrase
→ Want to list? Ko ēnei

You now have all ten. Use them.

*Tip: The best writers don't use all ten types in every paragraph — they choose deliberately. Read your writing aloud and ask: does every sentence sound different from the last? If not, vary the type.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — One prompt, ten types**

I give you one topic — together we write one sentence of EACH type about it.

- Topic: your school. Write a Ko identity sentence and a He description sentence.
- Write a Kei te sentence (now), an I sentence (past), and a Ka sentence (narrative).
- Write a Kāore sentence and a complex ahakoa sentence.
- Write a comparative, a fronted phrase, and a Ko ēnei list. Ten sentence types, one topic.

_Discussion prompt: Look at the ten sentences about your school. Pick the three that feel most alive — most like real te reo, not just an exercise. Why do those three feel stronger? What do they have that the others don't?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Sentence type showcase**

Choose a topic you care about and write all ten sentence types.

- Choose your topic: sport, whānau, nature, music, food, or another.
- Write sentences in types 1–5: Ko, He, Kei te, I, Ka.
- Write sentences in types 6–10: Kāore, ahakoa/complex, comparative, fronted, Ko ēnei.
- ✦ Bonus: Take your ten sentences and weave them into a genuine paragraph — reorder them, add connectives, cut anything that doesn't flow. Aim for a paragraph that reads like real te reo, not a list of exercise sentences.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero hou — A spiralled conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All Term 1 and 2 AOs · Full conversation practice*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 2.3 — Full conversation; AO 2.5 — Expressive vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — A full Term 2 conversation**

The best test of language learning is conversation. Today we have the most complex conversation of the year so far — drawing on everything from both terms. Listen to how naturally the structures flow when you stop thinking about them individually.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! He pēhea ōu hararei?
> **B:** He tino pai! I haere mātou ki te [ngahere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ngahere) ko tōku whānau. Ko ēnei ngā mea tino pai: he ātaahua ngā rākau, i ngāoro te awa, ā i kaukau mātou tokowhitu i te roto māhana!
> **A:** Ka rawe! He pēhea te āhua o te rangi i ērā rā?
> **B:** I makariri i te ata engari ka mahana i te ahiahi. Ahakoa he ua i tētahi rā, i haere tonu mātou ki waho.
> **A:** He aha tō āhua i ērā rā?
> **B:** I hari tino nui au! Ka ora tōku ngākau i te āhua o te taiao. Ko tōku māmā te tangata i ārahi i a mātou — he tangata mōhio, he tangata atawhai hoki ia.
> **A:** He ātaahua tērā. He aha tāu whainga mō tēnei wāhanga?
> **B:** Ko tāku whainga: ka ako māmā ake au i te reo Māori — ka kōrero au me tōku whānau i te kāinga!

This conversation uses:
✓ Past tense (I haere, i ngāoro, i makariri, i hari)
✓ Ka narrative (ka mahana, ka kaukau, ka ora)
✓ Plural pronouns (mātou, tokowhitu)
✓ Weather (makariri, mahana, ua)
✓ Feelings (i hari, Ka ora tōku ngākau)
✓ Character description (He tangata mōhio, he tangata atawhai)
✓ Ko ēnei list (Ko ēnei ngā mea tino pai…)
✓ Comparative (ako māmā ake)
✓ Complex (ahakoa he ua, i haere tonu mātou)
✓ Goal statement (Ko tāku whainga…)

That is the whole of Terms 1 and 2 in one natural conversation.

*Tip: Ko tāku whainga: ka kōrero au me tōku whānau i te kāinga. This is the goal — not just to speak te reo in class, but to take it home. What would you need to practise to have this conversation with your own whānau?*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Full conversation practice**

Pairs. Use the model conversation as a guide — but make it your own. Your holidays, your family, your feelings.

- Open with greetings and ask about holidays: Tēnā koe! He pēhea ōu hararei?
- Report what happened: past tense + people count + Ko ēnei list.
- Describe the weather: past + weather + ahakoa + kept going anyway.
- Share a feeling + describe a person + state your goal for this term.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tāku whainga: ka kōrero au me tōku whānau i te kāinga. Is this a realistic goal for you? What would your first te reo conversation with your whānau look like? What three sentences would you teach them to make it possible?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu kōrero hou**

Have your own full Term 2 conversation and write it up.

- Have a full conversation with a partner — at least 10 lines each — about your holidays.
- Use: past tense, Ka narrative, a plural pronoun, a weather sentence, a feeling, and a goal.
- Write up the conversation as a dialogue.
- ✦ Bonus: After writing your dialogue, annotate it — label every structure you used. How many different structures can you find? Aim for at least eight.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "I mahi kaha ngā ākonga. Heoi anō, i ngenge rātou." What does heoi anō signal?
  - Therefore — they worked hard as a result of being tired 
  - Moreover — they were also hard workers 
  - However — despite working hard, they were tired ✅
  - Because — tiredness caused them to work hard 
- What does "waihoki" mean?
  - however / but 
  - therefore 
  - moreover / and also ✅
  - because 

**Tuesday questions**

- Which of these correctly uses Ka for a narrative sequence?
  - Ka ara ia. Ka kai ia. Ka haere ia ki te kura. ✅
  - I ara ia. Ka kai ia. Kei te haere ia ki te kura. 
  - Ka ara ia. I kai ia inanahi. Ka haere ia. 
  - Ka ara, kai, haere ia ki te kura. 
- "I toa rātou. Heoi anō, i hūmārie tonu rātou." What does hūmārie mean?
  - exhausted 
  - angry 
  - humble / gentle / peaceful ✅
  - excited 

**Wednesday questions**

- Which sentence type uses "Ko ēnei" to introduce a list?
  - Identity sentence 
  - Narrative sentence 
  - Listed sentence ✅
  - Comparative sentence 
- "He nui ake ngā ākonga i tēnei akomanga i tērā." What sentence type is this?
  - Ko identity 
  - Kāore negative 
  - He comparative ✅
  - Ka narrative 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ko tāku whainga: ka kōrero au me tōku whānau i te kāinga." What does whainga mean?
  - problem 
  - memory 
  - goal / aim ✅
  - holiday 
- "Ka ora tōku ngākau i te āhua o te taiao." What does this express?
  - The weather made me tired 
  - My heart comes alive in the presence of nature ✅
  - The natural world is dangerous 
  - I am going outside to rest

### Week 10

#### Monday: Arotake — Term 2 full vocabulary and structure review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 consolidated · All grammar structures Terms 1 and 2*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings review; AO 2.3 — All structures; AO 2.5 — Vocabulary mastery

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The full two-term vocabulary celebration**

This is the last week of Term 2. Today we count everything you now know and celebrate it. By the end of today's lesson you will have a complete picture of your two-term te reo Māori journey.

> Two terms of learning. Here is what you can now do:
> 
> **COMMUNICATE ABOUT:**
> Yourself, your family, your school, your home, your feelings, your character, the weather, the seasons, numbers 1–100, dates, times, sporting events, holiday recounts, announcements
> 
> **STRUCTURES YOU CONTROL:**
> Ko · He · Kei te · I · Ka · Kāore e…ana · Kāore i · E hia / Tokohia · tōku/tāku/tōu/tāu · All 16 pronouns · Ahakoa · Mehemea · I te wā · Comparatives · Fronted phrases · Ko ēnei lists · Conjunctions (7+) · Connective adverbs (heoi anō, waihoki)

The Term 2 additions to your Term 1 base:

**TERM 1 base sentence:**
"Kei te haere au ki te kura."

**TERM 2 full sentence:**
"I te Rāpare, ko te tekau mā ono o Pipiri, i haere mātou tokotoru ki te kura i te iwa karaka i te ata — ahakoa i ua tino nui, i tino hari mātou nō te mea he kaupeka hākinakina ā te ahiahi!"

Translation: On Thursday the 16th of June, three of us went to school at 9 in the morning — although it rained heavily, we were very happy because there was a sports competition in the afternoon!

Count the structures: date, day, people count, time, ahakoa, feeling, nō te mea, Ka… You can do all of this now.

*Tip: Say that last sentence aloud. Slowly. "I te Rāpare, ko te tekau mā ono o Pipiri, i haere mātou tokotoru ki te kura…" You are speaking te reo Māori. Real, complex, beautiful te reo Māori.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Two-term vocabulary blitz**

Category blitz — fastest hands/voices win.

- ALL EMOTIONS: How many can we list in 60 seconds? hari, pōuri, mataku, māia… aim for 12.
- ALL WEATHER: Every weather word. ua, hau, hukarere, āniwaniwa… aim for 10.
- ALL 16 PRONOUNS: Together, in order — singular, dual, plural.
- WILD CARD: Make one sentence that includes a colour, a feeling, a number, and a conjunction. Go.

_Discussion prompt: You have now been learning te reo Māori for two terms — roughly 20 weeks, 400 minutes of class time. That is less than seven hours of instruction. In seven hours, you have built a communication toolkit of over 200 words and 15+ grammar structures. What does this tell you about what is possible with daily practice?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Two-term audit**

A personal vocabulary and structure audit.

- Write down every te reo Māori word you can remember in 5 minutes. Count them.
- Write the six tense/negative structures: Kei te, I, Ka, Kāore e…ana, Kāore i, E…ana.
- Write your favourite sentence from all of Term 2 — and say why it is your favourite.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your "two-term milestone" — a paragraph describing what you can now do in te reo that you could not do 20 weeks ago. Use as many structures as possible. This is your record of growth.

#### Tuesday: Arotake — Grammar and structure deep review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All grammar structures Terms 1–2 consolidated and tested*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Grammar consolidation; AO 2.4 — Tense mastery

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Grammar in the wild**

Today we review grammar not through rules but through real sentences — seeing structures at work in natural te reo. You will identify the structure in each sentence, and then build your own.

> Identify the structure in each sentence:
> **1. Ko Mere tōku tuakana.** → ___
> **2. Kei te mahi kaha rātou i waho i tēnei wā.** → ___
> **3. I haere māua ki te tāone i te Hātarei.** → ___
> **4. Ka ara ia, ka horoi ia i tōna kanohi, ka haere ia ki te kura.** → ___
> **5. Kāore mātou i toa i tērā kēmu — he kino tō mātou raru.** → ___
> **6. Ahakoa i ua kaha, i tākaro tonu mātou i te hākinakina.** → ___
> **7. He nui ake ngā ira o tō mātou kapa i ā rātou ira — e rua tekau mā whā ki e tekau mā toru.** → ___
> **8. I te ahiahi o Rāpare, i roto i te whare hui, ka kōrero tō mātou kaiako.** → ___

Answers:
1. Ko → Identity
2. Kei te + plural pronoun + adverb → Present
3. I + dual pronoun + past location → Past with people
4. Ka… Ka… Ka → Narrative sequence
5. Kāore i → Past negative + He description
6. Ahakoa → Complex sentence (concession)
7. He [adj] ake + numbers → Comparative with score
8. Fronted time + fronted place + Ka narrative

Every one of these you can write. Every one.

*Tip: When you see an unfamiliar te reo sentence, break it into parts: What is the tense marker? Who is the person? What is the action? What else is there? This analysis skill is what makes you independent — you can work out new sentences for yourself.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Grammar challenge**

Build sentences matching the structure I specify.

- Build a Ka narrative — five steps. Something that happened yesterday.
- Build a Kāore i sentence AND a Kāore e…ana sentence — both about the same topic.
- Build a He [adj] ake sentence with two numbers (a score or comparison).
- Build a fronted time + fronted place + I sentence — three layers, one sentence.

_Discussion prompt: Look at sentence 8 from the I Do: "I te ahiahi o Rāpare, i roto i te whare hui, ka kōrero tō mātou kaiako." Three fronted phrases before the main action. In English this would be unusual. In te reo Māori it is natural and elegant. Why does front-loading information feel different in te reo than in English?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Grammar self-test**

Test yourself on every structure.

- Write one sentence using each of the eight structures from I Do.
- Write a passage (5 sentences) that uses all of: Ko, I, Ka, Kāore i, ahakoa.
- Write the most complex sentence you can — use at least five different structures in one sentence.
- ✦ Bonus: Take a sentence you wrote in Week 1 of this term. Rewrite it now with everything you have learned. How different is it? What does the difference tell you?

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Showcase sentences: Terms 1 and 2

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · All sentence types Terms 1 and 2*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Showcase & celebration; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Your best te reo sentences of the year**

This is a celebration. Today you write the best te reo sentences of your life so far — drawing on everything from two terms. There is no new content today. Just you, your full toolkit, and the task of producing something beautiful.

> Here are examples of student showcase sentences:
> 
> **"I te ahiahi o Rāhoroi, ko tōku whānau me ōku hoa pukumahi i haere mātou tokotoru tekau ki te tākaro hākinakina — i toa mātou e rua tekau mā ono ki e tekau mā whā!"**
> (On Saturday afternoon, my family and my hardworking friends, thirty of us went to play sport — we won 26 to 14!)
> 
> **"Ko te taiao o Aotearoa tōku wāhi okioki — ā, ko ia hoki tōku ara ora."**
> (The natural environment of New Zealand is my place of rest — and it is also my pathway to life.)
> 
> **"Ka ora tōku ngākau i ngā kupu o tēnei reo, nō te mea ko te reo Māori te reo o ōku tīpuna — he taonga tuku iho tēnei."**
> (My heart comes alive at the words of this language, because te reo Māori is the language of my ancestors — it is a treasure passed down.)

What makes these sentences great:

→ They use REAL information (not just exercises)
→ They COMBINE multiple structures naturally
→ They say something the writer MEANS
→ They have a voice — you can hear a person behind the words

Notice:
"ko ia hoki tōku ara ora" — and it is also my pathway to life (ara ora — a beautiful new compound: ara + ora = path + life)
"he taonga tuku iho" — a treasure passed down (tuku iho = handed down through generations)

These are not just grammar exercises. They are statements about identity.

*Tip: The best sentence you can write today is not the longest or the most complex — it is the most true. Say something you mean. In te reo Māori.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Write together**

Class writes one showcase sentence together on the board. Everyone contributes one element.

- Start: who and when? Ko + person + time/date.
- What happened? I or Ka + action + place.
- Add detail: number + weather or feeling.
- Add meaning: ahakoa or nō te mea + result or reflection.

_Discussion prompt: He taonga tuku iho — a treasure passed down through generations. The phrase tuku iho means "handed down" — from ancestors to descendants. Te reo Māori is a taonga tuku iho. What does it mean to receive something that was passed down across generations? What responsibility does that bring?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your showcase sentences**

Write the best te reo Māori sentences you have ever written.

- Write your best SIMPLE sentence — punchy and true.
- Write your best COMPLEX sentence — with a subordinate clause and real meaning.
- Write your best DESCRIPTIVE sentence — rich with vocabulary.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your SHOWCASE SENTENCE — the single best te reo Māori sentence you can produce. Make it say something true about you, your world, or te reo Māori itself. Read it to the class.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He whakamātautau — End of Term 2 assessment

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · All six language modes: Whakarongo, Kōrero, Pānui, Tuhituhi, Mātakitaki, Whakaatu*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 2.3 — Full conversation; AO 2.5 — All vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The Term 2 standard**

Today is your end-of-term assessment. Before we begin, let's see what a full Term 2 level conversation looks like. This is the standard — it uses structures from both terms in a natural, warm exchange.

> **A:** Tēnā koe! Ko wai tōu ingoa? Nō hea koe?
> **B:** Ko Rangi tōku ingoa. Nō Ōtautahi ahau — he tāone ātaahua, he tāone māhana hoki i te raumati.
> **A:** He pēhea ōu hararei?
> **B:** He tino pai! I haere mātou tokowhā ki te ngahere. I ātaahua te rangi — ahakoa i [hau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hau) kaha i tētahi rā.
> **A:** He pēhea tōu āhua ināianei?
> **B:** Kei te hari au, kei te hīhī hoki — e pai ana ki a au te kura hou!
> **A:** He aha āu whainga mō tēnei wāhanga?
> **B:** Ko ōku whainga: ka ako ake au i ngā tae, ka mōhio ake au ki ngā tau, ā ka kōrero māmā ake au i te reo Māori.
> **A:** Ka rawe! Tēnā koe, tēnā koe, tēnā koe.
> **B:** Tēnā koe, tēnā koe, tēnā koe.

Assessment criteria — what this conversation shows:
✓ AO 1.1: Tēnā koe · correct greeting and closing
✓ AO 1.2: Ko [name] tōku ingoa · Nō [place] ahau
✓ AO 1.4: Origin and personal information
✓ AO 2.3: Extended kōrero with feelings, reasons, goals
✓ AO 2.4: Past events · Weather · Seasonal reference
✓ AO 2.5: Adjectives · Emotions · Character · Numbers
✓ Grammar: I, Ka, Kei te, ahakoa, Ko ēnei (implied), comparative (ake)

*Tip: Kei te hari au, kei te hīhī hoki — I am happy, I am also excited. Two feelings in one sentence. That is confident te reo. When you feel something strongly — name it twice.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final practice round**

One full practice conversation with a partner before the assessment.

- Greet, introduce, say where you're from with one description of your town.
- Describe your holidays: past tense + people count + weather + ahakoa.
- Say how you feel now: two emotions + a reason.
- State three goals for Term 3 using Ko ōku whainga: ka… ka… ā ka…

_Discussion prompt: Ko ōku whainga — my goals. What are your real goals for Term 3? Not just te reo goals — learning goals, relationship goals, personal goals. Can you say any of them in te reo Māori right now? What one goal would you most like to be able to say in te reo by the end of Term 3?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whakamātautau — End of Term 2**

Your end-of-term spoken assessment.

- **Task 1:** Full conversation — at least 10 lines each. Cover greeting, introduction, holidays, feelings, goals.
- **Task 2:** Self-assess honestly against the checklist.
- **Task 3:** Write your Term 3 goal in te reo: Ko tāku whainga mō Wāhanga Toru:
- ✦ Checklist: ☐ Greeted correctly (Tēnā koe) · ☐ Introduced self (Ko/Nō) · ☐ Used past tense (I) · ☐ Used Ka narrative · ☐ Expressed a feeling (Kei te [emotion]) · ☐ Used a number or date · ☐ Used a conjunction · ☐ Closed with Tēnā koe ×3

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Which sentence correctly uses Ka for a narrative sequence?
  - Ka ara ia, ka kai, ka haere ia ki te kura. ✅
  - I ara ia, ka kai ia, kei te haere ia. 
  - Ka ara ia inanahi, ka kai ia āpōpō. 
  - Ka ara. Kai. Haere ki te kura. 
- "Ko te reo Māori he taonga tuku iho." What does tuku iho mean?
  - passed down through generations ✅
  - difficult to learn 
  - belonging to one person only 
  - recently created 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ahakoa i ua kaha, i tākaro tonu mātou." What structure is this?
  - Past negative 
  - Ka narrative 
  - Complex concession with ahakoa ✅
  - Ko identity sentence 
- "He nui ake ngā ira o tō mātou kapa i ā rātou ira." What does this compare?
  - The size of two teams 
  - The scores of two teams ✅
  - The number of players 
  - The colours of two teams 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ko ia hoki tōku ara ora." What does ara ora mean?
  - learning journey 
  - favourite song 
  - pathway to life ✅
  - daily routine 
- What makes a "showcase sentence" different from an exercise sentence?
  - It is longer 
  - It uses more vocabulary 
  - It says something the writer truly means and has a real voice ✅
  - It always uses Ka 

**Thursday questions**

- "Kei te hari au, kei te hīhī hoki." What does hīhī mean here?
  - tired 
  - sad 
  - excited / eager ✅
  - surprised 
- "Ko ōku whainga: ka ako ake au…" — What does ako ake mean?
  - to stop learning 
  - to learn less 
  - to learn more / further ✅
  - to learn backwards
