## Term 3

### Week 1

#### Monday: Ngā wāhanga o te tinana — Parts of the body

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical characteristics · Grammar Level 1–2: Body vocabulary, E…ana progressive aspect*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Body parts & physical description; AO 1.5 — Location on body

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The body in te reo Māori**

This term we go deeper — more complex sentence structures, richer vocabulary, and longer conversations. We begin with the body, because body vocabulary connects everything: feelings (your heart, your stomach), action (your hands, your feet), and description (your face, your eyes). Listen and connect each word to what you feel.

> **[māhunga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81hunga)** — head
> **[rae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rae)** — forehead
> **kanohi** — face
> **[whatu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whatu)** / **[karu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=karu)** — eye(s)
> **[ihu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ihu)** — nose
> **[waha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waha)** — mouth
> **[taringa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=taringa)** — ear
> **niho** — teeth
> **[kaki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaki)** — neck / throat
> **[pokohiwi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pokohiwi)** — shoulder
> **[ringa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ringa)** / **[ringaringa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ringaringa)** — hand / arm
> **[ngūkū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C5%ABk%C5%AB)** — elbow
> **[puku](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=puku)** — stomach / belly
> **[tuarā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuar%C4%81)** — back
> **[waewae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waewae)** — leg / foot
> **[turi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=turi)** — knee
> **[waewae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waewae) matua** — right foot
> **waewae [tōngā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%8Dng%C4%81)** — left foot

Body parts in a sentence:

**Kei te [mamae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mamae) tōku māhunga.**
My head is sore. ([mamae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mamae) = sore/pain)

**He whero ōna karu.**
His/her eyes are red.

**I horoi ia i ōna niho.**
She brushed her teeth. (horoi = clean/wash)

**Kei te ngongo tōku kaki.**
My throat is sore/tight. (ngongo = tight/constricted)

Possessives with body parts:
→ Body parts are ō-category (they are part of you, senior to your control)
→ Use tōku, tōu, tōna, ō mātou, ō rātou

**tōku māhunga** — my head
**tōna kanohi** — his/her face
**ō mātou waewae** — our legs

*Tip: Body parts are ō-category — they are part of you, not things you control. So always tōku (not tāku) for body parts. Tōku māhunga, tōku ringa, tōku waewae.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Name and describe the body**

Point and name — I point to a body part, you name it in te reo. Then use it in a sentence.

- I point to your ear: **taringa**. Use it: **Kei te aha tōu taringa?** — What is wrong with your ear?
- Describe using He: **He nui ōna karu.** She has big eyes. **He iti tōna ihu.** His nose is small.
- Use past tense: **I mamae tōku puku inanahi.** My stomach was sore yesterday.
- Connect body + feeling: **Ka hōhipū tōku ngākau.** My heart leaps/jumps. (hōhipū = leap/jump with excitement)

_Discussion prompt: In English we say "I have a sore head" — but in te reo: "Kei te mamae tōku māhunga" — literally "right now sore my head." The pain is the subject, not you. What does this tell us about how te reo Māori thinks about physical experience?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Body vocabulary**

Use body vocabulary in real sentences.

- Write six body part sentences — three with Kei te and three with He.
- Write a past tense sentence about a time something hurt: I mamae tōku… nō te mea…
- Write a body sentence that connects to an emotion: Ka [emotion] tōku ngākau / puku / kanohi.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a seven-sentence "body check" — from head to toe, describe the state of each body part right now. Are you tired? Sore? Alert? Express it through the body.

#### Tuesday: E haere ana, e kai ana — The progressive aspect E…ana

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: E…ana (present progressive — ongoing action)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Progressive aspect; AO 2.4 — Ongoing action in time

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The action is still happening**

You know Kei te for what is happening right now. Today we learn a related but different structure — E…ana. While Kei te states a present action, E…ana emphasises that the action is ONGOING — still in progress, continuing, not yet finished. It is a subtle but powerful distinction.

> **Kei te kai ia.** — She is eating. (present state)
> **E kai ana ia.** — She is eating (and still going — the eating is ongoing)
> 
> **E mahi ana rātou i tēnei wā.**
> They are working right now (the work is ongoing).
> 
> **E ako ana tātou i te reo Māori.**
> We are learning te reo Māori (an ongoing process — it continues).
> 
> **I te wā e mahi ana ia, ka tae mai tōna hoa.**
> While she was working (ongoing), her friend arrived.
> 
> **Kāore ia e haere ana ki te kura āpōpō.**
> She is not going to school tomorrow (ongoing plan that is not happening).

When to use **E…ana** instead of **Kei te**:

→ When the action is clearly ONGOING and UNFINISHED:
"E hanga ana rātou i te whare." — They are building the house. (still under construction)

→ In relative clauses — describing something while it is happening:
"I te wā e ako ana mātou" — While we were learning

→ In negative future:
"Kāore ia e haere ana" — She is not going (as an ongoing fact)

→ For slower, ongoing processes:
"E tipu ana ngā rākau." — The trees are growing. (gradual ongoing process)

Kei te tends to be more immediate: Kei te kai au RIGHT NOW.
E…ana tends to be more continuous: E tipu ana te whenua — the land is ever growing.

*Tip: E…ana = still happening, ongoing. Think of it as the -ing that is still in progress. Kei te = happening now. E…ana = happening now AND continuing. For processes, use E…ana.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Still going**

I give you a Kei te sentence — you decide if E…ana would work better and why.

- "Kei te horoi ia i tōna kanohi." → Could this be E horoi ana ia? Yes — if the face-washing is ongoing, not just a moment.
- "E tipu ana te ngahere o Aotearoa." — The forests of NZ are growing. Why E…ana not Kei te? Because this is a long-term ongoing process.
- "I te wā e mahi ana ia, ka tangi tōna pēpi." — While she was working, her baby cried. Build a similar sentence with i te wā e…ana.
- "Kāore mātou e haere ana ki te marae āpōpō." — We are not going to the marae tomorrow. Why Kāore e…ana for a future negative plan?

_Discussion prompt: E ako ana tātou i te reo Māori — we are learning te reo Māori (and the learning is ongoing). This sentence describes what you are doing this whole year. How does adding the ongoing aspect change the feeling of the sentence compared to "Kei te ako tātou"?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The ongoing action**

Write sentences using E…ana for ongoing processes.

- Write three E…ana sentences about ongoing things happening in your world right now.
- Write an i te wā e…ana clause: "While [something was ongoing], [something happened]."
- Write a Kāore e…ana negative about something you are not going to do.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) using E…ana for ongoing background actions, Kei te for immediate actions, and Ka for events that punctuate the ongoing scene. E.g. describe a busy classroom.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Body and progressive in stories

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical description · AO 2.3: Extended narrative · Grammar Level 1–2: Body + E…ana in descriptive narrative*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Physical narrative; AO 2.3 — Descriptive story writing

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Describing action through the body**

Great storytelling uses the body — characters feel things in their stomach, see with wide eyes, and run with aching legs. Today we combine body vocabulary and E…ana to write vivid physical descriptions in te reo Māori.

> **E omaoma ana ia, e [mamae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mamae) ana ōna waewae, engari kāore ia e tū ana.**
> She is running, her legs aching, but she is not stopping.
> 
> **Ka tū ia. E hūhū ana tōna [ngākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ng%C4%81kau). Ka titiro ia ki runga ki ngā maunga.**
> She stood. Her heart was pounding. She looked up at the mountains.
> 
> **I te wā e haere ana mātou ki te ngahere, ka kite mātou i tētahi manu nui — e [rere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rere) ana ia i runga i ō mātou māhunga!**
> While we were walking to the forest, we saw a huge bird — it was flying above our heads!

Techniques for physical storytelling:

1. **Body + E…ana for ongoing physical state**:
"E mamae ana ōna waewae" — her legs aching (still ongoing)
"E hūhū ana tōna ngākau" — her heart pounding

2. **Short Ka sentence to break the ongoing action**:
"Ka tū ia." — She stopped. (Ka = sudden completed action breaking ongoing scene)

3. **i te wā e…ana + Ka for simultaneous action**:
"I te wā e haere ana mātou… ka kite mātou…" — While we were walking… we saw…

New words:
**hūhū** — to buzz / pound / vibrate (sound word from Term 1!)
**rere** — to fly / flow / move swiftly
**i runga i** — above / over

*Tip: E…ana sets the ongoing background. Ka punches through it with a sudden event. "E omaoma ana ia… Ka tū ia." She was running… She stopped. The contrast creates drama.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Physical storytelling**

Build a scene using body vocabulary and E…ana together.

- Set the ongoing scene: **E omaoma ana ngā tamariki i waho.** Now add a body detail: E mamae ana ō rātou…
- Break the scene with Ka: **Ka tū rātou katoa.** Why did they stop? Add a reason.
- Use i te wā e…ana: "While they were running, they saw…" → **I te wā e omaoma ana rātou, ka kite rātou i…**
- Close with a body reaction: heart, eyes, or legs responding to what happened.

_Discussion prompt: E hūhū ana tōna ngākau — her heart was pounding. The word hūhū is a sound word — it describes a buzzing, pulsing sound. Why might a language use a sound word to describe an emotional physical sensation like a pounding heart? What does it make you feel when you hear it?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Physical scene**

Write a short physical scene using body vocabulary and E…ana.

- Write a three-sentence ongoing scene using E…ana for two different body states.
- Add a Ka sentence that breaks the scene suddenly.
- Add an i te wā e…ana sentence — something seen or heard while the scene was ongoing.
- ✦ Bonus: Write an eight-sentence physical scene — a sports moment, a scary experience, or an exciting discovery. Use at least four body part words, E…ana for ongoing states, Ka for breaking events, and at least one sound word.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō te hauora — Health conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Physical feelings · AO 1.6: Politeness · AO 2.3: Extended communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Health & wellbeing; AO 1.6 — Politeness in health contexts

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — At the sick bay**

Today we use body vocabulary and feelings in a real scenario — visiting the school sick bay. This is practical, important language that combines body parts, E…ana, feelings, and politeness.

> **[Kaimahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Kaimahi) [hauora](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hauora):** Tēnā koe. He aha tāu raruraru?
> **Ākonga:** Kei te mamae tōku māhunga, kei te ngongo hoki tōku kaki.
> **Kaimahi:** Ā, he tino kino tērā. E [mamae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mamae) ana tōu māhunga i ēhea wā?
> **Ākonga:** I tīmata i tēnei ata. Ka [huri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=huri) noa tōku ao i ētahi wā.
> **Kaimahi:** Ka [mārama](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81rama). Kei te māuiui koe — me [okioki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=okioki) koe. He aha āu i kai ai mō tō parakuihi?
> **Ākonga:** Kāore au i kai i tēnei ata — i ngenge noa au.
> **Kaimahi:** Āe, ko tērā pea te [take](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=take). Me kai koe ināianei. Ka taea koe te hoki ki tōu [akomanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=akomanga) ā te rua karaka.

Health vocabulary:
**kaimahi hauora** — health worker / school nurse (kai- + hauora)
**raruraru** — problem / trouble
**ngongo** — tight / sore (throat)
**Ka huri noa tōku ao.** — My world just spins. (dizzy)
**huri** — to turn / spin
**ao** — world / cloud (here: the world around me)
**i ēhea wā** — at what times
**Ka mārama.** — I understand / I see. (literally: it is clear)
**me okioki koe** — you should rest (me = should/must — a gentle instruction)
**ko tērā pea te take** — that is perhaps the reason
**Ka taea koe te hoki** — You are able to go back

*Tip: Me + verb = should / must do something. Me okioki koe = you should rest. Me kai koe = you should eat. Me is a gentle way to give advice or instruction — softer than a command.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Sick bay roleplay**

Roleplay the sick bay scene. One person is the kaimahi hauora, one is the ākonga.

- Kaimahi asks: **He aha tāu raruraru?** Ākonga answers with two body complaints using Kei te mamae…
- Kaimahi asks: **E mamae ana tōu [body part] i ēhea wā?** Ākonga answers with a time: I tīmata i…
- Kaimahi uses me: **Me okioki koe / me kai koe / me inu wai koe.** (you should drink water)
- Kaimahi closes: **Ka taea koe te hoki ki tōu akomanga ā te [time] karaka.**

_Discussion prompt: Me okioki koe — you should rest. The word me here is much gentler than a command like "Okioki!" (Rest!). How does the choice of me versus a direct command change the relationship between the speaker and listener? When would you use me versus a direct instruction?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Sick bay dialogue**

Write a full sick bay dialogue with a partner.

- Write the ākonga's part: three complaint sentences using Kei te mamae, E…ana, and a time.
- Write the kaimahi's part: two me sentences giving advice.
- Include Ka huri noa tōku ao or another vivid body expression.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the full dialogue (10 lines). Include: He aha tāu raruraru, body vocabulary, E…ana, a time expression, me sentences, Ka mārama, and Ka taea koe te hoki ā te [time].

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What possessive category are body parts in te reo Māori?
  - ā-category — you control them 
  - ō-category — they are part of you and senior to your control ✅
  - Neither — they use a special category 
  - It depends on the body part 
- "Kei te mamae tōku māhunga." What does mamae mean?
  - tired 
  - sore / in pain ✅
  - spinning 
  - swollen 

**Tuesday questions**

- What is the main difference between "Kei te kai ia" and "E kai ana ia"?
  - They mean exactly the same thing 
  - E kai ana emphasises the eating is ongoing and continuing; Kei te is more immediate ✅
  - Kei te is past tense; E…ana is present 
  - E…ana is only used for future plans 
- "I te wā e mahi ana ia, ka tae mai tōna hoa." What happened while she was working?
  - She finished the work 
  - Her friend arrived ✅
  - She fell asleep 
  - She started eating 

**Wednesday questions**

- "E omaoma ana ia… Ka tū ia." What effect does the Ka sentence create?
  - It shows the running is continuing 
  - It creates drama by breaking the ongoing action with a sudden stop ✅
  - It shows the running happened in the past 
  - It describes where she ran 
- What does "rere" mean?
  - to fall 
  - to fly / flow / move swiftly ✅
  - to cry 
  - to laugh 

**Thursday questions**

- "Me okioki koe." What does me indicate here?
  - A question 
  - A past event 
  - A gentle instruction / you should do this ✅
  - A command as strong as E tū! 
- What does "Ka huri noa tōku ao" describe?
  - The world is changing 
  - A spinning / dizzy feeling ✅
  - The clouds are moving 
  - Time passing

### Week 2

#### Monday: Kua mutu — The perfect aspect: Kua

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Kua (perfect aspect — action completed with present relevance)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Perfect aspect — Kua; AO 2.3 — Completed action with relevance

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — It is done: Kua**

You have I for past events, Ka for narrative, and E…ana for ongoing actions. Today we meet the final tense marker — Kua. Kua describes an action that has been completed and whose result is still relevant right now. It is the difference between "I went" and "I have gone (and I am still gone)."

> **Kua [mutu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mutu) te [akoranga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=akoranga).**
> The lesson has finished. (and we are now in the after-lesson state)
> 
> **Kua [tae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tae) mai ia.**
> She has arrived. (she is now here as a result)
> 
> **Kua [huri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=huri) te ao.**
> The world has changed. (and remains changed)
> 
> **Kua [mārama](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81rama) au.**
> I have understood / I understand now. (the understanding has arrived)
> 
> **Kua [oti](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=oti) i a au.**
> I have finished it. ([oti](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=oti) = be completed, i a au = by me)
> 
> **[Kāore anō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=K%C4%81ore+an%C5%8D) kia mutu.**
> It has not finished yet. (kāore anō = not yet, kia = subjunctive with anō)

When to use **Kua**:

→ Something has just happened and the result matters NOW:
"Kua tae mai ia." — She has arrived. (so she is here now — that is why it matters)

→ A state that has come about:
"Kua mārama au." — I now understand. (the understanding has arrived)

→ Official announcements of completion:
"Kua mutu te hui." — The meeting has ended.

Compare:
**I mutu te akoranga.** — The lesson ended. (past fact, no connection to now)
**Kua mutu te akoranga.** — The lesson has ended. (so what comes next? — the result matters)

Negative: **Kāore anō kia mutu** — not yet finished
Question: **Kua mutu?** — Is it done yet?

*Tip: Kua = "has [done] and the result is relevant now." Think of it as an announcement — something has changed state, and that new state is what matters. Kua tae mai = has arrived (and is now present).*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Completed with relevance**

I describe a situation — you use Kua to express the completed state.

- The class has just ended. → **Kua mutu te akoranga.** What happens next as a result?
- You have just understood something difficult. → **Kua mārama au!** Use it with an exclamation.
- The food has all been eaten. → **Kua kainga te kai katoa.** (kainga = been eaten — passive)
- Ask if someone has finished: **Kua oti i a koe?** Answer yes: **Āe, kua oti i a au.**

_Discussion prompt: "Kua huri te ao" — the world has changed. This phrase appears often in te reo Māori writing and speech, especially when reflecting on change over time. Why is Kua the right marker here rather than I or Ka? What does the choice of Kua add to the meaning of "the world has changed"?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Kua sentences**

Use Kua for completed actions whose results matter now.

- Write three Kua sentences about things that have just happened in your classroom or school.
- Write a Kāore anō kia mutu sentence about something not finished yet.
- Write a Kua oti i a au sentence about something you have completed.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) using Kua, I, Ka, and Kei te all in the same passage — a moment-by-moment account of arriving at school today. Use each marker at least once and make sure each one is used correctly.

#### Tuesday: He aha ōu hiahia? — Wants, needs, and future intention

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: hiahia ki te, māku e, ka taea e au te*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Expressing wants & intentions; AO 2.4 — Future intention

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What do you want? What will you do?**

You can describe what is happening, what happened, and what has happened. Today we look forward — expressing what you want, what you intend to do, and what you are able to do. These are the structures of plans, goals, and dreams.

> **WANT TO:**
> **Kei te hiahia au ki te ako i te reo Māori.**
> I want to learn te reo Māori.
> 
> **WILL DO (first person):**
> **Māku e [horoi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=horoi) i ngā [rihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rihi).**
> I will wash the dishes. (māku = for me to do, e + verb = the action)
> 
> **WILL DO (third person):**
> **Māna e hoko i te [miraka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=miraka).**
> She will buy the milk. (māna = for her to do)
> 
> **ABLE TO:**
> **Ka taea e au te [kōrero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Drero) i te reo Māori.**
> I am able to speak te reo Māori.
> 
> **CANNOT:**
> **E kore e taea e au te haere āpōpō.**
> I will not be able to go tomorrow.

Three structures for future intention:

1. **Kei te hiahia au ki te [verb]** = I want to [verb]

2. **Māku / Māna / Mā rātou e [verb]** = I/she/they will [verb]
(mā + person + e + verb = that person is the one who will do it)

Mā- takes the person:
**māku** = for me to do
**māu** = for you to do
**māna** = for him/her to do
**mā mātou** = for us (excl.) to do

3. **Ka taea e au te [verb]** = I am able to [verb]
**E kore e taea e au te [verb]** = I am not able to [verb]

New word: **rihi** — dishes / plates

*Tip: Māku e [verb] = I will [verb] (I am the one who will do it). Māna e [verb] = she will [verb]. The mā- word tells you WHO is responsible for the action.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Plans and intentions**

Build sentences about what you want, will do, and can do.

- What do you want to do after school? **Kei te hiahia au ki te [verb] i muri i te kura.**
- Assign a task: **Māu e horoi i ngā rihi.** You will wash the dishes. **Māku e tahu i te ahi.** I will light the fire.
- What can you do in te reo? **Ka taea e au te kōrero / pānui / tuhituhi i te reo Māori.**
- What can you NOT do yet? **E kore e taea e au te [verb] ināianei — engari āpōpō…**

_Discussion prompt: Māku e [verb] — I am the one who will do this. This structure makes the person responsible very clear. In te ao Māori, naming who will do something is an act of commitment. How does saying "māku e" feel different from just saying "I will"? What does making yourself the named agent add?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Wants, plans, abilities**

Write sentences expressing what you want, plan, and can do.

- Write three hiahia sentences about things you want to do or learn this term.
- Write two māku e sentences — commit to two things you will do.
- Write two Ka taea e au te sentences about things you can now do in te reo.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "Term 3 goals" paragraph (7 sentences) — what you want to learn, what you commit to doing, what you can already do, and what you could not do yet but will be able to by end of term. Use hiahia, māku e, Ka taea, and E kore e taea.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — All tense markers together

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time mastery · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Kei te, I, Ka, Kua, E…ana in one passage*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Full tense mastery; AO 2.3 — Extended tense-controlled writing

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — All six tense markers in one passage**

You now have the full tense toolkit: Kei te, I, Ka, Kua, E…ana, and the negatives Kāore e…ana and Kāore i. Today we use all of them in a single passage — showing how a skilled te reo speaker moves through time fluidly.

> **Kua tae mai au ki te kura i tēnei ata.** (Kua — arrival complete, I am now here)
> **I haere au i te Rāhina** — he roa tōku huarahi. (I — specific past fact)
> **E haere ana tēnā kura ki te hākinakina ā tērā wiki** — ka tū tō mātou ope hei whakamau. (E…ana — ongoing plan; Ka — narrative result)
> **Kei te whakarite mātou mō tērā kaupeka ināianei.** (Kei te — what is happening right now)
> **Kāore anō kua oti ā mātou whakarite** — engari ka oti āpōpō. (Kua negative — not yet; Ka — future narrative)

Each marker's job in this passage:

**Kua** — arrival complete (I am here NOW as a result)
**I** — specific past fact with detail
**E…ana** — ongoing future plan (still being planned)
**Ka** — narrative move (what will happen as a result)
**Kei te** — what is happening right now
**Kāore anō kua** — not yet completed

New words:
**huarahi** — journey / path / road
**ope** — team / group / squad
**hei whakamau** — to compete against / to challenge (hei = for the purpose of, whakamau = focus on/oppose)
**whakarite** — to prepare / arrange

*Tip: Read any te reo passage and identify the tense marker of each sentence. Once you can identify them easily, you can use them accurately. The markers are the skeleton — everything else hangs on them.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — All markers, one story**

Together we write a story that uses all six markers deliberately. I specify which marker each sentence needs.

- Sentence 1 — Kua: Something has just been completed with present relevance.
- Sentence 2 — I: A specific past event with a time word.
- Sentence 3 — E…ana + Ka: An ongoing background action interrupted by something.
- Sentence 4 — Kei te: What is happening right now as a result.

_Discussion prompt: You have now learned six tense/aspect markers: Kei te, I, Ka, Kua, E…ana, and Kāore. English has twelve tenses. Te reo Māori has six markers that between them cover the same ground — but with a different underlying logic. What is the logic? What question does each marker answer?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — All six markers**

Write a passage using all six markers at least once each.

- Write a Kua sentence and a Kei te sentence — make sure the difference is clear.
- Write an E…ana sentence interrupted by a Ka sentence.
- Write an I sentence (specific past) and a Kāore anō kua sentence (not yet complete).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a complete eight-sentence passage about a project or event at school — use every marker at least once. Label each sentence with the marker you used and why.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero mō ngā whāinga — Goals and plans conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Future intention · AO 1.7: School language*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Goals & planning conversation; AO 2.4 — Future intention

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Planning and goal-setting**

Today we use Kua, hiahia, māku e, and Ka taea in a conversation about goals and plans — the kind of conversation you might have with a teacher, a coach, or a friend about where you are headed.

> **[Kaiwhakaako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Kaiwhakaako):** Tēnā koe. Kua mutu tō akoranga mō tēnei wāhanga — he pēhea tōu whakaaro?
> **Ākonga:** Kua mārama ake au ki ngā [hanganga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hanganga) katoa. Ka taea e au te tuhituhi i ngā [rerenga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rerenga) [pakiaka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pakiaka) ināianei.
> **Kaiwhakaako:** Ka pai! He aha āu whainga mō tērā wāhanga?
> **Ākonga:** Kei te hiahia au ki te ako i ngā kupu hou o te taiao. Māku hoki e tuhituhi i ōku whakaaro i ngā rā katoa.
> **Kaiwhakaako:** He whainga pai ērā. Ka taea e koe te kōrero māmā ake āpōpō?
> **Ākonga:** Āe — e kore e taea e au te kōrero māmā ake ināianei, engari ā tērā wāhanga, ka taea!
> **Kaiwhakaako:** Ko tāu [whakapono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakapono) tōu ara. Haere tonu!

New phrases for goals and planning:
**Kua mārama ake au ki…** — I now understand more about…
**rerenga pakiaka** — simple sentences (rerenga = sentence, pakiaka = root/basic)
**He whainga pai ērā.** — Those are good goals.
**Ka taea e koe te…** — Are you able to… / You can…
**Ko tāu whakapono tōu ara.** — Your belief is your path. (whakapono = belief/faith)
**Haere tonu!** — Keep going! / Press on!
**māmā ake** — more easily / more fluently (māmā = light/easy, ake = more)

*Tip: Haere tonu! — Keep going! Tonu means "still" or "continuously" — haere tonu = keep travelling. It is one of the most encouraging things you can say in te reo. Use it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Goal-setting conversation**

Build a goals conversation with a partner — one plays kaiwhakaako, one plays ākonga.

- Kaiwhakaako: **He pēhea tōu whakaaro mō tōu ako i tēnei wāhanga?** Ākonga answers with Kua mārama ake au ki…
- Kaiwhakaako: **He aha āu whainga mō tērā wāhanga?** Ākonga answers with hiahia and māku e.
- Kaiwhakaako: **Ka taea e koe te [skill] ināianei?** Ākonga answers honestly — Ka taea OR E kore e taea engari…
- Close: Kaiwhakaako says Ko tāu whakapono tōu ara. Haere tonu! Ākonga responds.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tāu whakapono tōu ara — your belief is your path. This connects to the Māori concept of whakapono (faith/belief) as something that shapes your direction. How does believing you can do something create the path toward doing it? Can you think of a time your belief opened a path for you?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whāinga — Your goals**

Write and share your Term 3 goals.

- Write three hiahia sentences about things you want to be able to do by end of Term 3.
- Write two māku e sentences — things you commit to doing regularly.
- Write what you can already do: Ka taea e au te… (three abilities).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full goals paragraph (8 sentences) — what you have achieved (Kua), what you can do (Ka taea), what you want (hiahia), what you commit to (māku e), what you cannot yet do (E kore e taea), and your path forward (Ko tāu whakapono tōu ara).

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Kua tae mai ia." What does this sentence tell us about her arrival?
  - She arrived a long time ago 
  - She arrived and as a result she is now here ✅
  - She is going to arrive soon 
  - She was arriving when something interrupted her 
- "Kāore anō kia mutu." What does this mean?
  - It has just finished 
  - It will never finish 
  - It has not finished yet ✅
  - It finished a long time ago 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Māna e hoko i te miraka." Who will buy the milk?
  - I will 
  - You will 
  - He/she will ✅
  - They will 
- "E kore e taea e au te haere āpōpō." What does this mean?
  - I do not want to go tomorrow 
  - I went yesterday instead 
  - I will not be able to go tomorrow ✅
  - I am going tomorrow reluctantly 

**Wednesday questions**

- Which marker signals a completed action whose result is relevant right now?
  - Kei te 
  - Ka 
  - I 
  - Kua ✅
- What does "whakarite" mean?
  - to compete 
  - to arrive 
  - to prepare / arrange ✅
  - to finish 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ko tāu whakapono tōu ara." What does whakapono mean?
  - hard work 
  - belief / faith ✅
  - goal 
  - journey 
- "Haere tonu!" — What does this expression encourage?
  - Go home! 
  - Stop what you are doing! 
  - Keep going! / Press on! ✅
  - Go slowly!

### Week 3

#### Monday: Nō hea koe? — Origin, iwi, hapū, and identity

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Personal information — iwi, hapū, maunga, awa · AO 2.1: Relationships*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Identity — iwi & hapū; AO 2.1 — Community relationships

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Deeper identity: where you truly come from**

In Week 4 of Term 1 you learned the basic pepeha — Ko [mountain] tōku maunga, Ko [river] tōku awa, Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Today we go deeper. We explore what iwi and hapū really mean, why they matter, and how to talk about community identity in te reo Māori.

> **Ko Tūhoe tōku [iwi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=iwi).**
> Tūhoe is my tribe/people.
> 
> **Ko Ngāti Awa tōku [hapū](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hap%C5%AB).**
> Ngāti Awa is my sub-tribe.
> 
> **Ko te Kāhui Ariki tōku whānau pani.**
> The royal family is my extended family of guardians.
> 
> **Nō reira ko ahau ko [name], nō [place], nō [iwi].**
> Therefore I am [name], from [place], of [iwi].
> 
> **He [uri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uri) ahau nō [ancestor].**
> I am a descendant of [ancestor].

Identity vocabulary:
**iwi** — tribe / people (literally: bones — the framework that holds a people together)
**hapū** — sub-tribe / pregnant (the sub-group that gives birth to new community)
**uri** — descendant / offspring
**tīpuna / tupuna** — ancestor / grandparent
**whakapapa** — genealogy / to layer (whaka = to make, papa = layer/foundation)
**tangata whenua** — the people of the land (the indigenous people)
**manuhiri** — visitor / guest
**Nō reira** — therefore / and so (as a conclusion)

The full pepeha structure:
1. Ko [maunga] tōku maunga.
2. Ko [awa] tōku awa.
3. Ko [waka] tōku waka.
4. Ko [iwi] tōku iwi.
5. Ko [hapū] tōku hapū.
6. Ko [marae] tōku marae.
7. Ko [school] tōku kura.
8. Ko [name] tōku ingoa.
9. Nō [place] ahau.

*Tip: Whakapapa literally means "to layer things" — it is the layering of generations, one on top of another, connecting you to your beginnings. When you say your whakapapa, you are saying where you come from — not just geographically, but in every sense.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — The full pepeha**

Build your full pepeha together. Each student adds one line.

- Start: Ko [your nearest mountain or landmark] tōku maunga. Ko [your nearest river] tōku awa.
- Add iwi/hapū if known: Ko [iwi] tōku iwi. Ko [hapū] tōku hapū. (If not known: Ko [school region] tōku whenua.)
- Add marae and school: Ko [marae] tōku marae. Ko [school name] tōku kura.
- Close: Ko [your name] tōku ingoa. Nō [your town] ahau. Nō reira ko ahau.

_Discussion prompt: Iwi means "bones" — the framework of a people. Hapū means "pregnant" — the sub-group that creates new life. Why do you think Māori used these body words for social structures? What does it tell us about how Māori thought about community?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu pepeha**

Write and practise your full pepeha.

- Write all nine lines of your pepeha. Research any lines you do not know.
- Practise saying it aloud until it flows without looking at the page.
- Say your pepeha to a partner. They listen and then say theirs.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (6 sentences) explaining what each line of your pepeha means to you personally. Why does it matter that your maunga is named? What does connecting to land in an introduction say about Māori values?

#### Tuesday: Te marae — The marae and its protocol

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Community · AO 1.6: Politeness and protocol · AO 2.1: Relationships*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Community & marae; AO 1.6 — Protocol & tikanga

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The marae: home of the people**

The marae is the heart of Māori community life. It is where people gather for pōhiri (formal welcomes), tangi (funerals), hui (meetings), and celebration. Today we learn the language of the marae — what the parts are called, what happens there, and how to talk about it respectfully.

> **Parts of the marae:**
> **[marae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=marae) [ātea](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81tea)** — the open space in front of the wharenui (sacred space for formal encounter)
> **[wharenui](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wharenui)** — the meeting house (the large house where people sleep and gather)
> **[wharekai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wharekai)** — dining room / food house
> **[wharepaku](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wharepaku)** — toilet facilities (paku = small, whare paku = small house)
> **[waharoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waharoa)** — gateway / entrance
> 
> **What happens at the marae:**
> **Ka haere mātou ki te marae.** We are going to the marae.
> **Ka [pōhiritia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C5%8Dhiritia) mātou e ngā [tangata whenua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tangata+whenua).** We were welcomed by the hosts.
> **Ka [whaikōrero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whaikōrero) ngā [rangatira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rangatira).** The leaders gave speeches.
> **Ka waiata tātou.** We all sang.

Marae protocol vocabulary:
**pōhiri** — formal welcome ceremony
**karanga** — the calling (women's ceremonial call of welcome)
**whaikōrero** — formal speech (whai = to pursue, kōrero = speech)
**haka** — dance / challenge
**hongi** — pressing of noses (sharing the breath of life)
**tangata whenua** — hosts (the people of the land)
**manuhiri** — guests / visitors
**Ka pōhiritia mātou.** — We were welcomed. (passive)
**Ka whaikōrero ngā rangatira.** — The leaders gave speeches.
**me noho** — we should sit / must sit (protocol instruction)

*Tip: On the marae, women lead through karanga (calling) and men lead through whaikōrero (speech). Both are essential. Neither is less important. The marae is a place of equal and complementary roles.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Marae language**

Practise marae vocabulary in sentences.

- Parts of marae: Point and name — wharenui, marae ātea, wharekai, waharoa.
- Describe an action: **Ka karanga ngā wāhine.** The women called. **Ka whaikōrero ngā tāne.** The men spoke.
- Passive: **Ka pōhiritia mātou e ngā tangata whenua.** We were welcomed by the hosts.
- Protocol instruction: **Me noho tātou ināianei.** We should all sit now.

_Discussion prompt: Ka pōhiritia mātou — we were welcomed. The passive voice here makes the welcome something that HAPPENED TO US — we received it. In te ao Māori, welcoming is a gift given by hosts to guests. How does the passive voice capture that relationship better than "the hosts welcomed us"?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Marae recount**

Write about a marae visit — real or imagined.

- Write what happened using Ka sentences: Ka haere mātou… Ka pōhiritia mātou… Ka waiata tātou…
- Name three parts of the marae using Ko ēnei: Ko ēnei ngā wāhanga o te marae: he wharenui, he wharekai, he marae ātea.
- Write a me sentence about marae protocol: Me [action] tātou i te marae.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full marae visit recount (8 sentences) — where you went, how you were welcomed (passive), what happened (Ka sequence), what you saw (Ko ēnei list), and how you felt (emotion vocabulary).

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Community and identity in extended writing

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 2.1: Community · AO 2.3: Extended writing*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Identity writing; AO 2.1 — Community in writing

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Writing about who you are and where you belong**

Today we combine the pepeha, the marae, and community vocabulary into extended writing. This is the kind of te reo writing you might produce for a school presentation, a wall display, or an end-of-year portfolio.

> **Ko Aoraki tōku maunga, ko Waitaki tōku awa.**
> **Ko Ngāi Tahu tōku iwi, ko Ngāti Māmoe tōku hapū.**
> **Nō Ōtautahi ahau.**
> **Ko au ko [name].**
> 
> I tōku ao, he nui ngā āhuatanga pai — ko tōku whānau, ko ōku hoa, ko tōku [kura](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kura).
> Ko ēnei ngā mea e mau ana ki a au: he aroha o tōku whānau, he kaha o ōku hoa, he [māramatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ramatanga) o tōku kura.
> He uri ahau nō ngā tīpuna nō reira he [kawenga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kawenga) tōku — me tiaki au i tēnei reo, i tēnei [tikanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tikanga).

New vocabulary for identity writing:
**e mau ana ki a au** — that hold fast to me / that I hold dear
**māramatanga** — understanding / enlightenment / clarity
**kawenga** — responsibility / obligation / burden
**tikanga** — correct practice / custom / culture
**He uri ahau nō ngā tīpuna.** — I am a descendant of the ancestors.
**nō reira he kawenga tōku** — therefore I have a responsibility
**me tiaki au i tēnei reo** — I should/must care for this language

Structure for identity writing:
1. Pepeha (mountain, river, iwi, hapū, place, name)
2. What matters to you (Ko ēnei ngā mea e mau ana ki a au)
3. Your identity statement (He uri ahau nō…)
4. Your responsibility (Nō reira he kawenga tōku — me [action])

*Tip: Me tiaki au i tēnei reo — I must care for this language. This is not just a sentence — it is a commitment. When you write it in your own pepeha, you are making te reo Māori part of your identity.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Extended identity writing**

We write an extended identity piece together, sentence by sentence.

- Pepeha: Ko [maunga] tōku maunga. Ko [awa] tōku awa. Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Nō [place] ahau.
- What matters: Ko ēnei ngā mea e mau ana ki a au: he [noun], he [noun], he [noun].
- Identity statement: He uri ahau nō ōku tīpuna nō reira…
- Responsibility close: Me tiaki au i tēnei reo, i tēnei tikanga, i tēnei ao.

_Discussion prompt: Me tiaki au i tēnei reo, i tēnei tikanga, i tēnei ao — I must care for this language, this culture, this world. Three parallel i phrases. What is the effect of that repetition? Why does saying three things in parallel make a stronger statement than just saying one?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Identity writing**

Write your own extended identity piece in te reo Māori.

- Write your full pepeha (all nine lines).
- Write Ko ēnei ngā mea e mau ana ki a au — three things you hold dear.
- Write your identity statement: He uri ahau nō…
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full identity piece (10 sentences) — pepeha, what matters, identity statement, and responsibility. Use: Ko, He, Kua (what you have become), hiahia, and me tiaki.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He pōhiri — A welcome ceremony

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.4: Community · AO 1.6: Protocol · AO 2.1: Relationships*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Formal greetings & welcome; AO 1.6 — Marae protocol

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Experiencing a pōhiri**

Today we experience the language of a pōhiri — a formal welcome ceremony. You will learn the key phrases, the structure, and what each part means. This is among the most important cultural language in te ao Māori.

> **THE <A CLASS="TE-AKA-LINK" HREF="HTTPS://MAORIDICTIONARY.CO.NZ/SEARCH?KEYWORDS=KARANGA" TARGET="_BLANK" REL="NOOPENER">KARANGA</A> (call of welcome — women):**
> **[Haere mai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Haere+mai), haere mai, haere mai!**
> Come, come, come! (Repeated three times — three is completion)
> 
> **THE <B><A CLASS="TE-AKA-LINK" HREF="HTTPS://MAORIDICTIONARY.CO.NZ/SEARCH?KEYWORDS=WHAIKŌRERO" TARGET="_BLANK" REL="NOOPENER">WHAIKŌRERO</A></B> (formal speech — opens and closes with):**
> **Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.**
> **Ko [mountain] tōku maunga…** (pepeha lines)
> **Nō reira, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.**
> 
> **THE WAIATA (song following each speech):**
> After each whaikōrero, a waiata is sung by that person's group.
> 
> **THE <B><A CLASS="TE-AKA-LINK" HREF="HTTPS://MAORIDICTIONARY.CO.NZ/SEARCH?KEYWORDS=HONGI" TARGET="_BLANK" REL="NOOPENER">HONGI</A></B> (greeting):**
> **Ka [hongi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hongi) tātou.** — We will press noses.
> **Ko te hongi he [tohu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tohu) o te [aroha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aroha).** — The hongi is a sign of love/compassion.

Pōhiri structure:
1. **Karanga** — women call the guests onto the marae ātea
2. **Whaikōrero** — formal speeches by hosts then guests (alternating)
3. **Waiata** — song after each speech
4. **Hongi** — pressing of noses to seal the welcome
5. **Kai** — sharing of food (the division between sacred and everyday is lifted)

After the hongi, manuhiri (guests) become tangata whenua (people of the place) — the distinction ends. This is profound: welcoming someone makes them part of you.

Key phrase:
**Nau mai, haere mai, tū mai, noho mai.**
Come, come, stand here, sit here. (full welcome phrase)

*Tip: Haere mai × 3 — three repetitions. In te ao Māori, saying something three times is a sign of completion and sincerity. Tēnā koutou × 3 at the end of a speech. The hongi shared three times. Three is the number of fullness.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Pōhiri practice**

Class performs a mini pōhiri — one group as tangata whenua, one as manuhiri.

- Tangata whenua: Karanga — Haere mai, haere mai, haere mai! (call three times)
- Tangata whenua whaikōrero: Tēnā koutou katoa. Ko [student pepeha lines]. Nō reira, tēnā koutou ×3.
- Manuhiri respond: Their whaikōrero — Ko [their pepeha]. Tēnā koutou ×3.
- Close: Ka hongi tātou. Ko te hongi he tohu o te aroha. Students share the hongi.

_Discussion prompt: After the hongi, manuhiri become tangata whenua — guests become people of the place. The distinction between "us" and "them" ends. What does this say about the Māori philosophy of welcome? How is this different from how welcome is understood in other contexts you know?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whaikōrero**

Write your own short whaikōrero to deliver in a pōhiri.

- Write your opening: Tēnā koutou katoa.
- Write your pepeha — all nine lines.
- Write two sentences about why you are here and what you bring: I haere mai ahau ki [reason]. Ka kawe mai ahau i [something you bring].
- ✦ Bonus: Write and practise your full whaikōrero (10–12 sentences). Open with Tēnā koutou katoa, give your pepeha, say why you are here, what you bring, what you hope for, and close with Nō reira, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What does "uri" mean?
  - ancestor 
  - descendant / offspring ✅
  - cousin 
  - elder 
- "He uri ahau nō ngā tīpuna." What does tīpuna mean?
  - children 
  - cousins 
  - ancestors / grandparents ✅
  - tribe 

**Tuesday questions**

- What is the marae ātea?
  - The dining room 
  - The gateway 
  - The open space in front of the wharenui — sacred space for formal encounter ✅
  - The sleeping area in the meeting house 
- "Ka pōhiritia mātou e ngā tangata whenua." What grammatical feature makes this sentence notable?
  - It uses Ka for future tense 
  - It uses the passive — we received the welcome from the hosts ✅
  - It uses an object marker i 
  - It uses Kua for completed action 

**Wednesday questions**

- What does "kawenga" mean?
  - ancestor 
  - mountain 
  - responsibility / obligation ✅
  - genealogy 
- "Me tiaki au i tēnei reo, i tēnei tikanga, i tēnei ao." What literary technique uses the three parallel i phrases?
  - Alliteration 
  - Simile 
  - Parallel structure / anaphora for emphasis ✅
  - Personification 

**Thursday questions**

- What happens after the hongi in a pōhiri?
  - The guests leave the marae 
  - The manuhiri become tangata whenua — the distinction between guests and hosts ends ✅
  - The hosts perform a haka 
  - The speeches begin 
- Why is something said or done three times in te ao Māori protocol?
  - Because three people must always agree 
  - Three repetitions signal completion and sincerity ✅
  - It is just a tradition with no meaning 
  - To allow everyone to hear clearly 

---

### Week 4

#### Monday: Ngā kupu hou o te kura — School vocabulary

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.7: Classroom language · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: School context vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.7 — School & classroom language; AO 2.3 — School communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — School life in te reo Māori**

AO 1.7 asks us to use and respond to classroom language. Today we build a rich school vocabulary so that more and more of your school day can happen in te reo Māori. The goal is for te reo to move from the classroom into the corridor, the playground, and the staffroom.

> **SCHOOL PLACES:**
> **akomanga** — classroom
> **[tari](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tari)** — office
> **whare [tākaro](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%81karo)** — gymnasium / sports room
> **[papa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=papa) tākaro** — playground
> **[pātaka](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%81taka)** — library / resource room (literally: storehouse)
> **kaukau** — swimming pool (also: to swim — same word)
> 
> **SCHOOL ACTIVITIES:**
> **Kei te ako mātou.** — We are learning.
> **Kei te [waihanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waihanga) mātou.** — We are creating/building.
> **Kei te [rangahau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rangahau) mātou.** — We are researching.
> **Kei te whakaaro mātou.** — We are thinking.
> **Kei te [taunakitia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=taunakitia) mātou.** — We are being assessed.

School subject names:
**pāngarau** — maths
**pānui** — reading
**tuhituhi** — writing
**akoranga reo** — language lesson
**akoranga pūtaiao** — science (pūtaiao = science/technology)
**akoranga tikanga** — social studies / cultural studies
**akoranga hākinakina** — PE / sports lesson
**akoranga toi** — art (toi = art/creativity)
**akoranga puoro** — music (puoro = music)

Classroom commands (AO 1.7):
**Tū mai!** — Stand up!
**Noho mai!** — Sit down!
**Titiro mai!** — Look this way!
**Whakarongo mai!** — Listen!
**Āta whakaaro!** — Think carefully!
**Mahi tahi!** — Work together!

*Tip: Akoranga = lesson. Put any subject after it: akoranga pāngarau (maths lesson), akoranga toi (art lesson). You can create any school subject name with this word.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — School day in te reo**

Describe your school day in te reo Māori — places, activities, subjects.

- Where are you? **Kei te akomanga au.** Kei te pātaka ngā ākonga. Kei te papa tākaro rātou.
- What subject? **Kei te ako mātou i te pāngarau.** I te akoranga toi tātou.
- Command and response: Teacher calls **Whakarongo mai!** — class responds by listening. Teacher says **Mahi tahi!** — class works in pairs.
- Report: **I te Rāapa, i ako mātou i te akoranga pūtaiao — i ātaahua ngā hua o ā mātou rangahau.**

_Discussion prompt: If your school moved to using te reo Māori for all classroom commands — Tū mai, Noho mai, Whakarongo mai — how do you think that would change the feel of the classroom? What would students need to make that work? What would teachers need?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — School day recount**

Write your school day in te reo Māori.

- Write your timetable for today — each subject using akoranga [subject].
- Write where you are in each lesson: Kei te [place] mātou i te [subject].
- Write three things you did using past tense: I te [time], i [activity] mātou i te [subject].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full school day recount (8 sentences) — every lesson, where you were, what you did, and how you felt about each one. Use Kei te, I, Ka, and E…ana at least once each.

#### Tuesday: He akoranga pai — What makes a good lesson

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended opinions · AO 1.7: School language · AO 2.5: Evaluative language*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Opinion & evaluation; AO 1.7 — School context

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Evaluating and giving opinions about learning**

Today we use evaluative language to talk about what makes learning good — what works, what doesn't, and what you prefer. This connects AO 2.3 (extended opinions) with AO 1.7 (school language).

> **He akoranga pai te akoranga Rāhina nō te mea…**
> Monday's lesson was a good lesson because…
> 
> **He pai ake te [mahi tahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mahi+tahi) i te mahi [kotahi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kotahi) ki a au.**
> Working together is better than working alone in my opinion.
> 
> **Ko tāku whakaaro, he nui ake ngā hua o te [kōrero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Drero) i te [tuhi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuhi).**
> My opinion is that speaking has more benefits than writing.
> 
> **I uaua ki a au te [subject] nō te mea…engari kua mārama ake au.**
> I found [subject] difficult because… but I now understand better.

Evaluative language in te reo:

**He akoranga pai/kino tērā.** — That was a good/bad lesson.
**Ko tāku whakaaro…** — In my opinion / My thought is…
**I uaua ki a au…** — I found it difficult… (personal evaluation)
**I ngāwari ki a au…** — I found it easy… (ngāwari = easy)
**Ka taea e au te…** — I am able to… (showing learning)
**He nui ake ngā hua o te X i te Y.** — X has more benefits than Y.
**He pai ake te X i te Y ki a au.** — X is better than Y for me.

New words:
**ngāwari** — easy / simple
**hua** — benefit / result (you've seen this before)
**mahi tahi** — working together (collaborative work)
**mahi kotahi** — working alone (kotahi = one/alone)

*Tip: Ko tāku whakaaro = my thought/opinion. Use this whenever you want to signal that what follows is your personal view. It is the te reo equivalent of "I think" or "in my opinion."*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Evaluate your learning**

Use evaluative language to assess your own learning.

- Pick a lesson from this week: **He akoranga pai tērā nō te mea…** Why was it good?
- Compare two ways of learning: **He pai ake te mahi tahi i te mahi kotahi ki a au nō te mea…**
- Self-evaluate: **I uaua ki a au te [skill/topic] engari kua mārama ake au nō te mea…**
- State what you can do now: **Ka taea e au te [skill] ināianei — kāore i taea e au i tērā wiki.**

_Discussion prompt: Ko tāku whakaaro, he nui ake ngā hua o te kōrero i te tuhi. Do you agree? Is speaking more valuable than writing for language learning? Or is it the opposite? Defend your view in te reo using He pai ake… nō te mea…_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Learning evaluation**

Write an honest evaluation of your learning this term.

- Write He akoranga pai/uaua tērā nō te mea about one lesson from this week.
- Write He pai ake te X i te Y ki a au about two ways of learning.
- Write I uaua ki a au te [topic] engari kua mārama ake au.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full learning evaluation paragraph (7 sentences) — what you have learned this term, what was hard, what was easy, what you prefer, what you can do now that you couldn't before, and what your next step is. Use evaluative language throughout.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — School, community, and identity together

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Identity · AO 1.7: School language · AO 2.1: Community · AO 2.3: Extended writing*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Identity in school context; AO 2.1 — School community

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — School as community: writing about your place**

Today we combine the school vocabulary, identity language, and community structures into extended writing about what your school means to you and your community. This is the kind of writing that goes on the wall, into a portfolio, or is shared with whānau.

> **Ko tōku kura ko [school name].**
> My school is [name].
> 
> **He kura ātaahua tōku kura — kei reira ōku hoa, ōku kaiako, ōku mōhiotanga katoa.**
> My school is a beautiful school — my friends, my teachers, my entire knowledge are there.
> 
> **I tōku kura, kei te ako mātou i ngā mea katoa — te reo Māori, te pāngarau, te toi, te hākinakina.**
> At my school, we learn everything — te reo Māori, maths, art, sport.
> 
> **Ko tōku kura he wāhi [tiaki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tiaki) mōku — ka [tipu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tipu) ōku whakaaro, ka tipu hoki tōku ngākau.**
> My school is a place of nurturing for me — my thoughts grow, my heart grows too.

New community and school vocabulary:
**mōhiotanga** — knowledge / understanding (mōhio + tanga)
**wāhi tiaki** — place of nurturing / safe place (wāhi = place, tiaki = guard/care)
**ka tipu** — grows (Ka for narrative/ongoing)
**i tōku kura** — at my school
**kei reira** — there / there it is (referring back)
**ko tōku kura he…** — my school is a… (He sentence about identity)

Structure for extended school writing:
1. Identity: Ko tōku kura ko [name].
2. Description: He [adjective] tōku kura nō te mea…
3. What happens there: I tōku kura, kei te ako mātou i…
4. What it means: Ko tōku kura he wāhi… mōku.

*Tip: Ko tōku kura he wāhi tiaki mōku — my school is a place of nurturing for me. Wāhi tiaki is a beautiful phrase — a place that guards and cares. What words would you use to describe your school as a place?*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — School identity writing**

Build a school identity piece together.

- Identity line: Ko tōku kura ko [school name]. Add a He description: He [adjective] tōku kura.
- Community line: Ko ēnei ngā tāngata o tōku kura: he kaiako [X], he ākonga [X]…
- What happens: I tōku kura kei te ako mātou i [list of subjects].
- Meaning line: Ko tōku kura he wāhi [word] mōku nō te mea…

_Discussion prompt: Ka tipu ōku whakaaro, ka tipu hoki tōku ngākau — my thoughts grow, my heart grows too. What does it mean for a school to grow both whakaaro (thoughts/intellect) and ngākau (heart/emotion)? What makes a school do both well?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — School identity piece**

Write your own extended school identity piece.

- Write your school identity opening: Ko tōku kura ko [name]. He [adjective] tōku kura.
- Write what happens there: Ko ēnei ngā mea pai o tōku kura… (Ko ēnei list).
- Write what your school means: Ko tōku kura he wāhi [word] mōku nō te mea…
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full school identity piece (10 sentences) — identity, description, community, what happens, what you learn, what it means to you, and close with Ka tipu ōku whakaaro, ka tipu hoki tōku ngākau i tōku kura.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He taumahatanga — Challenges and resilience

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.5: Evaluative vocabulary · AO 1.6: Discussing challenges*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Resilience & challenge conversation; AO 2.5 — Evaluative vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about challenges and getting through them**

Every learner faces challenges. Today we learn how to talk about challenges, struggles, and resilience in te reo Māori — an important set of language skills for real conversation.

> **A:** He pēhea tōu ako i tēnei wāhanga?
> **B:** I uaua ki a au i te tīmatanga — kāore au e mārama ana ki ngā hanganga katoa.
> **A:** He aha te mea i uaua rawa atu ki a koe?
> **B:** Ko te Kua — e kore au e mōhio ana ko ēhea wā me whakamahi ai i tēnā.
> **A:** Āe, he uaua tēnā. He aha āu i mahi ai hei āwhina i a koe?
> **B:** I pātai au ki tōku kaiako — i āwhina ia i a au. Kua mārama ake au ināianei.
> **A:** Ka pai tōu māia! Ko tērā te ara o te [ākonga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81konga) pai — me [pātai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=p%C4%81tai), me mahi, me āta whakaaro.
> **B:** Āe. Haere tonu!

Resilience vocabulary:
**taumahatanga** — challenge / burden (taumaha = heavy)
**māia** — brave / bold / resilient
**āwhina** — help / support
**pātai** — to ask a question (also: question)
**He aha āu i mahi ai hei āwhina i a koe?**
What did you do to help yourself?
**hei āwhina i a koe** — to help yourself (hei = for the purpose of)
**Ko tērā te ara o te ākonga pai.**
That is the path of a good student.
**me pātai, me mahi, me āta whakaaro**
must ask, must work, must think carefully
**e kore au e mōhio ana ko ēhea wā me whakamahi ai**
I don't know which times I should use it

*Tip: Me pātai, me mahi, me āta whakaaro — three me instructions for a good learner. Ask, work, think carefully. These three together describe almost everything it takes to learn well in any language.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Resilience conversation**

Build a conversation about a challenge you have faced and how you got through it.

- I ask: **He aha te mea i uaua rawa atu ki a koe i tēnei wāhanga?** Answer honestly.
- I ask: **He aha āu i mahi ai hei āwhina i a koe?** Answer with what you did: I pātai au ki… / I mahi au… / I āta whakaaro au.
- I confirm your progress: **Kua mārama ake koe ināianei.** You agree or add more.
- Close with the ākonga pai formula: **Ko tērā te ara o te ākonga pai — me pātai, me mahi, me āta whakaaro.**

_Discussion prompt: Ko tērā te ara o te ākonga pai — me pātai, me mahi, me āta whakaaro. Three things: ask, work, think carefully. Is there anything missing from this list? What would YOU add as a fourth quality of a good learner? Can you say it in te reo?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu taumahatanga**

Write about a challenge you faced and how you got through it.

- Name the challenge: He [noun] tōku taumahatanga — I uaua ki a au te [topic/skill].
- Describe what you did: I pātai au ki… / I mahi au… / I āta whakaaro au.
- Show your progress: Kua mārama ake au ināianei nō te mea…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full resilience account (8 sentences) — name the challenge, when it started, how it felt (emotion vocabulary), what you did about it (three me instructions), who helped, what changed, and how you feel now. Close with Haere tonu!

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What does "pātaka" mean in a school context?
  - gymnasium 
  - playground 
  - library / resource room (storehouse) ✅
  - office 
- "Kei te rangahau mātou." What activity are they doing?
  - creating / building 
  - thinking 
  - researching ✅
  - being assessed 

**Tuesday questions**

- What does "ngāwari" mean?
  - difficult 
  - easy / simple ✅
  - interesting 
  - boring 
- "He pai ake te mahi tahi i te mahi kotahi ki a au." What is being compared?
  - Two different school subjects 
  - Working together versus working alone ✅
  - Two different teachers 
  - Morning lessons versus afternoon lessons 

**Wednesday questions**

- What does "wāhi tiaki" mean?
  - a place to play 
  - a place of learning 
  - a place of nurturing / safe place ✅
  - a place to eat 
- "Ka tipu ōku whakaaro, ka tipu hoki tōku ngākau." What two things are growing?
  - The student's height and their strength 
  - Knowledge/thoughts and heart/emotion ✅
  - The school and the community 
  - Friends and teachers 

**Thursday questions**

- What does "āwhina" mean?
  - challenge 
  - burden 
  - ask a question 
  - help / support ✅
- "Me pātai, me mahi, me āta whakaaro." What are the three qualities of a good learner?
  - Listen, write, remember 
  - Ask, work, think carefully ✅
  - Read, speak, practise 
  - Study, sleep, repeat

### Week 5

#### Monday: Ngā kupu āhua — Adverbs of degree and intensity

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Descriptive language · Grammar Level 1–2: Degree adverbs tino, āhua, noa, rawa atu, atu, mai*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Degree adverbs & intensity; AO 2.3 — Expressive intensity

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — How much, how intense, how degree**

You know tino (very) and ake/rawa atu (more/most). Today we look at the full range of degree and intensity words in te reo Māori — the words that control how strong a statement feels.

> **tino** — very / truly
> **He tino pai tēnei.** — This is very good.
> 
> **[āhua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81hua)** — somewhat / quite / rather / quite / rather
> **He āhua uaua tēnei.** — This is quite difficult.
> 
> **[noa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=noa)** — just / merely / freely (reduces intensity or shows unconstrained action) / freely (reduces intensity or shows unconstrained action)
> **Ka haere noa ia.** — She just went / she wandered freely.
> 
> **[tonu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tonu)** — still / continuously / right (emphasises continuity) / right (emphasises continuity)
> **Kei te mahi tonu ia.** — She is still working.
> 
> **[anō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=an%C5%8D)** — again / still / also (adds repetition or equivalence) / also (adds repetition or equivalence)
> **Ka haere anō mātou.** — We will go again.
> 
> **[kē](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%93)** — indeed / actually / on the contrary (emphasises or corrects) / on the contrary (emphasises or corrects)
> **He pai kē tēnei!** — This is actually really good! / This is even better!

The degree adverb toolkit:

**tino** [adjective/verb] = very [adj]
**āhua** [adjective] = somewhat/quite [adj]
**[verb] noa** = just [verb] / freely [verb]
**[verb] tonu** = still [verb] / continuously [verb]
**[verb] anō** = [verb] again
**[adjective/verb] kē** = actually [adj] / on the contrary
**[verb] atu / mai** = [verb] away from me / toward me

Examples:
**Ka kai noa ia.** — She just eats (freely, casually).
**He pai tonu tēnei.** — This is still good / continuously good.
**Ka hoki anō ia.** — She came back again.
**He pai kē atu tēnei i tērā!** — This is even better than that!

New word:
**Ka [wani](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wani) kē!** — That is amazing! (wani = excellent, kē = indeed)

*Tip: Kē adds a "contrary to expectations" feel — "He pai kē!" means "it is actually good!" (you might not have expected it). Ka wani kē! = That is amazing! (even more than expected). Use kē when something surprises you positively.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Degree challenge**

I give you a plain sentence — you add a degree adverb and say how the meaning changes.

- "He pai tēnei." Add tino → **He tino pai tēnei.** Add āhua → **He āhua pai tēnei.** What changed?
- "Kei te mahi ia." Add tonu → **Kei te mahi tonu ia.** What does tonu add?
- "Ka haere ia." Add noa → **Ka haere noa ia.** Add anō → **Ka haere anō ia.** Different meanings?
- Express surprise: use kē — "This is actually really delicious!" → **He reka kē tēnei!** (reka = delicious)

_Discussion prompt: Ka haere noa ia (she just wanders freely) vs Ka haere tonu ia (she keeps going continuously). Both are about ongoing movement but with completely different feelings. What is the emotional difference? In what situation would you use each?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Degree sentences**

Use degree adverbs to add intensity and nuance to your sentences.

- Write three sentences using tino, āhua, and noa — one each.
- Write two sentences using tonu and anō — show the difference.
- Write a kē sentence that expresses pleasant surprise: **He [adjective] kē tēnei!**
- ✦ Bonus: Rewrite five plain sentences from your previous work by adding degree adverbs. Read the original and the new version aloud — notice how much more expressive the degree version sounds.

#### Tuesday: He aha te tikanga? — Explaining meaning and custom

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended explanation · AO 1.4: Cultural connection · Grammar Level 1–2: Explaining meaning structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Explanation & meaning; AO 1.4 — Cultural meaning

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Explaining what things mean**

Being able to EXPLAIN things in te reo — what a word means, why a custom exists, what a phrase does — is a sign of true fluency. Today we learn the structures for giving explanations and definitions in te reo Māori.

> **He aha te [tikanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tikanga) o tēnei kupu?**
> What is the meaning of this word?
> 
> **Ko te tikanga o "aroha" ko te…**
> The meaning of "aroha" is…
> 
> **Ko te tikanga o te hongi ko te tiaki i te [manawa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=manawa) ora.**
> The meaning of the hongi is the keeping of the breath of life.
> 
> **Nā reira, ka [kīia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%ABia) ko "aroha" tērā.**
> Therefore, that is called "aroha".
> 
> **He mea nui te [custom] nō te mea…**
> The [custom] is an important thing because…

Explanation structures:

1. **Ko te tikanga o [word/thing] ko te [explanation].**
The meaning of [word] is [explanation].

2. **He mea nui/pai/tapu [custom] nō te mea…**
[Custom] is important/good/sacred because…

3. **Nā reira, ka kīia ko [name] tērā.**
Therefore, that thing is called [name].

4. **Ko tā [word] mahi ko te [function].**
The function of [word] is [function].

New words:
**tikanga** — meaning / custom / correct practice
**manawa ora** — breath of life (manawa = breath, ora = life/wellbeing)
**tapu** — sacred / restricted / forbidden
**noa** — free from tapu / ordinary / unrestricted (also: just/freely)
**ka kīia** — is called / is named (passive)

*Tip: Ko te tikanga o [X] ko te [explanation]. This is the te reo pattern for defining something. Master this and you can explain any word or concept in te reo Māori.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Define and explain**

Use the explanation structures to define te reo words and cultural concepts.

- Define a word: **Ko te tikanga o "whakapapa" ko te raupapa o ngā tīpuna.** (raupapa = sequence/ordering)
- Explain a custom: **He mea nui te karanga nō te mea ko ia te reo o ngā wāhine ki ngā manuhiri.**
- Use the kīia structure: **Ko te wāhi e noho ai ngā tāngata i ngā hui, ka kīia ko "wharenui" tērā.**
- Define a term from this lesson: **Ko te tikanga o "tapu" ko te mea kua whakanoa rānei — he mea whakamōhio e ai ki te tikanga Māori.**

_Discussion prompt: Ko te tikanga o "manawa ora" ko te tiaki i te mauri o te tangata — the meaning of "breath of life" is the preservation of a person's life force. The hongi shares this breath. What does this tell us about how Māori understood human connection and greeting? Is there anything like this in other cultures you know?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Explain and define**

Write explanations of te reo words and cultural practices.

- Write Ko te tikanga o [word] ko te [explanation] for three te reo words you know.
- Write He mea nui [custom] nō te mea for one cultural practice.
- Write a ka kīia sentence: define something by what it is called.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "te reo glossary entry" for five terms from this term — each entry with Ko te tikanga o… ko te…, He mea nui/pai/tapu… nō te mea…, and one example sentence. This could go on a classroom wall.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Formal and informal register

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.6: Politeness conventions · AO 2.3: Register · Grammar Level 1–2: Formal vs informal language choices*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.6 — Formal & informal register; AO 2.3 — Register-appropriate language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Knowing when to be formal and when to be casual**

Good language users know that how you speak changes depending on who you are with and where you are. The same idea can be said formally (at a pōhiri) or casually (with your friends). Today we practise both registers in te reo Māori.

> **CASUAL (with friends, in class):**
> **Kia ora! He aha tōu ingoa?** — Hey! What's your name?
> **Ka pai tāu!** — Nice one! / Good on you!
> **E haere ana koe ki hea?** — Where are you going?
> **Āe noa [iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=iho)!** — Sure, no worries!
> 
> **FORMAL (pōhiri, meeting, addressing elders):**
> **Tēnā koe / Tēnā koutou katoa.** — Greetings (formal).
> **Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Nō [place] ahau.** (full pepeha)
> **Ka [rawe](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rawe) tāu i mahi ai.** — What you have done is excellent.
> **He nui tōku [mihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mihi) ki a koutou.** — My gratitude to you all is great.

Register markers in te reo Māori:

FORMAL signals:
→ Use full pepeha rather than just your name
→ Tēnā koe/kōrua/koutou rather than Kia ora
→ He nui tōku mihi ki a koe (formal gratitude)
→ Full sentence structures, no shortcuts
→ Ka rawe, Ka tino pai (formal praise)

CASUAL signals:
→ Kia ora (friendly, warm)
→ Ka pai! (casual praise)
→ Shorter sentences
→ Direct questions without elaborate framing
→ Āe noa iho (sure, no problem)

New formal phrases:
**He nui tōku mihi ki a koe.** — My gratitude to you is great.
**Ka rawe tāu i mahi ai.** — What you have done is excellent.
**Ka ārahina tātou e koe.** — You have led us. (passive)

*Tip: Register is about relationship and context, not about one being "better" than the other. Casual with friends shows warmth. Formal with elders shows respect. Knowing which to use is wisdom.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Switch the register**

I give you a casual sentence — you make it formal. Then I give you a formal one — you make it casual.

- Casual: "Kia ora! He aha tōu ingoa?" → Formal: **Tēnā koe. Ko wai tō ingoa?**
- Formal: "He nui tōku mihi ki a koutou." → Casual: **Kia ora koutou — ka nui ōku whakaaro ki a koutou!**
- Casual: "Ka pai tāu!" → Formal: **Ka rawe tāu i mahi ai. He mea nui tērā.**
- Now write your own pair — one casual, one formal — about the same situation.

_Discussion prompt: Āe noa iho — sure, no worries. Noa here makes it casual and easy. He nui tōku mihi ki a koe — my gratitude is great. The formal version uses nui (big) to show depth. The same idea — I'm thankful — expressed at completely different levels. What would happen if you used the formal phrase with a close friend? The casual one with an elder?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Register practice**

Write the same content in two different registers.

- Write a casual greeting to a friend and a formal greeting to a visitor — different words, same warmth.
- Write a casual "well done" and a formal "well done" — different register, same meaning.
- Write one sentence from your life in casual te reo, then rewrite it formally.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short speech (6 sentences) as if you were welcoming a visitor to your school — formal register throughout. Then rewrite it as something you would say to a friend visiting — casual register. Read both aloud and notice how different they feel.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero ā-reo — A language conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended conversation · AO 1.4: Cultural connection · AO 2.5: Language vocabulary*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Language & learning conversation; AO 1.4 — Cultural identity through language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about language itself**

Today we have a meta-conversation — we use te reo Māori to talk about te reo Māori. This is one of the highest-level language activities: using a language to reflect on itself, its beauty, its importance, and what it means to learn it.

> **A:** He aha tō whakaaro mō te ako i te reo Māori?
> **B:** Ko tāku whakaaro, he taonga nui rawa atu te reo Māori — he reo [rangatira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rangatira), he reo ātaahua.
> **A:** He aha ngā uauatanga i pā mai ki a koe?
> **B:** I uaua ki a au ngā hanganga o te tense — Ko Kua, Ko Ka — engari kua mārama ake au.
> **A:** He aha te mea i āwhina i a koe?
> **B:** Ko tēnei akoranga — ko tā mātou mahi tahi i ōku [āwhina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81whina). Ka taea e au te kōrero māmā ake ināianei.
> **A:** He aha tāu e hiahia ana ki te ako āpōpō?
> **B:** Kei te hiahia au ki te kōrero māmā ake me tōku whānau — ko tērā tōku moemoeā.

New vocabulary for language conversation:
**ngā uauatanga** — the difficulties (plural of uauatanga)
**i pā mai ki a koe** — that came to you / that you encountered (pā = touch/affect)
**tā mātou mahi tahi** — our working together (tā mātou = our ā-category thing)
**māmā ake** — more fluently / more easily
**moemoeā** — dream / aspiration
**He reo rangatira.** — It is a chiefly language.
**ko tērā tōku moemoeā** — that is my dream/aspiration

Asking about language learning:
**He aha ngā uauatanga i pā mai ki a koe?**
What difficulties did you encounter?
**He aha te mea i āwhina i a koe?**
What helped you?

*Tip: Ko tērā tōku moemoeā — that is my dream. Moemoeā means both "dream" (sleeping) and "aspiration" (waking dream). Using it for a learning goal connects your aspiration to something deep and important.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Language conversation**

Build a conversation about te reo Māori and what it means to learn it.

- I ask: **He aha tō whakaaro mō te ako i te reo Māori?** Answer with Ko tāku whakaaro…
- I ask: **He aha ngā uauatanga i pā mai ki a koe?** Answer honestly with I uaua ki a au…
- I ask: **He aha te mea i āwhina i a koe?** Answer with what helped: Ko tēnei akoranga / ko tōku hoa / ko…
- I ask: **Ko ēhea āu moemoeā mō tō reo Māori?** Answer with Kei te hiahia au ki te… Ko tērā tōku moemoeā.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku moemoeā ko te kōrero māmā me tōku whānau — my dream is to speak fluently with my family. Why might speaking te reo with your family feel like the ultimate goal? What does it mean when a language lives at home, not just at school?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tōu moemoeā**

Write about your te reo Māori journey and dream.

- Write Ko tāku whakaaro mō te reo Māori: (your opinion about te reo).
- Write the difficulties you encountered: I uaua ki a au… engari…
- Write what helped: Ko [person/thing] i āwhina i a au.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full language reflection (8 sentences) — your opinion of te reo Māori, what you found hard, what helped, what you can do now (Ka taea e au te…), and your dream for your te reo (Ko tērā tōku moemoeā). Read it to a partner.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka haere noa ia." What does "noa" add to this sentence?
  - She is going urgently 
  - She just goes / wanders freely, casually ✅
  - She is going again 
  - She is still going 
- "He pai kē tēnei!" What does "kē" signal?
  - This is somewhat good 
  - This is not good 
  - This is actually really good / even better than expected ✅
  - This is the same as before 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko te tikanga o te hongi ko te tiaki i te manawa ora." What does manawa ora mean?
  - the meeting house 
  - the breath of life ✅
  - the welcome ceremony 
  - the gathering of people 
- What does "tapu" mean?
  - ordinary / unrestricted 
  - wrong / incorrect 
  - sacred / restricted / forbidden ✅
  - beautiful 

**Wednesday questions**

- Which phrase signals a FORMAL register for expressing gratitude?
  - Ka pai! 
  - Āe noa iho! 
  - He nui tōku mihi ki a koe. ✅
  - Kia ora! 
- What does "Āe noa iho!" express?
  - I strongly agree 
  - I disagree completely 
  - Sure, no worries! (casual agreement) ✅
  - That is very difficult 

**Thursday questions**

- What does "moemoeā" mean?
  - challenge / difficulty 
  - the past 
  - dream / aspiration ✅
  - hard work 
- "He aha ngā uauatanga i pā mai ki a koe?" What is this question asking about?
  - Where you went 
  - What you like most 
  - What difficulties you encountered ✅
  - Who helped you
