#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- Which Term 3 structure expresses a completed action whose result is relevant now?
  - Kei te 
  - Ka 
  - I 
  - Kua ✅
- "Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko tōku whānau, ko tōku reo, ko tōku taiao." What are the three pou?
  - school, friends, sport 
  - family, language, natural world ✅
  - food, shelter, water 
  - work, rest, play 

**Tuesday questions**

- Identify the structure: "E tipu ana tōku māramatanga i ia rā, i ia rā."
  - Kei te present 
  - Ka narrative 
  - E…ana progressive — ongoing growth ✅
  - I past 
- "Ahakoa i uaua, i mahi tonu au." What does ahakoa signal?
  - a result — because it was hard I worked 
  - a time — when it was hard I worked 
  - a concession — DESPITE it being hard, I kept working ✅
  - a condition — if it is hard I will work 

**Wednesday questions**

- What is a "portfolio sentence"?
  - The longest sentence you can write 
  - A sentence for grammar practice only 
  - Your finest, most personal, most true te reo sentence — a declaration of who you are ✅
  - A sentence that uses every vocabulary word 
- "I tīmata au i tētahi kupu — ināianei, kua tipu ōku rerenga ki tētahi ao hou." What does "ao hou" mean?
  - a new day 
  - a new world / new realm ✅
  - a new language 
  - a new friend 

**Thursday questions**

- What is the correct order for a full pepeha?
  - Name → mountain → river → iwi → school → origin 
  - Mountain → river → waka → iwi → hapū → marae → school → name → origin ✅
  - Origin → name → iwi → mountain → river 
  - School → name → iwi → hapū → origin 
- "Ko tōku moemoeā: ka kōrero tōku whānau i te reo Māori." What does this dream express?
  - That the speaker will learn English 
  - That the speaker's family will speak te reo Māori ✅
  - That te reo Māori will be taught in all schools 
  - That the speaker will win a competition 

---

## Term 4

### Week 1

#### Monday: Tēnā koutou katoa — Formal greetings and register mastery

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.6: Politeness conventions · AO 1.2: Formal introductions*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Formal greetings; AO 1.6 — Register mastery

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The full greeting system**

Welcome to Term 4 — Kia Mārama, the term of fluency. This term we bring everything together and reach for genuine spontaneous conversation. We begin where te reo Māori always begins — with greetings. But this term, we master the FULL system — every register, every occasion, every nuance.

> **CASUAL (hoa, tamariki):**
> **Kia ora!** — Hello / Thank you
> **Kia ora kōrua/koutou!** — Hello you two / Hello everyone
> 
> **FORMAL SINGULAR:**
> **Tēnā koe.** — Greetings to you (one person)
> 
> **FORMAL DUAL:**
> **Tēnā kōrua.** — Greetings to you two
> 
> **FORMAL PLURAL:**
> **Tēnā koutou.** — Greetings to you all (3+)
> **Tēnā koutou katoa.** — Greetings to each and every one of you
> 
> **FAREWELL SYSTEM:**
> **Hei konā rā.** — Goodbye (speaker is leaving)
> **E noho rā.** — Goodbye (listener is staying)
> **Ā, ka kite anō.** — Until we meet again
> **[Mā te wā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=M%C4%81+te+w%C4%81).** — Until another time / Farewell (for a long parting)

The greeting system is built on the pronoun system you know:

koe (you, 1) → Tēnā koe
kōrua (you 2) → Tēnā kōrua
koutou (you 3+) → Tēnā koutou

For acknowledgement and gratitude:
**Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina.**
Thank you for your help.
(Tēnā koe + mō + reason for thanks)

**Tēnā koutou mō ā koutou mahi.**
Thank you all for your work.

For welcoming:
**Nau mai, haere mai!** — Welcome! Come in!
**Nau mai ki tō mātou kura.**
Welcome to our school.

New word:
**mō** — for / about / on account of (used for giving thanks)

*Tip: Tēnā koe mō [reason] = thank you for [reason]. This is the complete thank-you formula in formal te reo. Tēnā koe alone = greeting. Tēnā koe mō tāu mahi = thank you for your work.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Full greeting system practice**

Rapid fire — I describe the situation, you give the right greeting.

- Greeting the whole class formally: **Tēnā koutou katoa.** Now greet two students: **Tēnā kōrua.**
- Thanking a teacher for help: **Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina.** Thanking the whole class: **Tēnā koutou mō ā koutou mahi.**
- Saying goodbye as you leave: **Hei konā rā.** Staying while someone else leaves: **E noho rā.**
- Welcoming a visitor: **Nau mai, haere mai! Nau mai ki tō mātou kura.**

_Discussion prompt: E noho rā (you stay) vs Hei konā rā (farewell where I am). These two farewells tell you who is moving. Why does te reo Māori distinguish who is leaving and who is staying? What does this say about how Māori thinks about presence and movement?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Greeting mastery**

Use the full greeting system in real sentences.

- Write the correct greeting for: your teacher, two classmates, the whole school assembly.
- Write a formal thank-you: Tēnā koe/koutou mō… (three different reasons for thanks).
- Write the correct farewell for: you are leaving, your friend is leaving, it is the end of term.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full formal welcome speech (8 sentences) for a visitor to your school — Nau mai, greeting by role, thank them for coming, introduce the school, close with Tēnā koutou ×3.

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupu hono hou — Advanced linking and cohesion

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Advanced cohesion, discourse markers*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Advanced cohesion; AO 2.5 — Discourse markers

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Linking ideas like a fluent speaker**

Fluent speakers do not just string sentences together — they signal the relationship between ideas with precision. Today we learn the advanced linking toolkit — the words and phrases that mark transitions, summaries, contrasts, and conclusions in extended te reo Māori.

> **To add / expand:**
> **[waihoki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=waihoki)** — moreover / and also
> **ā, ko tērā anō** — and there is also this
> **[kāore noa iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C4%81ore+noa+iho)** — not only that
> 
> **To contrast:**
> **[heoi anō](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=heoi+an%C5%8D)** — however
> **engari rā** — but actually / however (stronger) / however (stronger)
> **ahakoa ōna āhuatanga** — despite its qualities
> 
> **To conclude:**
> **[nō reira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=n%C5%8D+reira)** — therefore / so
> **ko te [mutunga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mutunga)** — in conclusion
> **hei [whakakapi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakakapi)** — to conclude / to summarise / to summarise
> 
> **To illustrate:**
> **hei [tauira](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tauira)** — for example
> **pēnei i te** — such as / like
> **[arā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ar%C4%81)** — that is / namely

In use:

**He taonga te reo Māori. Kāore noa iho — he taonga ā-ahurea hoki.**
Te reo Māori is a treasure. Not only that — it is also a cultural treasure.

**He nui ngā hua o te ako i te reo Māori. Heoi anō, he uaua hoki.**
There are many benefits of learning te reo. However, it is also difficult.

**Ko te mutunga, me tiaki tātou i tēnei reo mō āpōpō ō ngā uri.**
In conclusion, we must protect this language for tomorrow's descendants.

**Hei tauira, ko te haka tētahi mahi toi rongonui — arā, he tohu aroha me he tohu kaha.**
For example, haka is a famous art form — namely, a sign of love and a sign of strength.

New words:
**ā-ahurea** — cultural (ā + ahurea = cultural/relating to culture)
**hei whakakapi** — to summarise / in summary

*Tip: Hei tauira = for example. Arā = namely / that is. Use these when you want to illustrate a point with a specific case. They signal: "I am about to give you evidence for what I just said."*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Advanced linking**

I give you two sentences — you choose the best advanced linking word and explain why.

- "He pai te reo Māori." + "He tino rangatira hoki." → Expanding: **Kāore noa iho — he tino rangatira hoki te reo Māori.**
- "He nui ngā ākonga i tēnei kura." + "Ko tētahi āhuatanga pai tērā." → Concluding: **Ko te mutunga, he kura nui tēnei.**
- "I mahi kaha mātou." + "I toa mātou." → Giving an example: **Hei tauira — i toa mātou i te kaupeka hākinakina o tērā wiki.**
- Write your own: an argument about kaitiakitanga using waihoki, heoi anō, and nō reira.

_Discussion prompt: Ko te mutunga — in conclusion. Hei whakakapi — to summarise. These are the phrases that close an argument. In te ao Māori, how you close a kōrero is as important as how you open it. Why? What does a strong conclusion do that a weak one doesn't?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Advanced linking sentences**

Use advanced discourse markers in extended writing.

- Write three sentences using waihoki, heoi anō, and hei tauira — one each.
- Write a Ko te mutunga or hei whakakapi conclusion sentence about something you believe.
- Use arā to clarify a point: state something general, then use arā to specify.
- ✦ Bonus: Write an argument paragraph (8 sentences) about te reo Māori. Use at least five different linking markers: kāore noa iho, heoi anō, hei tauira, arā, and Ko te mutunga.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — All structures, full fluency

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · All grammar structures Terms 1–4*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Full fluency writing; AO 2.5 — Complete vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Writing with everything you have**

This is Term 4, Week 1. You have three terms of te reo Māori behind you. Today we write without guardrails — using everything you know, in whatever order serves what you want to say. This is what fluency looks like.

> **A Term 4 student's passage:**
> 
> Ko au ko Hēmi. He ākonga au i tēnei kura, he kaitiaki hoki o tēnei taiao.
> I tīmata au i tētahi kupu — "Kia ora" — ināianei, kua [tipu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tipu) tōku reo ki tētahi mea nui ake.
> E ako tonu ana au — kāore anō kua oti — engari, ka taea e au te kōrero i ngā āhuatanga nui o tōku ao ināianei.
> Hei tauira: ka taea e au te [mihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mihi), ka taea e au te tuhituhi, ka taea e au te whakaaro i te reo Māori.
> Ko te mutunga, ko tēnei reo tōku ara ki ngā [tīpuna](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABpuna), ki ngā [uri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uri), ā ki tōku ake ngākau.

Notice every structure in that passage:

Ko (×2 — identity)
He (×2 — description)
I tīmata au — past
Kua tipu — perfect aspect
E ako tonu ana — E…ana progressive
Kāore anō kua oti — Kua negative
Ka taea e au te — ability (×3)
Hei tauira — for example
Ko te mutunga — in conclusion
Ko + Ko — identity statement chain

New phrase:
**tōku ake ngākau** — my very own heart (ake = own/particular when after a possessive)
**Tōku ake** = my very own (more emphatic than tōku alone)

*Tip: Tōku ake = my very own. Tāu ake = your very own. Ake after a possessive adds the sense of "own" — particular to you, belonging to no one else. Use it when you want to say something is truly, personally yours.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Free writing with everything**

I give you a topic — you write freely, using any structures that serve what you want to say.

- Topic: what te reo Māori means to you. Write four sentences — no structure requirements, just truth.
- Topic: your biggest achievement this year. Write four sentences — past, present, future all welcome.
- Topic: someone who has influenced you. Write four sentences — any structures, show don't tell.
- Read your sentences aloud. Which one sounds most like YOUR voice in te reo? Why?

_Discussion prompt: Ko tēnei reo tōku ara ki ngā tīpuna, ki ngā uri, ā ki tōku ake ngākau. The language is a path to the ancestors, to the descendants, and to my very own heart. Three destinations. Which of the three feels most real to you right now?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Free writing**

Write freely in te reo Māori for ten minutes. No constraints. Just truth.

- Write for 5 minutes without stopping — anything on your mind in te reo.
- Read back what you wrote. Underline the structures you used without thinking.
- Choose your best sentence from your free writing. Improve it once.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full free-writing piece (10 sentences) on any topic you choose. After writing, annotate every structure you used. Count them. This is evidence of your fluency.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He mihi whakamutunga — End of year farewell language

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings/farewells · AO 1.6: Politeness · AO 2.3: Extended formal communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Farewell language; AO 1.6 — Formal occasion language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Language for endings and new beginnings**

Term 4 is a time of endings and transitions — the end of the year, farewells to classmates, transitions to new classes. Today we learn the language of endings and new beginnings in te reo Māori.

> **Mā te wā.** — Until another time / Farewell (for a long parting)
> **Ā, ka kite anō.** — Until we meet again
> **Hei konā rā.** — Farewell (I am leaving)
> **E noho rā.** — Farewell (you stay, I go)
> 
> **For a year's end:**
> **I tēnei tau, kua tipu tātou katoa.**
> This year, we have all grown.
> 
> **He tau tino pai tēnei mōku.**
> This has been a very good year for me.
> 
> **Tēnā koutou katoa mō ā koutou mahi i tēnei tau.**
> Thank you all for your work this year.
> 
> **Ka nui ōku [mihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mihi) ki a koutou.**
> My greetings/gratitude to you all is great.

Transition language:
**Ā tērā tau** — next year
**Ā tērā kura** — at the next school
**Ka tipu tonu** — will keep growing
**Ka huri ake** — will turn / transition
**Me mōhio tātou** — we should know / let us remember
**Ko tōku inoi** — my wish / prayer
**Ka nui ōku mihi** — my greetings/thanks are great

Farewell speech structure:
1. Tēnā koutou katoa.
2. I tēnei tau, kua…
3. He tau [adjective] tēnei nō te mea…
4. Tēnā koutou mō…
5. Ko tōku inoi mō koutou: ka…
6. Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.

New phrase:
**Ko tōku inoi mō koutou** — my wish/prayer for you all

*Tip: Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou — my greetings and thanks to you all are great. This phrase does double work — it both greets and thanks. In te ao Māori, mihi (greeting/tribute) and gratitude are the same action.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Farewell conversation**

Build a year-end farewell exchange.

- Opening: **Tēnā koutou katoa. I tēnei tau, kua tipu tātou katoa.** What has grown? Add specifics.
- Thank: **Tēnā koutou mō ā koutou mahi i tēnei tau.** What specific things are you thanking them for?
- Wish: **Ko tōku inoi mō koutou: ka [hope for them next year].**
- Close: **Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou. Tēnā koutou ×3. Mā te wā.**

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku inoi mō koutou — my wish/prayer for you. What IS your wish for your classmates for next year? In te reo Māori? Try to say it. Ka [verb] koutou — may you all [verb]. This is one of the most generous things you can say to someone._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — End of year farewell speech**

Write a farewell speech for the end of the school year.

- Open: Tēnā koutou katoa. I tēnei tau, kua… (what has happened/changed).
- Thank: Tēnā koutou mō ā koutou mahi — be specific about three things.
- Wish: Ko tōku inoi mō koutou: ka [three wishes].
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full farewell speech (10 sentences) following the six-step structure. Deliver it to the class — without reading if possible.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- You are leaving a room and someone is staying. Which farewell do YOU say?
  - E noho rā 
  - Hei konā rā ✅
  - Mā te wā 
  - Ā ka kite anō 
- "Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina." What is the speaker doing?
  - Greeting someone formally 
  - Asking for help 
  - Thanking someone for their help ✅
  - Saying goodbye 

**Tuesday questions**

- Which linking word means "not only that" — used to expand on a point?
  - heoi anō 
  - nō reira 
  - kāore noa iho ✅
  - hei tauira 
- "Hei tauira, ko te haka tētahi mahi toi rongonui." What is the function of hei tauira?
  - To conclude an argument 
  - To introduce an example / illustration ✅
  - To contrast two ideas 
  - To ask a question 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Tōku ake ngākau." What does ake add after the possessive here?
  - It changes the meaning to "my friends' heart" 
  - It adds emphasis — my VERY OWN heart, particularly mine ✅
  - It makes it past tense 
  - It is a filler word with no meaning 
- Which statement best describes Term 4 fluency?
  - Knowing all the grammar rules by name 
  - Using structures automatically in service of what you want to say ✅
  - Writing the longest sentences possible 
  - Translating directly from English 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ko tōku inoi mō koutou." What does inoi mean?
  - challenge 
  - farewell 
  - wish / prayer ✅
  - work 
- "Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou." What does this sentence express?
  - I have many problems with you all 
  - My greetings and gratitude to you all are great ✅
  - We have much work to do together 
  - I wish you would come back 

---

### Week 2

#### Monday: He kōrero ā-waha — Oral storytelling techniques

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended oral communication · AO 2.5: Narrative vocabulary · Grammar Level 1–2: Oral storytelling structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Oral storytelling; AO 2.5 — Narrative vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Telling a story that grips the listener**

Oral storytelling — [kōrero pūrākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Drero+p%C5%ABr%C4%81kau) — is one of the oldest and most important skills in te ao Māori. Knowledge was kept in memory and transmitted through story. Today we learn the techniques that make a story gripping when spoken aloud.

> **OPENING the story:**
> **I tērā wā, i tērā wā…** — In that time, in that time… (traditional opening), in that time… (traditional opening)
> **Nō ngā [rā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81) o mua…** — In the days of old…
> **He pō, he pō…** — One night, one night… (repetition for effect)
> 
> **BUILDING tension:**
> **Ā, ka huri noa te ao.** — And the world just spun (something changed)
> **Ka [ohorere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ohorere) rātou katoa.** — They were all startled / taken by surprise
> **Kāore he tangata i kite.** — No one saw.
> 
> **CLOSING the story:**
> **Nō reira, ka [mutu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mutu).** — And so it ended.
> **Tērā ia te [kōrero](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Drero).** — That is the story.
> **Ka [huri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=huri).** — The telling turns. (traditional close). (traditional close)

Storytelling techniques:

1. **Repetition for emphasis**:
"I tērā wā, i tērā wā…" — repeating sets a rhythm and signals story time
"He pō, he pō…" — doubling creates atmosphere

2. **Short sentences for drama**:
"Ka tū ia. Ka titiro. Kāore he mea." — She stood. She looked. Nothing.

3. **Personifying nature**:
"Ka tangi te hau." — The wind cried. (from Term 1!)
"Ka ngunguru ngā whatitiri." — The thunder growled.

4. **Direct speech**:
"Ka mea ia: 'Āe, ka haere au.'" — She said: "Yes, I will go."

New words:
**pūrākau** — traditional story / myth / legend
**huri noa** — just spun/turned (unexpected change)
**Ka huri.** — The telling turns. (traditional story ending)

*Tip: I tērā wā, i tērā wā… — say it twice. Repetition at the opening tells the listener: we are leaving ordinary time and entering story time. This is ancient technology.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Story techniques in action**

We build a short story together using each technique.

- Open: **I tērā wā, i tērā wā, i noho tētahi kōtiro i te taha o te awa.** Set the scene — who, where.
- Build tension with short Ka sentences: **Ka rongo ia i tētahi oro. Ka tū ia. Ka titiro ia.**
- Use personification: **Ka ngunguru te ngahere. Ka tangi te hau i ngā rākau.**
- Close: **Nō reira, ka hoki ia ki tōna kāinga. Ka huri.**

_Discussion prompt: Ka huri — the telling turns. This traditional ending signals that the story is complete and the teller is returning control to the listeners. Why do oral cultures develop formal story openings and closings? What do they do for the listener that a written story opening and closing can't?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tell your story**

Write a short pūrākau using the oral storytelling techniques.

- Open with I tērā wā, i tērā wā or Nō ngā rā o mua — establish time, place, character.
- Build tension with three short Ka sentences — action, pause, action.
- Use at least one personification of nature: Ka [sound word] te [natural element].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full pūrākau (10 sentences) — traditional opening, scene-setting, rising action with tension, a moment of surprise (Ka ohorere…), resolution, and Ka huri close. Read it aloud to a partner in your best storytelling voice.

#### Tuesday: Ngā rerenga pakiaka — Complex subordinate sentences

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Complex subordination, relative clauses, purpose clauses*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Complex sentence mastery; AO 2.5 — Advanced structures

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The most complex sentence structures in te reo**

You have ahakoa, mehemea, i te wā, nō te mea. Today we add the final complex structures — relative clauses (describing noun phrases) and purpose clauses (saying why you do something). These are the structures that make te reo truly sophisticated.

> **RELATIVE CLAUSE (ai):**
> **Ko ia te tangata i [āwhina](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%81whina) ai i a mātou.**
> She is the person who helped us. (ai marks the relative clause)
> 
> **Ko tēnei te pukapuka e ako ana ngā ākonga.**
> This is the book the students are learning from.
> 
> **PURPOSE CLAUSE (hei… / mō te…):**
> **I haere au ki te [kura](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kura) hei ako i te reo Māori.**
> I went to school in order to learn te reo Māori.
> 
> **I mahi kaha ia mō te toa i te [whakataetae](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakataetae).**
> She worked hard in order to win the competition.
> 
> **REASON + PURPOSE together:**
> **I ako au nō te mea kei te hiahia au ki te mōhio — hei āwhina i ōku [uri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uri) āpōpō.**
> I learned because I want to know — in order to help my descendants tomorrow.

The relative clause marker **ai**:

Ai appears at the END of a relative clause — it turns a sentence back to describe a noun:

"Ko ia te tangata…" + "i āwhina ia i a mātou" → "Ko ia te tangata i āwhina **ai** i a mātou."
She is the person who helped us.

"Ko tēnei te wā…" + "ka kōrero tātou i tēnei wā" → "Ko tēnei te wā ka kōrero **ai** tātou."
This is the time we speak.

Purpose clause: **hei [verb]** = in order to [verb]
"I haere au ki te kura **hei** ako i te reo Māori."

New phrase:
**Ko tēnei te wā ka kōrero ai tātou.**
This is the time for us to speak.

*Tip: Ai at the end of a clause says: "look back — this clause is describing the noun I mentioned earlier." Think of it as an arrow pointing backward to the noun.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Complex structures**

Build relative clauses and purpose clauses.

- Relative clause: "She is the person who taught us." → **Ko ia te tangata i ako ai i a mātou.**
- Purpose clause: "I am learning te reo in order to speak with my whānau." → **Kei te ako au i te reo Māori hei kōrero me tōku whānau.**
- Combine: "I worked hard because I want to succeed — in order to help my community." → **I mahi kaha au nō te mea kei te hiahia au ki te angitu — hei āwhina i tōku hapori.**
- Write your own: a relative clause about your teacher, and a purpose clause about why you are here.

_Discussion prompt: "Hei āwhina i ōku uri āpōpō" — in order to help my descendants tomorrow. You are a Year 5-6 student. Your descendants are decades away. But you are already thinking about them through this language. What does it mean to learn something NOW for people who do not yet exist?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Complex sentences**

Write relative clauses and purpose clauses.

- Write two relative clauses using ai: Ko ia te tangata/wāhi/wā… [ai clause].
- Write two purpose clauses using hei: I/Kei te [action] au hei [purpose].
- Combine reason and purpose: I [action] nō te mea… hei…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (8 sentences) about your te reo learning journey using: a relative clause (ai), a purpose clause (hei), ahakoa, nō te mea, Kua, and Ko te mutunga. Make every structure serve something true.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Extended narrative writing

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended narrative · AO 2.5: Full vocabulary · Grammar Level 1–2: All narrative structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Extended narrative; AO 2.5 — Narrative vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — A full narrative in te reo Māori**

Today we write a complete narrative — beginning, middle, and end — using everything from four terms. This is the summit of te reo Māori writing for Year 5-6.

> **BEGINNING — set the scene:**
> I tērā wā, i tērā wā, i noho tētahi kōtiro, ko Hine, i roto i te ngahere nui o Aotearoa. He ngahere ātaahua, engari he ngahere mōhio hoki — i rongo ia i ngā mea katoa.
> 
> **MIDDLE — build, tension, crisis:**
> I tētahi rā, ka [rongo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=rongo) a Hine i tētahi [tangi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tangi) kino i roto i ngā rākau. Ka [ohorere](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ohorere) ia. Ka haere ia ki reira — ka kite ia i tētahi [manu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=manu) tino iti e mamae ana i tōna [parirau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=parirau).
> 
> **END — resolution, reflection:**
> Ka tango a Hine i te manu. Ka [tiaki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tiaki) ia i a ia ā te wā i ora ai. I te wā i rere anō te manu, ka tangi a Hine — ehara i te pōuri, he [hari](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hari) kē. Ka huri.

Narrative structure in te reo:

BEGINNING: I tērā wā + scene + character introduction
MIDDLE: Ka sequence + ohorere moment + crisis
END: Resolution + reflection + Ka huri

New words in this narrative:
**parirau** — wing
**i ora ai** — until it recovered (ora = well, ai = relative marker)
**ehara i te pōuri** — it was not sadness
**he hari kē** — it was actually joy (kē = on the contrary)

Notice the final line:
"Ehara i te pōuri, he hari kē."
Not sadness — actually joy. Kē creates the surprise twist. The tears at the end are tears of happiness, not grief.

*Tip: Ehara i te [expected thing] — he [actual thing] kē. This is the te reo pattern for a plot twist or surprise revelation. "It was not what you expected — it was actually this." Master this and your stories will always have a satisfying ending.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Build a narrative**

Together we build a full narrative using the three-part structure.

- BEGINNING: I tērā wā… + character + setting. Who? Where? What is the feeling?
- MIDDLE TENSION: Ka rongo ia i… + Ka ohorere ia. + Ka haere ia ki reira.
- CRISIS: Ka kite ia i… — describe what they found. Use body vocabulary and E…ana.
- END: Ka [resolution]. Ka [reflection]. Ehara i te [expected] — he [actual] kē. Ka huri.

_Discussion prompt: Ka tangi a Hine — ehara i te pōuri, he hari kē. The same action (crying) can mean opposite things. The kē creates the twist that reveals the true meaning. What does this teach us about the relationship between language and emotion? Can one word change everything?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Your pūrākau**

Write a complete original narrative in te reo Māori.

- Write your BEGINNING (3 sentences): I tērā wā + character + setting.
- Write your MIDDLE (4 sentences): rising action + Ka ohorere moment + crisis.
- Write your END (3 sentences): resolution + reflection + Ka huri.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full pūrākau (12 sentences minimum). Use: oral storytelling techniques (repetition, short Ka sentences, personification), all tense markers appropriately, body vocabulary, at least one relative clause (ai), and a kē twist ending.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero pūrākau — Storytelling conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Oral narrative · AO 2.5: Storytelling vocabulary · AO 1.1: Audience and formal register*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Oral narrative; AO 1.1 — Formal audience language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Stories as conversation**

In te ao Māori, stories are not just for entertainment — they teach, they connect, they heal. Today we tell stories to each other and talk ABOUT stories — discussing what they mean, what they teach, and what they connect us to.

> **A:** He aha tō kōrero pūrākau pai rawa atu?
> **B:** Ko tōku pūrākau pai rawa atu ko te kōrero mō Māui — ko ia tētahi rangatira o ngā pūrākau Māori.
> **A:** He aha tā tērā pūrākau e ako ana i a koe?
> **B:** Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te māia o te tangata — ahakoa he pakiaka noa ia, i toa ia i ngā mea tino nui.
> **A:** Ka pai tāu whakaaro. He aha tō pūrākau ake?
> **B:** I ngā rā o mua, ko tōku [kuia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kuia) te [kaipūrākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=kaip%C5%ABr%C4%81kau) o tō mātou whānau. Ka noho mātou ā te pō, ka kōrero ia i ngā kōrero o ō mātou [tīpuna](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABpuna).
> **A:** He taonga tino nui tērā. Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero.

New storytelling conversation vocabulary:
**kaipūrākau** — storyteller (kai- + pūrākau)
**rangatira o ngā pūrākau** — a chief of stories / a hero of legend
**pakiaka noa** — just an ordinary person / of humble origin
**tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te…** — what that story teaches is…
**ko tāu kōrero** — your words / your telling
**He aha tā tērā pūrākau e ako ana i a koe?**
What does that story teach you?
**Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te [lesson].**
What that story teaches is [lesson].

*Tip: Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te [lesson] — what that story teaches is [lesson]. This is the te reo pattern for discussing the moral or meaning of a story. Learn it and you can discuss any pūrākau.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Storytelling conversation**

Build a conversation about a story you know.

- I ask: He aha tō kōrero pūrākau pai rawa atu? Name a story and say why: Ko tōku pūrākau pai rawa atu ko… nō te mea…
- I ask: He aha tā tērā pūrākau e ako ana i a koe? Answer: Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te…
- I ask: He aha tō pūrākau ake? Do you have your own family story? Share a detail.
- I say: Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero. You respond with Tēnā koe hoki.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te māia o te tangata — what that story teaches is the bravery of a person. Every great story teaches something through action, not through statement. What story from your own life — something that actually happened to you — would make a good pūrākau? What would it teach?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He kōrero pūrākau**

Tell and discuss stories in te reo Māori.

- Name your favourite story (from any culture) and say why: Ko tōku pūrākau pai rawa atu ko… nō te mea…
- Write what it teaches: Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te…
- Describe a kaipūrākau from your own life — someone who told you stories.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a storytelling conversation (10 lines) with a partner — introduce a story, discuss what it teaches, share a memory of being told a story, and close with Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "I tērā wā, i tērā wā…" What is the purpose of this opening phrase?
  - It gives the exact date of the story 
  - Repetition signals entry into story time — leaving ordinary time ✅
  - It means "once upon a time" and nothing more 
  - It is a formal greeting before a story 
- What is the traditional te reo Māori story ending?
  - Nō reira ka mutu. 
  - Tērā ia te kōrero. 
  - Ka huri. ✅
  - Ko te mutunga. 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko ia te tangata i āwhina ai i a mātou." What does ai indicate?
  - Past tense 
  - The end of a relative clause — pointing back to describe "te tangata" ✅
  - Future intention 
  - A question marker 
- "I haere au ki te kura hei ako i te reo Māori." What does hei indicate here?
  - Past tense of going 
  - A purpose clause — in order to learn ✅
  - A negative 
  - A question 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ehara i te pōuri, he hari kē." What does kē signal in this sentence?
  - The feeling is past 
  - The feeling is very strong 
  - Contrary to expectation — it was actually joy, not sadness ✅
  - The speaker is confused 
- What are the three parts of a narrative structure in te reo?
  - Introduction, body, conclusion 
  - Beginning (scene), middle (tension/crisis), end (resolution/reflection) ✅
  - Problem, solution, evaluation 
  - Past, present, future 

**Thursday questions**

- What is a "kaipūrākau"?
  - a type of story 
  - a storyteller (kai- + pūrākau) ✅
  - a traditional opening phrase 
  - a formal greeting 
- "Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te māia o te tangata." What does this sentence discuss?
  - The title of the story 
  - The author of the story 
  - What the story teaches — the bravery of a person ✅
  - How long the story is 

---

### Week 3

#### Monday: He kōrero māpuna — Figurative language and imagery

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Figurative language · AO 2.3: Extended expressive communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Figurative language; AO 2.3 — Imagery & metaphor

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Language that paints pictures**

Te reo Māori is extraordinarily rich in figurative language — metaphor, simile, and imagery are woven into everyday speech. Today we explore the most beautiful and most useful figures of speech in te reo Māori.

> **SIMILE (āhua… ana):**
> **E oma ana ia āhua te [whatitiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whatitiri).**
> She runs like thunder.
> 
> **METAPHOR (Ko [X] ko [Y]):**
> **Ko te reo he [aho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aho) e [whiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whiri) ana i ngā tāngata.**
> Language is a cord that weaves people together.
> 
> **PERSONIFICATION:**
> **Ka tangi ngā [maunga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=maunga) i ngā ua.**
> The mountains weep in the rain.
> 
> **IMAGERY:**
> **He rau [kōpura](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Dpura) tōna kanohi i tōna kata.**
> Her face was a golden leaf when she laughed. (kōpura = gold/copper)

Te reo Māori figures of speech:

**Simile** uses āhua (like/as) or me he (like a):
"E oma ana ia āhua te whatitiri." — runs like thunder
"Ka māia ia me he maunga." — brave like a mountain

**Metaphor** uses Ko:
"Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata."
Language is a cord that weaves people together.

**Personification** — giving nature human actions:
"Ka tangi ngā maunga." — The mountains weep.
"Ka karanga te moana." — The sea calls.

New vocabulary:
**aho** — cord / string / thread
**whiri** — to weave / plait / braid
**kōpura** — gold / copper (colour)
**māia** — brave (you know this)
**rau kōpura** — golden leaf

*Tip: Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata — language is a cord that weaves people together. This is one of the most beautiful metaphors in te reo Māori. Learn it cold. Use it when you want to explain why language matters.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Figurative sentences**

Build similes, metaphors, and personification in te reo Māori.

- Simile: "She works like the wind." → **E mahi ana ia āhua te hau.** Build your own simile about a classmate.
- Metaphor: "He is a pillar of the community." → **Ko ia he pou o te hapori.** Build a Ko metaphor.
- Personification: give a natural element a human action. **Ka waiata te awa i ngā kōrero o tōna ara.**
- Write a simile + a metaphor about te reo Māori itself.

_Discussion prompt: Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata — language is a cord that weaves people together. What does it mean for language to WEAVE people? What happens to a community when the cord is cut? When has language created a connection for you?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Figurative language**

Use similes, metaphors, and personification in your writing.

- Write two similes using āhua or me he.
- Write two Ko metaphors about people, places, or language.
- Write two personification sentences using natural elements.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a paragraph (8 sentences) about your year of te reo Māori learning — using at least one simile, one metaphor, and one personification. Make it beautiful.

#### Tuesday: He kōrero whakaaro — Expressing opinions with sophistication

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended opinions · Grammar Level 1–2: Sophisticated opinion structures*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Sophisticated opinions; AO 2.5 — Evaluative language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Saying what you think, elegantly**

You have Ko tāku whakaaro (my thought), He pai ake (better), and debate language. Today we add the final layer of opinion expression — how to say what you think with nuance, qualification, and elegance.

> **STATING a strong opinion:**
> **[E ai ki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=E+ai+ki) āku whakaaro…** — According to my thinking…
> **Ko tōku [tūāhuatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%AB%C4%81huatanga) ko te…** — My position is that…
> 
> **QUALIFYING an opinion:**
> **I ētahi wā…** — Sometimes…
> **Ka mutu, ko tōku whakaaro…** — All things considered, my view is…
> **Ahakoa ōna āhuatanga…** — Despite its qualities…
> 
> **CONCEDING a point:**
> **He [tika](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tika) tāu ki tērā āhuatanga.** — You are right about that point.
> **Āe, he [pono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pono) tērā — engari rā…** — Yes, that is true — however…
> 
> **CLOSING a view:**
> **Nō reira, ko tōku whakaaro [mōtika](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C5%8Dtika) ko te…**
> Therefore, my considered view is…

Sophisticated opinion structures:

**E ai ki āku whakaaro** — more formal than Ko tāku whakaaro
**Ka mutu** — all things considered / when it is done
**mōtika** — correct / right / genuine
**Ko tōku whakaaro mōtika** — my considered/genuine view
**He pono tērā** — that is true
**Āe, he pono tērā — engari rā…**
Yes, that is true — however…

New words:
**mōtika** — correct / genuine / right
**Ka mutu** — all things considered / when all is said
**E ai ki** — according to
**tūāhuatanga** — position / characteristic

*Tip: Āe, he pono tērā — engari rā. "Yes, that is true — however." This is the most graceful way to concede a point before making your counter-argument. It shows you have listened. In te ao Māori, showing you have heard someone before you respond is a mark of respect.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Sophisticated opinions**

Express opinions with nuance and elegance.

- Strong position: E ai ki āku whakaaro, ko te reo Māori te taonga nui rawa atu o Aotearoa.
- Qualify: Ka mutu, ko tōku whakaaro… + your opinion on a topic with some nuance.
- Concede + counter: Āe, he pono tērā… engari rā… + your counter-argument.
- Close: Nō reira, ko tōku whakaaro mōtika ko te… + your final position.

_Discussion prompt: Āe, he pono tērā — engari rā. When you say "yes, that is true" before arguing your point, you show you have genuinely listened. This is the opposite of winning an argument — it is trying to reach understanding. What would happen if all debates worked this way?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Sophisticated opinion writing**

Write a sophisticated opinion piece in te reo Māori.

- State position: E ai ki āku whakaaro, ko [topic] te [opinion].
- Qualify: Ka mutu, i ētahi wā… (acknowledge complexity).
- Concede and counter: Āe, he pono tērā — engari rā…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full opinion piece (8 sentences) about te reo Māori, kaitiakitanga, or education. Use: E ai ki āku whakaaro, Ka mutu, Āe he pono tērā engari rā, Ko tōku whakaaro mōtika, and Ko te mutunga.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Year-long structure review

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs Terms 1–4 · All grammar structures consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Full year review; AO 2.5 — Complete vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The complete four-term picture**

This is Term 4 Week 3. Today we put the entire year on the table — every structure, every domain, every AO — and see the full picture of what you have built.

> **EVERYTHING you can now do:**
> 
> GREET & FAREWELL at every level of formality
> INTRODUCE yourself with a full pepeha
> DESCRIBE people, places, feelings, weather, nature, arts
> RECOUNT past events with I and Ka
> NARRATE stories with Ka and oral techniques
> EXPRESS ongoing actions with E…ana and Kua
> COMMIT to future actions with māku e and Ka taea
> NEGATE in present and past with Kāore
> ARGUE and persuade with taunakitanga and discourse markers
> EXPLAIN meanings with Ko te tikanga o…
> EVALUATE learning with ngāwari/uaua and Ka taea
> DISCUSS culture, art, environment, science, rights
> PERFORM a pepeha, a pōhiri, a whaikōrero

The year in one sentence:

**"Ko ahau ko [name] — he ākonga māia, he kaitiaki o tēnei reo rangatira; e ako tonu ana au, kua tipu tōku reo, ā ka kōrero māmā ake au i ia rā, i ia rā — nō reira, ko tōku moemoeā: ka toitū tēnei reo tapu mō ōku uri ā āpōpō."**

→ Ko identity
→ He description ×2
→ E…ana progressive
→ Kua perfect
→ Ka narrative
→ Nō reira conclusion
→ Ko moemoeā dream
→ Ka toitū endurance
→ Relative: mō ōku uri ā āpōpō (for my future descendants)

That is one year of daily practice in one sentence.

*Tip: Read that sentence aloud. Slowly. Every word of it is yours. You built this. Ka tino pai tāu mahi.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Year-long blitz**

The fastest comprehensive review of the year.

- PEPEHA: One student — full nine lines from memory. Class supports.
- STRUCTURES: Call out a structure — class gives an example sentence. Ko, He, Kei te, I, Ka, Kua, E…ana, Kāore, ahakoa, ai, hei…
- WHAKATAUKĪ: All four from Term 3 Week 8 — who can say them all?
- SHOWCASE: Each student says the single best te reo sentence they have ever produced.

_Discussion prompt: Ka tino pai tāu mahi — your work is genuinely, truly excellent. Not just good — tino good. You have earned this. What would your Week 1 Term 1 self think if they heard the sentence you just said?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Four-term audit**

Your final comprehensive vocabulary and structure audit.

- Write every te reo word you know in 5 minutes. Count them. Compare to Week 1.
- Write one sentence using each of the 14 structures from the I Do.
- Write your best sentence of the year — and why it is your best.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the year-summary sentence above in your own words — make every part true for you. Then write a second version that is even better.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero māmā — Spontaneous conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · AO 2.3: Spontaneous extended conversation*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Spontaneous conversation; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary in conversation

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The spontaneous conversation**

The highest goal of language learning is spontaneous conversation — talking freely, without a script, responding to what the other person actually says. Today we practise this. No models, no sentence frames — just conversation.

> **CONVERSATION STARTERS you know:**
> He pēhea tōu wiki? · He aha ngā mea hou? · Ko ēhea āu hiahia?
> He aha tō whakaaro mō…? · He aha tāu e tūmanako ana?
> Ko wai tōu hoa pai rawa atu? · He aha tā tērā pūrākau e ako ana?
> 
> **CONVERSATION EXTENDERS:**
> **Ā nā?** — And then? / What happened next?
> **Pērā?** — Like that? / Really?
> **He aha tāu e kī ana?** — What do you mean?
> **Ka pai tāu kōrero — heoi anō…** — Good point — however…
> **Nā wai rā…** — Eventually… / As time went on…
> **Ā koia!** — Exactly! / That's it!

Tools for keeping a conversation going:

**Ā nā?** — the simplest conversation extender. "And then?" invites the other person to continue.

**Pērā?** — "Like that?" shows you are surprised and engaged.

**Ā koia!** — "Exactly!" shows enthusiastic agreement. Use it to confirm and encourage.

**Nā wai rā…** — "Eventually / as time went on…" moves a story forward in time.

**He aha tāu e kī ana?** — "What do you mean?" — asking for clarification. This is one of the most important phrases in any language.

New word:
**Ā koia!** — Exactly! That's it! (an emphatic agreement)
**Nā wai rā** — Eventually / Over time / As things progressed

*Tip: Ā nā? is the single most useful conversation extender in any language. "And then?" makes the other person feel listened to and invites them to continue. Use it constantly.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Spontaneous conversation practice**

Pairs. Teacher gives a starting topic — students talk freely. No scripts.

- Topic 1: He pēhea tōu wiki? Start — extend with Ā nā? — agree with Ā koia! — disagree with Āe, he pono tērā engari rā…
- Topic 2: He aha tōu moemoeā mō te reo Māori? Start — ask He aha tāu e kī ana? for clarification.
- Topic 3: He aha te pūrākau pai rawa atu ki a koe? Start — extend with Nā wai rā…
- Topic 4: He aha tō whakaaro mō tō kura? Start — use Pērā? for surprise.

_Discussion prompt: He aha tāu e kī ana? — What do you mean? This question shows that you are genuinely trying to understand, not just waiting for your turn to speak. In te ao Māori, listening and understanding before responding is a value. How would conversations change if everyone used this question more?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Free conversation**

Have a fully spontaneous conversation with a partner in te reo Māori — 5 minutes, no script, any topic.

- Choose your topic together — something you both genuinely care about.
- Use at least three conversation extenders: Ā nā, Pērā, Ā koia, He aha tāu e kī ana.
- Try to include at least one whakataukī or figurative expression naturally.
- ✦ Bonus: Record your conversation (written transcript or voice note). After, count how many different structures and vocabulary domains you used naturally. This is evidence of your fluency.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "E oma ana ia āhua te whatitiri." What figure of speech is this?
  - metaphor 
  - personification 
  - simile ✅
  - alliteration 
- "Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata." What does aho mean?
  - river 
  - cord / string / thread ✅
  - mountain 
  - ancestor 

**Tuesday questions**

- "E ai ki āku whakaaro…" What does this phrase signal?
  - A question is coming 
  - A formal greeting 
  - The speaker is about to state their opinion ✅
  - A past event 
- "Āe, he pono tērā — engari rā…" What is the speaker doing?
  - Agreeing completely 
  - Conceding a point graciously before making a counter-argument ✅
  - Ending the discussion 
  - Asking for clarification 

**Wednesday questions**

- Which sentence correctly uses the relative clause marker ai?
  - Ko ia te tangata ai āwhina i a mātou 
  - Ko ia te tangata i āwhina ai i a mātou ✅
  - Ko ia te tangata āwhina ai 
  - I āwhina ai ia ko ia te tangata 
- "Ka tino pai tāu mahi." What does tino add to this sentence?
  - It makes the sentence past tense 
  - It makes the praise genuinely / truly excellent — more emphatic ✅
  - It makes it a question 
  - It negates the sentence 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ā koia!" What does this expression mean?
  - And then? 
  - What do you mean? 
  - Exactly! / That's it! ✅
  - Eventually 
- "He aha tāu e kī ana?" — What is the speaker doing?
  - Agreeing 
  - Asking for clarification — what do you mean? ✅
  - Ending the conversation 
  - Giving an opinion 

---

### Week 4

#### Monday: He kōrero mō āpōpō — Future, plans, and aspirations

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.4: Future time · Grammar Level 1–2: Future tense mastery*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Future & aspirations; AO 2.3 — Extended planning language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about the future with confidence**

You have hiahia, māku e, Ka taea, and Ka for future narrative. Today we consolidate the full future toolkit and add the most aspirational language — talking about what you hope for, dream of, and intend to make happen.

> **IMMEDIATE FUTURE (Ka):**
> **Ka haere au āpōpō.** — I will go tomorrow.
> 
> **INTENTION (māku/māna e):**
> **Māku e tiaki i tēnei reo.** — I will protect this language.
> 
> **HOPE (tūmanako):**
> **Kei te [tūmanako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C5%ABmanako) au ka [future hope].**
> I hope that [future hope] will happen.
> 
> **DREAM (moemoeā):**
> **Ko tōku [moemoeā](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=moemoe%C4%81): ka [dream].**
> My dream is: that [dream] will happen.
> 
> **ASPIRATION (wawata):**
> **Ko tōku [wawata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wawata): ka [aspiration].**
> My aspiration is: [aspiration].

The future language spectrum:

IMMEDIATE → Ka [verb] āpōpō (happening soon)
INTENTION → Māku e [verb] (I commit to this)
HOPE → Kei te tūmanako au ka… (I hope this happens)
DREAM → Ko tōku moemoeā: ka… (deep personal dream)
ASPIRATION → Ko tōku wawata: ka… (highest aspiration)

**wawata** — aspiration / deepest longing / what you reach for
**wawata** is stronger than moemoeā — it is not just a dream but a deep reaching-toward

New word:
**wawata** — deep aspiration / yearning
**angitu** — success / achievement
**Ka angitu koe.** — You will succeed.

*Tip: Moemoeā = dream (what you hope for in sleep and waking). Wawata = what you reach toward with your whole being — the deepest aspiration. Use moemoeā for personal dreams, wawata for the things that define your life direction.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Future language spectrum**

Express the same idea at different levels of future commitment.

- Immediate Ka: **Ka ako au i te reo Māori āpōpō.** Now make it an intention: **Māku e ako i te reo Māori.**
- Hope: **Kei te tūmanako au ka [something you hope will happen to your class next year].**
- Dream: **Ko tōku moemoeā: ka kōrero tōku whānau katoa i te reo Māori.**
- Aspiration: **Ko tōku [wawata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wawata): ka toitū tēnei reo tapu mō ōku uri ā āpōpō.**

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku wawata — my deepest aspiration. The word wawata contains the idea of reaching outward and upward. In te ao Māori, a wawata is not just a personal desire — it often connects to the wellbeing of others. What is YOUR wawata? Does it only affect you — or does it reach further?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Future and aspiration language**

Write about your future using all five levels of the future language spectrum.

- Write a Ka sentence about something happening soon.
- Write a māku e commitment about something you will do.
- Write a tūmanako hope about something you hope will happen.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full future statement (8 sentences) — immediate plans, intentions, hopes, a moemoeā, and a wawata. Make the wawata the most ambitious and true thing you say. Close with Ka angitu koe — You will succeed.

#### Tuesday: Ngā kupu hou whakamutunga — Final new vocabulary for the year

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Advanced vocabulary · AO 2.3: Extended communication · All AOs*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Advanced vocabulary; AO 2.3 — Full communication

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The final vocabulary additions**

These are the last new vocabulary words of the year — carefully chosen to round out your te reo Māori and give you a few more powerful tools for the end-of-year assessment and beyond.

> **For describing growth and change:**
> **tipu** — grow (you know this) · **[huri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=huri)** — change / turn · **[hou](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hou)** — new
> **Ka hou tōku whakaaro.** — My thinking has become new / I think differently.
> 
> **For describing connection:**
> **[honohono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=honohono)** — to connect / link
> **Ka honohono tēnei reo i ngā tāngata.**
> This language connects people.
> 
> **For describing achievement:**
> **[angitu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=angitu)** — success · **[toa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=toa)** — win/champion · **[eke](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=eke)** — rise/climb/score
> **Ka [angitu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=angitu) koe i āu mahi.** — You will succeed in your work.
> 
> **For describing legacy:**
> **[tuku iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuku+iho)** — passed down / handed down (generations) / handed down (generations)
> **taonga [tuku iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuku+iho)** — a treasure handed down
> **He taonga tuku iho tēnei reo.** — This language is a treasure handed down.

Final vocabulary — how each word connects to the year:

**honohono** — connects back to aho (cord) and whiri (weave). Language connects.
**angitu** — connects back to Ka taea (able) and toa (champion). You have succeeded.
**tuku iho** — connects back to uri (descendants) and tīpuna (ancestors). The chain of generations.
**Ka hou tōku whakaaro** — connects back to whakaaro (thought) and tipu (grow). Your thinking has grown new.

These are not random new words — each one closes a circle from earlier in the year.

New compound:
**honohono** = hono (join) + hono (repeated for intensity) = to deeply connect

*Tip: He taonga tuku iho tēnei reo — this language is a treasure passed down. You have heard taonga throughout the year. You have heard tuku iho in Term 3. Now they come together in your final week. The phrase was always waiting for you at the end of the year.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final vocabulary in action**

Use each new word in a sentence that connects it back to the year.

- Honohono: **Ka honohono tēnei reo i ōku hoa, i ōku tīpuna, i ōku uri katoa.** Who does it connect?
- Angitu: **Ka angitu koe — kua tino pai tāu mahi i tēnei tau.** What has been your angitu?
- Tuku iho: **He taonga tuku iho tēnei reo — nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri.**
- Hou: **Ka hou tōku whakaaro mō te reo Māori i tēnei tau.** How has your thinking changed?

_Discussion prompt: Nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri — from my ancestors to me, and from me to my descendants. You are in the middle of this chain. What did your ancestors give you through language? What will you give your descendants?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Final vocabulary sentences**

Use the final year vocabulary in sentences that are true and meaningful.

- Write a honohono sentence about what te reo Māori connects you to.
- Write an angitu sentence about something you have achieved this year.
- Write a taonga tuku iho sentence about the language.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a four-sentence statement about your year of te reo Māori — using honohono, angitu, tuku iho, and Ka hou tōku whakaaro. Make every word count.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — The year in sentences

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · All sentence types · Full year synthesis*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Year synthesis; AO 2.5 — Full expression

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Writing the year**

Today we do something special — we write about the whole year. Not as a summary, but as a piece of genuine te reo expression that captures what this year of language learning has been and meant.

> **A year in nine sentences — one structure per term/topic:**
> 
> Ko ahau ko [name] — he ākonga, he kaitiaki, he tangata o tēnei ao. (Ko · He)
> I [tīmata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABmata) au i tētahi kupu inanahi — ināianei, kua tipu tōku reo ki tētahi mea hou. (I · Kua)
> E ako tonu ana au — e huri ana tōku whakaaro i ia rā, i ia rā. (E…ana ×2)
> Ka taea e au te mihi, ka taea e au te tuhituhi, ka taea e au te whakaaro i te reo Māori. (Ka taea ×3)
> Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i a au ki ngā tīpuna, ki ngā uri, ki ngā hoa o tōku ao. (Ko metaphor · ai)
> Ahakoa he uauatanga, i mahi tonu au — nō te mea ko tōku moemoeā tōku ara. (Ahakoa · Nō te mea · Ko)
> He taonga [tuku iho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tuku+iho) tēnei reo — māku e tiaki, māku e [whakaahua](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakaahua) ake. (He · māku e ×2)
> Nō reira, ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo rangatira mō āpōpō ō ngā uri. (Nō reira · Ko wawata · Ka)
> Ka angitu koe. Ka angitu mātou katoa. Ka huri. (Ka ×3 · Ka huri)

Count the structures in those nine sentences:
Ko (×5), He (×4), I (past), Kua (perfect), E…ana (×2), Ka taea (×3), ai (relative), Ahakoa, Nō te mea, māku e (×2), Ko wawata, Ka (×4), Ka huri

That is the whole year in nine sentences. You can write every one of them.

Notice the final line:
"Ka angitu koe. Ka angitu mātou katoa. Ka huri."
You will succeed. We will all succeed. The telling turns.

Even the ending is a pūrākau technique — Ka huri closes the year the way it closes a story.

*Tip: Ka huri — the telling turns. This traditional story ending can also close a year. The year was a story. It had a beginning, a middle, and an end. Ka huri.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Write the year together**

Class writes one shared "year in sentences" — each student contributes one sentence.

- Student 1: A Ko identity sentence about who we are as a class.
- Student 2: A Kua sentence about what has been completed this year.
- Student 3: An E…ana sentence about what is still ongoing in our learning.
- Continue until every student has contributed. Close together: Ka angitu tātou katoa. Ka huri.

_Discussion prompt: Ka angitu koe. Ka angitu mātou katoa. Ka huri. Success is named for you individually, then for all of us together, then the year turns. Why does the order matter — you first, then all of us? What does it say about how achievement in te ao Māori is understood?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The year in your sentences**

Write your own "year in sentences" — nine sentences, one per major structure.

- Write sentences 1–3: Ko identity, Kua perfect, E…ana progressive.
- Write sentences 4–6: Ka taea ability, Ko metaphor with ai, Ahakoa + nō te mea.
- Write sentences 7–9: māku e commitment, Ko wawata, Ka huri.
- ✦ Bonus: Refine all nine until each one is truly beautiful and true. Read the whole passage aloud. This is your year. Own it.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero whakaaro — A philosophical conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended sophisticated conversation · All AOs*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Philosophical conversation; AO 2.5 — Sophisticated vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Talking about big ideas**

The most advanced thing you can do in a language is discuss abstract ideas — concepts like time, identity, belonging, language, and legacy. Today we have that conversation in te reo Māori.

> **A:** He aha te tikanga o te reo ki a koe ināianei, i tērā tau ake?
> **B:** I tērā tau, he mea ngaro noa iho te reo ki a au. Ināianei, ko ia tōku ara, tōku [hono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hono) ki ngā tīpuna.
> **A:** Ka pai tērā. He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe?
> **B:** I homai mai e ia ki a au he ara ki te ao hou — arā, ki ngā kupu o ōku tīpuna, ki ngā [mōhiotanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C5%8Dhiotanga) hou, ki ōku hoa hou.
> **A:** E ai ki āku whakaaro, ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata katoa. He tika tāu whakaaro?
> **B:** Āe, he tika — waihoki, ko te reo he [māramatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ramatanga) ki te ao mārama. Ka [honohono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=honohono) ia i te ao o nāianei ki te ao o ōku tīpuna.
> **A:** Ā koia! Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino [hōhonu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=h%C5%8Dhonu).
> **B:** Tēnā koe hoki.

New philosophical vocabulary:
**he mea ngaro noa iho** — just an invisible/lost thing (ngaro = invisible/lost, noa iho = just/merely)
**ara ki te ao hou** — a path to the new world
**māramatanga ki te ao mārama** — enlightenment to the world of light
**ao o nāianei** — the world of today (nāianei = nowadays)
**tino hōhonu** — very deep / profound (hōhonu = deep)
**Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu.**
Thank you for your very profound words.
**he aho e whiri ana** — a cord that weaves (from Week 3!)

*Tip: Ko te reo he māramatanga ki te ao mārama — the language is an enlightenment to the world of light. This brings together māramatanga (understanding/enlightenment) and ao mārama (world of light — both the realm of the living and the realm of clarity). Language as light.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Philosophical conversation**

Have a conversation about language, identity, and meaning.

- I ask: He aha te tikanga o te reo ki a koe ināianei? Answer with a metaphor or deep statement.
- I ask: He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe? Answer: I homai mai e ia ki a au he…
- I make a claim: Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata. You agree or add: Āe, waihoki…
- I close: Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu. You respond: Tēnā koe hoki.

_Discussion prompt: Ko te reo he māramatanga ki te ao mārama. Language as light, as enlightenment, as access to a brighter world. What does language actually give you access to that you would not have without it? Think specifically — what can you understand, feel, or know in te reo Māori that you cannot in English?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Philosophical writing**

Write your own philosophical reflection on language and identity.

- Answer: He aha te tikanga o te reo ki a koe? (Not "te reo Māori is important" — something deeper.)
- Write a metaphor: Ko te reo he [metaphor] — with an e…ana relative clause.
- Write what language has given you: I homai mai e ia ki a au he…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full philosophical reflection (8 sentences) — what language means to you, what it has given you, a whakataukī, a metaphor, a wawata, and close with Ko te reo he māramatanga ki te ao mārama — ko tērā tōku whakapono.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- What is the difference between moemoeā and wawata?
  - They mean exactly the same thing 
  - Moemoeā is a dream; wawata is a deeper aspiration/yearning — what you reach toward with your whole being ✅
  - Moemoeā is formal; wawata is casual 
  - Wawata is about the past; moemoeā is about the future 
- "Ka angitu koe." What does angitu mean?
  - you will leave 
  - you will succeed / achieve ✅
  - you will speak 
  - you will learn 

**Tuesday questions**

- "He taonga tuku iho tēnei reo." What does tuku iho mean?
  - recently created 
  - belonging to one person 
  - passed down through generations ✅
  - difficult to learn 
- What does "honohono" mean?
  - to weave clothing 
  - to separate / divide 
  - to deeply connect / link ✅
  - to speak formally 

**Wednesday questions**

- The passage ends with "Ka huri." In what other context is this phrase used?
  - As a formal greeting 
  - As a weather expression 
  - As the traditional close of an oral story (pūrākau) ✅
  - As a way to say goodbye 
- "Ko ia tōku ara, tōku hono ki ngā tīpuna." What two things is the language being called?
  - a path and a cord of connection ✅
  - a treasure and a dream 
  - a challenge and a reward 
  - a light and a gift 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ko te reo he māramatanga ki te ao mārama." What does māramatanga mean?
  - darkness / confusion 
  - difficulty / challenge 
  - enlightenment / understanding / clarity ✅
  - silence 
- "Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu." What does hōhonu mean?
  - short 
  - confusing 
  - loud 
  - deep / profound ✅

---

### Week 5

#### Monday: He kōrero mō ōku ara ako — My learning pathways

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended reflection · All AOs consolidated · Whakaaro ako (metacognition)*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Learning reflection; AO 2.5 — Metacognitive vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Thinking about your thinking**

In this second-to-last week, we turn inward — thinking about HOW we have learned, what strategies worked, and what kind of learner we have become. This is metacognition — thinking about thinking — expressed in te reo Māori.

> **He aha tāu [ara ako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ara+ako)?** — What is your learning strategy/pathway?
> 
> **Ko tōku ara ako ko te [strategy].**
> My learning strategy is [strategy].
> 
> **Ko ngā ara ako pai ki a au ko ēnei:**
> he kōrero, he [whakarongo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakarongo), he tuhituhi, he mahi tahi.
> The learning strategies that work for me are: speaking, listening, writing, working together.
> 
> **I tipu ōku ara ako i tēnei tau.**
> My learning strategies grew this year.
> 
> **Ka pai ake tōku [ako](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ako) ina [condition].**
> I learn better when [condition].

Metacognitive vocabulary:
**ara ako** — learning pathway / learning strategy
**whakaaro hōhonu** — deep thinking / reflection
**Ka pai ake tōku ako ina kōrero ana au.**
I learn better when I speak.

**He mōhiotanga ōku e pā ana ki te reo Māori.**
I have knowledge relating to te reo Māori.

**Kua tino pai ake tōku mōhio i…**
My understanding of [X] has become much better.

**I tipu ōku ara ako i tēnei tau nō te mea…**
My learning strategies grew this year because…

New words:
**metacognition (whakaaro mō te whakaaro)**
**mōhiotanga** — knowledge / understanding
**e pā ana ki** — relating to / concerning

*Tip: Ka pai ake tōku ako ina [condition] ana au. "I learn better when I [do this]." This is one of the most useful self-knowledge sentences in any language. Knowing HOW you learn best is as important as what you learn.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Learning reflection**

Build sentences about how you learn best.

- I ask: He aha tāu ara ako pai rawa atu? Answer: Ko tōku ara ako pai rawa atu ko te [strategy].
- I ask: Ka pai ake tōu ako ina aha? Answer: Ka pai ake tōku ako ina [condition].
- State growth: **Kua tino pai ake tōku mōhio i ngā hanganga o te reo Māori i tēnei tau.**
- State future strategy: **Māku e [strategy] i muri ake nei hei tiaki i tōku reo.**

_Discussion prompt: Ka pai ake tōku ako ina kōrero ana au — I learn better when I speak. Does this describe you? What IS your best learning strategy? If you could tell next year's te reo students one thing about how to learn it, what would you tell them?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Learning reflection writing**

Write a reflection on your learning strategies and growth.

- Write your best learning strategy: Ko tōku ara ako pai rawa atu ko te…
- Write when you learn best: Ka pai ake tōku ako ina…
- Write your growth: Kua tino pai ake tōku mōhio i… i tēnei tau.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full learning reflection (8 sentences) — your strategies, your growth, what was hard, what worked, what you wish you had done differently, and your advice to a future learner.

#### Tuesday: He hononga ki te ao — Connections to the world

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Connection to land and people · AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 2.3: Extended reflection*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Connections to world; AO 2.1 — Relationships & community

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What te reo Māori has connected you to**

Te reo Māori is not just a communication tool — it is a connector. Today we reflect on everything this language has connected you to across the year: people, places, ancestors, community, nature, and the future.

> **Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo:**
> These are the connections this language has made:
> 
> **Ko ōku [hoa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=hoa) hou** — my new friends
> **Ko ōku kaiako** — my teachers
> **Ko ngā [tīpuna](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=t%C4%ABpuna) o Aotearoa** — the ancestors of New Zealand
> **Ko ngā [uri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=uri) ō āpōpō** — the descendants of tomorrow
> **Ko te taiao o Aotearoa** — the natural world of New Zealand
> **Ko ngā [tikanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tikanga) o tōku ao** — the customs of my world
> 
> **Ka [honohono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=honohono) tēnei reo i ōku whakaaro, i ōku ngākau, i ōku ara katoa.**
> This language connects my thoughts, my heart, and all my pathways.

Reflecting on connections:

**Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo:**
These are the connections built by this language:
(hanga = build/make, ai = relative marker, e = by — passive)

**I honohono tēnei reo i a au ki…**
This language connected me to…

**Ka honohono tonu tēnei reo i a au ki ngā tāngata o tēnei ao.**
This language will continue to connect me to the people of this world.

New phrase:
**i hanga ai** — that was/has been built (ai = relative past)
**Ko ērā ngā [things] i hanga ai e tēnei reo.**
Those are the [things] built by this language.

*Tip: Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo — those are the connections built by this language. The passive (i hanga ai e) puts the language as the agent — it is the one that builds the connections. Language does something to you; you do not just use it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Connection reflection**

Reflect on what te reo Māori has connected you to.

- Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo: + Ko ēnei list of three connections.
- I honohono tēnei reo i a au ki [specific connection] nō te mea…
- Ka honohono tonu tēnei reo i a au ki [who/what] ā tōna wā.
- Close: Ko tōku wawata: ka honohono tēnei reo i ngā tāngata katoa o Aotearoa.

_Discussion prompt: Ka honohono tēnei reo i ōku whakaaro, i ōku ngākau, i ōku ara katoa — this language connects my thoughts, my heart, and all my pathways. Thoughts, heart, and pathways — three dimensions of a person. What would it mean for a language to connect all three? Has te reo Māori done that for you?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Connection writing**

Write about the connections te reo Māori has made for you.

- Write Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo + three specific connections.
- Write I honohono tēnei reo i a au ki [specific] nō te mea…
- Write what you hope it will connect you to in the future.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full connection reflection (8 sentences) — what te reo has connected you to (people, ancestors, nature, culture), how, why it matters, and your hope for those connections to grow.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — The end-of-year showcase

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · All sentence types · Full year showcase*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Year showcase; AO 2.5 — Full expression

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Your finest te reo Māori of the year**

This is the penultimate Wednesday lesson of the year. Today is the showcase — not a review, not an audit. A celebration. You write your finest te reo Māori. Not the most complex, not the longest — the most true, the most beautiful, the most you.

> The finest te reo sentences of the year — from any of you:
> 
> **"Ko tōku reo he ara māmā ki ngā tīpuna — ka huri noa tōku ao i ngā kupu o ōku mōrearea, ā ka mārama ake i ngā kupu o tōku reo."**
> (My language is a light path to the ancestors — my world just shifts in the words of my worries, and grows clearer in the words of my language.)
> 
> **"He ākonga noa iho ahau — engari ko tōku ngākau e tipu ana i ōku kupu i ia rā, i ia rā."**
> (I am just a learner — but my heart grows in my words day by day.)
> 
> **"Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū tōku whakapono, ka toitū hoki ahau."**
> (When the language endures, my belief endures, and I endure too.)

What makes these sentences the finest:

→ They use simple words in unexpected combinations
→ They reveal something true about the person
→ They use structures naturally, not as demonstrations
→ They would lose something if translated to English

"Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū tōku whakapono, ka toitū hoki ahau."

Three Ka toitū. The language endures. My belief endures. And then — ka toitū hoki AHAU. I endure too. The speaker is not just an observer of endurance — they are part of it. The final line makes the abstract personal.

This is what fluency looks like.

*Tip: The finest sentence you can write is not the one that uses the most structures — it is the one that uses exactly the right words to say exactly what you mean. Less is often more. Ka toitū ahau. That is enough.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Showcase sentences**

Write and share your finest sentences. Class listens — no feedback, just appreciation.

- Write in silence for 5 minutes — your finest sentence. No structure requirements.
- Write a second sentence that continues the first.
- Read both aloud to a partner. They listen. No comments — just presence.
- Class share: each person reads their best sentence. Everyone listens.

_Discussion prompt: Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū tōku whakapono, ka toitū hoki ahau. When the language endures, I endure. There is something radical about this claim — that YOUR survival is connected to the survival of te reo Māori. Do you believe it? In what sense might it be true?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The year showcase**

Write your finest te reo Māori of the year.

- Write your finest SENTENCE — not the most complex, the most true.
- Write your finest PARAGRAPH — three to five sentences, beautifully connected.
- Write your YEAR CLOSER — one sentence that closes your year of te reo Māori.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your complete "end of year te reo portfolio piece" — ten sentences that capture who you were, what you learned, what connected you, what you believe, and where you are going. Read it aloud to someone you trust.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He kōrero whakamutunga — The final conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · All six language modes · Full year assessed*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 2.3 — Full sophisticated conversation; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The full-year conversation**

This is the second-to-last lesson of the year. Today we see what a fully fluent Year 5-6 student can do in te reo Māori — the result of four terms of daily practice.

> **A:** Tēnā koe. Ko wai koe? He aha tō pepeha?
> **B:** [Full pepeha — all nine lines]
> **A:** Ka pai! He aha ngā pou o tōu ora?
> **B:** Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko tōku whānau, ko tōku reo, ko tōku taiao — ko ērā ngā mea e mau ana i a au.
> **A:** He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe i tēnei tau?
> **B:** I homai mai e ia ki a au he ara ki ngā tīpuna, he hononga ki ngā uri, ā he māramatanga ki tōku ake ao.
> **A:** He aha tō wawata mō tō reo Māori?
> **B:** Ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo tapu mō ōku uri — ā, ka honohono ia i ngā tāngata katoa o tēnei ao ātaahua.
> **A:** Ā koia! Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu.
> **B:** Tēnā koe hoki. Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe.

This conversation demonstrates every AO:
✓ AO 1.1: Tēnā koe + Ka nui ōku mihi
✓ AO 1.2: Full pepeha
✓ AO 1.4: Identity through maunga, awa, iwi, kura
✓ AO 2.1: Relationships (whānau, taiao)
✓ AO 2.2: Connection to land
✓ AO 2.3: Sophisticated extended kōrero
✓ AO 2.5: Pou, hononga, māramatanga, wawata, uri
✓ Grammar: Ko, He, E mau ana, I…ai (relative), māramatanga, Ka toitū, Ā koia

This is the standard. And you can do it.

*Tip: Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe — my greetings and thanks to you are great. This closes the conversation the way it began — with acknowledgement of the other person. In te ao Māori, beginning and ending with the other person is the mark of true manaaki.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final full practice**

Pairs — the complete full-year conversation. No script after the first line.

- Open formally + full pepeha from memory.
- Ko ngā pou o tōu ora ko… + He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe?
- He aha tō wawata mō tō reo Māori? + state your wawata.
- Close: Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu. Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe.

_Discussion prompt: Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe — my greetings and thanks to you are great. You are saying this to your conversation partner after they have spoken. But this year, who else do your mihi go to? Who has been part of your te reo journey? How would you say your mihi to them?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — The full-year conversation**

Have the full-year conversation with a partner — your finest te reo Māori.

- Full pepeha — all nine lines from memory.
- Ko ngā pou o tōu ora + He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe.
- He aha tō wawata + close with Ka nui ōku mihi.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the full conversation (14 lines minimum). Then annotate every structure and vocabulary domain you used. How many AOs can you find? How many grammar structures? This is your four-term record.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka pai ake tōku ako ina kōrero ana au." What does this sentence express?
  - I speak better than I learn 
  - I learn better when I speak ✅
  - Speaking is the only way to learn 
  - I prefer writing to speaking 
- What does "ara ako" mean?
  - learning result 
  - learning pathway / strategy ✅
  - learning language 
  - learning achievement 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko ērā ngā hononga i hanga ai e tēnei reo." What does "i hanga ai e tēnei reo" mean?
  - that I built through hard work 
  - that this language built / made (passive) ✅
  - that were destroyed by time 
  - that are yet to be made 
- What does "honohono" express in the context of language?
  - to separate people by language 
  - to deeply connect people through language ✅
  - to translate between languages 
  - to forget a language 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū tōku whakapono, ka toitū hoki ahau." What makes the final clause powerful?
  - It uses the most complex grammar 
  - It makes the abstract personal — the speaker themselves endures ✅
  - It is the longest clause 
  - It uses a whakataukī 
- What is the key difference between the finest sentence and the most complex sentence?
  - The finest sentence is always the longest 
  - The finest sentence uses the most vocabulary 
  - The finest sentence says exactly what you mean — truth over complexity ✅
  - The finest sentence always uses Kua 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe." When is this phrase used?
  - As an opening greeting only 
  - To express great gratitude and acknowledgement — often at the close of a conversation ✅
  - As a farewell when you are angry 
  - Only in formal whaikōrero 
- A full-year te reo conversation at Term 4 Week 5 level should include which of these?
  - Only greetings and farewells 
  - Only past tense sentences 
  - Full pepeha, sophisticated vocabulary, wawata, and at least six different grammar structures ✅
  - Only simple present tense sentences
