### Week 6

#### Monday: Arotake — Full year vocabulary review and celebration

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · Complete year review*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Full year greetings; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Everything you know: the full year celebration**

This is the final teaching week. Today we celebrate the full year — everything you have learned, everything you can do, and everything you carry forward. This is not a review drill. It is a celebration of mastery.

> Four terms. Here is the full picture:
> 
> **TERM 1 (Tūāhuatanga):** Ko · He · Kei te · Numbers 1–10 · greetings · food · places · wants · basic conversation
> 
> **TERM 2 (Kia Tipu):** I · Ka · Kāore · All 16 pronouns · Numbers 1–100 · dates/times · colours · comparatives · weather · seasons · emotions · character
> 
> **TERM 3 (Kia Rea):** Body · E…ana · Kua · māku e · Ka taea · pepeha · marae · haka · whakataukī · kaitiakitanga · science · formal/informal · argument
> 
> **TERM 4 (Kia Mārama):** Full greeting system · advanced cohesion · oral storytelling · relative clauses (ai) · purpose clauses (hei) · narrative structure · figurative language · philosophical conversation · wawata · Ka huri

What you can do that most adults in New Zealand cannot:

→ Deliver a full pepeha from memory
→ Explain a whakataukī and its deeper meaning
→ Have a philosophical conversation about language and identity in te reo
→ Write a narrative with traditional storytelling techniques
→ Argue a position with taunakitanga and discourse markers
→ Move fluently between six tense markers
→ Use all 16 personal pronouns correctly
→ Discuss kaitiakitanga, arts, community, and the natural world

You did this in four terms of daily 20-minute warm-ups. That is approximately 800 minutes — just over 13 hours of instruction.

Ka tino pai tāu mahi.

*Tip: Ka tino pai tāu mahi — your work is genuinely, truly excellent. This is the right closing phrase for the year. Not just "ka pai" — tino pai. You have earned every word of it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Full year celebration blitz**

One final blitz — the most joyful, celebratory version of everything you know.

- PEPEHA challenge: Three students deliver their full pepeha from memory. Class gives Tēnā koe after each.
- WHAKATAUKĪ round: Class says all four whakataukī together. Then explain Ko tā tērā pūrākau mōhio ko te…
- FAVOURITE SENTENCE: Every student says their single favourite te reo sentence of the year. No repeats.
- CLOSE together: Ka toitū tēnei reo i a tātou katoa. Ka toitū tātou i tēnei reo. Ka huri.

_Discussion prompt: Ka toitū tēnei reo i a tātou katoa — this language endures through all of us. And: Ka toitū tātou i tēnei reo — we endure through this language. The relationship goes both ways. The language needs you as much as you need it. Does that feel true? What does it mean to say you are part of the survival of a language?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Final year audit**

Your last vocabulary and reflection exercise of the year.

- Write every te reo word you know in 5 minutes. Count them.
- Write your five favourite sentences from the whole year.
- Write: Ko tāku koha ki tēnei reo: [what you give to the language — your commitment going forward].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "letter to the language" in te reo Māori — address te reo itself. Tell it what it has meant to you, what you will do to care for it, and what you hope for it. This is your final gift to your year.

#### Tuesday: He whakaaro whakamutunga — Final reflections

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · Metacognitive reflection · Full year*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Final reflection; AO 2.5 — Full expression

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Looking back, standing forward**

The last Tuesday of the year. Today we reflect on the journey — not to summarise, but to stand in this moment and feel the weight of what we have built. Then we turn to face forward.

> **Looking back:**
> **I tēnei tau, i [tipu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tipu) tōku reo, i tipu hoki tōku ngākau.**
> This year, my language grew, and my heart grew too.
> 
> **Ko ēnei ngā mea hou i ako ai au i tēnei tau:**
> he reo, he tikanga, he ao hou — ko tērā tōku taonga.
> These are the new things I learned this year: a language, a culture, a new world — that is my treasure.
> 
> **Standing forward:**
> **Ka haere au ki tērā tau me tēnei [taonga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=taonga) i ōku [ringaringa](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=ringaringa).**
> I go to next year with this treasure in my hands.
> 
> **Māku e [tiaki](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tiaki), māku e [whakaora](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whakaora), māku e whakaatu i tēnei reo [tapu](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=tapu).**
> I will protect it, I will nurture it, I will demonstrate this sacred language.

Final reflection vocabulary:
**i ōku ringaringa** — in my hands (ringaringa = hands)
**whakaora** — to heal / revitalise / give life to
**whakaatu** — to demonstrate / show / present
**Ko tērā tōku taonga.** — That is my treasure.
**Ko ēnei ngā mea hou i ako ai au:**
These are the new things I learned: (ai = relative clause marker)

Three commitments:
**Māku e tiaki** — I will protect it
**Māku e whakaora** — I will nurture/revitalise it
**Māku e whakaatu** — I will demonstrate it

Three māku e sentences in a row — a commitment in three parts.

*Tip: Māku e tiaki, māku e whakaora, māku e whakaatu — three commitments. In te ao Māori, a commitment said three times is a commitment made truly. Saying māku e three times signals that you are serious.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Three-part commitment**

Build your three-part commitment to te reo Māori.

- Look back: **I tēnei tau, i tipu tōku reo, i tipu hoki tōku [heart/mind/something specific].**
- Name what you carry: **Ka haere au ki tērā tau me [what you are taking with you].**
- Three commitments: **Māku e tiaki… Māku e whakaora… Māku e whakaatu…**
- Close together: **Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou katoa. Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.**

_Discussion prompt: Māku e tiaki, māku e whakaora, māku e whakaatu. Three commitments to the language. Which of these three feels most natural for you? Which feels most challenging? And which — if you only kept one — would matter most?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Final reflection**

Write your final reflection on the year in te reo Māori.

- I tēnei tau, i tipu tōku reo… describe three things that grew.
- Ka haere au ki tērā tau me [three things you are taking forward].
- Write your three māku e commitments.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your final full reflection (10 sentences) — the year in three sentences, what you carry, your three commitments, a whakataukī that guides you, and close with Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou katoa.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — The year-end showcase and certificate

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · Whakaatu (presenting) · Year celebration*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Year-end showcase; AO 1.1 — Formal presentation

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — He whakaaturanga — Presenting your year**

The final Wednesday lesson. Today each student prepares a short whakaaturanga — a presentation — of their year in te reo Māori. This is the Whakaatu (presenting) language mode in full action.

> **Structure for a whakaaturanga:**
> 
> **1. Open:** Tēnā koutou katoa.
> **2. Identity:** Ko [name] tōku ingoa. [Two pepeha lines].
> **3. Journey:** I tīmata au i Wāhanga Tahi me tētahi kupu — "Kia ora." Ināianei, kua tipu tōku reo ki tētahi mea nui ake.
> **4. Best sentence:** Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei: [your best sentence].
> **5. [Whakataukī](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Whakataukī):** Ko tōku whakataukī e tūāhuatia ana i a au ko tēnei: [whakataukī + explanation].
> **6. [Wawata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Wawata):** Ko tōku wawata mō tōku reo: [your aspiration].
> **7. Close:** Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.

The seven elements of the whakaaturanga:

1. **Formal greeting** — Tēnā koutou katoa
2. **Identity** — Ko sentences, pepeha (abbreviated)
3. **Journey** — where you started, where you are now (I, Kua)
4. **Best sentence** — your finest te reo of the year
5. **Whakataukī** — the proverb that represents you + Ko tā tērā mōhio ko te…
6. **Wawata** — your deepest aspiration for your reo
7. **Formal close** — Tēnā koutou ×3

Duration: approximately 60–90 seconds per student. That is enough for a full life in te reo Māori.

*Tip: Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu — my best sentence. Own it. Stand behind it. You chose it because it is true. When you say your best sentence in front of the class, say it like you mean it — because you do.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Whakaaturanga preparation**

Students prepare their whakaaturanga — practise with a partner first.

- Write the seven elements of your whakaaturanga — notes only, not a script.
- Practise with a partner: deliver it fully, they listen without interrupting.
- Partner gives one piece of feedback: "He pai tō [element]. Me [one suggestion]."
- Second run: incorporate the feedback. Aim for 60–90 seconds, from the heart.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei. Your best sentence of the year. What IS it? If you had to choose one sentence that captures something true about you in te reo Māori — what would it be? Choose it now. Write it down. It is yours._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Whakaaturanga**

Deliver your whakaaturanga to the class.

- Deliver your full whakaaturanga — all seven elements — to the class.
- Listen to your classmates' whakaaturanga — give a Tēnā koe after each one.
- Write your whakaaturanga as a final written record.
- ✦ Bonus: After the class whakaaturanga, write a response: "Ko ngā rerenga i pā mai ki a au ko ēnei…" (The sentences that moved me were these…) — choose three sentences from classmates and write why each one moved you.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — He whakamātautau — Final end-of-year assessment

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · All six language modes · Full year assessed*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.4 — Identity; AO 2.3 — Full sophisticated conversation; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary; AO 1.6 — Politeness

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The final assessment**

This is the last lesson of the year. The end-of-year assessment is a full spontaneous conversation — no script, no model to follow. Just you and your reo. This is what you have been building toward since Week 1 of Term 1.

> What a full-year assessment conversation might sound like:
> 
> **A:** Tēnā koe. Ko wai koe?
> **B:** [Full pepeha from memory]
> **A:** Ka pai! He aha ngā pou o tōu ora?
> **B:** Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko tōku whānau, ko tōku reo, ko tōku taiao.
> **A:** He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe i tēnei tau?
> **B:** I homai mai e ia ki a au he ara ki ngā tīpuna, he hononga ki ōku hoa, ā he māramatanga ki tōku ake ao.
> **A:** He aha tō wawata?
> **B:** Ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo tapu — ka [honohono](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=honohono) ia i ngā tāngata katoa o Aotearoa.
> **A:** Ā koia! Tēnā koe mō tāu kōrero tino hōhonu.
> **B:** Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe. Mā te wā.
> **A:** Mā te wā.

Final assessment criteria — what you are showing:

✓ Full pepeha (AO 1.2, 1.4)
✓ Sophisticated vocabulary: pou, hononga, māramatanga, wawata (AO 2.5)
✓ Complex structures: Ko ngā pou…, He aha…ai…, Ka toitū, Ka honohono (AO 2.3)
✓ Correct greeting and farewell system (AO 1.1, 1.6)
✓ Spontaneous extension beyond the question (AO 2.3)
✓ Natural closing: Ka nui ōku mihi. Mā te wā. (AO 1.1)

You do not need to be perfect. You need to be present, genuine, and in te reo Māori.

Ka angitu koe. You will succeed.

*Tip: Mā te wā — until another time. This is the farewell for a long parting. At the end of the school year, Mā te wā is right. You are not saying goodbye forever — you are saying: I will carry this language with me, and we will meet again in it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final preparation**

One last practice — quietly, with a partner, in your own words.

- Sit with your partner. Deliver your full assessment — no notes.
- They listen fully. Then they deliver theirs.
- After: Ko tāu rerenga pai rawa atu ko tēhea? Tell your partner their finest moment.
- Class together, final time: Ka toitū tēnei reo i a tātou. Ka huri.

_Discussion prompt: Mā te wā. Until another time. But in te reo, every time there is an "until another time" rather than a goodbye. The language is waiting for you. What do you say to it before you go?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whakamātautau — End of year**

Your final end-of-year assessment.

- **Task 1:** Full spontaneous conversation — at least 14 lines each. No script.
- **Task 2:** Self-assess against the criteria above. Be generous and honest.
- **Task 3:** Write your final sentence of the year: Ko tōku rerenga whakamutunga o tēnei tau ko tēnei: [sentence]. This is your seal on the year.
- ✦ Final checklist: ☐ Pepeha (9 lines) · ☐ Ko ngā pou o tōu ora · ☐ He aha tā tēnei reo i homai ai ki a koe · ☐ Wawata statement · ☐ Ka nui ōku mihi · ☐ Mā te wā

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka tino pai tāu mahi." What makes this different from "Ka pai tāu mahi"?
  - They mean exactly the same thing 
  - Tino adds genuine emphasis — truly/genuinely excellent, not just good ✅
  - Ka tino is a past tense marker 
  - Tino makes it a question 
- "Ko tāku koha ki tēnei reo." What does koha mean?
  - challenge 
  - question 
  - gift / contribution / offering ✅
  - complaint 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Māku e tiaki, māku e whakaora, māku e whakaatu." What is the significance of three māku e sentences?
  - It is a grammar requirement for lists 
  - Three statements signal a deep, complete commitment in te ao Māori ✅
  - It makes the sentence easier to remember 
  - The three verbs all start with wha- 
- What does "whakaora" mean?
  - to demonstrate 
  - to protect 
  - to heal / revitalise / give life to ✅
  - to forget 

**Wednesday questions**

- What are the seven elements of a whakaaturanga?
  - greeting, weather, topic, questions, answers, debate, farewell 
  - greeting, identity, journey, best sentence, whakataukī, wawata, close ✅
  - pepeha, whakataukī, debate, story, song, dance, farewell 
  - greeting, questions, answers, conclusions, thanks 
- "Ko ngā rerenga i pā mai ki a au ko ēnei." What does "i pā mai ki a au" mean?
  - that I wrote myself 
  - that were too difficult for me 
  - that moved/touched me ✅
  - that I memorised 

**Thursday questions**

- "Mā te wā." When is this farewell used rather than Hei konā rā?
  - For a short parting like the end of a lesson 
  - For a long parting — until another time (like the end of the year) ✅
  - Only at funerals 
  - Only between very formal people 
- What is the final criterion for the end-of-year assessment?
  - Perfect grammar with no errors 
  - The longest possible sentences 
  - Being present, genuine, and in te reo Māori — with spontaneous extension ✅
  - Reading from a prepared script

### Week 7

#### Monday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Greetings, pepeha, and identity (Terms 1–4)

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.1: Greetings · AO 1.2: Introductions · AO 1.4: Personal information · AO 1.6: Politeness — full spiral review*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings — full spiral; AO 1.2 — Introductions — full spiral; AO 1.4 — Identity — full spiral

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From Kia ora to Ko tōku wawata: the identity spiral**

Welcome to Week 7. These final four weeks are your spiral review — we revisit every major domain from the year, going deeper each time. Today we spiral back to where we began: greetings, introductions, and identity. But look how far these have grown since Term 1 Week 1.

> **TERM 1 WEEK 1 level:**
> Kia ora. Ko [name] tōku ingoa.
> 
> **TERM 4 WEEK 7 level:**
> Tēnā koutou katoa.
> Ko [maunga] tōku maunga, ko [awa] tōku awa.
> Ko [waka] tōku waka, ko [iwi] tōku iwi.
> Ko [hapū] tōku hapū, ko [marae] tōku marae.
> Ko [kura] tōku kura, ko [name] tōku ingoa.
> Nō [place] ahau. He uri ahau nō ōku tīpuna.
> Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko tōku whānau, ko tōku reo, ko tōku taiao.
> Ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo tapu mō ōku uri ā āpōpō.
> Nō reira, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.

What has grown from Term 1 to now:

TERM 1: Kia ora · Ko [name] tōku ingoa · Nō [place] ahau
TERM 2: Tēnā koe/kōrua/koutou · Thank-you formulas
TERM 3: Full 9-line pepeha · He uri ahau nō ōku tīpuna · Whaikōrero structure
TERM 4: Ko ngā pou o tōku ora · Ko tōku wawata · Ka nui ōku mihi

The identity statement has grown from one line to a full whakaaturanga.

New spiral phrase:
**Nō reira, ko ahau ko [name].**
Therefore, I am [name]. (Nō reira closes the pepeha and plants the speaker firmly in their identity)

*Tip: Nō reira, ko ahau ko [name] — therefore, I am [name]. This closing line of a pepeha is the most powerful. After all the mountain, river, iwi, and hapū — you arrive at yourself. The land and people make the person.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Identity spiral drill**

Speed round through the full identity progression — each level building on the last.

- Level 1 (T1): Kia ora. Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Nō [place] ahau. Say it fast and smooth.
- Level 2 (T2): Tēnā koe. Ko [name] tōku ingoa. Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina. Add a thank-you.
- Level 3 (T3): Full pepeha — all 9 lines from memory. Ko [maunga]… through Nō reira, ko ahau ko [name].
- Level 4 (T4): Add Ko ngā pou o tōku ora + Ko tōku wawata. The complete identity statement.

_Discussion prompt: Nō reira, ko ahau ko [name]. After the mountain, the river, the iwi — you arrive at yourself. The land and the people define you before you define yourself. In your culture or family, how is identity shaped by place and people? Is there a Western equivalent of the pepeha?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Full identity spiral**

Write and say your complete identity statement — all four levels combined.

- Write your full pepeha — all 9 lines — from memory. No looking.
- Add Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko… (three pillars).
- Add Ko tōku wawata: ka… (your deepest aspiration).
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full whakaaturanga incorporating all four levels — pepeha, pillars, wawata, best sentence of the year, whakataukī, and close with Ka nui ōku mihi. Practise delivering it in under 90 seconds.

#### Tuesday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Tense mastery (Terms 1–4)

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.4: Time · AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: All tense markers consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.4 — Tense mastery — full spiral; AO 2.3 — Extended tense-controlled writing

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — All six tense markers: the complete picture**

You have learned six tense and aspect markers across four terms. Today we spiral back through all of them — not as a list to memorise, but as a living system to feel and use. Each marker answers a different question about time.

> **Kei te** haere ia. — She is going. (RIGHT NOW — present state)
> **E haere ana** ia. — She is going (and still going — ONGOING)
> **I** haere ia inanahi. — She went yesterday. (COMPLETED PAST — specific event)
> **Ka** haere ia. — She goes / went / and then she went. (NARRATIVE — story movement)
> **Kua** haere ia. — She has gone. (PERFECT — completed, result matters now)
> **Ka haere** ia āpōpō. — She will go tomorrow. (FUTURE — Ka also signals future events)
> 
> **NEGATIVES:**
> **Kāore** ia **e haere ana.** — She is not going. (present negative)
> **Kāore** ia **i haere.** — She did not go. (past negative)
> **Kāore anō kua** haere ia. — She has not yet gone. (perfect negative)

The question each marker answers:

**Kei te** — What is happening RIGHT NOW?
**E…ana** — What is STILL HAPPENING, ongoing?
**I** — What HAPPENED at a specific time in the past?
**Ka** — What happened NEXT in the story / what WILL happen next?
**Kua** — What has BEEN COMPLETED whose result matters NOW?
**Ka [verb] āpōpō** — What WILL happen?

Remember: in te reo Māori, the VERB never changes. Only the MARKER changes. This is the fundamental beauty of the language — one verb, six markers, six relationships to time.

*Tip: One verb, six markers. Haere is always haere — going. What changes is your relationship to that going: is it now, ongoing, past, narrative, perfect, or future? The marker answers that question.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Tense spiral drill**

I give you one verb — you use it with all six markers in sequence.

- Verb: kai (eat). Six sentences: Kei te kai ia · E kai ana ia · I kai ia · Ka kai ia · Kua kai ia · Ka kai ia āpōpō.
- Verb: ako (learn). Your turn — six sentences with all six markers. Which feels most natural?
- Now add negatives: Kāore ia e kai ana · Kāore ia i kai · Kāore anō kua kai ia.
- Challenge: write one sentence that uses THREE different markers — different clauses, same paragraph.

_Discussion prompt: The verb never changes in te reo Māori — only the marker does. English changes the verb: "eat / ate / eaten / eating / will eat". If you were teaching a new learner of te reo Māori, how would you explain why the te reo system is actually EASIER than English? What is the hardest part to explain?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Tense spiral writing**

Use all six markers in a connected passage about your year.

- Write Kei te and E…ana sentences about right now in class.
- Write I and Ka sentences about what you did this term.
- Write Kua and Ka [future] sentences about what has been achieved and what comes next.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a "year in tenses" passage (10 sentences) — each sentence uses a different marker, tells a different moment of your year, and they connect to form one flowing account. Label each marker.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Spiral: Complex sentences from all terms

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · Grammar Level 1–2: All complex structures consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Complex sentence spiral; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary in complex sentences

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Every complex structure you know**

You have learned a full toolkit of complex sentence structures. Today we spiral back through all of them — the joining words, the subordinate clauses, the discourse markers — and practise moving between them fluently.

> **COORDINATING:** ā · engari · nō reira · me
> **SUBORDINATING:** nō te mea · ahakoa · mehemea · i te wā · ina…ana
> **RELATIVE CLAUSE:** [noun] + [clause] + ai
> **PURPOSE:** hei [verb]
> **DISCOURSE MARKERS:** waihoki · heoi anō · hei tauira · arā · Ko te mutunga · kāore noa iho
> **DEGREE:** tino · āhua · noa · tonu · anō · kē
> 
> **ALL IN ONE PASSAGE:**
> I ako au i te reo Māori i tēnei tau — he mea uaua, **engari** he mea pai **hoki**. **Ahakoa** he uauatanga, i mahi tonu au **nō te mea** ko tōku moemoeā tōku ara. **Kāore noa iho** — ko tēnei reo tōku hononga ki ngā tīpuna, **arā**, ki ngā uri hoki. **Ko te mutunga**, ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo **hei** oranga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri.

Count the complex structures in that passage:
engari (contrast)
hoki (also)
ahakoa (concession)
nō te mea (reason)
kāore noa iho (not only that)
arā (namely)
Ko te mutunga (in conclusion)
hei (purpose clause)

Eight complex structures in five sentences. Every one of them natural, every one of them in service of something true.

New spiral word:
**oranga** — wellbeing / health / vitality
**hei oranga** — for the wellbeing of
**Ka toitū tēnei reo hei oranga mō ngā uri.**
This language will endure for the wellbeing of descendants.

*Tip: Every complex structure you know is a tool for precision. Without them, you can say what happened. With them, you can say why, when, although, because, in order to, and despite. They are the difference between reporting and understanding.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Complex sentence spiral**

I give you a plain sentence — you expand it using three different complex structures.

- Plain: "I ako au i te reo Māori." → Add ahakoa, nō te mea, and hei. Three expansions.
- Plain: "He pai tēnei reo." → Add kāore noa iho, arā, and Ko te mutunga.
- Plain: "Ka haere mātou ki te kura." → Add i te wā, engari, and ā.
- Now write your own: one plain sentence about te reo Māori — expand it with five complex structures.

_Discussion prompt: Ko te mutunga, ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo hei oranga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri. Count the structures: Ko te mutunga (discourse marker), Ko (identity), Ka toitū (future), hei oranga (purpose), mō āpōpō ō ngā uri (time + possession). Five structures in one sentence. And yet it sounds natural. Why?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Complex sentence showcase**

Write a paragraph using every complex structure type at least once.

- Write a sentence with ahakoa + nō te mea in the same clause.
- Write a sentence with a relative clause (ai) + purpose clause (hei).
- Write a sentence using waihoki + arā + Ko te mutunga across three sentences.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your finest complex te reo paragraph (8 sentences) — use all five categories: coordinating, subordinating, relative, purpose, and discourse markers. Make it a genuine statement about your year.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — Spiral: The full-year conversation challenge

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · Full spontaneous conversation*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Full conversation spiral; AO 1.1 — Greetings spiral; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary spiral

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The full-year conversation: every domain**

Today we have the most comprehensive conversation of the year — touching every major domain from Terms 1–4. This is the spiral in conversation form: identity, past events, feelings, weather, community, arts, environment, whakataukī, and wawata — all in one exchange.

> **A:** Tēnā koe. Ko wai koe? [Full pepeha from B]
> **B:** [9-line pepeha] Nō reira, tēnā koe.
> **A:** He pēhea ōu hararei? He aha ngā mea hou?
> **B:** I haere māua ko tōku māmā ki te ngahere. I ātaahua te rangi — ahakoa i ua i tētahi rā, i tākaro tonu mātou i waho. Ka ora tōku ngākau i te āhua o te taiao.
> **A:** He aha tō whakataukī e tūāhuatia ana i a koe i tēnei wā?
> **B:** "Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū te tangata, ka toitū hoki te taiao." Ko tā tērā mōhio ko te hononga o ngā mea katoa.
> **A:** He aha tō wawata mō āpōpō?
> **B:** Ko tōku wawata: ka honohono tēnei reo i ngā tāngata katoa o Aotearoa — ka toitū hoki ia hei oranga mō ōku uri.
> **A:** [Ā koia](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=%C4%80+koia)! Ka nui ōku [mihi](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=mihi) ki a koe. Mā te wā.
> **B:** Mā te wā.

Every domain in that conversation:

Identity (T1/T3): full pepeha
Past events (T2): I haere māua · I ua i tētahi rā
Feelings (T2): Ka ora tōku ngākau
Weather (T2): i ātaahua te rangi · i ua
Nature (T2/T3): i te āhua o te taiao · i te ngahere
Concession (T1): ahakoa i ua, i tākaro tonu
Whakataukī (T3): stated and explained
Wawata (T4): Ko tōku wawata: ka honohono…
Purpose (T4): hei oranga mō ōku uri
Farewell (T4): Mā te wā

*Tip: Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū te tangata, ka toitū hoki te taiao. This whakataukī connects language, people, and the natural world. It is the perfect whakataukī for a conversation that has touched all three. Have it ready — it fits almost any deep te reo conversation.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Full-year conversation spiral**

Pairs. This conversation must touch every domain from the year. No scripts.

- Open: formal greeting + full pepeha. (T1/T3 identity)
- Exchange: holidays/past events + weather + feeling. (T2 domains)
- Deepen: whakataukī + explanation + wawata. (T3/T4 domains)
- Close: Ka nui ōku mihi + Mā te wā. (T4 farewell)

_Discussion prompt: Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū te tangata, ka toitū hoki te taiao. Language, people, natural world — all endure together. This year you have learned a language. In doing so, what else have you learned? What does learning te reo teach you about Aotearoa, about people, about yourself?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Full-year spiral conversation**

Have the most complete te reo conversation of your life — every domain, fully spontaneous.

- Full pepeha from memory — no hesitation.
- Past event + weather + feeling — at least four sentences.
- Whakataukī + explanation + wawata — your genuine answers.
- ✦ Bonus: Write up the full conversation (16+ lines). Annotate every domain and structure you used. How many of the 12 AOs appear in your conversation? Aim for all of them.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Nō reira, ko ahau ko [name]." Where does this line appear in a pepeha and what does it do?
  - It opens the pepeha as a greeting 
  - It closes the pepeha — planting the speaker firmly in their identity after naming land and people ✅
  - It is a farewell at the end of a speech 
  - It introduces the mountain and river 
- What is the correct order of elements in a full pepeha?
  - Name → mountain → river → iwi → school → origin 
  - Mountain → river → waka → iwi → hapū → marae → school → name → origin (Nō reira ko ahau) ✅
  - Origin → name → iwi → mountain → river 
  - School → name → iwi → hapū → origin 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Kua haere ia." What does Kua signal about this action?
  - She is going right now 
  - She was going in the past at a specific time 
  - She has gone and the result is relevant now ✅
  - She will go tomorrow 
- Which tense marker signals an ONGOING action that is still continuing?
  - Kei te 
  - I 
  - Ka 
  - E…ana ✅

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ka toitū tēnei reo hei oranga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri." What does "hei oranga" express?
  - a past event 
  - a purpose clause — for the wellbeing of ✅
  - a negative 
  - a comparison 
- "Kāore noa iho — ko tēnei reo tōku hononga ki ngā tīpuna." What does kāore noa iho signal?
  - It is not at all 
  - Not only that — there is more ✅
  - It is just nothing 
  - It is almost nothing 

**Thursday questions**

- "Ka toitū te reo, ka toitū te tangata, ka toitū hoki te taiao." What three things endure together?
  - language, school, community 
  - language, people, and the natural world ✅
  - language, ancestors, descendants 
  - language, culture, money 
- In the full-year conversation, which farewell is used for a long parting like the end of term?
  - Hei konā rā 
  - E noho rā 
  - Ā ka kite anō 
  - Mā te wā ✅

### Week 8

#### Monday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Descriptions, comparisons, and figurative language

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.5: Descriptions · AO 2.3: Opinions · Grammar Level 1–2: He, comparatives, simile, metaphor — full spiral*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.5 — Description spiral; AO 2.3 — Comparison & figurative language

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From He nui to Ko te reo he aho: the description spiral**

In Term 1 you learned He nui (it is big). By Term 4 you are writing Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata (language is a cord that weaves people). Today we spiral through every layer of description — adjectives, comparatives, superlatives, degree words, simile, and metaphor.

> **TERM 1 level:**
> He nui tēnei whare. He pai tōku kura.
> 
> **TERM 2 level:**
> He nui ake tērā whare i tēnei. He tino pai tōku kura — he pai rawa atu i ērā atu kura.
> 
> **TERM 3 level:**
> He tino ātaahua tōku kura, he wāhi tiaki mōku — ka tipu ōku whakaaro i konei.
> 
> **TERM 4 level (simile):**
> E tipu ana tōku ngākau āhua te [rākau](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=r%C4%81kau) i te tōanga o te [koanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=k%C5%8Danga).
> My heart grows like a tree in the opening of spring.
> 
> **TERM 4 level (metaphor):**
> Ko tōku kura he māramatanga ki te ao mārama — ko ia tōku [aho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aho) ki ngā tīpuna.

The description spiral:

He [adjective] → basic description
He [adj] ake → comparative
He [adj] rawa atu → superlative
He tino [adj] → intensified
[verb] āhua/me he [comparison] → simile
Ko [X] he [metaphor] → metaphor

New spiral phrase:
**i te tōanga o te kōanga** — in the opening of spring
**tō** — the opening / unfolding (of something)
**E tipu ana tōku ngākau āhua te rākau i te tōanga o te kōanga.**
My heart grows like a tree in the opening of spring.

*Tip: From He nui to Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata — that journey took you one year. The first sentence describes a size. The second describes a relationship between people and language. That is what learning a language does to your thinking.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Description spiral drill**

Take one noun through every level of description.

- Noun: te reo Māori. Level 1: He [adjective] te reo Māori.
- Level 2: He [adj] ake te reo Māori i [comparison].
- Level 3: He tino [adj] te reo Māori — he [adj] rawa atu.
- Level 4: Ko te reo Māori he [metaphor] — E [verb] ana ia āhua te [simile].

_Discussion prompt: E tipu ana tōku ngākau āhua te rākau i te tōanga o te kōanga. Heart. Tree. Spring. Three things connected through the word āhua (like). What is it about a tree opening in spring that captures how a heart grows? Why is this a better description than "my heart is growing"?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Full description spiral**

Write descriptions of your year at every level from basic to figurative.

- Write He [adj] and He [adj] ake sentences about te reo Māori.
- Write a tino/rawa atu superlative about your best learning moment.
- Write a simile (āhua/me he) about something that grew in you this year.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a Ko [X] he [metaphor] sentence about te reo Māori — something as beautiful as Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata. Then write a short paragraph continuing the metaphor.

#### Tuesday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Community, environment, and kaitiakitanga

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Connection to land · AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 2.4: Natural world — full spiral*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Land & community spiral; AO 2.4 — Environment & kaitiakitanga spiral

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From Nō hea koe to Ka ora te taiao ka ora tātou: the community spiral**

From Term 1 Week 4 (places and ki) to Term 3 Week 7 (kaitiakitanga and toitū) — the community and environment strand runs through the whole year. Today we spiral it all together: where you come from, who your community is, and your responsibility to the natural world.

> **TERM 1 level:**
> Nō Ōtautahi ahau. Kei te haere au ki te toa.
> 
> **TERM 2 level:**
> Nō Aotearoa ahau — he whenua ātaahua tēnei. Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai o tōku tāone.
> 
> **TERM 3 level:**
> Ko tātou ngā kaitiaki o tēnei taiao. Me tiaki tātou i te mauri o ngā awa, o ngā ngahere, o ngā maunga.
> 
> **TERM 4 level:**
> Ka ora te taiao, ka ora tātou — he pono tēnei. Ko tōku wawata: ka toitū te taiao o Aotearoa hei oranga mō ōku uri. E ai ki āku whakaaro, he kawenga ōu mō tēnei whenua ātaahua.

The community/environment spiral:

T1: Location (nō hea, ki hea, kei hea)
T2: Description of place (He ātaahua, Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai)
T3: Kaitiakitanga (me tiaki, mauri, toitū)
T4: Philosophical connection (Ka ora te taiao, wawata, kawenga)

Full statement:
**Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua — he kawenga ōku ki te tiaki i tōna oranga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri.**
I am a descendant of this land — I have a responsibility to protect its wellbeing for tomorrow's descendants.

New spiral phrase:
**Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua.**
I am a descendant of this land.
**tōna oranga** — its wellbeing / its vitality

*Tip: Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua — I am a descendant of this land. This sentence connects pepeha (belonging to land) with kaitiakitanga (responsibility to land). You are not separate from the land — you come FROM it, and you are responsible TO it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Community and environment spiral**

Spiral through every level of community and environment language.

- T1/T2: Nō [place] ahau. He [adjective] tōku whenua/tāone. Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai…
- T3: Ko tātou ngā kaitiaki o tēnei taiao. Me tiaki tātou i te mauri o… nō te mea…
- T4: Ka ora te taiao, ka ora tātou. Ko tōku wawata: ka toitū…
- Full: Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua — he kawenga ōku ki te tiaki i tōna oranga mō ōku uri.

_Discussion prompt: Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua. You are a descendant of the land. Not just of people — of the land itself. What would it mean to genuinely believe this? How would it change the way you live, eat, travel, and consume if you truly felt you were descended from the land and responsible to it?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Community and environment spiral**

Write your full community and environment statement — all four levels.

- Write Nō [place] ahau + He [adj] tōku whenua + Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai.
- Write Ko tātou ngā kaitiaki o tēnei taiao + Me tiaki tātou i… + reason.
- Write Ka ora te taiao, ka ora tātou + Ko tōku wawata mō te taiao.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full kaitiakitanga statement (10 sentences) — your origin, your community, what you love about your place, what threatens it, your responsibility, what you commit to, and your wawata for the natural world of Aotearoa.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Spiral: Argument, opinion, and persuasion

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended persuasive communication · Grammar Level 1–2: Full argument toolkit consolidated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Argument & persuasion spiral; AO 2.5 — Formal argument vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From He pai ake to Tōku tūāhuatanga: the argument spiral**

You began arguing in Term 1 with He pai ake (better than). By Term 4 you are writing full structured arguments with taunakitanga, waihoki, and Ko te mutunga. Today we spiral the full argument toolkit — from simple comparison to philosophical persuasion.

> **TERM 1 level:**
> He pai ake te hākinakina i te noho ki te kāinga ki a au nō te mea he hauora ake.
> 
> **TERM 2 level:**
> He pai ake te mahi tahi i te mahi kotahi — he nui ake ngā hua. Ka pai tāu whakaaro, engari rā…
> 
> **TERM 3 level:**
> Tōku tūāhuatanga: he nui ake ngā hua o te kaitiakitanga i ngā uauatanga. Tāku taunakitanga tuatahi: Ka ora te taiao, ka ora tātou.
> 
> **TERM 4 level:**
> E ai ki āku whakaaro, ko te reo Māori te taonga rangatira rawa atu o Aotearoa. Kāore noa iho — ko ia hoki te ara ki te ao o ōku tīpuna. Waihoki, ka honohono ia i ngā tāngata katoa. Ko te mutunga, me tiaki tātou i tēnei taonga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri.

The argument spiral:

T1: He pai ake + nō te mea (simple comparison + reason)
T2: Whakaae/whakaaro exchanges, He tika tāu engari rā
T3: Tūāhuatanga + taunakitanga tuatahi/tuarua
T4: E ai ki āku whakaaro + kāore noa iho + waihoki + Ko te mutunga

The four-term argument:
Simple → Dialogic → Structured → Philosophical

New spiral phrase:
**taonga rangatira rawa atu** — the most precious / chiefly treasure
**ko ia hoki te ara** — it is also the path

*Tip: Ko te mutunga — in conclusion. This is your power phrase. After all the evidence, all the concessions, all the comparisons — Ko te mutunga lands your final point with authority. Use it when you mean it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Argument spiral drill**

Build the same argument at four different levels of sophistication.

- T1 level: He pai ake te [X] i te [Y] ki a au nō te mea…
- T2 level: He tika tāu whakaaro — engari rā, ko tāku whakaaro he [counter-view].
- T3 level: Tōku tūāhuatanga: [position]. Tāku taunakitanga tuatahi: [evidence].
- T4 level: E ai ki āku whakaaro… Kāore noa iho… Waihoki… Ko te mutunga…

_Discussion prompt: Ko te mutunga, me tiaki tātou i tēnei taonga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri. A conclusion that points to future generations. The best arguments in te ao Māori often end by considering uri (descendants). Why? What does including future generations in an argument do to its moral weight?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Full argument spiral**

Write a four-level argument about te reo Māori — from simple to philosophical.

- T1 level: He pai ake te ako i te reo Māori i [alternative] nō te mea…
- T3 level: Tōku tūāhuatanga + two taunakitanga (tuatahi/tuarua).
- T4 level: E ai ki āku whakaaro + kāore noa iho + waihoki + Ko te mutunga.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your finest persuasive piece (10 sentences) about why te reo Māori matters — using the full argument toolkit from all four terms. This is your year-end statement of belief.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — Spiral: He kōrero ā-iwi — A community conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Community · AO 2.1: Relationships · AO 2.3: Extended conversation — all community domains*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.1 — Community relationships spiral; AO 1.4 — Identity & community spiral

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Everything about community in one conversation**

Today we spiral all the community language from the year — whānau, hapū, iwi, marae, school, environment, kaitiakitanga, relationships — into one rich conversation.

> **A:** Tēnā koe. Nō hea koe ā ko wai ōu iwi?
> **B:** Nō Ōtautahi ahau. Ko Ngāi Tahu tōku iwi, ko Ngāti Māmoe tōku hapū.
> **A:** He aha ngā āhuatanga pai rawa atu o tō rohe ki a koe?
> **B:** Ko ēnei: he ātaahua te taiao, he māia ngā tāngata, ā he rangatira hoki te reo o tēnei whenua.
> **A:** He aha tō kawenga ki tōu hapori?
> **B:** He kawenga ōku ki te tiaki i te taiao, ki te kōrero i te reo Māori, ā ki te whakaaro nui mō ngā uri — nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri.
> **A:** Ka wani kē! Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au.
> **B:** Tēnā koe. Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe mō tāu aro mai ki ōku kōrero.

New spiral phrases:
**tō rohe** — your region / area / territory
**Ko ēnei: [list]** — These are: (using Ko ēnei for listing community assets)
**nā ōku tīpuna ki a au** — from my ancestors to me
**ā māku ki ōku uri** — and from me to my descendants
**Ka wani kē!** — That is amazing! (from Term 3 Week 5!)
**Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au.**
Your words are an enlightenment to me.
**mō tāu aro mai ki ōku kōrero** — for turning toward my words / for listening to me
**aro mai** — turn toward / face toward / attend to

*Tip: Nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri — from my ancestors to me, and from me to my descendants. This sentence places you in the middle of a chain that stretches from the past to the future. You are not the beginning or the end — you are the bridge.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Community spiral conversation**

Build a community conversation touching every domain from the year.

- Open with formal greeting + Nō hea koe + ko wai ōu iwi/hapū.
- Share Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai rawa atu o tōku rohe: a Ko ēnei list.
- State your kawenga: He kawenga ōku ki te [three responsibilities].
- Close with nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri + Ka nui ōku mihi.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au — your words are an enlightenment to me. This is the highest compliment in a te reo conversation — you have not just been understood, you have brought light. When has someone's words brought light to YOU this year? In te reo or in any language?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Community spiral conversation**

Have a full community conversation — every domain from Terms 1–4.

- Exchange origins, iwi/hapū, and region descriptions.
- Share Ko ēnei ngā āhuatanga pai lists about your communities.
- State your kawenga and the nā ōku tīpuna ki a au chain.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the full conversation (14+ lines). Annotate which term each piece of language comes from — T1, T2, T3, or T4. How evenly distributed is it? Which term contributed the most to this conversation?

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "E tipu ana tōku ngākau āhua te rākau i te tōanga o te kōanga." What figure of speech is this?
  - metaphor 
  - personification 
  - simile — the heart grows LIKE a tree in spring ✅
  - alliteration 
- What is the difference between He nui (T1) and Ko te reo he aho e whiri ana i ngā tāngata (T4)?
  - One is a description of size; the other is a metaphor about language connecting people ✅
  - They mean the same thing 
  - One is formal, one is casual 
  - One uses past tense, one uses present 

**Tuesday questions**

- "Ko au he uri o tēnei whenua." What does this sentence claim about the speaker's relationship to the land?
  - The speaker owns the land 
  - The speaker is a guardian employed to protect the land 
  - The speaker is a descendant of the land — belonging to it ✅
  - The speaker is a visitor from another land 
- "Ka ora te taiao, ka ora tātou." What principle does this whakataukī-style sentence express?
  - The environment is more important than people 
  - Our wellbeing and the environment's wellbeing are inseparable ✅
  - We must protect the environment because we are told to 
  - The environment survives without people 

**Wednesday questions**

- What is "tūāhuatanga" in an argument context?
  - the conclusion 
  - the evidence 
  - your position / stance ✅
  - the introduction 
- "Ko te mutunga, me tiaki tātou i tēnei taonga mō āpōpō ō ngā uri." What does including "ngā uri" in a conclusion do?
  - It weakens the argument by looking too far ahead 
  - It adds moral weight by considering future generations ✅
  - It is required by te reo grammar 
  - It replaces the need for evidence 

**Thursday questions**

- "Nā ōku tīpuna ki a au, ā māku ki ōku uri." What chain does this sentence describe?
  - The chain of school learning 
  - The chain of money and resources 
  - The chain of responsibility from ancestors through the speaker to descendants ✅
  - The chain of greetings in a pōhiri 
- "Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au." What is the speaker expressing?
  - The other person is talking too much 
  - Your words have brought enlightenment / light to me ✅
  - I do not understand what you said 
  - Please speak more clearly

### Week 9

#### Monday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Arts, culture, and whakataukī

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 1.4: Cultural identity · AO 2.5: Arts and figurative language · AO 2.3: Extended cultural communication*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.4 — Arts & culture spiral; AO 2.5 — Whakataukī & figurative spiral

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — From toi whakairo to Ko te reo he aho: the culture spiral**

Today we spiral the arts, culture, and wisdom strand — haka, waiata, whakairo, raranga, whakataukī, tikanga. These are the domains that connect language to living culture. By Term 4, you can not just name them — you can explain, analyse, and connect them to identity.

> **TERM 3 level (naming):**
> Ko te haka tētahi mahi toi rongonui o Aotearoa.
> He tohu aroha te haka — he tohu kaha hoki.
> 
> **TERM 4 level (explaining):**
> Ko te tikanga o te haka ko te whakamana i te tangata me tōna hapori.
> Ko tā tēnei mahi toi mōhio ko te mana o te kaha me te aroha — ko ērā ngā mea e kore e wehewehea.
> 
> **WHAKATAUKĪ spiral:**
> "He aha te mea nui o te ao? He tangata, he tangata, he tangata."
> Ko tā tēnei whakataukī mōhio ko te pūtake o ngā mea katoa — ko ngā tāngata.

The culture spiral levels:

Naming: Ko te haka tētahi mahi toi.
Describing: He tohu aroha, he tohu kaha.
Explaining: Ko te tikanga o te haka ko te…
Connecting to identity: Ko tā tēnei mōhio ko te mana o te tangata.
Using as evidence: Hei tauira, ko te haka — arā, ko ia he tohu o ngā mea e kore e wehewehea.

New spiral phrase:
**whakamana** — to empower / to honour / to give authority to
**Ko te tikanga o te haka ko te whakamana i te tangata.**
The meaning of haka is to honour/empower the person.
**e kore e wehewehea** — that cannot be separated

*Tip: E kore e wehewehea — cannot be separated. Love and strength in haka. Language and identity in te reo. Land and people in kaitiakitanga. The most important things in te ao Māori e kore e wehewehea — they cannot be separated from each other.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Arts and culture spiral**

Spiral through every level of arts and culture language.

- Name three mahi toi: Ko ēnei ngā mahi toi ōku: he [X], he [Y], he [Z].
- Explain one: Ko te tikanga o te [art form] ko te [explanation].
- Give deeper meaning: Ko tā tēnei mahi toi mōhio ko te [deeper lesson].
- Use a whakataukī as evidence: Hei tauira, [whakataukī] — arā, [explanation].

_Discussion prompt: E kore e wehewehea — cannot be separated. What two things in YOUR life cannot be separated? Not just love and strength — something specific to you. And can you say it in te reo Māori?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Arts and culture spiral writing**

Write about art, culture, and wisdom at Term 4 level.

- Name your favourite art form and explain its tikanga: Ko te tikanga o te [art] ko te…
- Give its deeper meaning: Ko tā tēnei mōhio ko te…
- Use a whakataukī as evidence with hei tauira and arā.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full cultural explanation (8 sentences) of one art form or cultural practice — naming, describing, explaining tikanga, deeper meaning, whakataukī evidence, connection to identity, and a personal wawata about that art form.

#### Tuesday: Arotake ā-reo — Spiral: Full grammar — every structure in conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All Grammar Level 1–2 · All AOs — final spiral*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Full grammar spiral; AO 2.4 — Tense & structure mastery

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Every structure, natural and fluent**

This is the penultimate grammar lesson of the year. Today we use every major structure from the full year in a single natural passage — not to show off, but to feel how effortlessly they can work together when you are not thinking about them individually.

> **A four-term grammar showcase passage:**
> 
> Ko ahau ko [name] — he ākonga i tīmatahia noatia e tētahi kupu inanahi, ināianei, kua tipu ōku kupu ki tētahi ao hou.
> 
> E ako tonu ana au, kāore anō kua oti — engari, ahakoa he uauatanga i ōku ara, i mahi tonu au nō te mea ko tōku moemoeā tōku māramatanga.
> 
> Ka taea e au te mihi, ka taea e au te tuhituhi, ka taea e au te whakaaro i tēnei reo rangatira — ko ia te [aho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aho) e [whiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whiri) ana i a au ki ngā tīpuna.
> 
> Māku e tiaki. Māku e whakaora. Māku e whakaatu.
> 
> Ko te mutunga, ko tōku wawata: ka toitū tēnei reo tapu hei oranga mō ōku uri ā āpōpō. Ka huri.

Every structure in that passage:
Ko (identity ×2)
He (description)
I (past: i tīmatahia)
Kua (perfect: kua tipu, kua oti)
E…ana (progressive: E ako tonu ana)
Kāore anō kua (perfect negative)
Ahakoa (concession)
Nō te mea (reason)
Ko + moemoeā (dream)
Ka taea (ability ×3)
Ko [metaphor] (Ko ia te aho…)
ai (relative: e whiri ana i a au)
Māku e (commitment ×3)
Ko te mutunga (conclusion)
Ko + wawata (aspiration)
Hei oranga (purpose)
Ka huri (story close)

17 different structures. One passage. And it sounds like real te reo.

*Tip: When you stop thinking about structures and start thinking about what you want to say, the structures take care of themselves. That is fluency. That is what this year has been building toward.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final grammar spiral**

Read the showcase passage together aloud — then each student writes their own version.

- Open: Ko ahau ko [name] — he ākonga i tīmatahia noatia e tētahi kupu inanahi, ināianei…
- Middle: E ako tonu ana au, kāore anō kua oti — ahakoa… nō te mea…
- Achievement: Ka taea e au te… ka taea e au te… ka taea e au te…
- Commitment + close: Māku e tiaki. Māku e whakaora. Ko tōku wawata: ka… Ka huri.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku moemoeā tōku māramatanga — my dream is my understanding/clarity. Not wealth, not fame, not even success — māramatanga. Clarity of thought and understanding. Is this your dream too? What does māramatanga mean to you — in te reo and in life?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Final grammar showcase**

Write YOUR version of the four-term grammar showcase passage.

- Write Ko + He sentences about your identity and your character.
- Write I + Kua + E…ana about your learning journey.
- Write Ka taea sentences about what you can do + Ko metaphor.
- ✦ Bonus: Write your full four-term grammar showcase (12 sentences) — every structure from the year, in service of something true about you and your te reo journey. End with Ka huri.

#### Wednesday: Hanga rerenga — Spiral: He mihi ki a tātou — A tribute to each other

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — AO 2.3: Extended communication · AO 2.5: Full vocabulary · AO 1.1: Formal acknowledgement*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Tribute writing; AO 1.1 — Formal acknowledgement

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Writing tributes to people who matter**

In Term 2 Week 8 you wrote tributes to people you admire using He tangata atawhai, He tangata pukumahi, and Ko ia te pou o tōku ao. Today we spiral back to that — but at Term 4 level, writing tributes that use the full year's vocabulary and express deep appreciation.

> **TERM 2 level:**
> Ko Hēmi tōku hoa pai rawa atu. He tangata atawhai, he tangata ngākaunui hoki ia.
> Ka ora tōku ngākau i tōna āhua.
> 
> **TERM 4 level:**
> Ko [name] tētahi o ngā tāngata nui rawa atu i tōku ao — ko ia he pou o tōku oranga.
> I āwhina ia i a au i ngā wā katoa e uaua ana ōku ara — e kore e [wareware](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wareware) ana i a au tōna [atawhai](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=atawhai).
> Ko tā tōna āhua mōhio ki a au ko te tikanga o te [toa takitini](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=toa+takitini) — ka toa tātou i a ia.
> Nō reira, tēnā koe [name] mō tāu āwhina, mō tāu aroha, mō tāu oranga ki a au.

The tribute spiral:

T2 level: He tangata [character] · Ko ia te pou · Ka ora tōku ngākau
T4 level: Ko ia he pou o tōku oranga · e kore e wareware ana · ko tā tōna āhua mōhio · tēnā koe mō tāu [X, Y, Z]

New tribute vocabulary:
**e kore e wareware ana i a au** — I will never forget (wareware = forget)
**ko tā tōna āhua mōhio ki a au** — what their character teaches me
**ka toa tātou i a ia** — we are victorious/strong through them
**mō tāu aroha** — for your love/compassion
**mō tāu oranga ki a au** — for your life force toward me

*Tip: Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina, mō tāu aroha, mō tāu oranga ki a au. Three mō phrases — three things you are grateful for. When you say someone's contributions in three, you are honouring them in the way te ao Māori honours everything important — three times, three ways.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Tribute writing**

Write a tribute to someone in this class — your teacher, a classmate, or someone who helped you learn te reo this year.

- Identity + character: Ko [name] tētahi o ngā tāngata nui rawa atu i tōku ao. He tangata [X], he tangata [Y] hoki ia.
- What they did: I āwhina ia i a au i [specific moment/way] — e kore e wareware ana i a au.
- What their character teaches: Ko tā tōna āhua mōhio ki a au ko te [lesson].
- Close: Nō reira, tēnā koe [name] mō tāu [X], mō tāu [Y], mō tāu [Z].

_Discussion prompt: Mō tāu oranga ki a au — for your life force toward me. Oranga (wellbeing/vitality) is something you can give to another person — through your presence, your manaakitanga, your care. Who has given their oranga to you this year in this classroom? How would you say it in te reo Māori?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He mihi ki tētahi**

Write a full tribute in te reo Māori to someone important to your learning journey this year.

- Identity + Ko ia he pou o tōku [oranga/ako/ngākau].
- What they did: I āwhina ia i a au i [specific moment] + e kore e wareware.
- What they taught you: Ko tā tōna āhua mōhio ki a au ko te [lesson].
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full tribute (8 sentences) — identity, character (2 traits shown not told), what they did, what they taught you, how they have changed you (Kua hou tōku whakaaro nō tōna āwhina), and close with Tēnā koe mō tāu [X, Y, Z]. Read it aloud to them if you can.

#### Thursday: Kōrero — Spiral: He kōrero ā-tau — A year in conversation

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · Final comprehensive spiral conversation*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Year spiral conversation; AO 2.5 — Full year vocabulary; AO 1.1 — Formal greetings

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The year in one conversation**

This is the penultimate Thursday lesson. Today we have a conversation that spirals through every term — a conversation that could only happen at the end of Year 5-6 te reo Māori, because it requires everything you have learned.

> **A:** Tēnā koe. Ko wai koe, ā he aha tō pepeha?
> **B:** [Full 9-line pepeha + Nō reira ko ahau ko B]
> **A:** Ka pai! I tīmata koe i hea i tō ako i te reo Māori?
> **B:** I tīmata au i Wāhanga Tahi me tētahi kupu — "Kia ora." Ināianei, e ako tonu ana au, kua tipu tōku reo ki tētahi mea nui ake.
> **A:** He aha ngā mea i uaua rawa atu ki a koe?
> **B:** I uaua ki a au ngā hanganga o te tense — Ko Kua, Ko Ka. Engari, ahakoa i uaua, i mahi tonu au nō te mea ko tōku moemoeā tōku māramatanga.
> **A:** He aha tāu whakataukī e tūāhuatia ana i a koe?
> **B:** "Ehara tāku toa i te toa takitahi, he toa takitini." Ko tā tēnā mōhio ko te mana o tēnei akomanga — ko tā tātou mahi tahi i āwhina i a mātou katoa.
> **A:** Ā koia! Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au. Mā te wā.
> **B:** Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koe. Mā te wā.

This conversation touches every term:
T1: "Kia ora" as the starting word
T2: I tīmata au (past) · kua tipu (perfect) · e ako tonu ana (E…ana)
T3: Whakataukī + ko tā tēnā mōhio · moemoeā
T4: ko tā tātou mahi tahi (possession) · he māramatanga · Mā te wā

New phrase:
**ko tā tātou mahi tahi** — our working together (ā-category possession of the group)
**i āwhina i a mātou katoa** — helped all of us

*Tip: "Kia ora" was your first word. Mā te wā is how you close the year. Between those two phrases is everything. That is your year in te reo Māori.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Year spiral conversation**

The final group conversation practice — every term represented, no scripts.

- T1: Tēnā koe + full pepeha from memory.
- T2: I tīmata au i… ināianei kua tipu tōku reo ki…
- T3: Whakataukī + Ko tā tēnā mōhio ko te…
- T4: Ko tāu kōrero he māramatanga ki a au + Mā te wā.

_Discussion prompt: "Kia ora" was your first word. What will your LAST word in te reo Māori be — not at the end of today, but someday, in your life? What do you hope to be saying in te reo Māori in five years? In twenty?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Year spiral conversation**

Have the full year spiral conversation with a partner — touch every term.

- Full pepeha + Nō reira ko ahau ko [name].
- Learning journey: I tīmata + Kua tipu + E ako tonu ana.
- Whakataukī + explanation + moemoeā.
- ✦ Bonus: Write the full conversation (16+ lines). After, write a reflection: He aha te mea i mihi ana koe ki a koe ake? (What did you appreciate about yourself?) Ka angitu koe.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ko te tikanga o te haka ko te whakamana i te tangata." What does whakamana mean?
  - to punish 
  - to empower / honour / give authority to ✅
  - to teach 
  - to separate 
- "E kore e wehewehea" — in the context of haka and aroha/kaha, what does this phrase mean?
  - it should be separated into parts 
  - it cannot be separated — the two things are inseparable ✅
  - it will never be performed again 
  - it does not exist 

**Tuesday questions**

- How many different grammar structures appear in the four-term showcase passage?
  - 5 
  - 9 
  - 17 ✅
  - 23 
- "Ko tōku moemoeā tōku māramatanga." What aspiration does this express?
  - My dream is wealth and success 
  - My dream is my understanding/clarity ✅
  - My dream is to become a teacher 
  - My dream is to travel 

**Wednesday questions**

- "E kore e wareware ana i a au tōna atawhai." What does wareware mean?
  - to forgive 
  - to remember 
  - to forget ✅
  - to thank 
- "Tēnā koe mō tāu āwhina, mō tāu aroha, mō tāu oranga ki a au." What is the significance of three mō phrases?
  - It is required by grammar 
  - Three phrases honour the person in the way te ao Māori honours important things — three times, three ways ✅
  - It makes the sentence longer 
  - Three is the number of people being thanked 

**Thursday questions**

- "I tīmata au i Wāhanga Tahi me tētahi kupu — Kia ora." What does this sentence reflect on?
  - The difficulty of te reo Māori 
  - The start of the learning journey — from a single word ✅
  - A complaint about Term 1 
  - The importance of greeting properly 
- In the year spiral conversation, what two phrases mark the beginning and end of the whole year?
  - "Tēnā koe" and "E noho rā" 
  - "Kia ora" as the first word and "Mā te wā" as the year-end farewell ✅
  - "Ko wai koe" and "Ka huri" 
  - "He aha" and "Ka mutu"

### Week 10

#### Monday: He rā whakanui — Celebration and culmination day

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · Whakaatu (presenting) · Full year celebrated*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Formal celebration; AO 2.3 — Full year expression; AO 2.5 — Complete vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — He rā whakanui: a day of celebration**

Welcome to the final week. This week is a celebration of what you have built. Today is whakanui — honouring and making great. We do not review or test. We celebrate, perform, and share.

> **The whakanui structure for today:**
> 
> **1. Mihi whakatūwhera:** Teacher opens with Tēnā koutou katoa + acknowledgement of the year.
> **2. Pepeha pōhiri:** Each student delivers their pepeha — class responds with Tēnā koe after each.
> **3. Waiata tahi:** Class sings a waiata together — Ka mate, or another known waiata.
> **4. Kōrero pūrākau:** Two or three students share their pūrākau from Term 4 Week 2.
> **5. He whakaaro:** Each student shares their favourite sentence of the year.
> **6. Mihi whakamutunga:** Teacher closes with Ko tā tātou mahi tahi i hanga ai tēnei — Ka nui ōku mihi ki a koutou katoa.

The language of celebration:

**He rā whakanui tēnei.** — This is a day of celebration.
**Ka whakanuitia ā tātou mahi.** — Our work is being honoured/celebrated.
**Ko tā tātou mahi tahi i hanga ai tēnei.**
This is what our working together has built.

**Mihi whakatūwhera:** opening tribute
**Mihi whakamutunga:** closing tribute
**whakanui** — to celebrate / to make great / to honour
**Ka whakanuitia** — is being celebrated/honoured (passive)
**He tino nui ōku mihi ki a koutou katoa.**
My tributes/thanks to you all are very great.

*Tip: Whakanui — to make great, to celebrate, to honour. When you celebrate someone's achievement, you are not just praising them — you are making them great in the eyes of everyone present. That is what whakanui does.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Whakanui preparation**

Prepare for the full class whakanui celebration.

- Pepeha: Every student practises their full pepeha one final time. No looking — from the heart.
- Favourite sentence: Write your favourite te reo sentence of the year. Say it to yourself. Own it.
- Pūrākau: Two volunteers prepare to share their pūrākau from Term 4 Week 2.
- Waiata: Class practices the opening of Ka Mate or another class waiata together.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tā tātou mahi tahi i hanga ai tēnei — this is what our working together has built. What has the class built together? Not just language skills — but what kind of learning community? What has this class been to you?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — Whakanui contribution**

Prepare your contribution to the class whakanui celebration.

- Your pepeha: write it from memory, check it, practise until it is completely yours.
- Your favourite sentence: write it, say why it is your favourite.
- A whakaaro for the class: Ko tā tō tātou akomanga hanga ai ko… (What your class has built is…)
- ✦ Bonus: Write a short mihi to the class (5 sentences) — acknowledge everyone, name three things the class has done together, and close with He tino nui ōku mihi ki a koutou katoa.

#### Tuesday: He whakaaturanga — The end-of-year performance

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · Whakaatu (presenting) — final performance*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Final performance; AO 1.1 — Formal presentation; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary performance

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — Your whakaaturanga: the full performance**

Today is the end-of-year whakaaturanga — each student performs their full presentation to the class. This is the Whakaatu (presenting) language mode in its fullest expression. You have been preparing for this all year.

> **The complete whakaaturanga structure:**
> 
> **1.** Tēnā koutou katoa.
> **2.** Ko [maunga] tōku maunga… [full pepeha — all 9 lines]
> **3.** Nō reira ko ahau ko [name].
> **4.** I tīmata au i Wāhanga Tahi me tētahi kupu — "Kia ora." Ināianei, kua tipu tōku reo ki tētahi mea nui ake.
> **5.** Ko ngā pou o tōku ora ko tōku whānau, ko tōku reo, ko tōku taiao.
> **6.** Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei: [your best sentence].
> **7.** Ko tōku whakataukī e tūāhuatia ana i a au ko tēnei: [whakataukī]. Ko tā tērā mōhio ko te [meaning].
> **8.** Ko tōku wawata: [your deepest aspiration for your reo].
> **9.** Tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou, tēnā koutou katoa.

Nine elements, approximately 90 seconds:

1. Formal opening (Tēnā koutou katoa)
2. Pepeha (all 9 lines)
3. Nō reira ko ahau (arrival at yourself)
4. Learning journey (I tīmata + Kua tipu)
5. Pillars of life (Ko ngā pou o tōku ora)
6. Best sentence (Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu)
7. Whakataukī + meaning (Ko tā tērā mōhio)
8. Wawata (deepest aspiration)
9. Formal close (Tēnā koutou ×3)

This is not a test. This is a gift — to yourself, to your class, and to te reo Māori.

*Tip: Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei — my best sentence of this year is this. When you say it, say it slowly. Let everyone hear it. It is yours. You made it. And it will exist in this classroom forever.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Final preparation**

Last preparation round — then the performances begin.

- Silent practice: 3 minutes. Eyes closed. Pepeha from memory, then the rest.
- Partner run-through: full whakaaturanga to your partner. They give one word of feedback.
- Class: who goes first? Volunteers? Or a karakia to open?
- Perform: each student delivers their whakaaturanga. Class responds with Tēnā koe after each.

_Discussion prompt: Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei. Before you perform — what IS your best sentence? Not the most impressive one. The most true. Say it quietly to yourself right now. Feel it. Now you are ready._

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whakaaturanga**

Deliver your whakaaturanga to the class.

- Deliver all nine elements — from Tēnā koutou katoa to Tēnā koutou ×3.
- After everyone has performed, write a mihi: Ko ngā rerenga i pā mai ki a au ko ēnei — [three classmates' sentences that moved you].
- Write your own sentence about the class: Ko tā tō tātou akomanga hanga ai ko…
- ✦ Bonus: Write a full reflection on delivering your whakaaturanga (6 sentences) — how it felt to say your pepeha in front of everyone, which part felt most true, what you want to remember about this moment.

#### Wednesday: He koha ki te reo — A gift to the language

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs · Creative expression · Final creative writing*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 2.3 — Creative gift to the language; AO 2.5 — Full year vocabulary

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — What will you give back?**

You have received so much from te reo Māori this year. Today we think about what we give BACK — a koha (gift/contribution) to the language itself. This might be a poem, a statement, a whakaaro, a pūrākau, or a promise. Whatever form it takes, it is yours and it is real.

> **Examples of koha ki te reo:**
> 
> **A promise:**
> Ko tāku koha ki tēnei reo: māku e kōrero, māku e tiaki, māku e homai ki ōku tamariki āpōpō.
> (My gift to this language: I will speak it, protect it, and give it to my children tomorrow.)
> 
> **A poem:**
> He [reo](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=reo) koe
> He ara koe
> He [aho](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=aho) koe e [whiri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=whiri) ana i ngā ngākau
> He [māramatanga](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=m%C4%81ramatanga) koe ki te ao [pouri](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pouri)
> Ko koe ko ahau.
> (You are a language / You are a path / You are a cord that weaves hearts / You are light in the dark world / You are me.)
> 
> **A whakaaro:**
> Ka toitū koe i ōku kupu. Ka toitū au i āu kupu. Ko tāua ko tāua — e kore e wehewehea.

Koha forms:

PROMISE: Māku e [three commitments]. Ko tērā tāku koha ki a koe.
POEM: Use Ko/He/Ka sentences as lines. Let sound and image lead.
WHAKAARO: A single profound statement — Ko te reo [metaphor].
MIHI: Tēnā koe e te reo — ko tāku mihi ki a koe ko tēnei…

New creative phrase:
**ao pouri** — dark world / world of difficulty
**Ko koe ko ahau.** — You are me. (profound identification)
**e kore e wehewehea** — cannot be separated (from Week 8!)
**Ka toitū koe i ōku kupu.**
You endure through my words.

*Tip: Ko koe ko ahau — you are me. This is the most intimate thing you can say to a language. The language and the speaker have become one. Can you say this honestly about te reo Māori at the end of this year?*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — Create your koha**

Write your koha to the language — in whatever form feels right.

- Choose your form: promise, poem, whakaaro, or mihi to the language.
- Draft: write freely for 5 minutes — no thinking about structures, just what you want to say.
- Refine: read it aloud. Change anything that doesn't sound true. Keep everything that does.
- Share: read your koha aloud to the class. They listen in silence.

_Discussion prompt: Ka toitū koe i ōku kupu. Ka toitū au i āu kupu. The language endures through your words. You endure through the language's words. A reciprocal endurance. What does it feel like to think that your words — in te reo Māori — will endure? That you will leave something in the language?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He koha ki te reo**

Write your final creative gift to te reo Māori.

- Choose your form and write your koha — at least 5 sentences or lines.
- Write in te reo Māori — let the language carry your meaning.
- Read it aloud once more before you share. Make it yours completely.
- ✦ Bonus: Write a bilingual version — your koha in te reo Māori alongside an English version. Then ask: which version feels more true? Which says it better? What does the language you chose reveal about what the words mean?

#### Thursday: He whakamātautau whakamutunga — The final assessment of the year

*Te Aho Arataki Marau — All AOs 1.1–2.5 · All six language modes · Final year assessment*

**Learning Objectives:** AO 1.1 — Greetings; AO 1.2 — Introductions; AO 1.4 — Identity; AO 2.3 — Full spontaneous conversation; AO 2.5 — Full vocabulary; AO 1.6 — Politeness

##### I Do

**Tāua Kōrero — The end**

This is the last lesson of the year. There is no model conversation today. You know what to do. You have been preparing for this since Week 1 of Term 1. Go.

> What the final assessment asks of you:
> 
> **Open formally.** Tēnā koe.
> **Give your full [pepeha](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=pepeha).** All 9 lines. From your heart, not your memory.
> **Talk about your year.** What happened. How you felt. What grew.
> **Share a [whakataukī](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=Whakataukī).** The one that represents you. Explain its meaning.
> **State your [wawata](https://maoridictionary.co.nz/search?keywords=wawata).** Your deepest aspiration for your reo and your life.
> **Close.** Ka nui ōku mihi. Mā te wā.
> 
> That is the assessment. Not what structures you use — but whether you mean what you say.

What the assessor is listening for:

✓ Does this person MEAN what they are saying?
✓ Is the language NATURAL — not performed but inhabited?
✓ Do the structures SERVE the meaning, rather than the meaning serving the structures?
✓ Is the pepeha delivered with confidence and pride?
✓ Is the whakataukī CHOSEN, not recited?
✓ Is the wawata GENUINE — something the speaker actually aspires to?
✓ Is the farewell warm and complete?

You do not need to be perfect. You need to be present.

Ka angitu koe.

*Tip: Ka angitu koe. You will succeed. Not because you are ready — but because you have been ready since before you knew it. The year built you for this moment. Trust it.*

##### We Do

**Kia Tūhono — He karakia whakamutunga**

Before the assessments begin, the class says a karakia together — an acknowledgement and closing of the year.

- Together: **Unuhia, unuhia, unuhia ki uta!** (Lift it, lift it, lift it to the shore!)
- Together: **Kia wātea, kia māmā.** (Let it be free, let it be light.)
- Together: **Āe rā.** (Indeed.)
- Then: assessments begin. Each student takes their turn.

_Discussion prompt: Kia wātea, kia māmā — let it be free, let it be light. The karakia releases the class from the constraints of ordinary learning into the freedom of genuine expression. What does it mean to be "free and light" in a language? Have you felt that this year, even once?_

##### You Do

**Māu Anō — He whakamātautau whakamutunga**

Your final assessment. Your last act in te reo Māori this year.

- **Full spontaneous conversation** — no script. At least 16 lines. Every domain.
- **Self-assessment:** He aha tōku pai? He aha tōku uauatanga? He aha tāku ka ako tonu ai?
- **Final written record:** Ko tōku rerenga whakamutunga o tēnei tau ko tēnei: [your final sentence].
- ✦ Final checklist: ☐ Pepeha (9 lines, from the heart) · ☐ Learning journey (I/Kua/E…ana) · ☐ Whakataukī + meaning · ☐ Wawata (genuine) · ☐ Ka nui ōku mihi · ☐ Mā te wā · ☐ The whole thing meant.

#### Friday: Aromatawai — Quiz & Review


**Monday questions**

- "Ka whakanuitia ā tātou mahi." What does whakanui mean?
  - to test and grade 
  - to review and correct 
  - to celebrate / honour / make great ✅
  - to repeat and practise 
- "Ko tā tātou mahi tahi i hanga ai tēnei." What does this sentence reflect on?
  - The teacher's individual work 
  - What their working together has built as a class ✅
  - A past event from Term 1 
  - A future plan for next year 

**Tuesday questions**

- How many elements does the final whakaaturanga have?
  - 5 
  - 7 
  - 9 ✅
  - 12 
- "Ko tōku rerenga pai rawa atu i tēnei tau ko tēnei." What is the speaker doing?
  - Asking for the best sentence 
  - Sharing their finest sentence of the year ✅
  - Explaining a whakataukī 
  - Describing their school 

**Wednesday questions**

- "Ko koe ko ahau." In the context of language, what does this mean?
  - You and I are different 
  - You are my teacher 
  - The language and the speaker have become one — a profound identification ✅
  - I do not know you 
- "Ka toitū koe i ōku kupu. Ka toitū au i āu kupu." What relationship does this describe?
  - A competitive relationship between speaker and language 
  - A reciprocal endurance — the language endures through your words, you endure through the language's words ✅
  - A one-way gift from language to speaker 
  - A past memory 

**Thursday questions**

- The final assessment asks whether you MEAN what you say. What does this reveal about the purpose of language learning?
  - Grammar accuracy is the only goal 
  - Language learning is about performing for assessment 
  - Genuine expression — inhabiting the language rather than just performing it — is the highest achievement ✅
  - Vocabulary size determines success 
- "Kia wātea, kia māmā." What does this karakia invoke?
  - Difficulty and challenge 
  - Speed and accuracy 
  - Freedom and lightness — releasing from constraint into genuine expression ✅
  - Silence and stillness
